\chapter{Introduction to Binary Search}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,
    colback=white,
    colframe=secondaryblue,
    boxrule=1pt,
    arc=2mm,
    left=10pt,
    right=10pt
]
\textbf{\large\textcolor{primaryblue}{Basics, Algorithm Understanding, and Simple Applications}}
\end{tcolorbox}

\vspace{0.5cm}

\begin{center}
\textit{\textcolor{darkgray}{Difficulty: Beginner to CF 900 $\bullet$ 20 Problems}}
\end{center}

\section{Core Concept: Divide and Conquer}

Binary Search is an efficient algorithm for finding an item from a **sorted** list of items. It works by repeatedly dividing in half the portion of the list that could contain the item, until you've narrowed down the possible locations to just one.



\subsection{Why It Works}
In a sorted array, the value at the middle index tells us everything we need to know:
\begin{itemize}
    \item If $arr[mid] == target$, we are done.
    \item If $arr[mid] < target$, since the array is sorted, the target must be to the right. We discard the left half.
    \item If $arr[mid] > target$, the target must be to the left. We discard the right half.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Complexity Analysis}
Binary search significantly reduces the number of comparisons. For an array of size $n$, while linear search takes $O(n)$ time, binary search takes $O(\log n)$. 

\section{Implementation Techniques}

\subsection{Iterative Approach (Recommended)}
The iterative approach is usually preferred in competitive programming because it avoids the overhead of function calls and potential stack overflow for very large search spaces.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced, colback=darkgray!5!white, colframe=darkgray, title=C++ Template]
\begin{verbatim}
int low = 0, high = n - 1;
while (low <= high) {
    int mid = low + (high - low) / 2; // Prevents overflow
    if (arr[mid] == target) return mid;
    if (arr[mid] < target) low = mid + 1;
    else high = mid - 1;
}
return -1; // Not found
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Introduction to Binary Search: Complete Mastery}

\subsection{Complete Theoretical Foundation}

\subsubsection{Why Binary Search?}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=white,
    colframe=deepblue,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{The Divide and Conquer Paradigm}
]
\textbf{Why Binary Search Matters?}

Binary search is one of the most fundamental algorithms in computer science

\textbf{The Problem:}
\begin{verbatim}
Given: Sorted array of N elements
Task: Find target element (or determine it doesn't exist)

Linear search approach:
  Check each element: arr[0], arr[1], ..., arr[N-1]
  Time: O(N)
  For N=1,000,000: Up to 1 million comparisons!

Binary search approach:
  Use sortedness: Eliminate half the search space each step
  Time: O(log N)
  For N=1,000,000: Only ~20 comparisons!
  
Speedup: 50,000× faster!
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Core Insight:}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{|l|c|c|}
\hline
\rowcolor{lightgray}
\textbf{Array Size} & \textbf{Linear Search} & \textbf{Binary Search} \\
\hline
100 & 100 & 7 \\
\hline
1,000 & 1,000 & 10 \\
\hline
1,000,000 & 1,000,000 & 20 \\
\hline
1,000,000,000 & 1,000,000,000 & 30 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\textbf{Key Concepts:}

1. \textbf{The Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Prerequisite: Array must be SORTED

Algorithm:
  Initialize: low = 0, high = N-1
  
  while low <= high:
    mid = low + (high - low) / 2
    
    if arr[mid] == target:
      return mid  // Found!
    
    if arr[mid] < target:
      low = mid + 1  // Target in right half
    else:
      high = mid - 1  // Target in left half
  
  return -1  // Not found

Why this works:
  - Array sorted: arr[i] ≤ arr[j] for all i < j
  - At mid: We know exact position relative to target
  - Eliminate half: Guaranteed to contain/not contain target
  - Repeat: Search space halves each iteration
\end{verbatim}

2. \textbf{Correctness Proof:}
\begin{verbatim}
Loop invariant: If target exists, it's in arr[low..high]

Base case: Initially low=0, high=N-1
  If target exists, it's somewhere in array ✓

Inductive step:
  Suppose invariant holds at iteration start
  
  Case 1: arr[mid] == target
    Found! Return mid ✓
  
  Case 2: arr[mid] < target
    Since sorted: arr[i] < target for all i ≤ mid
    Therefore: target must be in arr[mid+1..high]
    Set low = mid+1
    Invariant maintained ✓
  
  Case 3: arr[mid] > target
    Since sorted: arr[i] > target for all i ≥ mid
    Therefore: target must be in arr[low..mid-1]
    Set high = mid-1
    Invariant maintained ✓

Termination:
  Each iteration: (high - low) decreases
  Eventually: low > high
  If target existed, would have been found
  Therefore: target doesn't exist ✓

Algorithm correct! ✓
\end{verbatim}

3. \textbf{Complexity Analysis:}
\begin{verbatim}
Time: O(log N)
  Each iteration: Constant work O(1)
  Number of iterations: O(log N)
  
  Why log N iterations:
    Start: N elements
    After 1: N/2 elements
    After 2: N/4 elements
    After k: N/2^k elements
    
    Stop when: N/2^k = 1
    Solve: $k = \log_2(N)$
    
    Therefore: O(log N) iterations

Space: O(1)
  Only use constant extra variables: low, high, mid
  No recursion stack (iterative version)

Comparison with alternatives:
  Linear search: O(N) time
  Binary search: O(log N) time
  
  For N=1M:
    Linear: 1,000,000 operations
    Binary: 20 operations
    Speedup: 50,000×!
\end{verbatim}

4. \textbf{Common Pitfalls:}
\begin{verbatim}
Pitfall 1: Integer overflow
  WRONG: mid = (low + high) / 2
    If low + high > INT_MAX: Overflow!
  
  CORRECT: mid = low + (high - low) / 2
    Equivalent but no overflow

Pitfall 2: Wrong loop condition
  WRONG: while (low < high)
    Misses case when low == high
  
  CORRECT: while (low <= high)
    Checks all possibilities

Pitfall 3: Off-by-one errors
  WRONG: low = mid or high = mid
    Infinite loop possible!
  
  CORRECT: low = mid + 1 or high = mid - 1
    Always makes progress

Pitfall 4: Unsorted array
  Binary search REQUIRES sorted array
  If unsorted: Results meaningless
  Must sort first (if allowed)

Pitfall 5: Floating point mid
  WRONG: mid = (low + high) / 2.0
    Integer division needed!
  
  CORRECT: mid = (low + high) / 2
    Integer arithmetic
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Key Insight:}
Binary search trades preprocessing (sorting) for fast queries!
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Pattern 1: Basic Search}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=successgreen!10,
    colframe=successgreen,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Basic Binary Search}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Standard Existence Check}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Given sorted array, find if target exists

Template:
  int binarySearch(vector<int>& arr, int target) {
    int low = 0;
    int high = arr.size() - 1;
    
    while (low <= high) {
      int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
      
      if (arr[mid] == target) {
        return mid;  // Found at index mid
      }
      
      if (arr[mid] < target) {
        low = mid + 1;  // Search right half
      } else {
        high = mid - 1;  // Search left half
      }
    }
    
    return -1;  // Not found
  }

Example: arr=[1,3,5,7,9], target=7

Iteration 1:
  low=0, high=4
  mid=2, arr[2]=5
  5 < 7, search right
  low=3

Iteration 2:
  low=3, high=4
  mid=3, arr[3]=7
  7 == 7, found!
  return 3 ✓

Time: O(log N)
Space: O(1)
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Type 2: Search Insert Position}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Find index where target should be inserted

Key insight: Return low when not found
  When loop ends with low > high:
    low = position where target belongs

Algorithm:
  int searchInsert(vector<int>& arr, int target) {
    int low = 0;
    int high = arr.size() - 1;
    
    while (low <= high) {
      int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
      
      if (arr[mid] == target) {
        return mid;  // Found
      }
      
      if (arr[mid] < target) {
        low = mid + 1;
      } else {
        high = mid - 1;
      }
    }
    
    return low;  // Insert position
  }

Why low is correct:
  Loop ends: low > high
  Specifically: low = high + 1
  
  All elements before low: < target
  All elements from low: ≥ target
  
  Therefore: Insert at position low

Example: arr=[1,3,5,7], target=4

Search for 4:
  Not found
  Loop ends: low=2, high=1
  
  arr[0]=1 < 4 ✓
  arr[1]=3 < 4 ✓
  arr[2]=5 > 4 ✓
  
  Insert at position 2 ✓

Result: [1,3,4,5,7]

Time: O(log N)
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Type 3: First Bad Version}

\textbf{Complete Example:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Find first version where test fails
  1, 2, ..., N versions
  All good until first bad
  Find first bad version

Pattern: Find first position where condition true

Algorithm:
  int firstBadVersion(int n) {
    int low = 1;
    int high = n;
    int result = n;
    
    while (low <= high) {
      int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
      
      if (isBadVersion(mid)) {
        result = mid;  // This could be first bad
        high = mid - 1;  // Check if earlier bad exists
      } else {
        low = mid + 1;  // mid is good, search right
      }
    }
    
    return result;
  }

Key insight: Track best answer so far
  When find bad version: Could be first
  Keep searching left for earlier bad

Example: versions=1,2,3,4,5, first_bad=3

Iteration 1:
  low=1, high=5, mid=3
  isBad(3)=true
  result=3, high=2

Iteration 2:
  low=1, high=2, mid=1
  isBad(1)=false
  low=2

Iteration 3:
  low=2, high=2, mid=2
  isBad(2)=false
  low=3

Loop ends: low=3, high=2
Return result=3 ✓

Time: O(log N)
Queries: O(log N) API calls
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Basic Binary Search Solutions]
// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 1.1: LeetCode 704 - Binary Search
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/binary-search/
// Difficulty: Easy
// Technique: Standard Binary Search
// ===============================================================

int search(vector<int>& nums, int target) {
    int low = 0;
    int high = nums.size() - 1;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        // Prevent overflow: Don't use (low + high) / 2
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (nums[mid] == target) {
            return mid;
        }
        
        if (nums[mid] < target) {
            low = mid + 1;  // Target in right half
        } else {
            high = mid - 1;  // Target in left half
        }
    }
    
    return -1;  // Not found
}

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

Standard binary search on sorted array
  - Array is sorted ascending
  - Each comparison eliminates half the search space
  - Guaranteed to find target if exists

Loop invariant: If target exists, it's in nums[low..high]

Base case: low=0, high=N-1
  If target exists, it's in array 

Maintenance:
  If nums[mid] < target:
    All elements <= mid are < target
    Target must be in nums[mid+1..high]
    Set low = mid+1 
  
  If nums[mid] > target:
    All elements >= mid are > target
    Target must be in nums[low..mid-1]
    Set high = mid-1 

Termination:
  low > high: Search space empty
  If target existed, would have found it
  Return -1 

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(log N)
  Each iteration: O(1) operations
  Iterations: $\log_2(N)$
  Total: O(log N)

Space: O(1)
  Only three variables: low, high, mid
  No recursion, no extra arrays

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Linear search:
   Time: O(N)
   For N=1M: 1M operations
   
   Our solution: $\log_2(1M)$ = 20 operations
   50,000X faster!

2. Recursive binary search:
   Time: O(log N) same
   Space: O(log N) call stack
   
   Our solution: O(1) space
   More space efficient

3. Jump search:
   Time: $O(\sqrt{N})$
   Works on sorted arrays
   
   Our solution: O(log N) < $O(\sqrt{N})$
   Faster asymptotically

4. Interpolation search:
   Time: O(log log N) best case
   O(N) worst case
   
   Our solution: O(log N) guaranteed
   More predictable

Trace for nums=[-1,0,3,5,9,12], target=9:

Iteration 1:
  low=0, high=5
  mid=2, nums[2]=3
  3 < 9, search right
  low=3

Iteration 2:
  low=3, high=5
  mid=4, nums[4]=9
  9 == 9, found!
  return 4 

Verification: nums[4] = 9 

Only 2 comparisons for array of 6 elements!
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 1.3: LeetCode 35 - Search Insert Position
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/search-insert-position/
// Difficulty: Easy
// Technique: Binary Search with Insert Position
// ===============================================================

int searchInsert(vector<int>& nums, int target) {
    int low = 0;
    int high = nums.size() - 1;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (nums[mid] == target) {
            return mid;  // Exact match
        }
        
        if (nums[mid] < target) {
            low = mid + 1;
        } else {
            high = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    
    // Not found: low is insertion position
    return low;
}

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

Problem: Find index to insert target maintaining sorted order

Key insight: When binary search fails
  Loop terminates: low > high
  Specifically: low = high + 1
  
  At this point:
    nums[0..high] < target
    nums[low..N-1] >= target
  
  Therefore: Insert at position low

Algorithm correctness:
  Case 1: target exists
    Found during search
    Return exact index 
  
  Case 2: target doesn't exist
    Loop ends with low > high
    low = correct insertion position 

Why low is insertion position:
  When loop ends:
    - All elements before low: < target
    - All elements from low: > target
  
  Insert at low maintains sorted order:
    nums[0..low-1] < target < nums[low..N-1]

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(log N)
  Binary search: O(log N)
  Return low: O(1)
  Total: O(log N)

Space: O(1)

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Linear search:
   for (int i = 0; i < N; i++)
     if (nums[i] >= target) return i
   
   Time: O(N)
   Our solution: O(log N)
   
   For N=1M: 50,000X faster!

2. Insert and sort:
   nums.insert(nums.end(), target)
   sort(nums.begin(), nums.end())
   find position
   
   Time: O(N log N)
   Our solution: O(log N)
   
   Much slower!

3. STL lower_bound:
   return lower_bound(nums.begin(), nums.end(), target) - nums.begin()
   
   Time: O(log N) same
   But: Less educational, same complexity
   
   Our solution: Shows understanding

Trace for nums=[1,3,5,6], target=5:

Iteration 1:
  low=0, high=3
  mid=1, nums[1]=3
  3 < 5, search right
  low=2

Iteration 2:
  low=2, high=3
  mid=2, nums[2]=5
  5 == 5, found!
  return 2 

Trace for nums=[1,3,5,6], target=2:

Iteration 1:
  low=0, high=3
  mid=1, nums[1]=3
  3 > 2, search left
  high=0

Iteration 2:
  low=0, high=0
  mid=0, nums[0]=1
  1 < 2, search right
  low=1

Loop ends: low=1, high=0
  nums[0]=1 < 2 
  nums[1]=3 > 2 

Return low=1 
Insert position correct!

Result: [1,2,3,5,6]
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 1.7: LeetCode 278 - First Bad Version
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/first-bad-version/
// Difficulty: Easy
// Technique: Binary Search for Threshold
// ===============================================================

// Forward declaration of API
bool isBadVersion(int version);

int firstBadVersion(int n) {
    int low = 1;
    int high = n;
    int result = n;  // Default to last version
    
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (isBadVersion(mid)) {
            // This is a bad version, but might not be first
            result = mid;
            high = mid - 1;  // Check for earlier bad version
        } else {
            // This is good, first bad must be after
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

Problem: Find FIRST version where test fails
  Versions: 1, 2, 3, ..., N
  Pattern: [good, good, ..., bad, bad, ...]
  Find: First "bad"

Key insight: Find leftmost position where condition true
  Standard binary search: Finds any occurrence
  This: Finds FIRST occurrence

Algorithm:
  Track best answer: result
  When find bad version:
    - Could be first, save it
    - But search left for earlier bad
  
  When find good version:
    - First bad must be to the right
    - Search right

Loop invariant: First bad is in [low, high] or is result

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(log N)
  Binary search iterations: log N
  Each iteration: O(1) + one API call
  Total: O(log N) API calls

Space: O(1)

API calls minimized:
  Only log N calls to isBadVersion()
  Linear search would take O(N) calls
  
  For N=1M: 20 calls vs 1M calls
  50,000X fewer API calls!

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Linear search from start:
   for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++)
     if (isBadVersion(i)) return i
   
   Time: O(N)
   API calls: O(N)
   
   Our solution: O(log N)
   Much more efficient!

2. Binary search without tracking result:
   Can miss first bad if not careful
   
   Our solution: Always tracks best answer
   Guaranteed correct

3. Search from end backwards:
   for (int i = n; i >= 1; i--)
     if (!isBadVersion(i)) return i+1
   
   Time: O(N) worst case
   Inefficient

Pattern recognition: Find first position where condition true
  Common in: threshold problems, first occurrence
  Solution: Binary search + track best answer + search left

Trace for n=5, first bad=4:
  Versions: [G, G, G, B, B]
            [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]

Iteration 1:
  low=1, high=5, mid=3
  isBad(3)=false (good)
  low=4

Iteration 2:
  low=4, high=5, mid=4
  isBad(4)=true (bad)
  result=4, high=3

Loop ends: low=4, high=3
Return result=4 

API calls: Only 2!
Linear would need 4 calls

First bad version found efficiently! 
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 1.19: LeetCode 69 - Sqrt(x)
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/sqrtx/
// Difficulty: Easy
// Technique: Binary Search on Answer
// ===============================================================

int mySqrt(int x) {
    if (x < 2) return x;
    
    int low = 1;
    int high = x / 2;  // sqrt(x) <= x/2 for x >= 2
    int result = 0;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        // Use division to avoid overflow: mid * mid might overflow
        if (mid <= x / mid) {
            result = mid;  // mid might be answer
            low = mid + 1;  // Try larger values
        } else {
            high = mid - 1;  // mid too large
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

Problem: Integer square root
  Find largest k such that $k^2$ <= x

Binary search on answer:
  Search space: [1, x/2]
  Monotonic: If $k^2$ > x, then $(k+1)^2$ > x too
  
  Find largest k where $k^2$ <= x

Algorithm correctness:
  Track best answer: result
  For each mid:
    If $mid^2$ <= x: mid could be answer, try larger
    If $mid^2$ > x: mid too large, try smaller

Why mid <= x/mid instead of mid*mid <= x:
  mid*mid can overflow for large x
  Division equivalent but safe
  
  $mid^2$ <= x <==> mid <= x/mid

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(log X)
  Search space: [1, X/2]
  Binary search: log(X/2) = O(log X)

Space: O(1)

For X=$10^9$:
  Only ~30 iterations
  vs Newton's method: Similar but more complex

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Linear search:
   for (int i = 1; i <= x; i++)
     if (i * i > x) return i-1
   
   Time: $O(\sqrt{X})$
   For X=1$10^9$: 31,623 iterations
   
   Our solution: 30 iterations
   1000X faster!

2. Built-in sqrt():
   return (int)sqrt(x)
   
   Not allowed (defeats purpose)
   Less educational

3. Newton's method:
   Iterative: x_{n+1} = (x_n + X/x_n) / 2
   
   Time: O(log X) similar
   Space: O(1)
   
   But: More complex, harder to implement correctly
   Our solution: Simpler, equally efficient

4. Exponential search + binary search:
   Find range first, then binary search
   
   Time: O(log X) same
   Complexity: Higher
   
   Our solution: Direct, simpler

Trace for x=8:
  Need: $\lfloor \sqrt{8} \rfloor = \lfloor 2.828\ldots \rfloor = 2$

Iteration 1:
  low=1, high=4, mid=2
  2 <= 8/2=4 \ding{51} ($2^2$ = 4 <= 8)
  result=2, low=3

Iteration 2:
  low=3, high=4, mid=3
  3 <= 8/3=2 \ding{55} ($3^2$ = 9 > 8)
  high=2

Loop ends: low=3, high=2
Return result=2 \ding{51}

Verification: 2^2 = 4 <= 8 \ding{51}
             3^2 = 9 > 8 \ding{51}

Correct integer square root!
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 2: Simple Applications}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=lightgray,
    colframe=accentorange,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Application Patterns}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Perfect Square Check}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Check if number is perfect square

Approach: Binary search for exact square root

Algorithm:
  bool isPerfectSquare(int num) {
    if (num < 2) return true;
    
    long low = 2;
    long high = num / 2;
    
    while (low <= high) {
      long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
      long square = mid * mid;
      
      if (square == num) {
        return true;  // Exact square!
      }
      
      if (square < num) {
        low = mid + 1;
      } else {
        high = mid - 1;
      }
    }
    
    return false;
  }

Why use long:
  mid * mid can exceed int range
  Use long to prevent overflow

Example: num=16
  Find: 4 such that 4² = 16

Binary search:
  low=2, high=8
  mid=5, 5²=25 > 16, high=4
  mid=3, 3²=9 < 16, low=4
  mid=4, 4²=16 = 16 ✓
  
Return true!

Time: O(log N)
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Type 2: Peak Element}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Find peak in mountain array
  Mountain: Increases then decreases
  Peak: arr[i-1] < arr[i] > arr[i+1]

Key insight: Compare with neighbors

Algorithm:
  int peakIndexInMountainArray(vector<int>& arr) {
    int low = 0;
    int high = arr.size() - 1;
    
    while (low < high) {  // Note: < not <=
      int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
      
      if (arr[mid] < arr[mid + 1]) {
        // Ascending: peak is to the right
        low = mid + 1;
      } else {
        // Descending: peak is mid or to left
        high = mid;
      }
    }
    
    return low;  // low == high at peak
  }

Why low < high (not <=):
  Always comparing mid with mid+1
  Need mid+1 to be valid
  
  When low == high: Found peak

Why different template:
  Standard: Finds exact match
  Peak: Finds position via comparisons
  
  Result: Slightly different loop

Example: arr=[0,2,1,0]
  Peak should be index 1 (value 2)

Binary search:
  low=0, high=3, mid=1
  arr[1]=2 > arr[2]=1 (descending)
  high=1
  
  low=0, high=1, mid=0
  arr[0]=0 < arr[1]=2 (ascending)
  low=1
  
  low=1, high=1, done
  
Return 1 ✓

Time: O(log N)
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Type 3: Arranging Coins}

\textbf{Complete Example:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: N coins arranged in staircase
  Row k has k coins
  Find maximum complete rows

Mathematical: Find largest k where:
  1 + 2 + ... + k ≤ N
  k(k+1)/2 ≤ N

Binary search on answer:
  Search for largest valid k

Algorithm:
  int arrangeCoins(int n) {
    long low = 0;
    long high = n;
    
    while (low <= high) {
      long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
      long coins_needed = mid * (mid + 1) / 2;
      
      if (coins_needed <= n) {
        low = mid + 1;  // mid rows possible
      } else {
        high = mid - 1;  // mid rows too many
      }
    }
    
    return high;  // Largest valid k
  }

Why return high (not low):
  Loop ends: low = high + 1
  high = largest k where k(k+1)/2 ≤ N
  
  That's exactly what we want!

Example: n=8
  Coins: O
         O O
         O O O
         O O (incomplete)
  
  Complete rows: 3

Binary search:
  Find k where k(k+1)/2 ≤ 8
  
  k=3: 3×4/2 = 6 ≤ 8 ✓
  k=4: 4×5/2 = 10 > 8 ✗
  
Answer: 3 ✓

Alternative: Quadratic formula
  k = (-1 + √(1 + 8n)) / 2
  But: Floating point issues
  
  Binary search: Integer, robust

Time: O(log N)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Application Solutions]
// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 1.18: LeetCode 367 - Valid Perfect Square
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/valid-perfect-square/
// Difficulty: Easy
// Technique: Binary Search for Exact Value
// ===============================================================

bool isPerfectSquare(int num) {
    if (num < 2) return true;  // 0 and 1 are perfect squares
    
    long low = 2;
    long high = num / 2;  // sqrt(num) <= num/2 for num >= 2
    
    while (low <= high) {
        long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        long square = mid * mid;
        
        if (square == num) {
            return true;  // Found exact square root
        }
        
        if (square < num) {
            low = mid + 1;
        } else {
            high = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    
    return false;  // No integer square root exists
}

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

Problem: Determine if num = $k^2$ for some integer k

Binary search for exact match:
  Search space: [2, num/2]
  Target: k where $k^2$ = num
  
  If found: Perfect square 
  If not found: Not perfect square 

Why use long:
  mid * mid can overflow int
  For num = $2^31$-1: mid = 46340
  $mid^2$ = 2 \times $10^9$ fits in long
  
  Use long to prevent overflow

Algorithm correctness:
  Try all possible square roots in range
  Binary search: O(log N) efficient
  
  If k^2 = num exists: Will find it
  If doesn't exist: Search completes without finding

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(log N)
  Search space: [2, N/2]
  Binary search: log(N/2) = O(log N)

Space: O(1)

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Using sqrt():
   return (int)sqrt(num) * (int)sqrt(num) == num
   
   Problem: Floating point precision
   sqrt(4) might return 1.9999999 or 2.0000001
   
   Our solution: Pure integer arithmetic
   No precision issues!

2. Linear search:
   for (long i = 2; i <= num/2; i++)
     if (i * i == num) return true
   
   Time: O(N)
   For N=$10^9$: Billions of iterations
   
   Our solution: ~30 iterations
   MillionX faster!

3. Newton's method:
   Iterative approximation
   
   Time: O(log N) similar
   Complexity: Higher
   Precision issues possible
   
   Our solution: Simpler, robust

Mathematical property:
  If num is perfect square:
    Exactly one k where $k^2$ = num
  
  If not:
    No integer k satisfies $k^2$ = num
  
  Binary search finds this definitively

Trace for num=16:

Iteration 1:
  low=2, high=8, mid=5
  $5^2$ = 25 > 16
  high=4

Iteration 2:
  low=2, high=4, mid=3
  $3^2$ = 9 < 16
  low=4

Iteration 3:
  low=4, high=4, mid=4
  $4^2$ = 16 = 16 
  return true

Found: 16 = $4^2$

Trace for num=14:

Iteration 1:
  low=2, high=7, mid=4
  $4^2$ = 16 > 14
  high=3

Iteration 2:
  low=2, high=3, mid=2
  $2^2$ = 4 < 14
  low=3

Iteration 3:
  low=3, high=3, mid=3
  $3^2$ = 9 < 14
  low=4

Loop ends: low=4, high=3
return false

14 is not a perfect square 
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 1.20: LeetCode 852 - Peak Index in Mountain Array
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/peak-index-in-a-mountain-array/
// Difficulty: Easy
// Technique: Binary Search on Bitonic Array
// ===============================================================

int peakIndexInMountainArray(vector<int>& arr) {
    int low = 0;
    int high = arr.size() - 1;
    
    while (low < high) {  // Note: < not <=
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (arr[mid] < arr[mid + 1]) {
            // We're in ascending part, peak is ahead
            low = mid + 1;
        } else {
            // We're in descending part or at peak
            // Peak is at mid or before
            high = mid;
        }
    }
    
    return low;  // low == high at peak
}

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

Mountain array property:
  arr[0] < arr[1] < ... < arr[peak] > ... > arr[N-2] > arr[N-1]
  
  Guaranteed to have exactly one peak

Key insight: Binary search on comparison
  If arr[mid] < arr[mid+1]: Ascending, peak ahead
  If arr[mid] > arr[mid+1]: Descending, peak at/before mid

Algorithm correctness:
  Loop invariant: Peak is in range [low, high]
  
  Base case: Initially [0, N-1]
    Peak exists in array 
  
  Maintenance:
    If ascending at mid:
      Peak must be in (mid, high]
      Set low = mid+1 
    
    If descending at mid:
      Peak must be in [low, mid]
      Set high = mid 
  
  Termination: low == high
    Converged to peak position 

Why use low < high (not <=):
  Always access arr[mid+1]
  Need mid+1 to be valid index
  
  When low == high: Found peak, done

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(log N)
  Each iteration: Halve search space
  Iterations: log N

Space: O(1)

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Linear search:
   for (int i = 0; i < N-1; i++)
     if (arr[i] > arr[i+1]) return i
   
   Time: O(N)
   Our solution: O(log N)
   
   For N=1M: 50,000X faster!

2. Check all elements:
   for each i: check if arr[i-1] < arr[i] > arr[i+1]
   
   Time: O(N)
   Unnecessary when binary search works

3. Ternary search:
   Divides into three parts
   
   Time: O(log_3 (N))
   Constants worse than binary
   
   Our solution: Simpler, equally good

Peak finding applications:
  - Find local maximum
  - Detect change points
  - Mountain/valley detection

Trace for arr=[0,1,0]:
  Peak at index 1

Iteration 1:
  low=0, high=2, mid=1
  arr[1]=1 > arr[2]=0
  Descending, peak at/before 1
  high=1

Iteration 2:
  low=0, high=1, mid=0
  arr[0]=0 < arr[1]=1
  Ascending, peak after 0
  low=1

Loop ends: low=1, high=1
Return 1 

Trace for arr=[0,2,1,0]:
  Peak at index 1

Iteration 1:
  low=0, high=3, mid=1
  arr[1]=2 > arr[2]=1
  Descending
  high=1

Iteration 2:
  low=0, high=1, mid=0
  arr[0]=0 < arr[1]=2
  Ascending
  low=1

Loop ends: low=1, high=1
Return 1 

Peak found efficiently in both cases!
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 1.17: LeetCode 441 - Arranging Coins
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/arranging-coins/
// Difficulty: Easy
// Technique: Binary Search on Mathematical Formula
// ===============================================================

int arrangeCoins(int n) {
    long low = 0;
    long high = n;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        long coins_needed = mid * (mid + 1) / 2;
        
        if (coins_needed <= n) {
            low = mid + 1;  // Can arrange mid rows
        } else {
            high = mid - 1;  // mid rows needs too many coins
        }
    }
    
    return high;  // Largest k where k(k+1)/2 <= n
}

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

Problem: Staircase with row k having k coins
  Row 1: 1 coin
  Row 2: 2 coins
  Row 3: 3 coins
  ...
  Row k: k coins
  
  Total for k rows: 1+2+...+k = k(k+1)/2

Find: Largest k where k(k+1)/2 <= n

Binary search on answer:
  Search space: [0, n]
  Monotonic: If k rows possible, so are 1..k-1
  
  Find largest valid k

Algorithm correctness:
  For each candidate k:
    Calculate coins needed: k(k+1)/2
    If <= n: k rows possible, try larger
    If > n: k rows impossible, try smaller
  
  Return largest k that works

Why return high:
  Loop ends: low = high + 1
  high = largest k where coins_needed <= n
  Exactly what we want! 

Why use long:
  mid * (mid + 1) can overflow int
  For n = $2^31$-1: mid = 65535
  mid * (mid+1) = 4 \times $10^9$ exceeds int
  
  Use long for safety

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(log N)
  Search space: [0, N]
  Binary search: log N

Space: O(1)

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Quadratic formula:
   k = (-1 + sqrt(1 + 8n)) / 2
   return (int)k
   
   Problem: Floating point precision
   sqrt might give 2.9999 instead of 3
   
   Our solution: Pure integer arithmetic
   No precision issues!

2. Linear search:
   int k = 0;
   while (k * (k+1) / 2 <= n) k++;
   return k-1;
   
   Time: $O(\sqrt{N})$
   For n=$10^9$: 31,623 iterations
   
   Our solution: ~30 iterations
   1000X faster!

3. Mathematical closed form:
   Can solve analytically
   But: Floating point issues
   
   Our solution: Robust integer binary search

Pattern: Binary search on mathematical constraint
  When: Monotonic mathematical relationship
  How: Binary search on parameter
  Result: O(log N) solution

Trace for n=5:
  Coins: 1 + 2 = 3 <= 5 \ding{51} (2 rows)
         1 + 2 + 3 = 6 > 5 \ding{55} (3 rows)
  
  Answer: 2 rows

Binary search:
  low=0, high=5

Iteration 1:
  mid=2
  coins=2\times 3/2=3 <= 5 
  low=3

Iteration 2:
  mid=4
  coins=4\times 5/2=10 > 5 
  high=3

Iteration 3:
  mid=3
  coins=3\times 4/2=6 > 5 
  high=2

Loop ends: low=3, high=2
Return high=2 

Correct: Can form 2 complete rows!
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Summary and Best Practices}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=primaryblue!10,
    colframe=primaryblue,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Binary Search: Foundation Mastery}
]
\textbf{Complete Pattern Guide:}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{lightgray}
\textbf{Pattern} & \textbf{Template} & \textbf{Use Case} \\
\hline
Exact search & low ≤ high, return mid & Find target \\
\hline
Insert position & low ≤ high, return low & Where to insert \\
\hline
First occurrence & Track result, search left & Threshold \\
\hline
Peak finding & low < high, compare neighbors & Mountain array \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\textbf{Implementation Checklist:}
\begin{itemize}
    \item  Use mid = low + (high - low) / 2
    \item  Check loop: while (low ≤ high) usually
    \item  Update: low = mid + 1 or high = mid - 1
    \item  Handle overflow: Use long if needed
    \item  Test edge cases: Empty, single element
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Common Mistakes:}
\begin{itemize}
    \item ✗ mid = (low + high) / 2 ← Overflow!
    \item ✗ low = mid or high = mid ← Infinite loop!
    \item ✗ while (low < high) ← Might miss answer!
    \item ✗ Unsorted array ← Binary search invalid!
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Complexity Achievement:}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Time: O(log N) vs O(N) linear
    \item Speedup: 50,000× for N=1M
    \item Space: O(1) iterative
\end{itemize}
\end{tcolorbox}


\section{Problem Set}

\subsection{Basic Search (6 Problems)}

% ==========================================
% CHAPTER 2: LOWER BOUND AND UPPER BOUND
% ==========================================

\chapter{Lower Bound and Upper Bound}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,
    colback=white,
    colframe=secondaryblue,
    boxrule=1pt,
    arc=2mm,
    left=10pt,
    right=10pt
]
\textbf{\large\textcolor{primaryblue}{Finding Boundaries, First and Last Occurrences}}
\end{tcolorbox}

\vspace{0.5cm}

\begin{center}
\textit{\textcolor{darkgray}{Difficulty: CF 900-1200 $\bullet$ 25 Problems}}
\end{center}

\section{The Concept of Boundaries}

In many scenarios, a simple existence check is not enough. We often need to find the range of indices where a target value exists, or the first element that satisfies a specific comparison. This is where \textbf{Lower Bound} and \textbf{Upper Bound} become essential.



\subsection{Definitions}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=5pt]
    \item \textbf{Lower Bound:} The smallest index $i$ such that $arr[i] \geq \text{target}$. If no such element exists, it points to the end of the array.
    \item \textbf{Upper Bound:} The smallest index $i$ such that $arr[i] > \text{target}$. 
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Finding Occurrences}
By combining these two, we can solve several common queries:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{First Occurrence:} This is exactly the Lower Bound (provided $arr[LB] == \text{target}$).
    \item \textbf{Last Occurrence:} This is $(\text{Upper Bound} - 1)$ (provided $arr[UB-1] == \text{target}$).
    \item \textbf{Total Count:} Calculated as $(\text{Upper Bound} - \text{Lower Bound})$.
\end{itemize}

\section{Implementation and STL}

\subsection{The STL Way (C++)}
The C++ Standard Template Library provides efficient functions that return iterators.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced, colback=darkgray!5!white, colframe=darkgray, title=C++ STL Example]
\begin{verbatim}
vector<int> v = {1, 2, 4, 4, 4, 6, 7};
// Lower bound of 4 is index 2
auto lb = lower_bound(v.begin(), v.end(), 4); 
// Upper bound of 4 is index 5
auto ub = upper_bound(v.begin(), v.end(), 4); 

int count = ub - lb; // Result: 3
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Custom Implementation}
To implement Lower Bound manually, the key is to keep searching even after finding the target to see if a smaller index exists.



\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced, colback=darkgray!5!white, colframe=darkgray, title=Manual Lower Bound]
\begin{verbatim}
int lowerBound(vector<int>& arr, int target) {
    int low = 0, high = arr.size() - 1, ans = arr.size();
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        if (arr[mid] >= target) {
            ans = mid;      // Potentially the first element
            high = mid - 1; // Look for even smaller index
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    return ans;
}
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Lower Bound and Upper Bound: Complete Mastery}

\subsection{Complete Theoretical Foundation}

\subsubsection{Why Boundaries Matter?}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=white,
    colframe=deepblue,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{The Boundary Search Paradigm}
]
\textbf{Why Lower/Upper Bound?}

Finding boundaries is more powerful than simple existence checks

\textbf{The Evolution:}
\begin{verbatim}
Basic binary search: "Does target exist?"
  Returns: Yes/No (or index)
  Limited information

Boundary search: "Where does target region start/end?"
  Returns: Exact boundaries
  Complete information about target range

Applications:
  - First occurrence of target
  - Last occurrence of target
  - Count of target elements
  - Insertion position
  - Range queries
  - Closest elements
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Key Definitions:}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{lightgray}
\textbf{Concept} & \textbf{Definition} \\
\hline
Lower Bound & First index where arr[i] ≥ target \\
\hline
Upper Bound & First index where arr[i] > target \\
\hline
First Occurrence & Lower bound (if arr[LB] == target) \\
\hline
Last Occurrence & Upper bound - 1 (if valid) \\
\hline
Count & Upper bound - Lower bound \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\textbf{Complete Theory:}

1. \textbf{Lower Bound Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Find smallest index i where arr[i] ≥ target

Key insight: Track best answer, search for better

Algorithm:
  int lowerBound(arr, target):
    low = 0
    high = N - 1
    result = N  // Default: Beyond array (not found)
    
    while low <= high:
      mid = low + (high - low) / 2
      
      if arr[mid] >= target:
        result = mid      // This could be answer
        high = mid - 1    // Search for earlier index
      else:
        low = mid + 1     // arr[mid] < target, go right

    return result

Why this works:
  - When arr[mid] ≥ target: Candidate found
  - But might be earlier occurrence
  - Keep searching left: high = mid - 1
  - Track best result seen so far

Example: arr=[1,2,4,4,4,7,9], target=4
  
  Iteration 1: mid=3, arr[3]=4
    4 ≥ 4 ✓, result=3, search left
    
  Iteration 2: mid=1, arr[1]=2
    2 < 4, search right
    
  Iteration 3: mid=2, arr[2]=4
    4 ≥ 4 ✓, result=2, search left
  
  Loop ends: result=2
  First 4 is at index 2 ✓

Time: O(log N)
\end{verbatim}

2. \textbf{Upper Bound Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Find smallest index i where arr[i] > target

Similar to lower bound, but condition: arr[mid] > target

Algorithm:
  int upperBound(arr, target):
    low = 0
    high = N - 1
    result = N  // Default: Beyond array
    
    while low <= high:
      mid = low + (high - low) / 2
      
      if arr[mid] > target:
        result = mid      // This could be answer
        high = mid - 1    // Search for earlier index
      else:
        low = mid + 1     // arr[mid] ≤ target, go right
    
    return result

Key difference from lower bound:
  Lower: arr[mid] >= target (includes equal)
  Upper: arr[mid] > target (strict greater)

Example: arr=[1,2,4,4,4,7,9], target=4

  Upper bound = first element > 4
  arr[5]=7 is first element > 4
  
  Return: 5 ✓

Relationship:
  Lower bound of X = First ≥ X
  Upper bound of X = First > X = Lower bound of (X+1)
\end{verbatim}

3. \textbf{First and Last Occurrence:}
\begin{verbatim}
Using boundaries to find range:

First occurrence:
  index = lowerBound(arr, target)
  if index < N and arr[index] == target:
    return index
  else:
    return -1  // Not found

Last occurrence:
  index = upperBound(arr, target) - 1
  if index >= 0 and arr[index] == target:
    return index
  else:
    return -1  // Not found

Count of target:
  first = lowerBound(arr, target)
  last = upperBound(arr, target)
  count = last - first

Why this works:
  [lower_bound, upper_bound) contains all occurrences
  Range: [first_occurrence, last_occurrence + 1)
  Count: upper - lower

Example: arr=[1,2,4,4,4,7,9], target=4
  lower_bound = 2
  upper_bound = 5
  
  First occurrence: arr[2]=4 ✓
  Last occurrence: arr[5-1]=arr[4]=4 ✓
  Count: 5-2=3 ✓

Visual:
  Index: 0  1  2  3  4  5  6
  Array: 1  2  4  4  4  7  9
            ↑        ↑
            LB       UB
         First    (Last+1)
\end{verbatim}

4. \textbf{Comparison with Standard Binary Search:}
\begin{verbatim}
Standard Binary Search:
  - Finds any occurrence
  - Returns immediately when found
  - Can't distinguish multiple occurrences

Lower/Upper Bound:
  - Finds specific boundary
  - Continues searching after finding
  - Provides complete range information

Example: arr=[4,4,4,4,4], target=4

Standard BS: Returns 2 (middle occurrence)
  Gives: "4 exists"
  Missing: How many? Where's first/last?

Lower Bound: Returns 0 (first occurrence)
Upper Bound: Returns 5 (after last)
  Count: 5-0=5 ✓
  First: 0 ✓
  Last: 4 ✓
  Complete information!

When to use which:
  - Existence only: Standard BS
  - First/last/count: Lower/Upper bound
  - Range queries: Boundaries
  - Insertion point: Lower bound
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Key Insight:}
Boundaries provide COMPLETE information about target range in O(log N)!
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Pattern 1: Lower/Upper Bound Implementation}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=successgreen!10,
    colframe=successgreen,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Boundary Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Lower Bound Implementation}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Find first index where arr[i] ≥ target

Template:
  int lowerBound(vector<int>& arr, int target) {
    int low = 0;
    int high = arr.size() - 1;
    int result = arr.size();  // Default: not found
    
    while (low <= high) {
      int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
      
      if (arr[mid] >= target) {
        result = mid;      // Save answer
        high = mid - 1;    // Search left for better
      } else {
        low = mid + 1;     // Too small, search right
      }
    }
    
    return result;
  }

Key points:
  1. Initialize result = N (beyond array)
  2. When condition met: Save and search left
  3. Final result: Leftmost satisfying index

Example: arr=[1,3,5,5,5,7,9], target=5

Iteration 1: low=0, high=6, mid=3
  arr[3]=5 ≥ 5 ✓
  result=3, high=2

Iteration 2: low=0, high=2, mid=1
  arr[1]=3 < 5
  low=2

Iteration 3: low=2, high=2, mid=2
  arr[2]=5 ≥ 5 ✓
  result=2, high=1

Loop ends: low=2, high=1
Return result=2 ✓

First 5 at index 2!

Time: O(log N)
Space: O(1)
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Type 2: Upper Bound Implementation}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Find first index where arr[i] > target

Template:
  int upperBound(vector<int>& arr, int target) {
    int low = 0;
    int high = arr.size() - 1;
    int result = arr.size();
    
    while (low <= high) {
      int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
      
      if (arr[mid] > target) {
        result = mid;
        high = mid - 1;
      } else {
        low = mid + 1;
      }
    }
    
    return result;
  }

Difference from lower bound:
  Lower: arr[mid] >= target
  Upper: arr[mid] > target

Example: arr=[1,3,5,5,5,7,9], target=5

Search for first element > 5:
  arr[5]=7 is first > 5
  Return: 5 ✓

Usage:
  Last occurrence = upperBound(target) - 1
  Count = upperBound(target) - lowerBound(target)

Time: O(log N)
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Type 3: Closest Element}

\textbf{Complete Example:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Find element closest to target

Approach: Use lower bound + check neighbors

Algorithm:
  int findClosest(vector<int>& arr, int target) {
    int n = arr.size();
    
    // Lower bound gives first ≥ target
    int idx = lowerBound(arr, target);
    
    // Check candidates
    int closest = arr[0];
    int minDiff = abs(arr[0] - target);
    
    // Check element at lower bound
    if (idx < n) {
      int diff = abs(arr[idx] - target);
      if (diff < minDiff) {
        minDiff = diff;
        closest = arr[idx];
      }
    }
    
    // Check previous element
    if (idx > 0) {
      int diff = abs(arr[idx-1] - target);
      if (diff < minDiff) {
        minDiff = diff;
        closest = arr[idx-1];
      }
    }
    
    return closest;
  }

Why check idx and idx-1:
  Lower bound: First ≥ target
  Closest could be:
    1. Element at lower bound (if close)
    2. Previous element (just before target)

Example: arr=[1,3,7,9], target=6

Lower bound of 6: index 2 (arr[2]=7)
Candidates:
  arr[2]=7: diff = |7-6| = 1
  arr[1]=3: diff = |3-6| = 3

Closest: 7 (smaller difference) ✓

Time: O(log N)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Lower/Upper Bound Solutions]
// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 2.1: Implement Lower Bound
// Link: https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/problems/lower-bound-1660286326/1
// Difficulty: Easy
// Technique: Lower Bound Template
// ===============================================================

int lowerBound(vector<int>& arr, int target) {
    int n = arr.size();
    int low = 0;
    int high = n - 1;
    int result = n;  // Default: beyond array (not found)
    
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (arr[mid] >= target) {
            result = mid;      // Potential answer
            high = mid - 1;    // Search for earlier index
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;     // arr[mid] < target
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

Lower bound definition:
  Smallest index i where arr[i] >= target
  
  If no such element: Return N (array size)

Algorithm strategy:
  Track best answer seen
  When condition met: Save and search left
  When condition not met: Search right

Loop invariant: result is best answer found so far

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(log N)
  Binary search: Halve space each iteration
  Iterations: log N

Space: O(1)
  Only constant variables

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Linear search:
   for (int i = 0; i < N; i++)
     if (arr[i] >= target) return i
   
   Time: O(N)
   Our solution: O(log N)
   
   For N=1M: 50,000X faster!

2. Standard binary search:
   Finds any occurrence
   Can't guarantee first occurrence
   
   Our solution: Guarantees leftmost

3. STL lower_bound:
   Same complexity O(log N)
   But: Less educational
   
   Our solution: Shows understanding

Comparison with upper bound:
  Lower: arr[mid] >= target (includes equal)
  Upper: arr[mid] > target (strict)
  
  Lower finds first >=
  Upper finds first >

Trace for arr=[1,2,4,4,4,7,9], target=4:

Iteration 1:
  low=0, high=6, mid=3
  arr[3]=4 >= 4 
  result=3, high=2

Iteration 2:
  low=0, high=2, mid=1
  arr[1]=2 < 4
  low=2

Iteration 3:
  low=2, high=2, mid=2
  arr[2]=4 >= 4 
  result=2, high=1

Loop ends: low=2, high=1
Return result=2 

Verification:
  arr[2]=4 is first element >= 4 
  arr[1]=2 < 4 

Lower bound correct!

Applications:
  - First occurrence
  - Insertion position
  - Range query start
  - Binary search variant
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 2.2: Implement Upper Bound
// Link: https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/problems/upper-bound-1660286326/1
// Difficulty: Easy
// Technique: Upper Bound Template
// ===============================================================

int upperBound(vector<int>& arr, int target) {
    int n = arr.size();
    int low = 0;
    int high = n - 1;
    int result = n;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (arr[mid] > target) {
            result = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

Upper bound definition:
  Smallest index i where arr[i] > target
  
  Strict inequality (not >=)

Difference from lower bound:
  Lower: arr[mid] >= target
  Upper: arr[mid] > target
  
  Single character change, different meaning!

Algorithm correctness:
  When arr[mid] > target: Save and search left
  When arr[mid] <= target: Search right
  
  Finds first strictly greater element

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(log N)
Space: O(1)

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Linear search:
   Time: O(N)
   Our solution: O(log N)

2. Lower bound + increment:
   Not always correct
   
   Example: arr=[1,2,2,3], target=2
     Lower bound: 1 (first 2)
     Lower + 1: 2 (second 2, not 3!)
   
   Our solution: Directly finds 3 

Relationship with lower bound:
  upper_bound(X) = lower_bound(X+1)
  
  But: Only for integers
  For floats: Not exact

Trace for arr=[1,2,4,4,4,7,9], target=4:

Iteration 1:
  low=0, high=6, mid=3
  arr[3]=4 NOT > 4
  low=4

Iteration 2:
  low=4, high=6, mid=5
  arr[5]=7 > 4 
  result=5, high=4

Iteration 3:
  low=4, high=4, mid=4
  arr[4]=4 NOT > 4
  low=5

Loop ends: low=5, high=4
Return result=5 

Verification:
  arr[5]=7 is first element > 4 
  arr[4]=4 NOT > 4 

Upper bound correct!

Usage examples:
  Last occurrence = upper_bound - 1
  Count = upper_bound - lower_bound
  Next greater = arr[upper_bound]
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 2.8: LeetCode 34 - Find First and Last Position
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/find-first-and-last-position-of-element-in-sorted-array/
// Difficulty: Medium
// Technique: Lower and Upper Bound Combination
// ===============================================================

vector<int> searchRange(vector<int>& nums, int target) {
    int n = nums.size();
    
    // Find first occurrence (lower bound)
    int first = -1;
    int low = 0, high = n - 1;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (nums[mid] >= target) {
            if (nums[mid] == target) {
                first = mid;
            }
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    
    // If not found, return [-1, -1]
    if (first == -1) {
        return {-1, -1};
    }
    
    // Find last occurrence (upper bound - 1)
    int last = -1;
    low = 0;
    high = n - 1;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (nums[mid] > target) {
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            if (nums[mid] == target) {
                last = mid;
            }
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    
    return {first, last};
}

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

Problem: Find [first, last] indices of target

Two-phase approach:
  1. Find first occurrence (lower bound)
  2. Find last occurrence (upper bound - 1)

First occurrence:
  Use lower bound logic
  Save index when nums[mid] == target
  Continue searching left

Last occurrence:
  Use upper bound logic
  Save index when nums[mid] == target
  Continue searching right

Algorithm correctness:
  Lower bound: Finds leftmost >= target
  Upper bound - 1: Finds rightmost == target
  
  Together: Complete range [first, last]

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(log N)
  Two binary searches: 2 \times O(log N) = O(log N)

Space: O(1)

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Linear search for range:
   for (int i = 0; i < N; i++)
     if (nums[i] == target) track range
   
   Time: O(N)
   Our solution: O(log N)

2. Standard BS + expand:
   Find any occurrence
   Expand left and right linearly
   
   Time: O(N) worst case
   Example: All elements same
   
   Our solution: Always O(log N)

3. Single binary search:
   Can't find both first and last
   Need two separate searches
   
   Our solution: Correct approach

STL alternative:
  auto lower = lower_bound(nums.begin(), nums.end(), target);
  auto upper = upper_bound(nums.begin(), nums.end(), target);
  
  But: Less educational
  Our solution: Shows understanding

Trace for nums=[5,7,7,8,8,10], target=8:

Find first occurrence:
  low=0, high=5, mid=2
  nums[2]=7 < 8
  low=3
  
  low=3, high=5, mid=4
  nums[4]=8 == 8 
  first=4, high=3
  
  low=3, high=3, mid=3
  nums[3]=8 == 8 
  first=3, high=2
  
  Loop ends: first=3

Find last occurrence:
  low=0, high=5, mid=2
  nums[2]=7 < 8
  low=3
  
  low=3, high=5, mid=4
  nums[4]=8 == 8 
  last=4, low=5
  
  low=5, high=5, mid=5
  nums[5]=10 > 8
  high=4
  
  Loop ends: last=4

Return [3, 4] 

Verification:
  nums[3]=8 (first) 
  nums[4]=8 (last) 
  Range contains exactly target 

Complete range found!
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 2: Counting and Queries}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=lightgray,
    colframe=accentorange,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Counting Patterns}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Count Occurrences}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Count how many times target appears

Using boundaries:
  count = upperBound(target) - lowerBound(target)

Why this works:
  [lower_bound, upper_bound) = range of target
  Count = upper - lower

Algorithm:
  int countOccurrences(vector<int>& arr, int target) {
    int lower = lowerBound(arr, target);
    int upper = upperBound(arr, target);
    return upper - lower;
  }

Example: arr=[1,2,4,4,4,7,9], target=4
  lower_bound(4) = 2
  upper_bound(4) = 5
  count = 5 - 2 = 3 ✓

Visual:
  Index: 0  1  2  3  4  5  6
  Array: 1  2  4  4  4  7  9
            ↑        ↑
            LB       UB
         
  Range [2, 5) contains 3 elements

Time: O(log N)
  Two binary searches: 2 × O(log N)
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Type 2: Range Queries}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Count elements in range [L, R]

Using boundaries:
  count = upperBound(R) - lowerBound(L)

Why this works:
  upperBound(R): First > R
  lowerBound(L): First ≥ L
  
  Range [lowerBound(L), upperBound(R)) = [L, R]

Algorithm:
  int countInRange(vector<int>& arr, int L, int R) {
    int left = lowerBound(arr, L);
    int right = upperBound(arr, R);
    return right - left;
  }

Example: arr=[1,3,5,7,9,11], query=[3,7]

lowerBound(3) = 1 (first ≥ 3 is arr[1]=3)
upperBound(7) = 4 (first > 7 is arr[4]=9)

Count = 4 - 1 = 3
Elements: 3, 5, 7 ✓

Multiple queries:
  Sort array once: O(N log N)
  Each query: O(log N)
  Q queries: O(Q log N)
  
Total: O(N log N + Q log N)

Time: O(log N) per query
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Type 3: K-th Missing}

\textbf{Complete Example:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Find K-th missing positive in sorted array

Key insight: Count how many numbers missing up to index

Missing count at index i:
  Expected numbers: arr[i]
  Present numbers: i + 1
  Missing: arr[i] - (i + 1)

Binary search on this count:

Algorithm:
  int findKthPositive(vector<int>& arr, int k) {
    int low = 0;
    int high = arr.size() - 1;
    
    while (low <= high) {
      int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
      int missing = arr[mid] - (mid + 1);
      
      if (missing < k) {
        low = mid + 1;
      } else {
        high = mid - 1;
      }
    }
    
    // K-th missing = arr[high] + (k - missing_at_high)
    if (high < 0) {
      return k;  // All missing before array
    }
    
    int missing_at_high = arr[high] - (high + 1);
    return arr[high] + (k - missing_at_high);
  }

Example: arr=[2,3,4,7,11], k=5

Index: 0  1  2  3  4
Array: 2  3  4  7  11
Miss:  1  1  1  3  6

Missing count progression:
  At index 0: 2-1=1 (missing: 1)
  At index 1: 3-2=1 (missing: 1)
  At index 2: 4-3=1 (missing: 1)
  At index 3: 7-4=3 (missing: 1,5,6)
  At index 4: 11-5=6 (missing: 1,5,6,8,9,10)

Find where missing ≥ 5:
  At index 4: missing=6 ≥ 5
  
K-th missing = 11 - (6-5) = 10? No...
Actually: After 7, missing [5,6]
  5th missing = 9 ✓

Time: O(log N)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Counting Solutions]
// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 2.15: Number of Occurrences
// Link: https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/problems/number-of-occurrence2259/1
// Difficulty: Easy
// Technique: Upper Bound - Lower Bound
// ===============================================================

int countOccurrences(vector<int>& arr, int target) {
    int n = arr.size();
    
    // Find lower bound (first >= target)
    int lower = n;
    int low = 0, high = n - 1;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        if (arr[mid] >= target) {
            lower = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    
    // If target not in array
    if (lower == n || arr[lower] != target) {
        return 0;
    }
    
    // Find upper bound (first > target)
    int upper = n;
    low = 0;
    high = n - 1;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        if (arr[mid] > target) {
            upper = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    
    return upper - lower;
}

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

Problem: Count occurrences of target in sorted array

Key insight: Use boundaries
  Lower bound: First occurrence
  Upper bound: After last occurrence
  Count: upper - lower

Algorithm correctness:
  Range [lower, upper) contains all occurrences
  Length = upper - lower = count

Special cases:
  1. Target not in array:
     lower = upper
     count = 0 
  
  2. Single occurrence:
     upper = lower + 1
     count = 1 
  
  3. Multiple occurrences:
     upper - lower = exact count 

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(log N)
  Two binary searches: 2 \times O(log N)
  Independent of count!

Space: O(1)

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Linear count:
   int count = 0;
   for (int x : arr)
     if (x == target) count++;
   
   Time: O(N)
   Our solution: O(log N)
   
   For N=1M: 50,000X faster!

2. Standard BS + expand:
   Find any occurrence
   Expand left and right counting
   
   Time: O(N) worst case
   All elements same
   
   Our solution: Always O(log N)

3. Map/unordered_map:
   Count while building map
   
   Space: O(N)
   Our solution: O(1)

Boundary formula:
  count = upperBound - lowerBound
  
  This is a fundamental pattern!

Trace for arr=[1,1,2,2,2,2,3], target=2:

Find lower bound:
  Binary search for first 2
  lower = 2

Find upper bound:
  Binary search for first > 2
  upper = 6

Count = 6 - 2 = 4 

Verification:
  arr[2]=2, arr[3]=2, arr[4]=2, arr[5]=2
  Four occurrences 

Pattern recognition:
  Whenever counting in sorted array
  Think: upper_bound - lower_bound!
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 2.4: CF Edu - Fast Search
// Link: https://codeforces.com/edu/course/2/lesson/6/1/practice/contest/283911/problem/D
// Difficulty: Medium
// Technique: Range Query with Boundaries
// ===============================================================

vector<int> fastSearch(vector<int>& arr, vector<pair<int,int>>& queries) {
    sort(arr.begin(), arr.end());
    
    vector<int> results;
    
    for (auto [L, R] : queries) {
        // Find first >= L
        int left = lower_bound(arr.begin(), arr.end(), L) - arr.begin();
        
        // Find first > R
        int right = upper_bound(arr.begin(), arr.end(), R) - arr.begin();
        
        results.push_back(right - left);
    }
    
    return results;
}

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

Problem: Count elements in range [L, R] for Q queries

Key insight: Boundaries define range
  lowerBound(L): First >= L
  upperBound(R): First > R
  
  Range [lowerBound(L), upperBound(R)) = [L, R]

Algorithm correctness:
  Elements in [L, R]:
    - Must be >= L (starts at lowerBound(L))
    - Must be <= R (ends before upperBound(R))
  
  Count = upperBound(R) - lowerBound(L)

Visual example: arr=[1,3,5,7,9], query=[3,7]
  
  Index: 0  1  2  3  4
  Array: 1  3  5  7  9
         
  L=3: lowerBound = 1 (arr[1]=3)
  R=7: upperBound = 4 (arr[4]=9)
  
  Count = 4 - 1 = 3
  Elements: 3, 5, 7 

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log N + Q log N)
  Sort array: O(N log N)
  Each query: O(log N)
  Q queries: O(Q log N)

Space: O(1) excluding output

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Linear search per query:
   for each query:
     count = 0
     for each element:
       if L <= element <= R: count++
   
   Time: O(Q \times N)
   For Q=10^5, N=10^5: 10^10 operations
   
   Our solution: O(N log N + Q log N) = 10^6
   10,000X faster!

2. Segment tree:
   Build: O(N log N)
   Query: O(log N)
   
   Time: Similar O(N log N + Q log N)
   Complexity: Much higher
   
   Our solution: Simpler, same complexity

3. No sorting:
   Can't use binary search
   Must linear search each query
   
   Our solution: Sort once, fast queries

Multiple query pattern:
  Preprocess: Sort array
  Query: Binary search
  
  Amortize preprocessing over many queries

Trace for arr=[2,5,1,7,3,4,8], queries=[[2,4],[3,6]]:

After sort: arr=[1,2,3,4,5,7,8]

Query [2, 4]:
  lowerBound(2) = 1 (arr[1]=2)
  upperBound(4) = 4 (arr[4]=5)
  count = 4 - 1 = 3
  Elements: 2, 3, 4 

Query [3, 6]:
  lowerBound(3) = 2 (arr[2]=3)
  upperBound(6) = 5 (arr[5]=7)
  count = 5 - 2 = 3
  Elements: 3, 4, 5 

Results: [3, 3] 

Efficient range queries achieved!
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 2.21: LeetCode 1539 - K-th Missing Positive Number
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/kth-missing-positive-number/
// Difficulty: Easy
// Technique: Binary Search on Missing Count
// ===============================================================

int findKthPositive(vector<int>& arr, int k) {
    int n = arr.size();
    int low = 0;
    int high = n - 1;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        // Count of missing numbers up to arr[mid]
        int missing = arr[mid] - (mid + 1);
        
        if (missing < k) {
            low = mid + 1;
        } else {
            high = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    
    // If high < 0, all missing before array start
    if (high < 0) {
        return k;
    }
    
    // Calculate k-th missing from position high
    int missing_at_high = arr[high] - (high + 1);
    return arr[high] + (k - missing_at_high);
}

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

Problem: Find K-th missing positive integer

Key insight: Count missing numbers at each position

Missing count at index i:
  Expected: 1, 2, 3, ..., arr[i]
  Present: i + 1 elements
  Missing: arr[i] - (i + 1)

Binary search on missing count:
  Find index where missing count crosses K

Algorithm correctness:
  Binary search finds:
    high = largest index with missing < K
  
  K-th missing is just after arr[high]
  Calculation: arr[high] + (k - missing_at_high)

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(log N)
  Binary search on array
  Independent of K value!

Space: O(1)

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Linear simulation:
   Iterate 1, 2, 3, ...
   Count missing until reach K
   
   Time: O(arr[N-1])
   For large arrays: Very slow
   
   Our solution: O(log N)
   Much faster!

2. Set approach:
   Insert all into set
   Check 1, 2, 3, ... for missing
   
   Time: O(N + K)
   Space: O(N)
   
   Our solution: O(log N) time, O(1) space

3. Count all missing:
   Generate all missing numbers
   Return K-th
   
   Space: O(max(arr) - N)
   Our solution: O(1)

Missing count formula:
  missing_at_i = arr[i] - (i + 1)
  
  This is the key insight!

Trace for arr=[2,3,4,7,11], k=5:

Calculate missing at each index:
  i=0: arr[0]=2, missing=2-1=1 (missing: 1)
  i=1: arr[1]=3, missing=3-2=1 (missing: 1)
  i=2: arr[2]=4, missing=4-3=1 (missing: 1)
  i=3: arr[3]=7, missing=7-4=3 (missing: 1,5,6)
  i=4: arr[4]=11, missing=11-5=6 (missing: 1,5,6,8,9,10)

Binary search for missing >= 5:
  low=0, high=4, mid=2
  missing=1 < 5, low=3
  
  low=3, high=4, mid=3
  missing=3 < 5, low=4
  
  low=4, high=4, mid=4
  missing=6 >= 5? No, 6 >= 5, high=3
  
Actually:
  Find high where missing < k
  high=3, missing_at_3=3

K-th missing after arr[3]:
  arr[3]=7
  Need 5-3=2 more missing after 7
  Missing after 7: 8, 9
  5th missing = 9 

Return: 7 + (5-3) = 9 

Efficient K-th missing found!
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Summary and Best Practices}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=primaryblue!10,
    colframe=primaryblue,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Lower/Upper Bound: Complete Mastery}
]
\textbf{Complete Pattern Guide:}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{lightgray}
\textbf{Concept} & \textbf{Formula} & \textbf{Use Case} \\
\hline
Lower Bound & First ≥ target & First occurrence \\
\hline
Upper Bound & First > target & After last occurrence \\
\hline
First & Lower bound & Start of range \\
\hline
Last & Upper bound - 1 & End of range \\
\hline
Count & Upper - Lower & Occurrences \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\textbf{Implementation Templates:}
\begin{itemize}
    \item  Lower: if (arr[mid] >= target)
    \item  Upper: if (arr[mid] > target)
    \item  Track result, search left when found
    \item  Default result = N (beyond array)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Common Patterns:}
\begin{itemize}
    \item First occurrence: lowerBound
    \item Last occurrence: upperBound - 1
    \item Count: upperBound - lowerBound
    \item Range query: UB(R) - LB(L)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Complexity Achievement:}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Time: O(log N) per query
    \item vs Linear: 50,000× faster
    \item Complete range info
\end{itemize}
\end{tcolorbox}
