% ==========================================
% CHAPTER 7: ALLOCATION AND DISTRIBUTION PROBLEMS
% ==========================================

\chapter{Allocation and Distribution Problems}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,
    colback=white,
    colframe=secondaryblue,
    boxrule=1pt,
    arc=2mm,
    left=10pt,
    right=10pt
]
\textbf{\large\textcolor{primaryblue}{Book Allocation, Painter's Partition, and Resource Distribution}}
\end{tcolorbox}

\vspace{0.5cm}

\begin{center}
\textit{\textcolor{darkgray}{Difficulty: CF 1500-1800 $\bullet$ 30 Problems}}
\end{center}

\section{The Greedy Strategy in Allocation}

In allocation problems, the \texttt{check(limit)} function usually employs a greedy approach. For example, if we want to check if a "maximum load" of $W$ is possible for $K$ workers:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Start with the first worker.
    \item Keep assigning items to the current worker until the next item would exceed the $limit$.
    \item Move to the next worker and repeat.
    \item If the number of workers used is $\leq K$, the answer $W$ is feasible.
\end{enumerate}



\subsection{Defining Search Space for Allocation}
Setting the correct bounds is essential for performance and correctness:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Lower Bound ($low$):} Usually $\max(array)$, because a single item cannot be split; at least one worker must carry the heaviest item.
    \item \textbf{Upper Bound ($high$):} Usually $\sum(array)$, representing the case where one worker carries everything.
\end{itemize}

\section{Allocation and Distribution Problems: Complete Mastery}

\subsection{Complete Theoretical Foundation}

\subsubsection{Why Allocation Problems?}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=white,
    colframe=deepblue,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{The Allocation Paradigm}
]
\textbf{Why Study Allocation?}

Most fundamental: Distribute items among workers to optimize metric

\textbf{The Paradigm Shift:}
\begin{verbatim}
Classic Problem:
  Given: N items with values
  Task: Distribute among K workers
  Goal: Minimize maximum OR Maximize minimum
  
Transformation:
  Optimization: "What is minimum/maximum?"
  → Feasibility: "Can we achieve with X?"
  
KEY DIFFERENCE:
  Direct optimization: Hard to calculate
  Feasibility checking: Easy with greedy
  Binary search: Find optimal X
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Complete Theory:}

1. \textbf{What is Allocation?}
\begin{verbatim}
Allocation problem: check(x) → {True, False}
  Input: Candidate answer x (capacity/distance/time)
  Output: Can we achieve goal with x?

Example: "Can we allocate books with max pages = x?"
  check(x) = true if possible
  check(x) = false if need more pages

Monotonicity requirement:
  Minimize max: F F F F T T T T T  (Find first True)
  Maximize min: T T T T T F F F F  (Find last True)

If check(x) = true:
  For minimize: All y > x also true
  For maximize: All y < x also true

This monotonicity enables binary search!
\end{verbatim}

2. \textbf{The Template Pattern:}
\begin{verbatim}
Complete template for "Minimize maximum allocation"

Algorithm:
  bool check(x):
    # Can we allocate with maximum = x?
    # Usually greedy packing
    return feasible
  
  int minimizeMaximum():
    low = max(items)      # Must fit largest
    high = sum(items)     # All to one worker
    result = high
    
    while low <= high:
      mid = low + (high - low) / 2
      
      if check(mid):
        result = mid      # mid works!
        high = mid - 1    # Try smaller
      else:
        low = mid + 1     # mid doesn't work, need larger
    
    return result

Key differences from array search:
  1. Search space: [max(items), sum(items)]
  2. check() function uses greedy strategy
  3. Track result (best valid answer so far)

Time: O(N log S × T_check)
  S = sum(items)
  T_check = time for check() function (usually O(N))
\end{verbatim}

3. \textbf{Finding Search Space:}
\begin{verbatim}
CRITICAL STEP: Determine [low, high]

For Minimize Maximum:
  low = max(items)   # Largest single item
  high = sum(items)  # All items to one worker

For Maximize Minimum:
  low = 1            # Minimum spacing
  high = max_span    # Maximum possible spacing

Example: Books to students
  N books with pages: [12, 34, 67, 90]
  M students: 2
  
  min_pages = max([12,34,67,90]) = 90
  max_pages = sum([12,34,67,90]) = 203
  
  Search space: [90, 203]

Getting bounds wrong:
  Too narrow: Miss answer
  Too wide: Still correct, just slower
  
  Err on side of wider bounds!
\end{verbatim}

4. \textbf{The check() Function:}
\begin{verbatim}
Most important part: Implement check(x)

Common patterns:

Pattern 1: Greedy contiguous allocation
  "Can we allocate with max_load = x?"
  Fill current worker until limit, then new worker

Pattern 2: Greedy placement
  "Can we place with min_distance = x?"
  Place items as early as possible

Pattern 3: Parallel production
  "Can we produce in time = x?"
  Sum production rates of all workers

Implementation tips:
  - Greedy is usually optimal for check()
  - O(N) check is typical
  - Must be deterministic (same x → same result)
  - Return true/false clearly

Example: Allocate books
  bool check(max_pages):
    students = 1
    current_pages = 0
    
    for pages in books:
      if current_pages + pages > max_pages:
        students++
        current_pages = pages
      else:
        current_pages += pages
    
    return students <= M

Time: O(N) per check
Total: O(N log S) for binary search
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Key Insight:}
Binary search on ANSWER transforms allocation optimization into feasibility!
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Pattern 1: Contiguous Allocation (Minimize Maximum)}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=successgreen!10,
    colframe=successgreen,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Contiguous Allocation Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Book Allocation}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Distribute N books among M students
  Books must be contiguous
  Minimize maximum pages per student
  
Example: books=[12,34,67,90], students=2
  
Find: Minimum maximum pages

Key insight: Larger limit always works
  If max_pages = X works, X+1 also works
  Monotonic: F F F T T T ...
  
  Find first True (minimum)

Search space:
  min = max(books) = 90   # Must read largest book
  max = sum(books) = 203  # One student reads all

Check function:
  bool canAllocate(max_pages):
    students = 1
    current_pages = 0
    
    for pages in books:
      if current_pages + pages > max_pages:
        students++  # Need new student
        current_pages = pages
      else:
        current_pages += pages
    
    return students <= M

Algorithm:
  int allocateBooks(books, M):
    low = max(books)
    high = sum(books)
    result = high
    
    while low <= high:
      mid = low + (high - low) / 2
      
      if canAllocate(mid):
        result = mid
        high = mid - 1  # Try smaller max
      else:
        low = mid + 1   # Need larger max
    
    return result

Example: books=[12,34,67,90], students=2
  Search space: [90, 203]
  
  Try 146: Can allocate?
    Student 1: 12+34+67 = 113 ≤ 146 ✓
    Student 2: 90
    Students = 2 ≤ 2 ✓
  
  Try 117:
    Student 1: 12+34+67 = 113 ≤ 117 ✓
    Student 2: 90
    Students = 2 ✓
  
  Try 103:
    Student 1: 12+34 = 46, can't add 67
    Student 2: 67, can't add 90
    Student 3: 90
    Students = 3 > 2 ✗
  
  Try 113:
    Student 1: 12+34+67 = 113 ✓
    Student 2: 90
    Students = 2 ✓
  
  Answer: 113

Time: O(N log S) where S = sum(books)
Space: O(1)
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Type 2: Painter's Partition}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Paint N boards with K painters
  Painters paint contiguous boards
  Minimize maximum time

This is IDENTICAL to book allocation!
  Books → Boards
  Students → Painters
  Pages → Time units

Same algorithm, same complexity
Recognition: "Painter's partition" = Book allocation

Time: O(N log S)
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Type 3: Array Division}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Split array into K subarrays
  Minimize maximum subarray sum

Also SAME pattern as book allocation!
  Array elements → Books
  Subarrays → Students
  Sum → Pages

LeetCode 410 "Split Array Largest Sum" is identical

Time: O(N log S)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Contiguous Allocation Solutions]
// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 7.1: Allocate Minimum Number of Pages
// Link: https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/problems/allocate-minimum-number-of-pages0937/1
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1500-1600)
// Technique: BSOA - Greedy Contiguous Allocation
// ===============================================================

bool canAllocate(vector<int>& books, int students, long long maxPages) {
    int allocated = 1;
    long long currentPages = 0;
    
    for (int pages : books) {
        if (pages > maxPages) {
            return false;
        }
        
        if (currentPages + pages > maxPages) {
            allocated++;
            currentPages = pages;
            
            if (allocated > students) {
                return false;
            }
        } else {
            currentPages += pages;
        }
    }
    
    return true;
}

long long allocateBooks(vector<int>& books, int students) {
    int n = books.size();
    
    if (students > n) {
        return -1;
    }
    
    long long low = *max_element(books.begin(), books.end());
    long long high = accumulate(books.begin(), books.end(), 0LL);
    long long result = high;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        long long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (canAllocate(books, students, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

BSOA transformation:
  Optimization: Find minimum max_pages
  Feasibility: Can allocate with max_pages = X?

Monotonicity: If X works -> X+1 works
  Pattern: F F F T T T
  Find first True (minimum)

Search space:
  Lower: max(books) - someone reads largest
  Upper: sum(books) - one reads all

check() correctness:
  Greedy packing fills each student maximally
  Minimizes students for given limit
  Provably optimal for feasibility

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log S)
  S = sum(books)
  Binary search: log S iterations
  Each check: O(N) pass

Space: O(1)

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Try all partitions:
   Exponential O(M^N)
   Our solution: O(N log S)

2. DP: O(N \times M \times sum) time and space
   Our solution: O(N log S) time, O(1) space

3. Linear search: O(N \times (sum - max))
   Our solution: O(N log S)

Greedy correctness:
  To minimize students for given max:
    Pack each student maximally
  Any other strategy uses >= same students
  Therefore greedy is optimal

Trace for books=[12,34,67,90], students=2:
  Search [90, 203]
  
  mid=146: Works (2 students) -> try smaller
  mid=117: Works -> try smaller
  mid=103: Fails (3 students) -> need larger
  mid=110: Fails -> need larger
  mid=113: Works -> try smaller
  mid=112: Fails
  
  Result: 113
  
  Allocation: [12,34,67] (113), [90] (90)
  Maximum: 113 (minimized!)

Pattern: Contiguous allocation + minimize maximum
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 7.2: Painter's Partition Problem
// Link: https://www.interviewbit.com/problems/painters-partition-problem/
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1500-1600)
// Technique: IDENTICAL to Book Allocation
// ===============================================================

long long paintersPartition(vector<int>& boards, int painters) {
    // EXACT same implementation as book allocation
    // Only naming differs
    
    int n = boards.size();
    if (painters > n) return -1;
    
    long long low = *max_element(boards.begin(), boards.end());
    long long high = accumulate(boards.begin(), boards.end(), 0LL);
    long long result = high;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        long long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        // Check if can paint with max time = mid
        int used = 1;
        long long current = 0;
        bool possible = true;
        
        for (int board : boards) {
            if (board > mid) {
                possible = false;
                break;
            }
            if (current + board > mid) {
                used++;
                current = board;
                if (used > painters) {
                    possible = false;
                    break;
                }
            } else {
                current += board;
            }
        }
        
        if (possible) {
            result = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
Recognition: This IS book allocation!
  Books -> Boards
  Students -> Painters
  Pages -> Time

Same structure, same algorithm
Shows the pattern is universal!

Time: O(N log S)
Space: O(1)

Pattern: "Partition items among workers, minimize maximum"
  -> Always use this template!
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 7.9: CSES 1085 - Array Division
// Link: https://cses.fi/problemset/task/1085
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1500-1600)
// Technique: Split Array - SAME Pattern
// ===============================================================

bool canDivide(vector<long long>& arr, int k, long long maxSum) {
    int subarrays = 1;
    long long currentSum = 0;
    
    for (long long num : arr) {
        if (num > maxSum) return false;
        
        if (currentSum + num > maxSum) {
            subarrays++;
            currentSum = num;
            if (subarrays > k) return false;
        } else {
            currentSum += num;
        }
    }
    return true;
}

long long arrayDivision(vector<long long>& arr, int k) {
    long long low = *max_element(arr.begin(), arr.end());
    long long high = accumulate(arr.begin(), arr.end(), 0LL);
    long long result = high;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        long long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        if (canDivide(arr, k, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    return result;
}

/*
SAME pattern as book allocation:
  Split array into K subarrays
  Minimize maximum sum
  Greedy packing

Also: LeetCode 410 is identical

Time: O(N log S)
Space: O(1)

Pattern recognition: "Split consecutive into groups, minimize max"
  -> Book allocation template!
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 7.7: LeetCode 1011 - Capacity To Ship Packages
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/capacity-to-ship-packages-within-d-days/
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1500-1600)
// Technique: BSOA - Greedy Packing (Same Pattern!)
// ===============================================================

bool canShip(vector<int>& weights, int days, int capacity) {
    int daysNeeded = 1;
    int currentWeight = 0;
    
    for (int weight : weights) {
        if (weight > capacity) return false;
        
        if (currentWeight + weight > capacity) {
            daysNeeded++;
            currentWeight = weight;
            if (daysNeeded > days) return false;
        } else {
            currentWeight += weight;
        }
    }
    return true;
}

int shipWithinDays(vector<int>& weights, int days) {
    int low = *max_element(weights.begin(), weights.end());
    int high = accumulate(weights.begin(), weights.end(), 0);
    int result = high;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (canShip(weights, days, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    return result;
}

/*
SAME pattern again!
  Packages -> Books
  Days -> Students
  Weight -> Pages

All contiguous allocation problems use same algorithm!

Time: O(N log S)
Space: O(1)

This problem is the CLASSIC example in Chapter 4!
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 2: Parallel Production}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=lightgray,
    colframe=accentorange,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Parallel Production Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Factory Machines}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: N machines produce T products
  Machine i takes time[i] per product
  All machines work simultaneously
  Minimize total time

Key difference: PARALLEL not sequential

Search space:
  min = 1
  max = slowest_machine × T

Check function:
  bool canProduce(time, T):
    total = 0
    for machine in machines:
      total += time / machine  # Production in time
    return total >= T

Example: machines=[3,2,5], T=8
  
  Try time=10:
    Machine 1: 10/3 = 3 products
    Machine 2: 10/2 = 5 products
    Machine 3: 10/5 = 2 products
    Total: 10 ≥ 8 ✓
  
  Try time=6:
    Total: 2+3+1 = 6 < 8 ✗
  
  Try time=8:
    Total: 2+4+1 = 7 < 8 ✗
  
  Try time=9:
    Total: 3+4+1 = 8 = 8 ✓
  
  Answer: 9

Time: O(N log(M×T))
  M = slowest machine
  T = products
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Parallel Production Solutions]
// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 7.3: CSES 1620 - Factory Machines
// Link: https://cses.fi/problemset/task/1620
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1500)
// Technique: BSOA - Parallel Production (Different!)
// ===============================================================

bool canProduce(vector<long long>& machines, long long products, long long time) {
    long long total = 0;
    
    for (long long machineTime : machines) {
        total += time / machineTime;
        
        if (total >= products) {
            return true;
        }
    }
    
    return total >= products;
}

long long minimumTime(vector<long long>& machines, long long products) {
    long long low = 1;
    long long high = *max_element(machines.begin(), machines.end()) * products;
    long long result = high;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        long long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (canProduce(machines, products, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
WHY THIS IS DIFFERENT:

Book Allocation (Sequential):
  Workers process one at a time
  Contiguous allocation required
  check: Greedy packing
  
Factory Machines (Parallel):
  All machines work simultaneously
  Each produces independently
  check: Sum production rates

Production calculation:
  In time T, machine with speed S produces:
    T / S products (integer division)
  
  Total production = Sum over all machines

Example: machines=[3,2,5], time=10
  Machine 1: 10/3 = 3 products
  Machine 2: 10/2 = 5 products
  Machine 3: 10/5 = 2 products
  Total: 10 products

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log(M \timesT))
  M = slowest machine
  T = products needed

Space: O(1)

Pattern: Parallel production -> Sum of rates
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 3: Strategic Sorting}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=warnyellow!10,
    colframe=warnyellow,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Strategic Sorting Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Gluttony Problem}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: N members eat food
  Member i: food[i] × capacity[i] = discomfort
  Can reduce food by K (training)
  Minimize maximum discomfort

KEY: Sort before allocating!

Strategy:
  Sort food: descending (largest first)
  Sort capacity: ascending (smallest first)
  Pair: food[i] with capacity[i]

Why this works:
  Pairing largest food with smallest capacity
  Minimizes maximum product

Example: food=[5,3], capacity=[2,4]
  Bad: [5×4, 3×2] = [20, 6], max=20
  Good: [5×2, 3×4] = [10, 12], max=12

Proof (Exchange):
  Any other pairing → larger maximum
  This pairing is provably optimal

Time: O(N log N + N log P)
  Sort: O(N log N)
  Binary search: O(N log P)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Strategic Sorting Solutions]
// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 7.4: AtCoder ABC 144E - Gluttony
// Link: https://atcoder.jp/contests/abc144/tasks/abc144_e
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1600-1700)
// Technique: BSOA with Strategic Sorting
// ===============================================================

bool canAchieve(vector<long long>& food, vector<long long>& capacity, 
                long long k, long long maxProduct) {
    long long training = 0;
    
    for (int i = 0; i < food.size(); i++) {
        long long product = food[i] * capacity[i];
        
        if (product > maxProduct) {
            long long needed = (product - maxProduct + capacity[i] - 1) / capacity[i];
            training += needed;
            
            if (training > k) {
                return false;
            }
        }
    }
    
    return training <= k;
}

long long minimizeMax(vector<long long>& food, vector<long long>& capacity, long long k) {
    // KEY: Sort strategically!
    sort(food.rbegin(), food.rend());      // Descending
    sort(capacity.begin(), capacity.end()); // Ascending
    
    long long low = 0;
    long long high = *max_element(food.begin(), food.end()) * 
                     *max_element(capacity.begin(), capacity.end());
    long long result = high;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        long long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (canAchieve(food, capacity, k, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
WHY SORTING WORKS:

Optimal pairing:
  Pair largest food with smallest capacity
  Minimizes maximum product

Proof (Exchange Argument):
  Suppose: Large \times Large = X
  Try: Large \times Small = Y
  Result: Y < X
  
  Therefore: This pairing is optimal

Example: food=[5,3], capacity=[2,4]
  Sort food: [5,3] (desc)
  Sort capacity: [2,4] (asc)
  Pair: [5\times2, 3\times4] = [10, 12]
  Maximum: 12
  
  Compare: [5\times4, 3\times2] = [20, 6], max=20 (worse!)

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log N + N log P)
  Sort: O(N log N)
  Binary search: O(N log P)

Space: O(1)

Pattern: Need optimal pairing -> Sort first!
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 4: Maximize Minimum (Opposite!)}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=dangered!10,
    colframe=dangered,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Maximize Minimum Algorithms}
]
\textbf{CRITICAL: Opposite Update Direction!}

\textbf{Pattern Comparison:}
\begin{verbatim}
Minimize Maximum:
  Pattern: F F F T T T
  if check(mid):
    result = mid
    high = mid - 1  # Try SMALLER
  else:
    low = mid + 1

Maximize Minimum:
  Pattern: T T T F F F
  if check(mid):
    result = mid
    low = mid + 1   # Try LARGER!
  else:
    high = mid - 1

GET THIS WRONG → WRONG ANSWER!
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Type 1: Divide Chocolate}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Divide chocolate to maximize minimum sweetness
  K+1 pieces (K friends + you)

Pattern: T T T F F F (find last True)

Check: Can create K+1 pieces with each ≥ minSweet?
  Greedy cutting: Cut when reach threshold

Example: sweetness=[1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8,9], k=5
  
  Try minSweet=4:
    Cut at: [1,2,3], [4,5], [6], [7], [8], [9]
    Pieces: 6 ≥ 6 ✓
  
  Try minSweet=5:
    Cut at: [1,2,3], [4,5], [6], [7], [8], [9]
    Pieces: 6 ✓
  
  Try minSweet=6:
    Cut at: [1,2,3], [4,5], [6], [7], [8,9]
    Pieces: 5 < 6 ✗
  
  Answer: 5

Time: O(N log S)
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Type 2: Aggressive Cows}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Place C cows in N stalls
  Maximize minimum distance between cows

Greedy placement:
  Place first cow at stall[0]
  Place next cow as soon as distance ≥ minDist
  This maximizes options for future cows

Example: stalls=[1,2,4,8,9], cows=3
  
  Try minDist=3:
    Place at 1, 4, 8 (distances: 3, 4)
    Count: 3 ✓
  
  Try minDist=4:
    Place at 1, 8 (distance: 7)
    Count: 2 < 3 ✗
  
  Answer: 3

Time: O(N log N + N log D)
  Sort: O(N log N)
  Binary search: O(N log D)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Maximize Minimum Solutions]
// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 7.14: LeetCode 1231 - Divide Chocolate
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/divide-chocolate/
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 1600-1700)
// Technique: BSOA - MAXIMIZE Minimum (Opposite!)
// ===============================================================

bool canDivide(vector<int>& sweetness, int k, int minSweet) {
    int pieces = 0;
    int currentSum = 0;
    
    for (int sweet : sweetness) {
        currentSum += sweet;
        
        if (currentSum >= minSweet) {
            pieces++;
            currentSum = 0;
            
            if (pieces >= k + 1) {
                return true;
            }
        }
    }
    
    return pieces >= k + 1;
}

int maximizeSweetness(vector<int>& sweetness, int k) {
    int low = *min_element(sweetness.begin(), sweetness.end());
    int high = accumulate(sweetness.begin(), sweetness.end(), 0) / (k + 1);
    int result = 0;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (canDivide(sweetness, k, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            low = mid + 1;  // MAXIMIZE: Try LARGER!
        } else {
            high = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
OPPOSITE PATTERN: Maximize Minimum

Book Allocation: Minimize maximum
  Pattern: F F F T T T
  Update: high = mid - 1 when found

Divide Chocolate: MAXIMIZE minimum
  Pattern: T T T F F F
  Update: low = mid + 1 when found

CRITICAL: Get update direction right!

WHY THIS IS CORRECT:

Monotonicity: If minSweet X works -> X-1 works
  Smaller threshold -> easier to achieve
  Pattern: T T T F F F

Find last True = Maximum feasible minimum

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log S)
  S = sum(sweetness)

Space: O(1)

Pattern: "Maximize minimum" -> Update low when found!
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 7.17: SPOJ AGGRCOW - Aggressive Cows
// Link: https://www.spoj.com/problems/AGGRCOW/
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1600-1700)
// Technique: BSOA - Maximize Minimum Distance
// ===============================================================

bool canPlaceCows(vector<int>& stalls, int cows, int minDist) {
    int count = 1;
    int lastPosition = stalls[0];
    
    for (int i = 1; i < stalls.size(); i++) {
        if (stalls[i] - lastPosition >= minDist) {
            count++;
            lastPosition = stalls[i];
            
            if (count >= cows) {
                return true;
            }
        }
    }
    
    return false;
}

int aggressiveCows(vector<int>& stalls, int cows) {
    sort(stalls.begin(), stalls.end());
    
    int n = stalls.size();
    int low = 1;
    int high = stalls[n-1] - stalls[0];
    int result = 0;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (canPlaceCows(stalls, cows, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            low = mid + 1;  // MAXIMIZE: Try larger distance
        } else {
            high = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
NON-CONTIGUOUS placement:
  Unlike book allocation
  Can place at ANY stall

Greedy strategy:
  Place cows as EARLY as possible
  This maximizes options for future cows

Proof: Early placement is never suboptimal

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log N + N log D)
  Sort: O(N log N)
  Binary search: O(N log D)
  D = max distance

Space: O(1)

Pattern: "Maximize minimum distance" -> Greedy early placement
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 7.18: LeetCode 1552 - Magnetic Force Between Two Balls
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/magnetic-force-between-two-balls/
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1600)
// Technique: SAME as Aggressive Cows
// ===============================================================

bool canPlaceBalls(vector<int>& position, int m, int minForce) {
    int count = 1;
    int lastPos = position[0];
    
    for (int i = 1; i < position.size(); i++) {
        if (position[i] - lastPos >= minForce) {
            count++;
            lastPos = position[i];
            if (count >= m) return true;
        }
    }
    return false;
}

int maxDistance(vector<int>& position, int m) {
    sort(position.begin(), position.end());
    
    int low = 1;
    int high = position.back() - position[0];
    int result = 0;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        if (canPlaceBalls(position, m, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            low = mid + 1;
        } else {
            high = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    return result;
}

/*
Recognition: This IS aggressive cows!
  Cows -> Balls
  Stalls -> Positions
  Distance -> Force

SAME algorithm proves the pattern!

Time: O(N log N + N log D)
Space: O(1)
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 5: Complex Scheduling}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=infobox!10,
    colframe=infobox,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Complex Scheduling Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Shooting Game (Deadline Scheduling)}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Shoot N balloons at different heights
  Each rises at different speed
  Minimize maximum height reached

Key: Deadline scheduling (EDF)

Strategy:
  For each balloon: Calculate deadline
    deadline = (max_height - initial_height) / speed
  
  Sort by deadline
  Shoot in deadline order
  Check if all deadlines met

Time: O(N log N + N log H × N log N)
  Can optimize to O(N² log H)
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Type 2: Minimum Speed (Train Scheduling)}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Minimum speed to reach on time
  N trains with distances
  Must wait for next hour (ceiling)
  Last train: no waiting

Check: Can arrive with speed S?
  For each train except last:
    time += ceil(distance / speed)
  Last train: time += distance / speed (no ceiling)
  
  return time <= hour

Time: O(N log V)
  V = maximum speed
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Complex Scheduling Solutions]
// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 7.21: AtCoder ABC 023D - Shooting Game
// Link: https://atcoder.jp/contests/abc023/tasks/abc023_d
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 1700)
// Technique: BSOA with EDF Scheduling
// ===============================================================

bool canAchieve(vector<pair<long long, long long>>& balloons, long long maxPenalty) {
    int n = balloons.size();
    vector<pair<long long, int>> deadlines;
    
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        long long h = balloons[i].first;
        long long s = balloons[i].second;
        
        if (h > maxPenalty) return false;
        
        long long deadline = (maxPenalty - h) / s;
        deadlines.push_back({deadline, i});
    }
    
    sort(deadlines.begin(), deadlines.end());
    
    long long time = 0;
    for (auto [d, idx] : deadlines) {
        if (time > d) return false;
        time++;
    }
    return true;
}

long long minimizeMaxPenalty(vector<pair<long long, long long>>& balloons) {
    long long low = 0;
    long long high = 0;
    
    for (auto [h, s] : balloons) {
        high = max(high, h + (long long)balloons.size() * s);
    }
    
    long long result = high;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        long long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        if (canAchieve(balloons, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    return result;
}

/*
DEADLINE SCHEDULING (EDF):
  Calculate deadline for each balloon
  Sort by deadline (earliest first)
  Shoot in deadline order
  
Proof: EDF is optimal for unit-time tasks

Time: O(N log N + N log H \times N log N)
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 7.23: LeetCode 1870 - Minimum Speed to Arrive on Time
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/minimum-speed-to-arrive-on-time/
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1600)
// Technique: BSOA with Train Scheduling
// ===============================================================

bool canArrive(vector<int>& dist, double hour, int speed) {
    double time = 0.0;
    int n = dist.size();
    
    for (int i = 0; i < n - 1; i++) {
        time += ceil((double)dist[i] / speed);
        if (time > hour) return false;
    }
    
    time += (double)dist[n-1] / speed;
    return time <= hour;
}

int minSpeedOnTime(vector<int>& dist, double hour) {
    int low = 1;
    int high = 1e7;
    int result = -1;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        if (canArrive(dist, hour, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    return result;
}

/*
TRAIN SCHEDULING:
  Must wait for next hour (ceiling)
  Last train: no waiting (exact time)

Key: Different time calculation for last train

Time: O(N log V)
  V = maximum speed
*/
\end{lstlisting}

% ==========================================
% CHAPTER 8: AGGRESSIVE COWS AND PLACEMENT
% ==========================================

\chapter{Aggressive Cows and Placement Problems}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,
    colback=white,
    colframe=secondaryblue,
    boxrule=1pt,
    arc=2mm,
    left=10pt,
    right=10pt
]
\textbf{\large\textcolor{primaryblue}{Maximize Minimum Distance, Placement Optimization}}
\end{tcolorbox}

\vspace{0.5cm}

\begin{center}
\textit{\textcolor{darkgray}{Difficulty: CF 1500-1800 $\bullet$ 25 Problems}}
\end{center}

\section{Theoretical Foundation}

\subsection{The Maximize-Minimum Paradigm}
When we see the phrase "maximize the minimum distance," we are dealing with a monotonic decision problem. 
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Monotonicity:} If we can place $C$ items with a minimum distance $X$, we can also place them with any distance $Y < X$.
    \item \textbf{Decision Function:} Can we place $C$ items such that for every pair $(i, j)$, $|pos_i - pos_j| \geq X$?
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Greedy Placement Strategy}
The \texttt{check(distance)} function is almost always solved greedily. Because we want to save as much space as possible for future items, we always place the next item at the \textbf{earliest possible valid position}.



\begin{enumerate}
    \item Sort the available positions $P$ in ascending order.
    \item Place the first item at $P[0]$.
    \item Iterate through the remaining positions. Place the next item at $P[i]$ only if $P[i] - \text{last\_placed\_position} \geq \text{distance}$.
    \item If we manage to place all items, return \texttt{true}.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Aggressive Cows and Placement Problems: Complete Mastery}

\subsection{Complete Theoretical Foundation}

\subsubsection{Why Placement Problems?}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=white,
    colframe=deepblue,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{The Maximize-Minimum Paradigm}
]
\textbf{Why Study Placement Optimization?}

Most fundamental: Maximize minimum distance between placed items

\textbf{The Paradigm Shift:}
\begin{verbatim}
Classic Problem:
  Given: N positions available
  Task: Place K items
  Goal: Maximize minimum distance between any two items
  
Transformation:
  Optimization: "What is maximum min_distance?"
  → Feasibility: "Can we place K items with distance ≥ X?"
  
KEY DIFFERENCE:
  Direct optimization: Hard to calculate
  Feasibility checking: Easy with greedy placement
  Binary search: Find optimal X
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Complete Theory:}

1. \textbf{What is Maximize-Minimum?}
\begin{verbatim}
Placement problem: check(x) → {True, False}
  Input: Candidate minimum distance x
  Output: Can we place K items with all distances ≥ x?

Example: "Can we place C cows with min distance = x?"
  check(x) = true if possible
  check(x) = false if impossible

Monotonicity requirement:
  Pattern: T T T T T F F F F  (Find last True)

If check(x) = true:
  All y < x also true (smaller distance easier)
  Find LAST True = MAXIMUM feasible distance

This monotonicity enables binary search!

CRITICAL DIFFERENCE from minimize-maximum:
  Minimize max: F F F T T T (find first True)
  Maximize min: T T T F F F (find last True)
  
  UPDATE DIRECTION IS OPPOSITE!
\end{verbatim}

2. \textbf{The Template Pattern:}
\begin{verbatim}
Complete template for "Maximize minimum distance"

Algorithm:
  bool check(x):
    # Can we place K items with min_distance ≥ x?
    # Usually greedy placement
    return feasible
  
  int maximizeMinimum():
    low = 1                # Minimum possible distance
    high = max_span        # Maximum possible span
    result = 0
    
    while low <= high:
      mid = low + (high - low) / 2
      
      if check(mid):
        result = mid      # mid works!
        low = mid + 1     # Try LARGER (OPPOSITE!)
      else:
        high = mid - 1    # mid doesn't work, try smaller
    
    return result

Key differences from minimize-maximum:
  1. Pattern: T T T F F F (not F F F T T T)
  2. Update: low = mid + 1 when found (not high = mid - 1)
  3. Find last True (not first True)

Time: O(N log D × T_check)
  D = max_distance - min_distance
  T_check = time for check() function (usually O(N))
\end{verbatim}

3. \textbf{Finding Search Space:}
\begin{verbatim}
CRITICAL STEP: Determine [low, high]

For Maximize Minimum Distance:
  low = 1               # Minimum possible spacing
  high = positions[N-1] - positions[0]  # Maximum span

Example: Aggressive cows
  Stalls: [1, 2, 4, 8, 9], Cows: 3
  
  min_distance = 1 (adjacent placement)
  max_distance = 9 - 1 = 8 (cows at ends)
  
  Search space: [1, 8]

Getting bounds wrong:
  Too narrow: Miss answer
  Too wide: Still correct, just slower
  
  Err on side of wider bounds!
\end{verbatim}

4. \textbf{The Greedy Placement Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Most important: Greedy placement is OPTIMAL

Greedy Algorithm:
  1. Sort positions in ascending order
  2. Place first item at positions[0]
  3. For remaining items:
     - Place at earliest position where distance ≥ min_dist
  4. Count items placed

Why greedy works:
  Theorem: Early placement maximizes options for future items
  
  Proof: Placing later never gives more options
         Placing early always better or equal
         Therefore: Greedy is optimal

Example: Stalls=[1,2,4,8,9], cows=3, min_dist=3
  Place cow 1 at 1
  Next valid: 1+3=4, place at 4 ✓
  Next valid: 4+3=7, place at 8 ✓
  Total: 3 cows placed ✓

Implementation:
  bool check(min_dist):
    count = 1
    last_position = positions[0]
    
    for i in 1..N-1:
      if positions[i] - last_position >= min_dist:
        count++
        last_position = positions[i]
        
        if count >= K:
          return true
    
    return count >= K

Time: O(N) per check
Total: O(N log D) for binary search
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Key Insight:}
Binary search on ANSWER + greedy placement solves maximize-minimum problems!
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Pattern 1: Aggressive Cows (Classic)}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=successgreen!10,
    colframe=successgreen,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Aggressive Cows Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Original Aggressive Cows}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Place C cows in N stalls
  Maximize minimum distance between any two cows
  
Example: stalls=[1,2,4,8,9], cows=3
  
Find: Maximum minimum distance

Key insight: Smaller distance always works
  If min_distance = X works, X-1 also works
  Monotonic: T T T T F F F ...
  
  Find last True (maximum)

Search space:
  min = 1             # Adjacent placement
  max = stalls[N-1] - stalls[0]  # Cows at ends

Greedy placement:
  bool canPlaceCows(min_dist):
    count = 1
    last_pos = stalls[0]
    
    for i in 1..N-1:
      if stalls[i] - last_pos >= min_dist:
        count++
        last_pos = stalls[i]
    
    return count >= C

Algorithm:
  int aggressiveCows(stalls, C):
    sort(stalls)  # MUST sort first!
    
    low = 1
    high = stalls[N-1] - stalls[0]
    result = 0
    
    while low <= high:
      mid = low + (high - low) / 2
      
      if canPlaceCows(mid):
        result = mid
        low = mid + 1   # Try LARGER distance
      else:
        high = mid - 1  # Too large, try smaller
    
    return result

Example: stalls=[1,2,4,8,9], cows=3
  Search space: [1, 8]
  
  Try 4: Can place?
    Place at 1, 8 (distance 7 ≥ 4)
    Count: 2 < 3 ✗
  
  Try 2:
    Place at 1, 4, 8 (distances: 3, 4)
    Count: 3 ✓
  
  Try 3:
    Place at 1, 4, 8 (distances: 3, 4)
    Count: 3 ✓
  
  Try 4: Already know fails
  
  Answer: 3

Time: O(N log N + N log D)
  Sort: O(N log N)
  Binary search: O(N log D)
  D = max_distance

Space: O(1)
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Type 2: Magnetic Force Between Balls}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Place m balls in n baskets
  Maximize minimum magnetic force (distance)

This is IDENTICAL to aggressive cows!
  Cows → Balls
  Stalls → Baskets
  Distance → Force

Same algorithm, same complexity

Time: O(N log N + N log D)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Aggressive Cows Solutions]
// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 8.1: SPOJ AGGRCOW - Aggressive Cows
// Link: https://www.spoj.com/problems/AGGRCOW/
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1600-1700)
// Technique: BSOA - Maximize Minimum Distance
// ===============================================================

bool canPlaceCows(vector<int>& stalls, int cows, int minDist) {
    int count = 1;
    int lastPosition = stalls[0];
    
    for (int i = 1; i < stalls.size(); i++) {
        if (stalls[i] - lastPosition >= minDist) {
            count++;
            lastPosition = stalls[i];
            
            if (count >= cows) {
                return true;
            }
        }
    }
    
    return false;
}

int aggressiveCows(vector<int>& stalls, int cows) {
    sort(stalls.begin(), stalls.end());  // MUST sort!
    
    int n = stalls.size();
    int low = 1;
    int high = stalls[n-1] - stalls[0];
    int result = 0;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (canPlaceCows(stalls, cows, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            low = mid + 1;  // MAXIMIZE: Try larger distance
        } else {
            high = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

BSOA transformation:
  Optimization: Find maximum min_distance
  Feasibility: Can place C cows with distance >= X?

Monotonicity: If X works -> X-1 works
  Pattern: T T T F F F
  Find last True (maximum)

Search space:
  Lower: 1 - minimum spacing
  Upper: stalls[N-1] - stalls[0] - maximum span

Greedy placement:
  Place as EARLY as possible
  This maximizes options for future cows

Proof of greedy:
  Theorem: Early placement is optimal
  
  Proof: Consider two strategies:
    Early: Place at first valid position
    Late: Place later than necessary
    
    Early gives more positions for future items
    Late restricts future options
    
    Therefore: Early (greedy) is optimal 

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log N + N log D)
  Sort: O(N log N)
  Binary search: log D iterations
  Each check: O(N)
  D = stalls[N-1] - stalls[0]

Space: O(1)

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Try all distances:
   for d in 1..max_distance:
     if check(d): result = d
   
   Time: O(N \times D)
   For large D: Very slow
   
   Our solution: O(N log D)

2. Try all placements:
   Generate all C-combinations
   Check distances for each
   
   Time: O(C(N,C) \times $C^2$)
   Exponential!
   
   Our solution: O(N log D)

3. DP approach:
   State: (position, cows_placed, min_distance)
   
   Complex state space
   
   Our solution: Clean BSOA

Greedy correctness:
  To maximize minimum distance:
    Place cows as early as possible
  
  This is optimal because:
    - Gives most room for remaining cows
    - Never suboptimal to place earlier
    - Proven by exchange argument

Trace for stalls=[1,2,4,8,9], cows=3:

Sort: [1,2,4,8,9]
Search [1, 8]:

Try dist=4:
  Place at 1
  Next >= 5: Place at 8
  Count: 2 < 3 \ding{55}
  high=3

Try dist=2:
  Place at 1
  Next >= 3: Place at 4
  Next >= 6: Place at 8
  Count: 3 \ding{51}
  result=2, low=3

Try dist=3:
  Place at 1
  Next >= 4: Place at 4
  Next >= 7: Place at 8
  Count: 3 \ding{51}
  result=3, low=4

Try dist=4: Already know fails

Return result=3   \ding{51}

Placement: [1, 4, 8]
Distances: 3, 4
Minimum: 3 (maximized!)

Pattern: Maximize minimum distance
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 8.2: LeetCode 1552 - Magnetic Force Between Two Balls
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/magnetic-force-between-two-balls/
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1600)
// Technique: IDENTICAL to Aggressive Cows
// ===============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    bool canPlaceBalls(vector<int>& position, int m, int minForce) {
        int count = 1;
        int lastPos = position[0];
        
        for (int i = 1; i < position.size(); i++) {
            if (position[i] - lastPos >= minForce) {
                count++;
                lastPos = position[i];
                if (count >= m) return true;
            }
        }
        return false;
    }
    
    int maxDistance(vector<int>& position, int m) {
        sort(position.begin(), position.end());
        
        int n = position.size();
        int low = 1;
        int high = position[n-1] - position[0];
        int result = 0;
        
        while (low <= high) {
            int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
            
            if (canPlaceBalls(position, m, mid)) {
                result = mid;
                low = mid + 1;  // MAXIMIZE
            } else {
                high = mid - 1;
            }
        }
        
        return result;
    }
};

/*
Recognition: This IS aggressive cows!
  Cows -> Balls
  Stalls -> Positions
  Distance -> Magnetic force

SAME algorithm, same pattern
Shows universality of the technique!

Time: O(N log N + N log D)
Space: O(1)

Pattern: "Maximize minimum spacing"
  -> Always use aggressive cows template!
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 8.14: LeetCode 2517 - Maximum Tastiness of Candy Basket
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/maximum-tastiness-of-candy-basket/
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1600)
// Technique: SAME Pattern Again!
// ===============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    bool canSelect(vector<int>& price, int k, int minDiff) {
        int count = 1;
        int lastPrice = price[0];
        
        for (int i = 1; i < price.size(); i++) {
            if (price[i] - lastPrice >= minDiff) {
                count++;
                lastPrice = price[i];
                if (count >= k) return true;
            }
        }
        return false;
    }
    
    int maximumTastiness(vector<int>& price, int k) {
        sort(price.begin(), price.end());
        
        int n = price.size();
        int low = 0;
        int high = price[n-1] - price[0];
        int result = 0;
        
        while (low <= high) {
            int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
            
            if (canSelect(price, k, mid)) {
                result = mid;
                low = mid + 1;
            } else {
                high = mid - 1;
            }
        }
        
        return result;
    }
};

/*
SAME pattern once more!
  Cows -> Candies
  Stalls -> Prices
  Distance -> Tastiness (price difference)

All maximize-minimum-distance problems use this!

Time: O(N log N + N log D)
Space: O(1)
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 2: Social Distancing (With Intervals)}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=lightgray,
    colframe=accentorange,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Interval-Based Placement Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Social Distancing}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Place N cows in disjoint intervals
  Maximize minimum distance
  
Example: intervals=[[0,10],[20,30]], cows=4

Extension of aggressive cows:
  Not discrete positions: Continuous ranges
  Can place anywhere in intervals

Greedy placement:
  For each interval [start, end]:
    position = max(start, last_position + min_dist)
    while position <= end:
      place_cow(position)
      position += min_dist

Search space: [1, max_span]

Time: O(N log N + N log D)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Interval Placement Solutions]
// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 8.3: USACO - Social Distancing
// Link: http://www.usaco.org/index.php?page=viewproblem2&cpid=1038
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 1700-1800)
// Technique: BSOA - Intervals Extension
// ===============================================================

bool canPlaceCowsIntervals(vector<pair<long long, long long>>& intervals, 
                           int cows, long long minDist) {
    int placed = 0;
    long long lastPos = -1e18;  // Very negative
    
    for (auto [start, end] : intervals) {
        long long position = max(start, lastPos + minDist);
        
        while (position <= end) {
            placed++;
            lastPos = position;
            
            if (placed >= cows) {
                return true;
            }
            
            position += minDist;
        }
    }
    
    return false;
}

long long socialDistancing(vector<pair<long long, long long>>& intervals, int cows) {
    sort(intervals.begin(), intervals.end());
    
    long long low = 1;
    long long high = intervals.back().second - intervals[0].first;
    long long result = 0;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        long long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (canPlaceCowsIntervals(intervals, cows, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            low = mid + 1;  // MAXIMIZE
        } else {
            high = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
WHY THIS IS DIFFERENT:

Aggressive Cows (Discrete):
  Fixed positions: [1,2,4,8,9]
  Can only place at these points
  
Social Distancing (Continuous):
  Intervals: [[0,10],[20,30]]
  Can place at ANY position within intervals

Greedy placement:
  For each interval:
    Start at earliest valid position
    Place cows with min_dist spacing
    Continue until interval ends

Example: intervals=[[0,10],[20,30]], cows=4, dist=5
  Interval 1: Place at 0, 5, 10 (3 cows)
  Interval 2: Next valid is 15, but start is 20
              Place at 20, 25 (2 cows)
  Total: 5 cows >= 4 

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log N + N log D \times L/D)
  N = number of intervals
  D = distance
  L = total length
  
  Approximates O(N log D) for practical inputs

Space: O(1)

Pattern: Continuous intervals -> Same greedy, adapted check
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 3: Divide Chocolate (Maximize Minimum Sum)}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=warnyellow!10,
    colframe=warnyellow,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Maximize Minimum Sum Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Divide Chocolate}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Divide chocolate bar
  K+1 pieces (K friends + you)
  Maximize minimum sweetness

Different from distance:
  Not spacing between items
  Value of pieces themselves

Greedy cutting:
  Cut when reach minimum threshold
  This maximizes minimum value

Search space: [min_element, sum/(K+1)]

Time: O(N log S)
  S = sum(sweetness)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Maximize Minimum Sum Solutions]
// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 8.8: LeetCode 1231 - Divide Chocolate
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/divide-chocolate/
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 1600-1700)
// Technique: BSOA - Maximize Minimum Sum
// ===============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    bool canDivide(vector<int>& sweetness, int k, int minSweet) {
        int pieces = 0;
        int currentSum = 0;
        
        for (int sweet : sweetness) {
            currentSum += sweet;
            
            if (currentSum >= minSweet) {
                pieces++;
                currentSum = 0;
                
                if (pieces >= k + 1) {
                    return true;
                }
            }
        }
        
        return pieces >= k + 1;
    }
    
    int maximizeSweetness(vector<int>& sweetness, int k) {
        int low = *min_element(sweetness.begin(), sweetness.end());
        int high = accumulate(sweetness.begin(), sweetness.end(), 0) / (k + 1);
        int result = 0;
        
        while (low <= high) {
            int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
            
            if (canDivide(sweetness, k, mid)) {
                result = mid;
                low = mid + 1;  // MAXIMIZE
            } else {
                high = mid - 1;
            }
        }
        
        return result;
    }
};

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

Different from distance:
  Not placing items with spacing
  Dividing into pieces with values

Greedy cutting:
  Cut when sum reaches threshold
  This maximizes minimum piece value

Proof: Cutting earlier -> smaller minimum
       Cutting at threshold -> optimal

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log S)
  S = sum(sweetness)

Space: O(1)

Pattern: "Maximize minimum value" (not distance)
  Similar BSOA but different check()
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 8.9: CF 1201C - Maximum Median
// Link: https://codeforces.com/problemset/problem/1201/C
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1600-1700)
// Technique: BSOA - Maximize Median Value
// ===============================================================

bool canAchieveMedian(vector<long long>& arr, long long k, long long targetMedian) {
    int n = arr.size();
    int medianIdx = n / 2;
    long long operations = 0;
    
    for (int i = medianIdx; i < n; i++) {
        if (arr[i] < targetMedian) {
            operations += targetMedian - arr[i];
            
            if (operations > k) {
                return false;
            }
        }
    }
    
    return operations <= k;
}

long long maximumMedian(vector<long long>& arr, long long k) {
    sort(arr.begin(), arr.end());
    
    int n = arr.size();
    long long low = arr[n / 2];
    long long high = arr[n / 2] + k;
    long long result = low;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        long long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (canAchieveMedian(arr, k, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            low = mid + 1;  // MAXIMIZE
        } else {
            high = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
Maximize median after K increments:
  Only increase elements from median onward
  These directly affect median value

Greedy: Increase median and all after it

Time: O(N log N + N log K)
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 4: Minimize Maximum Distance (Opposite Again!)}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=dangered!10,
    colframe=dangered,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Minimize Maximum Distance Algorithms}
]
\textbf{CRITICAL: Back to Minimize-Maximum Pattern!}

\textbf{Pattern Comparison:}
\begin{verbatim}
Maximize Minimum (Aggressive Cows):
  Pattern: T T T F F F
  Update: low = mid + 1 when found
  
Minimize Maximum (This pattern):
  Pattern: F F F T T T
  Update: high = mid - 1 when found
  
BACK to the minimize-maximum pattern!
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Type 1: Minimize Max Distance to Gas Station}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Add K gas stations
  Minimize maximum distance between stations

Check: Can achieve max_dist with K stations?
  For each gap: stations_needed = ceil(gap / max_dist) - 1

Search space: [0, max_initial_gap]

Time: O(N log(max_gap))
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Minimize Maximum Distance Solutions]
// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 8.11: LeetCode 774 - Minimize Max Distance to Gas Station
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/minimize-max-distance-to-gas-station/
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 1700)
// Technique: BSOA - Minimize Maximum (Floating Point!)
// ===============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    bool canAchieve(vector<int>& stations, int k, double maxDist) {
        int needed = 0;
        
        for (int i = 0; i < stations.size() - 1; i++) {
            double gap = stations[i+1] - stations[i];
            
            if (gap > maxDist) {
                needed += (int)(gap / maxDist);
                
                if (needed > k) {
                    return false;
                }
            }
        }
        
        return needed <= k;
    }
    
    double minmaxGasDist(vector<int>& stations, int k) {
        double low = 0.0;
        double high = 0.0;
        
        for (int i = 0; i < stations.size() - 1; i++) {
            high = max(high, (double)(stations[i+1] - stations[i]));
        }
        
        // Floating point binary search
        while (high - low > 1e-6) {
            double mid = low + (high - low) / 2.0;
            
            if (canAchieve(stations, k, mid)) {
                high = mid;  // MINIMIZE: Try smaller
            } else {
                low = mid;
            }
        }
        
        return low;
    }
};

/*
MINIMIZE MAXIMUM (Not maximize minimum!):
  Pattern: F F F T T T
  Update: high = mid when found

Floating point binary search:
  while (high - low > epsilon)
  Or: 100 fixed iterations

Time: O(N \times iterations)
  iterations = 60 for epsilon=1e-6

Pattern: "Minimize maximum gap" -> This template
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 8.19: AtCoder ABC 174E - Logs
// Link: https://atcoder.jp/contests/abc174/tasks/abc174_e
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1600-1700)
// Technique: BSOA - Minimize Maximum Log Length
// ===============================================================

bool canAchieve(vector<long long>& logs, int k, long long maxLength) {
    long long cuts = 0;
    
    for (long long log : logs) {
        if (log > maxLength) {
            cuts += (log + maxLength - 1) / maxLength - 1;
            
            if (cuts > k) {
                return false;
            }
        }
    }
    
    return cuts <= k;
}

long long minimizeMaxLog(vector<long long>& logs, int k) {
    long long low = 1;
    long long high = *max_element(logs.begin(), logs.end());
    long long result = high;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        long long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (canAchieve(logs, k, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            high = mid - 1;  // MINIMIZE
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
Minimize maximum log length after K cuts:
  Pattern: F F F T T T
  Update: high = mid - 1

Cuts calculation:
  log L, max_length M
  Pieces: ceil(L/M)
  Cuts: ceil(L/M) - 1

Time: O(N log(max_log))
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\begin{tcolorbox}[

    colframe=primaryblue,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Placement Problems: Complete Mastery}
]
\textbf{All Patterns Covered:}

\textbf{Pattern 1: Maximize Minimum Distance (3 problems)}
\begin{itemize}
\item 8.1 Aggressive Cows - Complete with proof
\item 8.2 Magnetic Force - Same pattern
\item 8.14 Maximum Tastiness - Same pattern
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Pattern 2: Interval-Based Placement (1 problem)}
\begin{itemize}

\end{itemize}

\textbf{Pattern 3: Maximize Minimum Sum (2 problems)}
\begin{itemize}

\item 8.9 Maximum Median - Median optimization
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Pattern 4: Minimize Maximum Distance (2 problems)}
\begin{itemize}

\item 8.19 Minimize Max Log Length - Cut optimization
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Key Recognition:}
\begin{itemize}
\item "Maximize minimum distance" → Aggressive cows (T T T F F F)
\item "Continuous intervals" → Adapted greedy
\item "Maximize minimum value" → Similar but different check
\item "Minimize maximum" → Opposite pattern (F F F T T T)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{CRITICAL: Update Direction!}
\begin{itemize}
\item Maximize minimum: low = mid + 1 when found
\item Minimize maximum: high = mid - 1 when found
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Complexity: O(N log D) typical for all patterns!}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Distance Maximization (7 Problems)}

\subsection{Placement \& Positioning (11 Problems)}

\problembox{8.15}{Router Placement}{Place $K$ routers with range $R$ to cover $N$ houses along a road.}{https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/problems/router-placement/1}

\problembox{8.16}{LeetCode 1870: Min Speed to Arrive}{Optimal speed placement across multiple train segments to meet a deadline.}{https://leetcode.com/problems/minimum-speed-to-arrive-on-time/}
