% ==========================================
% CHAPTER 9: SPEED AND TIME-BASED PROBLEMS
% ==========================================

\chapter{Speed and Time-Based Problems}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,
    colback=white,
    colframe=secondaryblue,
    boxrule=1pt,
    arc=2mm,
    left=10pt,
    right=10pt
]
\textbf{\large\textcolor{primaryblue}{Koko Eating Bananas, Minimum Speed, Time Optimization}}
\end{tcolorbox}

\vspace{0.5cm}

\begin{center}
\textit{\textcolor{darkgray}{Difficulty: CF 1500-1800 $\bullet$ 25 Problems}}
\end{center}

\section{Theoretical Foundation}

\subsection{The Time-Rate Relationship}
The most fundamental principle used in these problems is the inverse relationship between speed ($v$) and time ($t$):
\[ t = \frac{Distance}{Speed} \]
In discrete programming problems (like Koko eating bananas), the time spent on a single task is usually:
\[ t_i = \lceil \frac{Work_i}{Rate} \rceil = \lfloor \frac{Work_i + Rate - 1}{Rate} \rfloor \]



\subsection{Monotonicity in Time}
Let $f(v)$ be the total time taken at speed $v$.
\begin{itemize}
    \item If $f(v) \leq \text{Deadline}$, then any speed $v' > v$ will also satisfy the deadline.
    \item Our goal is to find the minimum $v$ such that $f(v) \leq \text{Deadline}$.
\end{itemize}

\section{Speed and Time-Based Problems: Complete Mastery}

\subsection{Complete Theoretical Foundation}

\subsubsection{Why Speed and Time Problems?}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=white,
    colframe=deepblue,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{The Time-Rate Relationship}
]
\textbf{Why Study Speed Optimization?}

Most fundamental: Inverse relationship between speed and time

\textbf{The Core Relationship:}
\begin{verbatim}
Fundamental Formula:
  Time = Work / Speed
  Speed = Work / Time
  
Discrete Version (Ceiling):
  Time = ceil(Work / Speed)
  Time = (Work + Speed - 1) / Speed  (integer division)
  
Example: Eat 11 bananas at speed 4/hour
  Time = ceil(11 / 4) = ceil(2.75) = 3 hours
  Or: (11 + 4 - 1) / 4 = 14 / 4 = 3

KEY INSIGHT:
  Faster speed → Less time (inverse relationship)
  This monotonicity enables binary search!
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Complete Theory:}

1. \textbf{What is a Speed Problem?}
\begin{verbatim}
Speed optimization: check(speed) → {True, False}
  Input: Candidate speed
  Output: Can we finish within deadline at this speed?

Example: "Can we eat all bananas at speed K within H hours?"
  check(K) = true if total_time ≤ H
  check(K) = false if total_time > H

Monotonicity:
  Pattern: F F F F T T T T T  (Find first True)

If check(speed) = true:
  All higher speeds also true (faster → quicker)
  Find FIRST True = MINIMUM speed needed

This monotonicity enables binary search!
\end{verbatim}

2. \textbf{The Template Pattern:}
\begin{verbatim}
Complete template for "Minimum speed to meet deadline"

Algorithm:
  bool check(speed):
    total_time = 0
    for each task:
      total_time += ceil(task_work / speed)
    return total_time <= deadline
  
  int minimumSpeed():
    low = 1              # Minimum possible speed
    high = max_work      # Maximum useful speed
    result = high
    
    while low <= high:
      mid = low + (high - low) / 2
      
      if check(mid):
        result = mid      # mid works!
        high = mid - 1    # Try slower (smaller)
      else:
        low = mid + 1     # mid doesn't work, need faster
    
    return result

Key points:
  1. Pattern: F F F T T T (minimize speed)
  2. Update: high = mid - 1 when found
  3. Ceiling division for discrete time

Time: O(N log(max_work) × T_check)
  T_check = time for check() function (usually O(N))
\end{verbatim}

3. \textbf{Finding Search Space:}
\begin{verbatim}
CRITICAL STEP: Determine [low, high]

For Minimum Speed:
  low = 1                # Slowest possible
  high = max(work)       # Fast enough for largest task in 1 unit
  
  Or more precisely:
  high = max(work) if single task matters
  high = sum(work) if total matters

Example: Koko eating bananas
  piles = [3, 6, 7, 11], H = 8
  
  min_speed = 1 (slowest)
  max_speed = 11 (eat largest pile in 1 hour)
  
  Search space: [1, 11]

Getting bounds wrong:
  Too narrow: Miss answer
  Too wide: Still correct, just slower
  
  Err on side of wider bounds!
\end{verbatim}

4. \textbf{The Ceiling Division Pattern:}
\begin{verbatim}
Most important: Handle discrete time correctly

Ceiling division methods:

Method 1: Using ceil() function
  time = ceil((double)work / speed)
  Problem: Floating point precision issues

Method 2: Integer arithmetic (PREFERRED)
  time = (work + speed - 1) / speed
  Advantage: Exact, no floating point

Why it works:
  ceil(a/b) = floor((a + b - 1) / b)
  
  Example: work=11, speed=4
    ceil(11/4) = ceil(2.75) = 3
    (11 + 4 - 1) / 4 = 14 / 4 = 3 ✓

Implementation:
  long long time_needed = 0;
  for (int work : tasks) {
    time_needed += (work + speed - 1) / speed;
  }

Time: O(N) per check
Total: O(N log(max_work)) for binary search
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Key Insight:}
Binary search on SPEED transforms time optimization into feasibility checking!
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Pattern 1: Minimum Speed (Koko Type)}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=successgreen!10,
    colframe=successgreen,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Minimum Speed Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Koko Eating Bananas}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Eat all banana piles within H hours
  Find minimum eating speed K
  
Example: piles=[3,6,7,11], H=8
  
Find: Minimum speed K

Key insight: Faster speed always works
  If speed K works, K+1 also works
  Monotonic: F F F T T T ...
  
  Find first True (minimum)

Search space:
  min = 1         # Slowest speed
  max = max(piles) # Eat largest in 1 hour

Time calculation:
  For each pile: hours = ceil(pile / K)
  Total: sum of all hours

Check function:
  bool canEat(K, H):
    total_hours = 0
    for pile in piles:
      total_hours += ceil(pile / K)
      # Or: (pile + K - 1) / K
    return total_hours <= H

Example: piles=[3,6,7,11], H=8
  
  Try K=4:
    Hours: ceil(3/4) + ceil(6/4) + ceil(7/4) + ceil(11/4)
         = 1 + 2 + 2 + 3 = 8 hours ≤ 8 ✓
  
  Try K=3:
    Hours: 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 = 10 > 8 ✗
  
  Answer: 4

Time: O(N log M) where M = max(piles)
Space: O(1)
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Type 2: Minimum Speed to Arrive}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Travel on N trains to reach on time
  Must wait for next hour between trains
  Last train: no waiting
  Find minimum speed

Key difference: Ceiling for waiting, exact for last

Time calculation:
  For trains 0..N-2: ceil(dist[i] / speed)
  For train N-1: dist[N-1] / speed (exact!)

Time: O(N log(max_speed))
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Minimum Speed Solutions]
// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 9.1: LeetCode 875 - Koko Eating Bananas
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/koko-eating-bananas/
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1500-1600)
// 
// Problem Statement:
// Koko loves to eat bananas. There are n piles of bananas, the i-th pile 
// has piles[i] bananas. The guards have gone and will come back in h hours.
// Koko can decide her bananas-per-hour eating speed of k. Each hour, she 
// chooses some pile of bananas and eats k bananas from that pile. If the 
// pile has less than k bananas, she eats all of them instead and will not 
// eat any more bananas during this hour.
// Return the minimum integer k such that she can eat all the bananas within h hours.
//
// Technique: BSOA - Minimize Speed with Ceiling Division
// ===============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    bool canEat(vector<int>& piles, int h, int k) {
        long long hours = 0;
        
        for (int pile : piles) {
            // Ceiling division: (pile + k - 1) / k
            hours += (pile + k - 1) / k;
            
            // Early termination
            if (hours > h) {
                return false;
            }
        }
        
        return hours <= h;
    }
    
    int minEatingSpeed(vector<int>& piles, int h) {
        // Search space: [1, max(piles)]
        int low = 1;
        int high = *max_element(piles.begin(), piles.end());
        int result = high;
        
        while (low <= high) {
            int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
            
            if (canEat(piles, h, mid)) {
                result = mid;
                high = mid - 1;  // Try slower
            } else {
                low = mid + 1;   // Need faster
            }
        }
        
        return result;
    }
};

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

Problem Analysis:
  - N piles of bananas
  - H hours available
  - Eat at speed K bananas/hour
  - Find minimum K

BSOA transformation:
  Optimization: Find minimum speed K
  Feasibility: Can eat all piles in H hours at speed K?

Monotonicity: If speed K works -> K+1 works
  Pattern: F F F T T T
  Find first True (minimum)

Search space:
  Lower: 1 - minimum possible speed
  Upper: max(piles) - eat largest pile in 1 hour

Ceiling division:
  Each pile takes: ceil(pile / K) hours
  Using: (pile + K - 1) / K avoids floating point

Example calculation:
  pile = 11, K = 4
  Hours = (11 + 4 - 1) / 4 = 14 / 4 = 3
  Verify: ceil(11/4) = ceil(2.75) = 3  \ding{51}

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log M)
  N = number of piles
  M = max(piles)
  
  Binary search: log M iterations
  Each check: O(N)

Space: O(1)

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Try all speeds:
   for k in 1..max(piles):
     if check(k): return k
   
   Time: O(N \times max(piles))
   For max=10^9: Very slow!
   
   Our solution: O(N log M)

2. Mathematical formula:
   Try to compute directly
   
   Problem: No closed form exists
   Ceiling divisions make it complex
   
   Our solution: Binary search finds exact answer

3. Greedy approach:
   Try to distribute hours optimally
   
   Problem: No clear greedy strategy
   Multiple piles, complex interactions
   
   Our solution: BSOA is clean and correct

Ceiling division importance:
  Can't eat fraction of banana in an hour
  Must complete pile or wait
  Ceiling captures this discrete nature

Trace for piles=[3,6,7,11], H=8:

Search [1, 11]:

Try K=6:
  Hours: (3+5)/6 + (6+5)/6 + (7+5)/6 + (11+5)/6
       = 8/6 + 11/6 + 12/6 + 16/6
       = 1 + 1 + 2 + 2 = 6 <= 8  \ding{51}
  result=6, high=5

Try K=3:
  Hours: 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 = 10 > 8 \ding{55}
  low=4

Try K=4:
  Hours: 1 + 2 + 2 + 3 = 8 <= 8  \ding{51}
  result=4, high=3

Try K=3: Already know fails

Return result=4 \ding{51}

Verification:
  Speed 4: [3->1h, 6->2h, 7->2h, 11->3h] = 8h \ding{51}
  Speed 3: [3->1h, 6->2h, 7->3h, 11->4h] = 10h \ding{55}

Minimum speed = 4
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 9.2: LeetCode 1870 - Minimum Speed to Arrive on Time
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/minimum-speed-to-arrive-on-time/
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1600)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You are given a floating-point number hour, representing the amount of time
// you have to reach the office. To commute to the office, you must take n trains
// in sequential order. You are also given an integer array dist of length n,
// where dist[i] describes the distance (in kilometers) of the i-th train ride.
// Each train can only depart at an integer hour, so you may need to wait between
// each train ride. Return the minimum positive integer speed (in km/h) that all
// the trains must travel at for you to reach the office on time, or -1 if 
// impossible.
//
// Technique: BSOA - Train Scheduling with Ceiling Time
// ===============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    bool canArrive(vector<int>& dist, double hour, int speed) {
        double time = 0.0;
        int n = dist.size();
        
        // All trains except last: must wait for next hour
        for (int i = 0; i < n - 1; i++) {
            time += ceil((double)dist[i] / speed);
            
            if (time > hour) {
                return false;
            }
        }
        
        // Last train: no waiting, exact time
        time += (double)dist[n-1] / speed;
        
        return time <= hour;
    }
    
    int minSpeedOnTime(vector<int>& dist, double hour) {
        int n = dist.size();
        
        // Impossible case: need at least n-1 hours for waiting
        if (hour <= n - 1) {
            return -1;
        }
        
        int low = 1;
        int high = 1e7;  // Maximum reasonable speed
        int result = -1;
        
        while (low <= high) {
            int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
            
            if (canArrive(dist, hour, mid)) {
                result = mid;
                high = mid - 1;
            } else {
                low = mid + 1;
            }
        }
        
        return result;
    }
};

/*
WHY THIS IS DIFFERENT:

Key difference: Train scheduling
  - Must wait for next hour between trains
  - Last train: no waiting (can arrive mid-hour)

Time calculation:
  Trains 0..N-2: ceil(dist / speed) - round up
  Train N-1: dist / speed - exact time

Example: dist=[1,3,2], hour=6, speed=1
  Train 0: ceil(1/1) = 1 hour, wait to hour 1
  Train 1: ceil(3/1) = 3 hours, wait to hour 4
  Train 2: 2/1 = 2 hours (exact)
  Total: 1 + 3 + 2 = 6 hours \ding{51}

WHY THIS IS CORRECT:

Impossible case:
  Need at least N-1 hours for waiting
  If hour <= N-1: impossible

Search space:
  high = 10^7 (large enough for constraints)

Time: O(N log(max_speed))
Space: O(1)

Pattern: Same minimize speed, different time calculation
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 9.3: LeetCode 1283 - Find the Smallest Divisor Given a Threshold
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/find-the-smallest-divisor-given-a-threshold/
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1500-1600)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Given an array of integers nums and an integer threshold, we will choose a
// positive integer divisor, divide all the array by it, and sum the division's
// result. Find the smallest divisor such that the result mentioned above is
// less than or equal to threshold.
// Each result of the division is rounded to the nearest integer greater than
// or equal to that element. (For example: 7/3 = 3 and 10/2 = 5).
//
// Technique: BSOA - Minimize Divisor (Same Pattern!)
// ===============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    bool checkDivisor(vector<int>& nums, int threshold, int divisor) {
        long long sum = 0;
        
        for (int num : nums) {
            sum += (num + divisor - 1) / divisor;  // Ceiling division
            
            if (sum > threshold) {
                return false;
            }
        }
        
        return sum <= threshold;
    }
    
    int smallestDivisor(vector<int>& nums, int threshold) {
        int low = 1;
        int high = *max_element(nums.begin(), nums.end());
        int result = high;
        
        while (low <= high) {
            int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
            
            if (checkDivisor(nums, threshold, mid)) {
                result = mid;
                high = mid - 1;
            } else {
                low = mid + 1;
            }
        }
        
        return result;
    }
};

/*
SAME PATTERN as Koko!
  Bananas -> Numbers
  Hours -> Threshold
  Speed -> Divisor

Ceiling division for each number
Sum must be <= threshold

Time: O(N log M) where M = max(nums)
Space: O(1)

Pattern: "Minimize rate/divisor with threshold" -> This template
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 2: Minimum Time (Factory Machines Type)}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=lightgray,
    colframe=accentorange,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Minimum Time Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Factory Machines}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: N machines produce T products
  Machine i takes time[i] per product
  All work in parallel
  Find minimum time

Production calculation:
  In time T, machine i produces: T / time[i] products
  Total: sum of all productions

Check: Can produce T products in time X?
  total = sum(X / machine[i])
  return total >= T

Search space: [1, slowest × T]

Time: O(N log(M×T))
  M = slowest machine
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Minimum Time Solutions]
// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 9.8: CSES 1620 - Factory Machines
// Link: https://cses.fi/problemset/task/1620
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1500)
//
// Problem Statement:
// A factory has n machines which can be used to make products. Your goal is
// to make a total of t products. For each machine, you know the number of
// seconds it needs to make a single product. The machines can work simultaneously.
// What is the shortest time needed to make t products?
//
// Technique: BSOA - Parallel Production
// ===============================================================

bool canProduce(vector<long long>& machines, long long products, long long time) {
    long long total = 0;
    
    for (long long machineTime : machines) {
        total += time / machineTime;
        
        if (total >= products) {
            return true;
        }
    }
    
    return total >= products;
}

long long minimumTime(vector<long long>& machines, long long products) {
    long long low = 1;
    long long high = *max_element(machines.begin(), machines.end()) * products;
    long long result = high;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        long long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (canProduce(machines, products, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
WHY THIS IS DIFFERENT:

Pattern: Minimize TIME (not speed)
  But same BSOA approach!

Production rate:
  Machine with speed S produces T/S in time T
  All machines work simultaneously
  Total = sum of individual productions

Example: machines=[3,2,5], products=8, time=10
  Machine 1: 10/3 = 3 products
  Machine 2: 10/2 = 5 products
  Machine 3: 10/5 = 2 products
  Total: 10 products >= 8 \ding{51}

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log(M \times T))
  M = slowest machine
  T = products

Space: O(1)

Pattern: "Minimize time with parallel work" -> This template
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 9.6: LeetCode 2187 - Minimum Time to Complete Trips
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/minimum-time-to-complete-trips/
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1600)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You are given an array time where time[i] denotes the time taken by the
// i-th bus to complete one trip. Each bus can make multiple trips successively.
// You are also given an integer totalTrips, which denotes the number of trips
// all buses should make in total. Return the minimum time required for all
// buses to complete at least totalTrips trips.
//
// Technique: BSOA - Same as Factory Machines!
// ===============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    bool canComplete(vector<int>& time, int totalTrips, long long givenTime) {
        long long trips = 0;
        
        for (int busTime : time) {
            trips += givenTime / busTime;
            
            if (trips >= totalTrips) {
                return true;
            }
        }
        
        return false;
    }
    
    long long minimumTime(vector<int>& time, int totalTrips) {
        long long low = 1;
        long long high = (long long)*min_element(time.begin(), time.end()) * totalTrips;
        long long result = high;
        
        while (low <= high) {
            long long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
            
            if (canComplete(time, totalTrips, mid)) {
                result = mid;
                high = mid - 1;
            } else {
                low = mid + 1;
            }
        }
        
        return result;
    }
};

/*
IDENTICAL to factory machines!
  Machines -> Buses
  Products -> Trips
  Machine time -> Bus time

Same parallel production pattern

Time: O(N log(M \times T))
Space: O(1)
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 3: Poisoned Dagger (Time Intervals)}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=warnyellow!10,
    colframe=warnyellow,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Time Interval Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Poisoned Dagger}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Hit dragon at specific times
  Each hit: poison for K damage/sec
  Poison duration: until next hit or K seconds
  Find minimum K to kill dragon

Damage calculation:
  Hit at times: t1, t2, ..., tn
  
  Damage from hit i:
    If next hit at t(i+1): min(K, t(i+1) - ti)
    If last hit: K (full duration)

Example: hits=[1,5], health=11, try K=7
  Hit 1: damage = min(7, 5-1) = 4
  Hit 5: damage = 7 (last hit)
  Total: 11 damage ≥ 11 ✓

Search space: [1, health]

Time: O(N log H) where H = health
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Time Interval Solutions]
// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 9.7: CF 1613C - Poisoned Dagger
// Link: https://codeforces.com/contest/1613/problem/C
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1600-1700)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You are playing a computer game. In this game, you need to kill a dragon
// that has h units of health. You make n attacks, the i-th attack is at the
// moment t_i. Each attack deals k damage per second to the dragon until the
// next attack or for k seconds if it's the last attack. Find the minimum k
// such that the total damage is at least h.
//
// Technique: BSOA - Time Interval Damage
// ===============================================================

bool canKillDragon(vector<long long>& attacks, long long health, long long k) {
    long long totalDamage = 0;
    int n = attacks.size();
    
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        if (i < n - 1) {
            // Damage until next attack
            totalDamage += min(k, attacks[i+1] - attacks[i]);
        } else {
            // Last attack: full k damage
            totalDamage += k;
        }
        
        if (totalDamage >= health) {
            return true;
        }
    }
    
    return totalDamage >= health;
}

long long minDamagePerSecond(vector<long long>& attacks, long long health) {
    long long low = 1;
    long long high = health;
    long long result = high;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        long long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (canKillDragon(attacks, health, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
WHY THIS IS DIFFERENT:

Time intervals matter!
  Poison duration: Until next hit OR k seconds
  
  For hit i:
    If i < n-1: damage = min(k, attacks[i+1] - attacks[i])
    If i == n-1: damage = k

Example: attacks=[1,5], health=11
  
  Try k=7:
    Hit 1: min(7, 5-1) = 4 damage
    Hit 5: 7 damage (last)
    Total: 11 >= 11 \ding{51}
  
  Try k=6:
    Hit 1: min(6, 4) = 4
    Hit 5: 6
    Total: 10 < 11 \ding{55}

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log H)
  N = number of attacks
  H = health

Space: O(1)

Pattern: "Time-based damage intervals" -> This template
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 9.15: LeetCode 1482 - Minimum Number of Days to Make m Bouquets
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/minimum-number-of-days-to-make-m-bouquets/
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1600)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You are given an integer array bloomDay, an integer m and an integer k.
// You want to make m bouquets. To make a bouquet, you need to use k adjacent
// flowers from the garden. The garden consists of n flowers, the i-th flower
// will bloom in the bloomDay[i] and then can be used in exactly one bouquet.
// Return the minimum number of days you need to wait to be able to make m
// bouquets from the garden. If it is impossible to make m bouquets return -1.
//
// Technique: BSOA - Time-Based Availability
// ===============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    bool canMakeBouquets(vector<int>& bloomDay, int m, int k, int day) {
        int bouquets = 0;
        int flowers = 0;
        
        for (int bloom : bloomDay) {
            if (bloom <= day) {
                flowers++;
                
                if (flowers == k) {
                    bouquets++;
                    flowers = 0;
                    
                    if (bouquets >= m) {
                        return true;
                    }
                }
            } else {
                flowers = 0;  // Break adjacency
            }
        }
        
        return bouquets >= m;
    }
    
    int minDays(vector<int>& bloomDay, int m, int k) {
        int n = bloomDay.size();
        
        // Impossible case
        if ((long long)m * k > n) {
            return -1;
        }
        
        int low = *min_element(bloomDay.begin(), bloomDay.end());
        int high = *max_element(bloomDay.begin(), bloomDay.end());
        int result = -1;
        
        while (low <= high) {
            int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
            
            if (canMakeBouquets(bloomDay, m, k, mid)) {
                result = mid;
                high = mid - 1;
            } else {
                low = mid + 1;
            }
        }
        
        return result;
    }
};

/*
Time-based availability:
  Flowers bloom at different days
  Need k ADJACENT flowers for bouquet
  
Adjacency tracking:
  If flower not bloomed: reset counter
  Count consecutive bloomed flowers

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log D)
  D = max(bloomDay)

Space: O(1)

Pattern: "Time + adjacency constraint" -> This check
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 4: Floating Point Binary Search}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=dangered!10,
    colframe=dangered,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Floating Point Binary Search}
]
\textbf{Type 1: PIE (Equal Division)}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Divide pies into equal pieces
  Maximize piece size
  F friends need F pieces

Floating point binary search:
  while (high - low > epsilon)
  Or: Fixed 100 iterations

Piece counting:
  For pie with area A, piece size S:
    pieces = floor(A / S)

Search space: [0, max_area]

Time: O(N × iterations)
  iterations ≈ 60 for epsilon = 1e-6
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Floating Point Solutions]
// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 9.12: SPOJ PIE - Pie Distribution
// Link: https://www.spoj.com/problems/PIE/
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1600-1700)
//
// Problem Statement:
// My birthday is coming up and traditionally I'm serving pie. Not just one pie,
// no, I have a number N of them, of various tastes and of various sizes. F of
// my friends are coming to my party and each of them gets a piece of pie. This
// should be one piece of one pie, not several small pieces since that looks
// messy. All of my friends get the same amount of pie (but probably not the same
// type). What is the largest possible piece size all of my friends can get?
//
// Technique: BSOA - FLOATING POINT Binary Search
// ===============================================================

const double PI = acos(-1.0);
const double EPS = 1e-6;

bool canDivide(vector<double>& areas, int friends, double pieceSize) {
    int pieces = 0;
    
    for (double area : areas) {
        pieces += (int)(area / pieceSize);
        
        if (pieces >= friends) {
            return true;
        }
    }
    
    return pieces >= friends;
}

double maxPieceSize(vector<int>& radii, int friends) {
    int n = radii.size();
    
    // Calculate areas
    vector<double> areas;
    for (int r : radii) {
        areas.push_back(PI * r * r);
    }
    
    double low = 0.0;
    double high = *max_element(areas.begin(), areas.end());
    
    // Floating point binary search
    for (int iter = 0; iter < 100; iter++) {
        double mid = (low + high) / 2.0;
        
        if (canDivide(areas, friends, mid)) {
            low = mid;  // Can achieve, try larger
        } else {
            high = mid;  // Too large, try smaller
        }
    }
    
    return low;
}

/*
FLOATING POINT Binary Search:
  
Instead of: while (low <= high)
Use: Fixed iterations (100)

Or: while (high - low > epsilon)

Why 100 iterations?
  Each iteration halves range
  100 iterations: 2^100 precision (more than enough!)

Piece counting:
  pie area A, piece size S
  pieces = floor(A / S)

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N \times iterations)
  iterations = 100 (fixed)

Space: O(N) for areas

Pattern: "Continuous optimization" -> Floating point BS

Alternative: Epsilon-based
  while (high - low > 1e-6) {
    double mid = (low + high) / 2.0;
    if (check(mid)) low = mid;
    else high = mid;
  }
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Summary and Best Practices}

    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=primaryblue!10,
    colframe=primaryblue,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Speed and Time Problems: Complete Mastery}
]

\end{itemize}

\textbf{CRITICAL: Ceiling Division!}
\begin{itemize}
\item Use: (work + speed - 1) / speed
\item NOT: ceil(work / speed) - floating point issues!
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Complexity: O(N log M) typical for all patterns!}

% CHAPTER 10: CUTTING AND DIVISION PROBLEMS

\chapter{Cutting and Division Problems}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,
    colback=white,
    colframe=secondaryblue,
    boxrule=1pt,
    arc=2mm,
    left=10pt,
    right=10pt
]
\textbf{\large\textcolor{primaryblue}{Wood Cutting, Rope Cutting, and Partition Problems}}
\end{tcolorbox}

\vspace{0.5cm}

\begin{center}
\textit{\textcolor{darkgray}{Difficulty: CF 1500-1800 $\bullet$ 25 Problems}}
\end{center}

\section{Theoretical Foundation}

\subsection{The Harvesting Logic}
In cutting problems, we define a threshold $H$. Every element $A[i]$ that exceeds $H$ contributes to our total resource $R$:
\[ R = \sum \max(0, A[i] - H) \]
This function is \textbf{monotonically decreasing} with respect to $H$. As $H$ increases, $R$ decreases. This property is what allows us to binary search for the perfect $H$.



\subsection{Division into K Pieces}
When cutting a rope of length $L$ into pieces of length $X$, the number of pieces obtained is $\lfloor L/X \rfloor$. Our goal is usually to maximize $X$ such that the total pieces from all ropes $\geq K$.
\[ \sum \lfloor \text{Length}_i / X \rfloor \geq K \]

\section{Cutting and Division Problems: Complete Mastery}

\subsection{Complete Theoretical Foundation}

\subsubsection{Why Cutting and Division?}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=white,
    colframe=deepblue,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{The Harvesting and Division Paradigm}
]
\textbf{Why Study Cutting Problems?}

Most fundamental: Resource harvesting and optimal division

\textbf{The Core Patterns:}
\begin{verbatim}
Pattern 1: Harvesting (Wood Cutting)
  Given: Resources above threshold
  Formula: R = sum(max(0, A[i] - H))
  Goal: Find H to harvest exactly M units
  Property: R decreases as H increases (monotonic!)

Pattern 2: Division (Rope Cutting)
  Given: Items to divide into pieces
  Formula: Pieces = sum(floor(Length[i] / X))
  Goal: Find max X to get at least K pieces
  Property: Pieces decrease as X increases (monotonic!)

KEY INSIGHT:
  Both are monotonic → Enable binary search!
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Complete Theory:}

1. \textbf{What is a Cutting Problem?}
\begin{verbatim}
Cutting problem: check(x) → {True, False}
  Input: Candidate threshold/length
  Output: Can we achieve goal with this value?

Example 1: "Can we harvest M wood with saw height H?"
  check(H) = sum(max(0, tree[i] - H)) >= M

Example 2: "Can we get K pieces with length X?"
  check(X) = sum(floor(rope[i] / X)) >= K

Monotonicity:
  Harvesting: T T T T F F F F  (Find last True = max H)
  Division: T T T F F F F      (Find last True = max X)

This monotonicity enables binary search!
\end{verbatim}

2. \textbf{The Template Pattern:}
\begin{verbatim}
TEMPLATE 1: Maximize Harvest Height (Wood Cutting)

Algorithm:
  bool check(H):
    collected = 0
    for tree in trees:
      collected += max(0, tree - H)
    return collected >= M
  
  int maximizeHeight():
    low = 0              # Cut nothing
    high = max(trees)    # Cut at max height
    result = 0
    
    while low <= high:
      mid = low + (high - low) / 2
      
      if check(mid):
        result = mid      # mid works!
        low = mid + 1     # Try higher (MAXIMIZE)
      else:
        high = mid - 1    # mid too high, lower it
    
    return result

TEMPLATE 2: Maximize Piece Length (Rope Cutting)

Algorithm:
  bool check(X):
    pieces = 0
    for rope in ropes:
      pieces += rope / X  # Integer division
    return pieces >= K
  
  int maximizeLength():
    low = 1              # Minimum length
    high = max(ropes)    # Maximum possible
    result = 0
    
    while low <= high:
      mid = low + (high - low) / 2
      
      if check(mid):
        result = mid      # mid works!
        low = mid + 1     # Try longer (MAXIMIZE)
      else:
        high = mid - 1    # mid too long, shorten
    
    return result

Key points:
  1. Pattern: T T T F F F (maximize threshold/length)
  2. Update: low = mid + 1 when found
  3. Monotonic decreasing relationship

Time: O(N log(max_value))
\end{verbatim}

3. \textbf{Finding Search Space:}
\begin{verbatim}
CRITICAL STEP: Determine [low, high]

For Harvest Problems (Wood Cutting):
  low = 0                # Don't cut anything
  high = max(resources)  # Cut at maximum height

For Division Problems (Rope Cutting):
  low = 1                # Minimum piece length
  high = max(lengths)    # Maximum possible length

Example: Wood cutting
  trees = [20, 15, 10, 17], M = 7
  
  min_height = 0 (harvest everything)
  max_height = 20 (harvest nothing)
  
  Search space: [0, 20]

Getting bounds wrong:
  Too narrow: Miss answer
  Too wide: Still correct, just slower
  
  Err on side of wider bounds!
\end{verbatim}

4. \textbf{The Harvesting Formula:}
\begin{verbatim}
Most important: Monotonic relationship

Harvesting:
  Wood collected at height H:
    Wood = sum(max(0, tree[i] - H))
  
  As H increases: Wood decreases
  H=0: Collect everything
  H=max: Collect nothing
  
  Monotonic decreasing!

Division:
  Pieces from length L with size X:
    Pieces = floor(L / X)
  
  As X increases: Pieces decrease
  X=1: Maximum pieces
  X=L: Minimum pieces (1)
  
  Monotonic decreasing!

Example: trees=[20,15,10,17]
  H=0:  Wood = 20+15+10+17 = 62
  H=10: Wood = 10+5+0+7 = 22
  H=15: Wood = 5+0+0+2 = 7
  H=20: Wood = 0+0+0+0 = 0

Clear monotonic decrease!

Time: O(N) per check
Total: O(N log(max_value)) for binary search
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Key Insight:}
Binary search on THRESHOLD transforms resource optimization into feasibility!
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Pattern 1: Wood Cutting (Maximize Harvest)}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=successgreen!10,
    colframe=successgreen,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Wood Cutting Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: EKO Wood Cutting}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Cut trees to harvest at least M wood
  Find maximum saw height H
  
Example: trees=[20,15,10,17], M=7
  
Find: Maximum height H

Key insight: Higher cut → Less wood
  If height H works, H-1 also works
  Monotonic: T T T T F F F ...
  
  Find last True (maximum)

Search space:
  min = 0         # Harvest everything
  max = max(trees) # Harvest nothing

Harvesting formula:
  Wood = sum(max(0, tree[i] - H))

Check function:
  bool canHarvest(H, M):
    wood = 0
    for tree in trees:
      wood += max(0, tree - H)
    return wood >= M

Example: trees=[20,15,10,17], M=7
  
  Try H=15:
    Wood: (20-15) + (15-15) + 0 + (17-15)
        = 5 + 0 + 0 + 2 = 7 ≥ 7 ✓
  
  Try H=16:
    Wood: 4 + 0 + 0 + 1 = 5 < 7 ✗
  
  Answer: 15

Time: O(N log M) where M = max(trees)
Space: O(1)
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Type 2: Log Cutting (Minimize Maximum)}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Cut logs to minimize maximum length
  Make K cuts total
  
OPPOSITE pattern: Minimize maximum (not maximize)
  Pattern: F F F T T T
  Update: high = mid - 1 when found

Cuts calculation:
  Log length L, max length M
  Cuts needed: ceil(L/M) - 1

Time: O(N log(max_log))
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Wood Cutting Solutions]
// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 10.1: SPOJ EKO - Eko Wood Cutting
// Link: https://www.spoj.com/problems/EKO/
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1500-1600)
// 
// Problem Statement:
// Lumberjack Mirko needs to chop down M metres of wood. His chainsaw cuts 
// all trees at a certain height H above ground. He then takes all the wood 
// above height H. For example, if the tree heights are [20,15,10,17] and 
// he sets H=15, he gets (20-15)+(15-15)+(10-15)+(17-15) = 5+0+0+2 = 7 metres.
// Find the maximum height H such that Mirko gets at least M metres of wood.
//
// Technique: BSOA - Maximize Harvest Height
// ===============================================================

bool canHarvest(vector<long long>& trees, long long M, long long height) {
    long long wood = 0;
    
    for (long long tree : trees) {
        if (tree > height) {
            wood += tree - height;
            
            if (wood >= M) {
                return true;
            }
        }
    }
    
    return wood >= M;
}

long long maxSawHeight(vector<long long>& trees, long long M) {
    long long low = 0;
    long long high = *max_element(trees.begin(), trees.end());
    long long result = 0;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        long long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (canHarvest(trees, M, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            low = mid + 1;  // MAXIMIZE: Try higher
        } else {
            high = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

Problem Analysis:
  - N trees with heights
  - Need M metres of wood
  - Cut at height H: collect sum(max(0, tree[i] - H))
  - Find maximum H

BSOA transformation:
  Optimization: Find maximum height H
  Feasibility: Can harvest M wood with height H?

Monotonicity: If H works -> H-1 works
  Lower cut -> More wood collected
  Pattern: T T T T F F F
  Find last True (maximum)

Search space:
  Lower: 0 - cut everything
  Upper: max(trees) - cut nothing

Harvesting formula:
  Wood = sum(max(0, tree - H))
  
  Only trees taller than H contribute
  Each contributes (height - H)

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log M)
  N = number of trees
  M = max(trees)
  
  Binary search: log M iterations
  Each check: O(N)

Space: O(1)

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Try all heights:
   for h in 0..max(trees):
     if check(h) and check(h+1)==false:
       return h
   
   Time: O(N \times max(trees))
   For max=10^9: Very slow!
   
   Our solution: O(N log M)

2. Greedy approach:
   Try to find height directly
   
   Problem: No formula exists
   Multiple trees, complex interactions
   
   Our solution: Binary search finds exact

3. Sort and calculate:
   Sort trees, try to compute
   
   Problem: Still need to search
   
   Our solution: Direct binary search

Harvesting correctness:
  Higher cut -> Less wood
  This monotonicity guarantees:
    - If H works, H-1 also works
    - Binary search finds maximum H

Trace for trees=[20,15,10,17], M=7:

Search [0, 20]:

Try H=10:
  Wood: 10+5+0+7 = 22 >= 7 \ding{51}
  result=10, low=11

Try H=15:
  Wood: 5+0+0+2 = 7 >= 7 \ding{51}
  result=15, low=16

Try H=18:
  Wood: 2+0+0+0 = 2 < 7 \ding{55}
  high=17

Try H=16:
  Wood: 4+0+0+1 = 5 < 7 \ding{55}
  high=15

Try H=15: Already know works

Return result=15 \ding{51}

Verification:
  H=15: Wood = 5+0+0+2 = 7 \ding{51}
  H=16: Wood = 4+0+0+1 = 5 \ding{55}

Maximum height = 15
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 10.2: AtCoder ABC 174E - Logs
// Link: https://atcoder.jp/contests/abc174/tasks/abc174_e
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1600-1700)
//
// Problem Statement:
// There are N logs. The length of the i-th log is A_i. You can perform the
// following operation at most K times:
//   - Choose one log and cut it into two logs.
// Find the minimum possible value of the maximum length of a log.
//
// Technique: BSOA - Minimize Maximum Length
// ===============================================================

bool canAchieve(vector<long long>& logs, int k, long long maxLength) {
    long long cuts = 0;
    
    for (long long log : logs) {
        if (log > maxLength) {
            // Cuts needed: ceil(log/maxLength) - 1
            cuts += (log + maxLength - 1) / maxLength - 1;
            
            if (cuts > k) {
                return false;
            }
        }
    }
    
    return cuts <= k;
}

long long minimizeMaxLog(vector<long long>& logs, int k) {
    long long low = 1;
    long long high = *max_element(logs.begin(), logs.end());
    long long result = high;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        long long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (canAchieve(logs, k, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            high = mid - 1;  // MINIMIZE: Try smaller
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
WHY THIS IS DIFFERENT:

OPPOSITE pattern: Minimize maximum (not maximize!)
  Pattern: F F F T T T
  Update: high = mid - 1 when found

Cuts calculation:
  Log length L, target max M
  
  Pieces needed: ceil(L / M)
  Cuts needed: pieces - 1
  
  Formula: ceil(L/M) - 1
         = (L + M - 1) / M - 1

Example: log=20, max=7
  Pieces: ceil(20/7) = 3
  Cuts: 2

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log(max_log))
Space: O(1)

Pattern: "Minimize maximum length" -> This template
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 10.5: LeetCode 1760 - Minimum Limit of Balls in a Bag
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/minimum-limit-of-balls-in-a-bag/
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1600)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You are given an integer array nums where the i-th bag contains nums[i] balls.
// You are also given an integer maxOperations. You can perform the following
// operation at most maxOperations times:
//   - Take any bag of balls and divide it into two new bags with a positive
//     number of balls.
// Your penalty is the maximum number of balls in a bag. You want to minimize
// your penalty after the operations.
//
// Technique: BSOA - Minimize Maximum (Division)
// ===============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    bool canAchieve(vector<int>& nums, int maxOps, int penalty) {
        int operations = 0;
        
        for (int num : nums) {
            if (num > penalty) {
                operations += (num - 1) / penalty;
                
                if (operations > maxOps) {
                    return false;
                }
            }
        }
        
        return operations <= maxOps;
    }
    
    int minimumSize(vector<int>& nums, int maxOperations) {
        int low = 1;
        int high = *max_element(nums.begin(), nums.end());
        int result = high;
        
        while (low <= high) {
            int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
            
            if (canAchieve(nums, maxOperations, mid)) {
                result = mid;
                high = mid - 1;
            } else {
                low = mid + 1;
            }
        }
        
        return result;
    }
};

/*
SAME pattern as logs!
  Minimize maximum by division
  
Operations calculation:
  Bag with N balls, target max M
  Operations: ceil(N/M) - 1
            = (N-1) / M

Time: O(N log(max(nums)))
Space: O(1)
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 2: Rope Cutting (Maximize Length)}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=lightgray,
    colframe=accentorange,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Rope Cutting Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Rope Division}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Cut N ropes into K equal pieces
  Find maximum piece length
  
Formula: Pieces = sum(floor(rope[i] / length))

Example: ropes=[8,5,7], K=10
  
  Try length=2:
    Pieces: 4 + 2 + 3 = 9 < 10 ✗
  
  Try length=1:
    Pieces: 8 + 5 + 7 = 20 ≥ 10 ✓

Pattern: T T T F F F (maximize length)

Search space: [1, max(ropes)]

Time: O(N log(max_rope))
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Rope Cutting Solutions]
// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 10.13: LeetCode 1891 - Cutting Ribbons
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/cutting-ribbons/
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1600)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You are given an integer array ribbons, where ribbons[i] represents the
// length of the i-th ribbon, and an integer k. You may cut any of the ribbons
// into any number of segments of positive integer lengths, or perform no cuts.
// For example, if you have a ribbon of length 4, you can:
//   - Keep the ribbon of length 4
//   - Cut it into two ribbons of lengths 3 and 1
//   - Cut it into two ribbons of lengths 2 and 2, etc.
// Your goal is to obtain k ribbons of all the same positive integer length.
// Return the maximum possible positive integer length that you can obtain k
// ribbons, or 0 if you cannot obtain k ribbons of the same length.
//
// Technique: BSOA - Maximize Piece Length
// ===============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    bool canCut(vector<int>& ribbons, int k, int length) {
        int pieces = 0;
        
        for (int ribbon : ribbons) {
            pieces += ribbon / length;
            
            if (pieces >= k) {
                return true;
            }
        }
        
        return pieces >= k;
    }
    
    int maxLength(vector<int>& ribbons, int k) {
        int low = 1;
        int high = *max_element(ribbons.begin(), ribbons.end());
        int result = 0;
        
        while (low <= high) {
            int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
            
            if (canCut(ribbons, k, mid)) {
                result = mid;
                low = mid + 1;  // MAXIMIZE
            } else {
                high = mid - 1;
            }
        }
        
        return result;
    }
};

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

Division formula:
  Ribbon length L, piece length P
  Pieces = floor(L / P)

Monotonicity: Longer pieces -> Fewer total
  Pattern: T T T F F F

Example: ribbons=[9,7,5], k=3
  
  Try length=5:
    Pieces: 1+1+1 = 3 >= 3 \ding{51}
  
  Try length=6:
    Pieces: 1+1+0 = 2 < 3 \ding{55}

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log(max_ribbon))
Space: O(1)

Pattern: "Maximize equal piece length" -> This template
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 10.8: SPOJ PIE - Pie Division
// Link: https://www.spoj.com/problems/PIE/
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1600-1700)
//
// Problem Statement:
// [Same as Chapter 9 - included for cutting/division context]
// My birthday is coming up and traditionally I'm serving pie. Not just one pie,
// I have a number N of them, of various tastes and sizes. F of my friends are
// coming to my party and each of them gets a piece of pie. Find the maximum
// volume of pie each friend can get if all receive equal amounts.
//
// Technique: BSOA - FLOATING POINT Division
// ===============================================================

const double PI = acos(-1.0);

bool canDivide(vector<double>& areas, int friends, double volume) {
    int pieces = 0;
    
    for (double area : areas) {
        pieces += (int)(area / volume);
        
        if (pieces >= friends) {
            return true;
        }
    }
    
    return pieces >= friends;
}

double maxVolume(vector<int>& radii, int friends) {
    vector<double> areas;
    for (int r : radii) {
        areas.push_back(PI * r * r);
    }
    
    double low = 0.0;
    double high = *max_element(areas.begin(), areas.end());
    
    // Floating point binary search
    for (int iter = 0; iter < 100; iter++) {
        double mid = (low + high) / 2.0;
        
        if (canDivide(areas, friends, mid)) {
            low = mid;  // MAXIMIZE
        } else {
            high = mid;
        }
    }
    
    return low;
}

/*
Floating point division:
  Same pattern but continuous
  100 iterations for precision

Time: O(N \times 100)
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 3: Array Division (Minimize Maximum Sum)}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=warnyellow!10,
    colframe=warnyellow,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Array Division Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Array Division}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Split array into K subarrays
  Minimize maximum sum
  
This is contiguous allocation!
  Same as book allocation from Chapter 7

Check: Greedy packing
  Fill each subarray until limit

Pattern: F F F T T T (minimize maximum)

Time: O(N log S) where S = sum(array)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Array Division Solutions]
// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 10.18: CSES 1085 - Array Division
// PROBLEM 10.19: LeetCode 410 - Split Array Largest Sum
// 
// Problem Statement (LeetCode 410):
// Given an array nums which consists of non-negative integers and an integer m,
// you can split the array into m non-empty continuous subarrays.
// Write an algorithm to minimize the largest sum among these m subarrays.
//
// Technique: BSOA - Contiguous Allocation (Chapter 7 pattern!)
// ===============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    bool canDivide(vector<int>& nums, int m, long long maxSum) {
        int subarrays = 1;
        long long currentSum = 0;
        
        for (int num : nums) {
            if (num > maxSum) return false;
            
            if (currentSum + num > maxSum) {
                subarrays++;
                currentSum = num;
                if (subarrays > m) return false;
            } else {
                currentSum += num;
            }
        }
        return true;
    }
    
    int splitArray(vector<int>& nums, int m) {
        long long low = *max_element(nums.begin(), nums.end());
        long long high = accumulate(nums.begin(), nums.end(), 0LL);
        long long result = high;
        
        while (low <= high) {
            long long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
            
            if (canDivide(nums, m, mid)) {
                result = mid;
                high = mid - 1;
            } else {
                low = mid + 1;
            }
        }
        return result;
    }
};

/*
Recognition: This IS book allocation!
  From Chapter 7
  "Cutting" array into subarrays

Time: O(N log S)
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 4: Maximize Minimum (Division Context)}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=dangered!10,
    colframe=dangered,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Maximize Minimum Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Divide Chocolate}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Divide chocolate to maximize minimum
  K+1 pieces (K friends + you)

OPPOSITE pattern from minimize maximum!
  Pattern: T T T F F F
  Update: low = mid + 1 when found

This is from Chapter 8!
  Maximize minimum sweetness

Time: O(N log S)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Maximize Minimum Solutions]
// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 10.21: LeetCode 1231 - Divide Chocolate
//
// Problem Statement:
// [From Chapter 8 - included in cutting/division context]
// You have one chocolate bar that consists of some chunks. Each chunk has its
// own sweetness given by the array sweetness. You want to share the chocolate
// with your K friends so you start cutting the chocolate bar into K+1 pieces
// using K cuts, each piece consists of some consecutive chunks.
// Being generous, you will eat the piece with the minimum total sweetness and
// give the other pieces to your friends. Find the maximum total sweetness of
// the piece you can get by cutting the chocolate bar optimally.
//
// Technique: BSOA - Maximize Minimum (from Chapter 8)
// ===============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    bool canDivide(vector<int>& sweetness, int k, int minSweet) {
        int pieces = 0;
        int currentSum = 0;
        
        for (int sweet : sweetness) {
            currentSum += sweet;
            
            if (currentSum >= minSweet) {
                pieces++;
                currentSum = 0;
                if (pieces >= k + 1) return true;
            }
        }
        return pieces >= k + 1;
    }
    
    int maximizeSweetness(vector<int>& sweetness, int k) {
        int low = *min_element(sweetness.begin(), sweetness.end());
        int high = accumulate(sweetness.begin(), sweetness.end(), 0) / (k + 1);
        int result = 0;
        
        while (low <= high) {
            int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
            
            if (canDivide(sweetness, k, mid)) {
                result = mid;
                low = mid + 1;  // MAXIMIZE
            } else {
                high = mid - 1;
            }
        }
        return result;
    }
};

/*
Pattern: MAXIMIZE minimum (Chapter 8 pattern)
  In cutting/division context
  
Greedy cutting: Cut when reach threshold

Time: O(N log S)
*/

// ===============================================================
// PROBLEM 10.24: LeetCode 2226 - Maximum Candies Allocated to K Children
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/maximum-candies-allocated-to-k-children/
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1600)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You are given a 0-indexed integer array candies. Each element in the array
// denotes a pile of candies of size candies[i]. You can divide each pile into
// any number of sub piles, but you cannot merge two piles together.
// You are also given an integer k. You should allocate piles of candies to k
// children such that each child gets the same number of candies. Each child
// can take at most one pile of candies and some piles may go unused.
// Return the maximum number of candies each child can get.
//
// Technique: BSOA - Maximize Division Size
// ===============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    bool canAllocate(vector<int>& candies, long long k, int size) {
        long long children = 0;
        
        for (int candy : candies) {
            children += candy / size;
            
            if (children >= k) {
                return true;
            }
        }
        
        return children >= k;
    }
    
    int maximumCandies(vector<int>& candies, long long k) {
        int low = 1;
        int high = *max_element(candies.begin(), candies.end());
        int result = 0;
        
        while (low <= high) {
            int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
            
            if (canAllocate(candies, k, mid)) {
                result = mid;
                low = mid + 1;  // MAXIMIZE
            } else {
                high = mid - 1;
            }
        }
        
        return result;
    }
};

/*
Division to maximize piece size:
  Each pile divided into pieces
  Give K children equal amounts
  Maximize the amount

Same rope cutting pattern!

Time: O(N log(max(candies)))
*/
\end{lstlisting}
