% CHAPTER 11: MEDIAN AND KTH ELEMENT PROBLEMS
% ==========================================

\chapter{Median and Kth Element Problems}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,
    colback=white,
    colframe=secondaryblue,
    boxrule=1pt,
    arc=2mm,
    left=10pt,
    right=10pt
]
\textbf{\large\textcolor{primaryblue}{Median Finding, Kth Smallest/Largest with Binary Search}}
\end{tcolorbox}

\vspace{0.5cm}

\begin{center}
\textit{\textcolor{darkgray}{Difficulty: CF 1600-1900 $\bullet$ 20 Problems}}
\end{center}

\section{Theoretical Foundation}

\subsection{Binary Search on Value Range}
To find the $k$-th smallest element in a complex structure (like a matrix or union of arrays), we don't search for an index. Instead, we search for a \textbf{value} $X$ such that:
\[ \text{Count of elements } \leq X \text{ is at least } k \]
This count function is monotonic. If $X$ increases, the count of elements $\leq X$ also increases.



\subsection{The Median of Two Sorted Arrays}
This is a classic $O(\log(\min(N, M)))$ problem. Instead of merging, we partition both arrays into two halves such that the left side contains the smaller half of the combined elements. We binary search on the partition index of the smaller array and adjust based on the boundary values:
\[ L1 \leq R2 \quad \text{and} \quad L2 \leq R1 \]

\section{Median and Kth Element Problems: Complete Mastery}

\subsection{Complete Theoretical Foundation}

\subsubsection{Why Median and Kth Element?}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=white,
    colframe=deepblue,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{Binary Search on Value Range}
]
\textbf{Why Study Median/Kth Element?}

Most fundamental: Find element by rank without sorting

\textbf{The Paradigm Shift:}
\begin{verbatim}
Traditional Approach:
  Sort entire structure → O(N log N) or worse
  Access k-th element directly
  
Binary Search Approach:
  Search on VALUE range, not indices
  Count elements ≤ X
  Find value where count(≤ X) = k
  
KEY DIFFERENCE:
  Traditional: Sort then access
  Binary search: Never sort, count instead
  Often faster for complex structures!
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Complete Theory:}

1. \textbf{What is Value Range Binary Search?}
\begin{verbatim}
Value range search: check(x) → count ≤ x
  Input: Candidate value x
  Output: How many elements are ≤ x?

Example: Find 5th smallest in matrix
  check(10) = 8 elements ≤ 10
  check(5) = 4 elements ≤ 5
  check(7) = 5 elements ≤ 7 ✓

Monotonicity:
  If count(≤ X) = k, then count(≤ X+1) ≥ k
  Larger value → More elements counted
  Pattern: 0 1 2 3 4 5 5 5 6 7 8 ...
                    ↑
            First occurrence of k

This enables binary search on values!
\end{verbatim}

2. \textbf{The Template Pattern:}
\begin{verbatim}
TEMPLATE: Find k-th smallest element

Algorithm:
  int countLessEqual(X):
    # Count elements ≤ X in structure
    # Usually O(N) or O(N log N)
    return count
  
  int kthSmallest(k):
    low = min_possible_value
    high = max_possible_value
    result = low
    
    while low <= high:
      mid = low + (high - low) / 2
      count = countLessEqual(mid)
      
      if count >= k:
        result = mid
        high = mid - 1  # Try smaller value
      else:
        low = mid + 1   # Need larger value
    
    return result

Key points:
  1. Search on VALUE (not index)
  2. Count function must be monotonic
  3. Find smallest value with count ≥ k
  4. Pattern: F F F T T T (find first True)

Time: O(log(max-min) × T_count)
  T_count = time to count elements
\end{verbatim}

3. \textbf{Finding Search Space:}
\begin{verbatim}
CRITICAL STEP: Determine [low, high]

For Matrix Problems:
  low = matrix[0][0] (top-left)
  high = matrix[n-1][n-1] (bottom-right)
  
For Array Problems:
  low = min(array)
  high = max(array)

For Multiplication Table:
  low = 1 (1×1)
  high = N × N (N×N)

Example: Sorted matrix [[1,5,9],[10,11,13],[12,13,15]]
  min = 1, max = 15
  Search space: [1, 15]

Getting bounds wrong:
  Too narrow: Miss answer
  Too wide: Still correct, just slower
  
  Err on side of wider bounds!
\end{verbatim}

4. \textbf{The Counting Function:}
\begin{verbatim}
Most important: Efficient counting

Pattern 1: Sorted Matrix (Row-wise)
  For each row:
    Use binary search or linear scan
  Time: O(N log N) or O(N²)

Pattern 2: Multiplication Table
  For row i:
    Elements ≤ X: min(X/i, N)
  Time: O(N)

Pattern 3: Two Sorted Arrays
  Use two pointers
  Time: O(N + M)

Example: Matrix [[1,5,9],[10,11,13],[12,13,15]], X=11
  Row 0: Elements ≤ 11: [1,5,9] = 3
  Row 1: Elements ≤ 11: [10,11] = 2
  Row 2: Elements ≤ 11: [] = 0
  Total: 5 elements ≤ 11

Time: O(N) per row, O(N²) total
Can optimize with binary search: O(N log N)
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Key Insight:}
Binary search on VALUE + efficient counting solves median/kth problems!
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Pattern 1: Kth Smallest in Sorted Matrix}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=successgreen!10,
    colframe=successgreen,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Sorted Matrix Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Row-wise Sorted Matrix}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Find k-th smallest in matrix
  Rows sorted, columns sorted
  
Example: [[1,5,9],[10,11,13],[12,13,15]], k=8
  
Find: 8th smallest element

Key insight: Count elements ≤ X
  count(11) = 5
  count(12) = 7
  count(13) = 9 ≥ 8 ✓

Search space:
  min = matrix[0][0]
  max = matrix[n-1][n-1]

Counting (Staircase approach):
  Start from bottom-left or top-right
  Move optimally through matrix
  Time: O(N) per count

Algorithm:
  int countLessEqual(X):
    count = 0
    row = n-1, col = 0
    
    while row >= 0 and col < n:
      if matrix[row][col] <= X:
        count += row + 1
        col++
      else:
        row--
    
    return count

Time: O(N log(max-min))
  Search: log(max-min)
  Count: O(N) per iteration

Space: O(1)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Sorted Matrix Solutions]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 11.7: LeetCode 378 - Kth Smallest Element in a Sorted Matrix
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/kth-smallest-element-in-a-sorted-matrix/
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1600-1700)
// 
// Problem Statement:
// Given an n x n matrix where each of the rows and columns is sorted in
// ascending order, return the kth smallest element in the matrix.
// Note that it is the kth smallest element in the sorted order, not the
// kth distinct element.
//
// Example:
// Input: matrix = [[1,5,9],[10,11,13],[12,13,15]], k = 8
// Output: 13
// Explanation: The elements in the matrix are [1,5,9,10,11,12,13,13,15],
// and the 8th smallest number is 13
//
// Technique: BSOA - Binary Search on Value Range
// ==============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    int countLessEqual(vector<vector<int>>& matrix, int target) {
        int n = matrix.size();
        int count = 0;
        int row = n - 1;
        int col = 0;
        
        // Staircase approach from bottom-left
        while (row >= 0 && col < n) {
            if (matrix[row][col] <= target) {
                count += row + 1;  // All elements in this column up to row
                col++;
            } else {
                row--;
            }
        }
        
        return count;
    }
    
    int kthSmallest(vector<vector<int>>& matrix, int k) {
        int n = matrix.size();
        
        int low = matrix[0][0];
        int high = matrix[n-1][n-1];
        int result = low;
        
        while (low <= high) {
            int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
            int count = countLessEqual(matrix, mid);
            
            if (count >= k) {
                result = mid;
                high = mid - 1;
            } else {
                low = mid + 1;
            }
        }
        
        return result;
    }
};

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

Problem Analysis:
  - n \times n matrix, rows and columns sorted
  - Find k-th smallest element
  - Don't merge/sort (too slow for large matrices)

BSOA transformation:
  Optimization: Find k-th smallest value
  Feasibility: How many elements <= X?

Monotonicity: count(<= X) increases with X
  Pattern: 1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8 9 ...
                      |^
              First occurrence >= k

Search space:
  Lower: matrix[0][0] - smallest element
  Upper: matrix[n-1][n-1] - largest element

Staircase counting:
  Start from bottom-left (or top-right)
  
  From bottom-left:
    If matrix[row][col] <= target:
      All elements above in column <= target
      Count += row + 1
      Move right
    Else:
      Move up
  
  Why this works:
    Matrix sorted in rows and columns
    Moving optimally covers all elements
    Time: O(N) per count

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log(max-min))
  max-min = value range
  Binary search: log(max-min)
  Each count: O(N)
  
  For n \times n matrix: O(N log(max-min))
  Better than O($N^2$ log ($N^2$)) merge approach!

Space: O(1)

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Merge and sort:
   Flatten matrix to array
   Sort: O($N^2$ log($N^2$))
   Access k-th: O(1)
   
   Total: O($N^2$ log N)
   Our solution: O(N log(max-min))
   
   For large value range: Similar
   For small value range: Our solution much better!

2. Min heap:
   Extract k times from heap
   
   Time: O(k log N)
   Space: O(N)
   
   For k close to $N^2$: O($N^2$ log N)
   Our solution: O(N log(max-min))

3. Binary search on each row:
   For each row: Binary search for k-th
   
   Time: O($N^2$ log N)
   Our solution: O(N log(max-min))

Staircase correctness:
  Matrix sorted in rows and columns
  From bottom-left:
    - Right: Larger values
    - Up: Smaller values
  
  Decision at each step:
    If current <= target: Count column, move right
    If current > target: Move up
  
  Covers all elements in O(N) moves

Trace for matrix=[[1,5,9],[10,11,13],[12,13,15]], k=8:

Search [1, 15]:

Try mid=8:
  count(<=8) from bottom-left:
    (2,0)=12 > 8: up to (1,0)=10 > 8: up to (0,0)=1 <= 8
    Count col 0: 1 elements, move right
    (0,1)=5 <= 8: Count 1, right
    (0,2)=9 > 8: up, done
  Total: 2 < 8 \ding{55}
  low=9

Try mid=12:
  count(<=12):
    (2,0)=12 <= 12: Count 3, right
    (2,1)=13 > 12: up to (1,1)=11 <= 12: Count 2, right
    (1,2)=13 > 12: up to (0,2)=9 <= 12: Count 1, right
  Total: 6 < 8 \ding{55}
  low=13

Try mid=14:
  count(<=14):
    (2,0)=12: Count 3, right
    (2,1)=13: Count 3, right
    (2,2)=15 > 14: up
    (1,2)=13 <= 14: Count 2, right
  Total: 8 >= 8 \ding{51}
  result=14, high=13

Try mid=13:
  count(<=13):
    All elements: 1,5,9,10,11,12,13,13
  Total: 8 >= 8 \ding{51}
  result=13, high=12

Return result=13 \ding{51}

Pattern: "k-th in sorted matrix" -> Value range BS + staircase count
*/

// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 11.2: Median in a Row-wise Sorted Matrix
// Link: https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/problems/median-in-a-row-wise-sorted-matrix1527/1
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1600)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Given a row-wise sorted matrix of size R*C where R and C are always odd,
// find the median of the matrix. The median is the middle element when all
// elements are arranged in sorted order.
//
// Technique: BSOA - Find Median Using Value Range BS
// ==============================================================

int countLessEqual(vector<vector<int>>& matrix, int target) {
    int count = 0;
    
    for (auto& row : matrix) {
        // Binary search in each row
        count += upper_bound(row.begin(), row.end(), target) - row.begin();
    }
    
    return count;
}

int findMedian(vector<vector<int>>& matrix) {
    int rows = matrix.size();
    int cols = matrix[0].size();
    int totalElements = rows * cols;
    int medianPosition = totalElements / 2 + 1;  // Middle element
    
    int low = INT_MAX;
    int high = INT_MIN;
    
    // Find min and max in matrix
    for (auto& row : matrix) {
        low = min(low, row[0]);
        high = max(high, row[cols-1]);
    }
    
    int result = low;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        int count = countLessEqual(matrix, mid);
        
        if (count >= medianPosition) {
            result = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
Row-wise sorted only:
  Can't use staircase (columns not sorted)
  Use binary search in each row

Median position: (R \times C)/2 + 1
  For odd total: Middle element

Counting:
  For each row: upper_bound gives count
  Time: O(R log C) per check

Total time: O(R log C \times log(max-min))
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 2: Multiplication Table Kth Element}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=lightgray,
    colframe=accentorange,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Multiplication Table Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: N×N Multiplication Table}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Find k-th smallest in multiplication table
  Table[i][j] = i × j
  Don't generate table (too large!)
  
Counting formula:
  Row i: Elements ≤ X
    Count = min(X/i, N)
  
  All rows: sum(min(X/i, N)) for i=1..N

Example: N=3, X=6
  Row 1: min(6/1, 3) = 3 [1,2,3]
  Row 2: min(6/2, 3) = 3 [2,4,6]
  Row 3: min(6/3, 3) = 2 [3,6]
  Total: 8 elements ≤ 6

Search space: [1, N×N]

Time: O(N log(N²))
  Search: log(N²) = 2 log N
  Count: O(N)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Multiplication Table Solutions]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 11.9: CSES 2422 - Multiplication Table
// Link: https://cses.fi/problemset/task/2422
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1600-1700)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Your task is to find the kth smallest number in a multiplication table of
// size n \times n. The rows and columns of the table are numbered from 1 to n,
// and the value at position (i,j) is i*j.
//
// Technique: BSOA - Mathematical Counting (No Table Generation!)
// ==============================================================

long long countLessEqual(long long n, long long target) {
    long long count = 0;
    
    for (long long i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        count += min(target / i, n);
    }
    
    return count;
}

long long kthSmallest(long long n, long long k) {
    long long low = 1;
    long long high = n * n;
    long long result = 1;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        long long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        long long count = countLessEqual(n, mid);
        
        if (count >= k) {
            result = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
WHY THIS IS BRILLIANT:

Never generate table!
  For n=10^6: Table has 10^12 elements
  Can't store in memory
  
  Our solution: No storage needed!

Mathematical counting:
  Row i contains: i, 2i, 3i, ..., n \times i
  
  Elements <= X in row i:
    Count multiples of i that are <= X
    Count = floor(X / i)
    But at most N elements per row
    Therefore: min(X/i, N)

Example: N=5, X=10, row 3
  Multiples: 3, 6, 9, 12, 15
  <= 10: [3, 6, 9]
  Count: min(10/3, 5) = min(3, 5) = 3 \ding{51}

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log($N^2$))
  Binary search: log($N^2$) iterations
  Each count: O(N)
  
  For N=10^6: ~60 iterations \times 10^6 = 60M operations
  Feasible!

Space: O(1)

Alternative (generate table):
  Space: O($N^2$)
  For N=10^6: 10^12 elements - IMPOSSIBLE!
  
  Our solution: O(1) space

Pattern: "k-th in multiplication table" -> Math counting, no generation
*/

// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 11.17: LeetCode 668 - Kth Smallest Number in Multiplication Table
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/kth-smallest-number-in-multiplication-table/
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 1700-1800)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Nearly everyone has used the Multiplication Table. The multiplication table
// of size m x n is an integer matrix mat where mat[i][j] == i * j (1-indexed).
// Given three integers m, n, and k, return the kth smallest element in the
// m x n multiplication table.
//
// Technique: SAME as CSES Multiplication Table
// ==============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    int countLessEqual(int m, int n, int target) {
        int count = 0;
        
        for (int i = 1; i <= m; i++) {
            count += min(target / i, n);
        }
        
        return count;
    }
    
    int findKthNumber(int m, int n, int k) {
        int low = 1;
        int high = m * n;
        int result = 1;
        
        while (low <= high) {
            int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
            
            if (countLessEqual(m, n, mid) >= k) {
                result = mid;
                high = mid - 1;
            } else {
                low = mid + 1;
            }
        }
        
        return result;
    }
};

/*
IDENTICAL to CSES problem!
  Only difference: m \times n instead of n \times n
  
Same mathematical counting approach

Time: O(m \times log(m \times n))
Space: O(1)
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 3: Median of Two Sorted Arrays}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=warnyellow!10,
    colframe=warnyellow,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Two Array Median Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Median of Two Sorted Arrays}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Find median of two sorted arrays
  Merge would be O(M+N)
  Can we do O(log(min(M,N)))?

Partition approach:
  Partition both arrays such that:
    - Left side: smaller half of combined elements
    - Right side: larger half
  
  Partition A at i, B at j:
    leftA = A[i-1], rightA = A[i]
    leftB = B[j-1], rightB = B[j]
  
  Valid partition:
    leftA ≤ rightB AND leftB ≤ rightA
  
  Median: max(leftA, leftB) or average

Binary search on partition index!

Time: O(log(min(M,N)))
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Two Array Median Solutions]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 11.1: LeetCode 4 - Median of Two Sorted Arrays
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/median-of-two-sorted-arrays/
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 1800-1900)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Given two sorted arrays nums1 and nums2 of size m and n respectively,
// return the median of the two sorted arrays.
// The overall run time complexity should be O(log (m+n)).
//
// Example:
// Input: nums1 = [1,3], nums2 = [2]
// Output: 2.00000
// Explanation: merged array = [1,2,3] and median is 2.
//
// Technique: Binary Search on Partition Index
// ==============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    double findMedianSortedArrays(vector<int>& nums1, vector<int>& nums2) {
        // Ensure nums1 is smaller for efficiency
        if (nums1.size() > nums2.size()) {
            return findMedianSortedArrays(nums2, nums1);
        }
        
        int m = nums1.size();
        int n = nums2.size();
        int totalLeft = (m + n + 1) / 2;
        
        int low = 0;
        int high = m;
        
        while (low <= high) {
            int partitionA = low + (high - low) / 2;
            int partitionB = totalLeft - partitionA;
            
            int leftA = (partitionA == 0) ? INT_MIN : nums1[partitionA - 1];
            int rightA = (partitionA == m) ? INT_MAX : nums1[partitionA];
            int leftB = (partitionB == 0) ? INT_MIN : nums2[partitionB - 1];
            int rightB = (partitionB == n) ? INT_MAX : nums2[partitionB];
            
            if (leftA <= rightB && leftB <= rightA) {
                // Found correct partition
                if ((m + n) % 2 == 1) {
                    return max(leftA, leftB);
                } else {
                    return (max(leftA, leftB) + min(rightA, rightB)) / 2.0;
                }
            } else if (leftA > rightB) {
                high = partitionA - 1;
            } else {
                low = partitionA + 1;
            }
        }
        
        return 0.0;
    }
};

/*
WHY THIS IS CORRECT:

Partition approach:
  Divide both arrays into left and right halves
  Left side: smaller (m+n)/2 elements
  Right side: larger (m+n)/2 elements

Partition indices:
  A partitioned at i: A[0..i-1] | A[i..m-1]
  B partitioned at j: B[0..j-1] | B[j..n-1]
  
  Constraint: i + j = (m+n+1)/2
  Therefore: j = (m+n+1)/2 - i

Valid partition:
  All left <= All right
  
  Check: A[i-1] <= B[j] AND B[j-1] <= A[i]

Median:
  Odd total: max(leftA, leftB)
  Even total: (max(leftA, leftB) + min(rightA, rightB)) / 2

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(log(min(m,n)))
  Binary search on smaller array
  Each check: O(1)

Space: O(1)

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Merge arrays:
   Time: O(m+n)
   Space: O(m+n)
   
   Our solution: O(log(min(m,n))) time, O(1) space

2. Two pointers to k/2:
   Time: O(k) where k=(m+n)/2
   
   Our solution: O(log(min(m,n)))

Binary search logic:
  If leftA > rightB: Partition A too far right
    Move left: high = partitionA - 1
  
  If leftB > rightA: Partition A too far left
    Move right: low = partitionA + 1

Pattern: "Median of two sorted" -> Partition binary search
*/

// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 11.8: K-th Element of Two Sorted Arrays
// Link: https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/problems/k-th-element-of-two-sorted-array1317/1
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1700)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Given two sorted arrays arr1 and arr2 of size N and M respectively and
// an element K. The task is to find the element that would be at the k'th
// position of the final sorted array.
//
// Technique: Similar Partition Approach
// ==============================================================

int kthElement(vector<int>& arr1, vector<int>& arr2, int k) {
    int m = arr1.size();
    int n = arr2.size();
    
    if (m > n) {
        return kthElement(arr2, arr1, k);
    }
    
    int low = max(0, k - n);
    int high = min(k, m);
    
    while (low <= high) {
        int cut1 = low + (high - low) / 2;
        int cut2 = k - cut1;
        
        int left1 = (cut1 == 0) ? INT_MIN : arr1[cut1 - 1];
        int right1 = (cut1 == m) ? INT_MAX : arr1[cut1];
        int left2 = (cut2 == 0) ? INT_MIN : arr2[cut2 - 1];
        int right2 = (cut2 == n) ? INT_MAX : arr2[cut2];
        
        if (left1 <= right2 && left2 <= right1) {
            return max(left1, left2);
        } else if (left1 > right2) {
            high = cut1 - 1;
        } else {
            low = cut1 + 1;
        }
    }
    
    return -1;
}

/*
Same partition approach as median!
  Find k-th element directly
  
Time: O(log(min(m,n)))
Space: O(1)
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 4: Kth Smallest Pair Distance}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=dangered!10,
    colframe=dangered,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Pair Distance Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Kth Smallest Distance Pair}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Find k-th smallest |nums[i] - nums[j]|
  Don't generate all O(N²) pairs!
  
Value range BS:
  Search on distance value
  Count pairs with distance ≤ X
  
Counting with two pointers:
  Sort array first
  For each i: Find largest j where arr[j] - arr[i] ≤ X
  
Time: O(N log N + N log(max_diff))
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Pair Distance Solutions]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 11.12: LeetCode 719 - Find K-th Smallest Pair Distance
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/find-k-th-smallest-pair-distance/
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 1700-1800)
//
// Problem Statement:
// The distance of a pair of integers a and b is defined as the absolute
// difference between a and b. Given an integer array nums and an integer k,
// return the kth smallest distance among all the pairs nums[i] and nums[j]
// where 0 <= i < j < nums.length.
//
// Technique: BSOA - Binary Search on Distance + Two Pointers
// ==============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    int countPairsWithDistanceLessEqual(vector<int>& nums, int distance) {
        int count = 0;
        int n = nums.size();
        int j = 0;
        
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            while (j < n && nums[j] - nums[i] <= distance) {
                j++;
            }
            count += j - i - 1;
        }
        
        return count;
    }
    
    int smallestDistancePair(vector<int>& nums, int k) {
        sort(nums.begin(), nums.end());
        
        int low = 0;
        int high = nums.back() - nums[0];
        int result = 0;
        
        while (low <= high) {
            int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
            
            if (countPairsWithDistanceLessEqual(nums, mid) >= k) {
                result = mid;
                high = mid - 1;
            } else {
                low = mid + 1;
            }
        }
        
        return result;
    }
};

/*
WHY THIS IS BRILLIANT:

Avoid generating O($N^2$) pairs!
  For N=10^4: Would need 10^8 pairs
  
Value range BS:
  Search on distance [0, max-min]
  Much smaller search space!

Two-pointer counting:
  After sorting:
    For each i: Find largest j where nums[j]-nums[i] <= dist
    Pairs from i: (i,i+1), (i,i+2), ..., (i,j-1)
    Count: j - i - 1
  
  Time: O(N) per count

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log N + N log D)
  Sort: O(N log N)
  Binary search: log D iterations
  Each count: O(N)
  D = max difference

Space: O(1) (ignoring sort space)

Alternative (generate all pairs):
  Generate: O($N^2$)
  Sort: O($N^2$ log ($N^2$))
  Access k-th: O(1)
  
  For N=10^4: ~10^8 operations
  Our solution: ~10^5 operations
  
  1000X faster!

Pattern: "k-th smallest pair distance" -> Value BS + two pointers
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Summary and Best Practices}

\section{The Synergy of BS and Greedy}

The core idea is to transform an optimization problem ("What is the best $X$?") into a decision problem ("Is $X$ achievable?"). 

\subsection{Why Greedy?}
The `check(mid)` function often requires us to make a series of local choices to see if we can satisfy the global constraint. If the problem exhibits the **Greedy Choice Property**, we can efficiently implement the check function in $O(N)$ or $O(N \log N)$ time.

\subsection{Common Scenarios}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Resource Partitioning:} Can we fit all tasks into $K$ days if each day has capacity $X$? (Greedy: Fill each day as much as possible).
    \item \textbf{Interval Placement:} Can we place $K$ items with minimum distance $X$? (Greedy: Place the next item at the earliest possible valid spot).
    \item \textbf{Scheduling:} Can we complete all jobs by time $T$? (Greedy: Process jobs with the earliest deadlines first).
\end{itemize}

\section{Binary Search with Greedy: Complete Mastery}

\subsection{Complete Theoretical Foundation}

\subsubsection{Why Combine Binary Search and Greedy?}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=white,
    colframe=deepblue,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{The Synergy of BS and Greedy}
]
\textbf{Why This Combination is Powerful?}

Ultimate technique: BSOA framework + Greedy validation

\textbf{The Core Synergy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Hard Optimization Problem:
  "Find the best X that satisfies constraints"
  Direct optimization: Often NP-hard
  
Binary Search Transformation:
  "Can we achieve goal with value X?"
  Reduces to YES/NO decision problem
  
Greedy Validation:
  Make locally optimal choices
  Verify feasibility in O(N) or O(N log N)
  
COMBINED POWER:
  Binary Search: O(log range) iterations
  Greedy Check: O(N) per iteration
  Total: O(N log range) - Efficient!
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Complete Theory:}

1. \textbf{The Greedy Choice Property}
\begin{verbatim}
Definition: Local optimal choices lead to global optimum

When does greedy work in check()?
  1. Greedy stays ahead property
  2. Exchange argument applies
  3. Problem has matroid structure

Three main greedy patterns in BSOA:

Pattern 1: Greedy Packing
  Fill containers/time slots maximally
  Example: Job scheduling, resource allocation
  
Pattern 2: Greedy Selection
  Choose earliest/latest valid option
  Example: Interval selection, placement
  
Pattern 3: Greedy Ordering
  Process in optimal order (EDF, etc.)
  Example: Task scheduling, deadlines

Key insight: If greedy solves decision problem,
            BS finds optimal answer!
\end{verbatim}

2. \textbf{The Combined Template:}
\begin{verbatim}
UNIVERSAL TEMPLATE: BSOA + Greedy

bool greedyCheck(value):
  # Make greedy choices to verify value
  # Return: Is value feasible?
  
  # Common patterns:
  # - Pack items greedily
  # - Select items greedily
  # - Process in greedy order
  
  return feasible

int bsWithGreedy():
  low = min_possible_value
  high = max_possible_value
  result = initialize
  
  while low <= high:
    mid = low + (high - low) / 2
    
    if greedyCheck(mid):
      result = mid
      # Minimize: high = mid - 1
      # Maximize: low = mid + 1
    else:
      # Opposite update
  
  return result

Complexity Analysis:
  Binary Search: O(log(high - low))
  Greedy Check: O(N) or O(N log N)
  Total: O(N log(high - low)) typical
\end{verbatim}

3. \textbf{Proving Greedy Correctness:}
\begin{verbatim}
Essential for interviews and competitions!

Proof Method 1: Exchange Argument
  1. Assume non-greedy solution N is optimal
  2. Show can exchange to greedy solution G
  3. Prove G is at least as good as N
  4. Conclude: Greedy is optimal
  
  Example: Earliest deadline first (EDF)

Proof Method 2: Greedy Stays Ahead
  1. Show greedy is better after 1 step
  2. Induction: Prove greedy stays ahead
  3. Conclude: Greedy reaches goal first
  
  Example: Activity selection

Proof Method 3: Contradiction
  1. Assume greedy fails
  2. Show this leads to contradiction
  3. Conclude: Greedy must work
  
  Example: Fractional knapsack

For BSOA problems:
  Usually exchange argument or stays ahead
\end{verbatim}

4. \textbf{Common Pitfalls to Avoid:}
\begin{verbatim}
Pitfall 1: Assuming greedy without proof
  Always verify greedy correctness!
  Counterexample: 0-1 knapsack (greedy fails)

Pitfall 2: Wrong greedy strategy
  Example: Longest interval first vs Earliest end first
  Test with small examples!

Pitfall 3: Inefficient greedy implementation
  Should be O(N) or O(N log N)
  Not O(N²) or worse

Pitfall 4: Forgetting to sort
  Many greedy algorithms need sorted data
  Example: Interval scheduling by end time

Pitfall 5: Off-by-one in counting
  Careful with inclusive/exclusive ranges
  Test boundary cases!
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Key Insight:}
BSOA finds answer space, Greedy validates efficiently!
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Pattern 1: Job Scheduling with Deadlines}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=successgreen!10,
    colframe=successgreen,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Job Scheduling Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Maximum Profit Job Scheduling}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Schedule jobs with start, end, profit
  Maximize total profit
  Jobs cannot overlap

Greedy + Binary Search:
  1. Sort jobs by end time
  2. For each job: Binary search for last non-overlapping
  3. DP: dp[i] = max(skip, take + dp[j])

Not pure BSOA but uses BS for efficiency!

Time: O(N log N)
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Type 2: Rent Optimization (SPOJ)}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Maximize profit from non-overlapping rentals
  Similar to job scheduling
  
Same approach with binary search

Time: O(N log N)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Job Scheduling Solutions - NEW PROBLEMS]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 12.8: LeetCode 1235 - Maximum Profit in Job Scheduling
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/maximum-profit-job-scheduling/
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 1800-1900)
//
// Problem Statement:
// We have n jobs, where every job is scheduled to be done from startTime[i]
// to endTime[i], obtaining a profit of profit[i].
// You're given the startTime, endTime and profit arrays, return the maximum
// profit you can take such that there are no two jobs in the subset with
// overlapping time range.
// If you choose a job that ends at time X you will be able to start another
// job that starts at time X.
//
// Technique: Greedy Sorting + Binary Search + DP
// ==============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    int jobScheduling(vector<int>& startTime, vector<int>& endTime, vector<int>& profit) {
        int n = startTime.size();
        vector<tuple<int, int, int>> jobs;
        
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            jobs.push_back({endTime[i], startTime[i], profit[i]});
        }
        
        // Greedy: Sort by end time
        sort(jobs.begin(), jobs.end());
        
        vector<int> dp(n);
        dp[0] = get<2>(jobs[0]);
        
        for (int i = 1; i < n; i++) {
            int currentProfit = get<2>(jobs[i]);
            int currentStart = get<1>(jobs[i]);
            
            // Binary search for last non-overlapping job
            int left = 0, right = i - 1;
            int lastNonOverlap = -1;
            
            while (left <= right) {
                int mid = left + (right - left) / 2;
                if (get<0>(jobs[mid]) <= currentStart) {
                    lastNonOverlap = mid;
                    left = mid + 1;
                } else {
                    right = mid - 1;
                }
            }
            
            int includeProfit = currentProfit;
            if (lastNonOverlap != -1) {
                includeProfit += dp[lastNonOverlap];
            }
            
            dp[i] = max(dp[i-1], includeProfit);
        }
        
        return dp[n-1];
    }
};

/*
WHY GREEDY SORTING IS CORRECT:

Greedy Strategy: Sort by end time
  Process jobs in order of completion

Why this works:
  Theorem: Earliest finish time is optimal for interval scheduling
  
  Proof (Exchange Argument):
    Assume: Solution S uses job with later end time
    Exchange: Replace with earlier ending job
    Result: More time for remaining jobs
    
    Therefore: Earliest end time is optimal \ding{51}

Binary Search Role:
  Find last compatible job efficiently
  Instead of O(N) scan: O(log N) search

DP with BS:
  dp[i] = max profit ending at or before job i
  For each job: Either skip or take + compatible jobs
  
  With BS: O(N log N) total
  Without BS: O($N^2$)

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log N)
  Sort: O(N log N)
  For each job: O(log N) BS + O(1) DP
  Total: O(N log N)

Space: O(N) for DP array

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Try all combinations:
   Time: O(2^N) - exponential!
   Our solution: O(N log N)

2. Greedy without DP:
   Just take earliest end time always
   Problem: Might miss higher profit jobs
   
   Our solution: DP considers all options

3. DP without BS:
   Time: O($N^2$) - quadratic
   Our solution: O(N log N) with BS optimization

Pattern: "Non-overlapping intervals + maximize" -> Sort + BS + DP
*/

// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 12.12: SPOJ RENT - Rent Your Airplane
// Link: https://www.spoj.com/problems/RENT/
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 1800-1900)
//
// Problem Statement:
// "ABEAS Corp." is a very small company that owns a single airplane. The
// customers of this company are important persons who arrive at the airport
// with short notice demanding to be transported immediately to their final
// destination. The airplane has to be rented for the entire period that it
// is used by a customer. Each customer i pays r_i to rent the airplane for
// the period [s_i, s_i + d_i). Your task is to compute the maximal profit.
//
// Technique: SAME as Job Scheduling - Sort + BS + DP
// ==============================================================

long long maxRentProfit(vector<tuple<int, int, int>>& rentals) {
    int n = rentals.size();
    
    // Sort by end time: start + duration
    sort(rentals.begin(), rentals.end(), [](auto& a, auto& b) {
        return get<0>(a) + get<1>(a) < get<0>(b) + get<1>(b);
    });
    
    vector<long long> dp(n);
    dp[0] = get<2>(rentals[0]);
    
    for (int i = 1; i < n; i++) {
        int start = get<0>(rentals[i]);
        int duration = get<1>(rentals[i]);
        int price = get<2>(rentals[i]);
        int endTime = start + duration;
        
        // Binary search for last compatible rental
        int left = 0, right = i - 1;
        int lastCompatible = -1;
        
        while (left <= right) {
            int mid = left + (right - left) / 2;
            int midEnd = get<0>(rentals[mid]) + get<1>(rentals[mid]);
            
            if (midEnd <= start) {
                lastCompatible = mid;
                left = mid + 1;
            } else {
                right = mid - 1;
            }
        }
        
        long long includeProfit = price;
        if (lastCompatible != -1) {
            includeProfit += dp[lastCompatible];
        }
        
        dp[i] = max(dp[i-1], includeProfit);
    }
    
    return dp[n-1];
}

/*
IDENTICAL pattern to job scheduling!
  Same greedy strategy
  Same binary search optimization
  Same DP structure

Time: O(N log N)
Space: O(N)

Recognition: "Non-overlapping intervals + profit" -> This template
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 2: Magic Ship with Periodic Wind}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=lightgray,
    colframe=accentorange,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Periodic Movement Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Magic Ship Problem}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Reach target with periodic wind
  Wind pattern repeats every N days
  Can move in 4 directions each day
  
Binary Search on Days:
  Check: Can reach in T days?
  
  Greedy movement:
    Use wind when helpful
    Move optimally when not
    
  After full cycles: Wind displacement is known
  Remaining days: Simulate greedily

Time: O(N × log(max_days))
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Periodic Movement Solutions - NEW PROBLEM]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 12.9: CF 1117C - Magic Ship
// Link: https://codeforces.com/problemset/problem/1117/C
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 1900-2000)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You are given starting position (x1, y1) and target (x2, y2). There is a
// periodic wind that changes each day according to string s (length n):
// U (up), D (down), L (left), R (right). Each day: first the wind moves you,
// then you can make one move in any direction. Find minimum days to reach
// target, or -1 if impossible.
//
// Technique: BSOA + Greedy Movement with Period Handling
// ==============================================================

bool canReach(long long days, long long x1, long long y1, 
              long long x2, long long y2, string& wind,
              long long windX, long long windY) {
    long long n = wind.size();
    
    // Full cycles of wind
    long long cycles = days / n;
    long long remainder = days % n;
    
    // Position after full cycles
    long long currX = x1 + cycles * windX;
    long long currY = y1 + cycles * windY;
    
    // Simulate remainder days with greedy movement
    for (long long i = 0; i < remainder; i++) {
        // Wind moves first
        if (wind[i] == 'U') currY++;
        else if (wind[i] == 'D') currY--;
        else if (wind[i] == 'L') currX--;
        else if (wind[i] == 'R') currX++;
        
        // Then we move optimally (greedily toward target)
        if (currX < x2) currX++;
        else if (currX > x2) currX--;
        else if (currY < y2) currY++;
        else if (currY > y2) currY--;
    }
    
    // Check if we can reach target with remaining days
    long long distX = abs(x2 - currX);
    long long distY = abs(y2 - currY);
    long long remainingDays = days - cycles * n - remainder;
    
    return distX + distY <= remainingDays;
}

long long magicShip(long long x1, long long y1, long long x2, long long y2, 
                    string wind) {
    // Calculate net wind displacement per cycle
    long long windX = 0, windY = 0;
    for (char c : wind) {
        if (c == 'U') windY++;
        else if (c == 'D') windY--;
        else if (c == 'L') windX--;
        else if (c == 'R') windX++;
    }
    
    // Initial distance
    long long dist = abs(x2 - x1) + abs(y2 - y1);
    
    // Binary search on days
    long long low = 0;
    long long high = 1e15;  // Very large upper bound
    long long result = -1;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        long long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (canReach(mid, x1, y1, x2, y2, wind, windX, windY)) {
            result = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

Problem Analysis:
  - Periodic wind (repeats every N days)
  - Each day: Wind moves, then we move
  - Find minimum days to reach target

Greedy Movement Strategy:
  After wind: Move toward target
  Direction priority: X first, then Y (or vice versa)
  
  Why greedy works:
    Each step closer is optimal
    No benefit to moving away

Period Optimization:
  Full cycles: Apply net wind displacement
  Remainder: Simulate day by day
  
  Why this works:
    Wind pattern repeats
    After K cycles: Position predictable
    Only simulate remainder

Binary Search:
  Search on number of days
  Check: Can reach in T days?
  
  Monotonicity: If T works -> T+1 works
  Pattern: F F F T T T

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N \times log(max_days))
  N = wind pattern length
  Binary search: log(max_days)
  Each check: O(N) simulation

Space: O(1)

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Simulate day by day:
   Time: O(answer) - could be 10^15!
   Our solution: O(N log(answer))

2. BFS pathfinding:
   Problem: Infinite grid
   State space: Infinite
   Our solution: Binary search handles it

3. Mathematical formula:
   Complex with periodic wind
   Hard to derive closed form
   Our solution: Clean simulation

Greedy correctness:
  Always move toward target when possible
  This minimizes distance each step
  Provably optimal for Manhattan distance

Pattern: "Periodic movement + reach target" -> BS + period optimization
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 3: Counting and Selection with Greedy}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=warnyellow!10,
    colframe=warnyellow,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Counting Selection Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Advertising Agency Problem}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Select K influencers to maximize reach
  Multiple influencers may have same reach
  Count ways to select with maximum reach

Greedy Selection:
  1. Sort by reach (descending)
  2. Take top K greedily
  3. Count combinations for ties

Combinatorics:
  If last reach appears M times
  And we need C more from these M
  Ways = C(M, C)

Time: O(N log N)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Counting Selection Solutions - NEW PROBLEM]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 12.13: CF 1475E - Advertising Agency
// Link: https://codeforces.com/problemset/problem/1475/E
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1700-1800)
//
// Problem Statement:
// An advertising agency has n different influencers. The i-th influencer can
// reach a_i people. The agency wants to select exactly k influencers to
// maximize the total reach. Since there may be multiple ways to select k
// influencers with the same maximum total reach, count the number of different
// ways to select k influencers with maximum total reach modulo 10^9+7.
//
// Technique: Greedy Selection + Combinatorics
// ==============================================================

const int MOD = 1e9 + 7;

long long factorial[1005];
long long invFactorial[1005];

long long power(long long base, long long exp, long long mod) {
    long long result = 1;
    while (exp > 0) {
        if (exp % 2 == 1) result = (result * base) % mod;
        base = (base * base) % mod;
        exp /= 2;
    }
    return result;
}

void precompute() {
    factorial[0] = 1;
    for (int i = 1; i <= 1000; i++) {
        factorial[i] = (factorial[i-1] * i) % MOD;
    }
    
    invFactorial[1000] = power(factorial[1000], MOD - 2, MOD);
    for (int i = 999; i >= 0; i--) {
        invFactorial[i] = (invFactorial[i+1] * (i+1)) % MOD;
    }
}

long long nCr(int n, int r) {
    if (r > n || r < 0) return 0;
    return (factorial[n] * invFactorial[r] % MOD) * invFactorial[n-r] % MOD;
}

long long advertisingAgency(vector<int>& reach, int k) {
    int n = reach.size();
    
    // Greedy: Sort descending
    sort(reach.rbegin(), reach.rend());
    
    // Find the k-th value (last selected)
    int kthValue = reach[k-1];
    
    // Count total occurrences of kthValue in array
    int totalCount = 0;
    for (int val : reach) {
        if (val == kthValue) totalCount++;
    }
    
    // Count how many kthValue are in top k
    int selectedCount = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < k; i++) {
        if (reach[i] == kthValue) selectedCount++;
    }
    
    // Ways to select = C(totalCount, selectedCount)
    return nCr(totalCount, selectedCount);
}

/*
WHY GREEDY SELECTION IS CORRECT:

Greedy Strategy: Select top K by reach
  
Proof:
  Theorem: Selecting largest elements maximizes sum
  
  Proof (Exchange Argument):
    Assume: Solution S selects smaller element x
    Exchange: Replace x with larger unselected y
    Result: Sum increases
    
    Therefore: Greedy (largest first) is optimal \ding{51}

Combinatorics:
  Problem: Multiple elements with same value
  
  Example: reach=[4,4,3,3,3], k=3
    Top 3: Must include both 4's
    Remaining: Choose 1 from three 3's
    Ways: C(3,1) = 3
  
  General: If last value appears M times total
          And we need C of them in selection
          Ways: C(M, C)

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log N)
  Sort: O(N log N)
  Counting: O(N)
  Combinatorics: O(1) with precompute

Space: O(1) excluding precomputed factorials

Pattern: "Select top K with ties" -> Greedy + combinatorics
*/

// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 12.18: CF 1156C - Match Points
// Link: https://codeforces.com/problemset/problem/1156/C
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1600-1700)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You are given n points on a line with coordinates a_1, a_2, ..., a_n.
// You want to match pairs of points. A pair (i,j) is valid if |a_i - a_j| >= d.
// Each point can be used in at most one pair. Find the maximum number of pairs.
//
// Technique: Greedy Two-Pointer Matching
// ==============================================================

int maxMatchPoints(vector<int>& points, int d) {
    int n = points.size();
    
    // Greedy: Sort points
    sort(points.begin(), points.end());
    
    int matches = 0;
    int i = 0;
    int j = n / 2;  // Start matching from middle
    
    // Greedy two-pointer approach
    while (i < n / 2 && j < n) {
        if (points[j] - points[i] >= d) {
            // Valid match found
            matches++;
            i++;
            j++;
        } else {
            // Need larger gap
            j++;
        }
    }
    
    return matches;
}

/*
Greedy Strategy:
  Sort points
  Match smallest with appropriate larger points
  
Why this works:
  Matching smallest with smallest valid partner
  Leaves maximum options for remaining points
  
Time: O(N log N)
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 4: Array Transformation with Greedy}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=dangered!10,
    colframe=dangered,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Transformation Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Max Median with Transformations}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Maximize subsegment median with operations
  Transform array: +1/-1 based on threshold
  Use prefix sums to check median
  
Binary Search on Median Value:
  For each candidate: Transform and check
  
Greedy transformation:
  Elements ≥ median: +1
  Elements < median: -1
  Positive sum → median achievable

Time: O(N² log(max_value))
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Transformation Solutions - NEW PROBLEM]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 12.20: CF 1486D - Max Median
// Link: https://codeforces.com/problemset/problem/1486/D
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 1900-2000)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You are given an array a of length n and an integer k. Find the maximum
// possible median of a subsegment of length at least k. The median is the
// middle element after sorting (for even length, the smaller middle element).
//
// Technique: BSOA + Greedy Transformation + Kadane
// ==============================================================

bool canAchieveMedian(vector<int>& arr, int k, int targetMedian) {
    int n = arr.size();
    
    // Greedy transformation: +1 if >= target, -1 if < target
    vector<int> transformed(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        transformed[i] = (arr[i] >= targetMedian) ? 1 : -1;
    }
    
    // Find if any subarray of length >= k has sum >= 1
    // Use sliding window with min prefix sum
    for (int i = k - 1; i < n; i++) {
        int currentSum = 0;
        int minPrefix = 0;
        int prefixSum = 0;
        
        // Check window ending at i
        for (int j = 0; j <= i; j++) {
            prefixSum += transformed[j];
            
            if (j >= k - 1) {
                // Valid window from some start to j
                if (j - k + 1 == 0) {
                    if (prefixSum >= 1) return true;
                } else {
                    if (prefixSum - minPrefix >= 1) return true;
                }
            }
            
            if (j >= k - 1) {
                minPrefix = min(minPrefix, prefixSum - transformed[j - k + 1] * (j - k + 2));
            }
        }
    }
    
    // Optimized approach: Kadane-like with constraint
    int maxSum = INT_MIN;
    for (int start = 0; start <= n - k; start++) {
        int sum = 0;
        for (int end = start; end < n; end++) {
            sum += transformed[end];
            if (end - start + 1 >= k) {
                maxSum = max(maxSum, sum);
            }
        }
    }
    
    return maxSum >= 1;
}

int maxMedian(vector<int>& arr, int k) {
    int low = *min_element(arr.begin(), arr.end());
    int high = *max_element(arr.begin(), arr.end());
    int result = low;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (canAchieveMedian(arr, k, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            low = mid + 1;  // Try larger median
        } else {
            high = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
WHY TRANSFORMATION WORKS:

Greedy transformation:
  Elements >= target: Count as +1 (support median)
  Elements < target: Count as -1 (oppose median)

Median property:
  If subarray has positive sum after transformation
  Then: More elements >= target than < target
  Therefore: Median >= target \ding{51}

Check: Find if any length-k+ subarray has sum >= 1
  Use modified Kadane's algorithm
  Maintain minimum prefix for best subarray

Time: O($N^2$ log(max_value))
  Can optimize to O(N log(max_value))

Pattern: "Maximize median" -> Transform + prefix sums
*/

// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 12.23: CF 1187C - Vasya and Arrays
// Link: https://codeforces.com/problemset/problem/1187/C
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1700-1800)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Given two arrays of lengths n and m, find if there exists an array such that
// it contains both arrays as contiguous subsegments and is sorted in
// non-decreasing order. If yes, construct any such array.
//
// Technique: Greedy Construction with Sorting Validation
// ==============================================================

vector<int> constructArray(vector<pair<int,int>>& segments1, 
                           vector<pair<int,int>>& segments2, int n) {
    vector<int> result(n + 1, 0);
    
    // Mark all constraints
    for (auto [l, r] : segments1) {
        // Segment [l,r] must be non-decreasing
        for (int i = l; i < r; i++) {
            // result[i] <= result[i+1]
        }
    }
    
    // Greedy construction: Assign values maintaining constraints
    result[0] = 1;  // Start with 1
    
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        // Check if position i must be >= i-1
        bool mustIncrease = false;
        
        for (auto [l, r] : segments1) {
            if (l <= i - 1 && i <= r) {
                mustIncrease = true;
                break;
            }
        }
        
        for (auto [l, r] : segments2) {
            if (l <= i - 1 && i <= r) {
                mustIncrease = true;
                break;
            }
        }
        
        if (mustIncrease) {
            result[i] = result[i-1];  // Non-decreasing
        } else {
            result[i] = result[i-1] + 1;  // Can increase
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
Greedy construction:
  Process positions left to right
  At each position: Assign minimum valid value
  
Validation:
  Check both arrays as subsegments
  Verify non-decreasing property

Time: O(N)
*/
\end{lstlisting}
