% CHAPTER 13: BINARY SEARCH ON FLOATING POINT
% ==========================================

\chapter{Binary Search on Floating Point}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,
    colback=white,
    colframe=secondaryblue,
    boxrule=1pt,
    arc=2mm,
    left=10pt,
    right=10pt
]
\textbf{\large\textcolor{primaryblue}{Real Number Search, Precision Handling, and Continuous Functions}}
\end{tcolorbox}

\vspace{0.5cm}

\begin{center}
\textit{\textcolor{darkgray}{Difficulty: CF 1800-2200 $\bullet$ 20 Problems}}
\end{center}

\section{Working with Real Numbers}

When searching in a continuous space, we cannot use `low <= high` because floating-point representations are not exact. Instead, we use one of two methods to terminate the loop.

\subsection{Method 1: Fixed Iterations}
The most robust way is to run the loop for a fixed number of iterations (usually 60 to 100). Since each iteration halves the search space, 100 iterations reduce the range by a factor of $2^{100}$, which is more than enough for any standard precision requirement.

\subsection{Method 2: Epsilon ($\epsilon$)}
Alternatively, we stop when $(high - low) < \epsilon$. Typically, $\epsilon = 10^{-7}$ or $10^{-9}$ depending on the problem's requirements.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced, colback=darkgray!5!white, colframe=darkgray, title=Floating Point BS Template]
\begin{verbatim}
double low = 0, high = 1e18;
for(int i = 0; i < 100; i++) { // Fixed iterations for precision
    double mid = low + (high - low) / 2.0;
    if (check(mid)) {
        ans = mid;
        low = mid; // For maximization
    } else {
        high = mid;
    }
}
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Continuous Functions and Roots}
Binary search is the standard method for finding the **roots of a continuous function** (where $f(x) = 0$). If $f(x)$ is monotonic, we can find the exact value of $x$ where the function crosses the x-axis.

\section{Binary Search on Floating Point: Complete Mastery}

\subsection{Complete Theoretical Foundation}

\subsubsection{Why Floating Point Binary Search?}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=white,
    colframe=deepblue,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{Working with Real Numbers}
]
\textbf{Why Continuous Search?}

Different from integers: Search in continuous space

\textbf{The Key Differences:}
\begin{verbatim}
Integer Binary Search:
  Terminates: when low > high
  Discrete values: 1, 2, 3, ...
  Exact answers possible
  
Floating Point Binary Search:
  Terminates: after fixed iterations OR epsilon
  Continuous values: 1.5, 2.7183, π, ...
  Approximate answers (precision limited)
  
KEY CHALLENGE:
  Cannot use low <= high (floating point precision)
  Need alternative termination conditions!
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Complete Theory:}

1. \textbf{Two Termination Methods:}
\begin{verbatim}
METHOD 1: Fixed Iterations (RECOMMENDED)
  Run loop for fixed count (60-100 iterations)
  
  Why this works:
    Each iteration: Range halves
    After 100 iterations: Range / 2^100
    2^100 ≈ 10^30
    More than enough for any precision!
  
  Code:
    for (int i = 0; i < 100; i++) {
      double mid = (low + high) / 2.0;
      if (check(mid)) {
        result = mid;
        low = mid;  // or high = mid
      } else {
        high = mid; // or low = mid
      }
    }
  
  Advantages:
    - Always terminates (no infinite loops)
    - Predictable behavior
    - Works for all problems

METHOD 2: Epsilon (ε) Threshold
  Stop when range < ε
  
  Typical values:
    ε = 10^-7 for most problems
    ε = 10^-9 for high precision
    ε = 10^-6 if time limit tight
  
  Code:
    while (high - low > 1e-7) {
      double mid = (low + high) / 2.0;
      if (check(mid)) {
        result = mid;
        low = mid;
      } else {
        high = mid;
      }
    }
  
  Caution:
    - Can infinite loop if ε too small
    - Depends on initial range
    - Fixed iterations more reliable

RECOMMENDATION: Use fixed iterations!
\end{verbatim}

2. \textbf{Floating Point Precision Issues:}
\begin{verbatim}
Common pitfalls:

Pitfall 1: Comparing floats with ==
  WRONG: if (x == y)
  RIGHT: if (abs(x - y) < 1e-9)
  
  Reason: Floating point arithmetic imprecise

Pitfall 2: Integer overflow in mid
  WRONG: mid = (low + high) / 2
  RIGHT: mid = low + (high - low) / 2.0
  
  Though less critical for doubles

Pitfall 3: Using low <= high
  Problem: May not terminate due to precision
  Solution: Use fixed iterations or epsilon

Pitfall 4: Too small epsilon
  Can cause infinite loop
  If high - low = 1e-10 and ε = 1e-15
  Loop may never terminate!

Best practices:
  1. Use fixed iterations (100)
  2. Avoid == comparisons
  3. Use abs(diff) < epsilon for validation
  4. Test with boundary values
\end{verbatim}

3. \textbf{The Template Pattern:}
\begin{verbatim}
UNIVERSAL TEMPLATE: Floating Point BS

// Method 1: Fixed iterations (RECOMMENDED)
double binarySearchFloat() {
  double low = min_value;
  double high = max_value;
  double result = low;
  
  for (int iter = 0; iter < 100; iter++) {
    double mid = low + (high - low) / 2.0;
    
    if (check(mid)) {
      result = mid;
      // Maximize: low = mid
      // Minimize: high = mid
    } else {
      // Opposite update
    }
  }
  
  return result;
}

// Method 2: Epsilon-based
double binarySearchEpsilon() {
  double low = min_value;
  double high = max_value;
  double eps = 1e-7;
  
  while (high - low > eps) {
    double mid = (low + high) / 2.0;
    
    if (check(mid)) {
      low = mid;  // or high = mid
    } else {
      high = mid; // or low = mid
    }
  }
  
  return low; // or (low + high) / 2
}

Key differences from integer BS:
  1. No result tracking needed (continuous)
  2. Update: low = mid or high = mid (not ±1)
  3. Termination: iterations or epsilon
  4. Return: Usually low or (low+high)/2
\end{verbatim}

4. \textbf{Applications of Floating Point BS:}
\begin{verbatim}
Common use cases:

Application 1: Finding roots
  f(x) = 0, find x
  Example: sqrt(x), polynomial roots

Application 2: Optimization
  Minimize/maximize continuous function
  Example: Meeting point, optimal ratio

Application 3: Precision search
  Integer answer exists but float check easier
  Example: Pie cutting, resource allocation

Application 4: Ternary search
  Find extremum of unimodal function
  Related but different technique

When to use float BS:
  - Answer can be non-integer
  - Continuous function to optimize
  - Precision matters (ε-based)
  - Root finding problems
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Key Insight:}
Fixed iterations eliminate precision worries!
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Pattern 1: Square Root and Nth Root}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=successgreen!10,
    colframe=successgreen,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Root Finding Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Square Root Implementation}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Implement sqrt(x) with precision
  Find y where y² = x
  
Floating point BS:
  Search space: [0, x]
  Check: mid² ≤ x?
  
100 iterations: Precision ~10^-30

Time: O(log(x) × iterations) = O(100)
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Type 2: Nth Root}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Find y where y^N = M
  
Search space: [0, M]
Check: mid^N ≤ M?

Careful: mid^N can overflow
Use: log comparison or careful multiplication

Time: O(100 × N) for naive power
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Root Finding Solutions - NEW PROBLEMS]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 13.7: LeetCode 69 - Sqrt(x) Implementation
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/sqrtx/
// Difficulty: Easy (CF 1200) - But with FLOAT precision!
//
// Problem Statement:
// Given a non-negative integer x, compute and return the square root of x.
// Since the return type is an integer, the decimal digits are truncated,
// and only the integer part of the result is returned.
// 
// EXTENSION: Implement with floating point precision (10^-7)
//
// Technique: Floating Point Binary Search
// ==============================================================

// Integer version (from problem)
int mySqrt(int x) {
    if (x == 0) return 0;
    
    long long low = 1;
    long long high = x;
    long long result = 1;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        long long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (mid * mid <= x) {
            result = mid;
            low = mid + 1;
        } else {
            high = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

// Floating point version (high precision)
double mySqrtFloat(double x) {
    if (x == 0.0) return 0.0;
    
    double low = 0.0;
    double high = max(1.0, x);  // Handle x < 1
    
    // Fixed iterations for precision
    for (int i = 0; i < 100; i++) {
        double mid = low + (high - low) / 2.0;
        double square = mid * mid;
        
        if (square <= x) {
            low = mid;
        } else {
            high = mid;
        }
    }
    
    return low;
}

/*
WHY FLOATING POINT BS IS CORRECT:

Function: f(x) = $x^2$ - target
  Monotonically increasing
  Root: Where f(x) = 0
  
Binary search finds this root!

Fixed iterations:
  100 iterations: Range / 2^100
  Initial range: At most target
  Final precision: target / 2^100
  
  For target = 10^9: Precision ~10^-21
  Way beyond double precision!

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(100) = O(1) constant
Space: O(1)

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Newton-Raphson:
   Faster convergence (quadratic)
   But: Requires derivative
   More complex implementation
   
   Our solution: Simple and reliable

2. Integer BS only:
   Can't get fractional precision
   Returns truncated integer
   
   Our solution: Full precision

3. Built-in sqrt():
   Obviously cheating in interview!
   Implementation detail: Uses similar method

Special case: x < 1
  sqrt(0.25) = 0.5 > 0.25
  Must set high = max(1, x)
  Not high = x!

Precision verification:
  double ans = mySqrtFloat(2.0);
  Should be = 1.414213562373095
  
  Check: ans * ans = 2.0
  abs(ans * ans - 2.0) < 1e-9 \ding{51}
*/

// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 13.8: Find Nth Root of M
// Link: https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/problems/find-nth-root-of-m5843/1
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1600)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You are given two numbers $n$ and $m$. The task is to find the $n$-th root of $m$, i.e., $\sqrt[n]{m}$.
// If the root is not an integer, return -1.
//
// EXTENSION: Find exact nth root with floating point precision
//
// Technique: Floating Point BS with Power Check
// ==============================================================

// Check if nth root exists as integer
int nthRootInteger(int n, int m) {
    double low = 1.0;
    double high = m;
    
    for (int i = 0; i < 100; i++) {
        double mid = low + (high - low) / 2.0;
        
        // Calculate mid^n carefully
        double power = 1.0;
        for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) {
            power *= mid;
        }
        
        if (power <= m) {
            low = mid;
        } else {
            high = mid;
        }
    }
    
    // Check if integer root
    int candidate = (int)round(low);
    long long check = 1;
    for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) {
        check *= candidate;
        if (check > m) return -1;
    }
    
    return (check == m) ? candidate : -1;
}

// Floating point version (exact root)
double nthRootFloat(int n, double m) {
    if (m == 0.0) return 0.0;
    if (n == 0) return 1.0;
    
    double low = 0.0;
    double high = max(1.0, m);
    
    for (int i = 0; i < 100; i++) {
        double mid = low + (high - low) / 2.0;
        
        // Calculate mid^n using log to avoid overflow
        // mid^n = exp(n * log(mid))
        double power = exp(n * log(mid));
        
        if (power <= m) {
            low = mid;
        } else {
            high = mid;
        }
    }
    
    return low;
}

/*
ADVANCED: Using logarithms

Instead of: power = mid^n (can overflow)
Use: power = exp(n \times log(mid))

Why this works:
  x^n = e^(n \times ln(x))
  
  Log properties:
    log(x^n) = n \times log(x)
    e^(log(x)) = x
  
  Therefore: x^n = exp(n \times log(x))

Advantages:
  - Avoids overflow for large n or mid
  - More numerically stable
  - Works for any n (even fractional!)

Caution:
  log(0) = -\infty (undefined)
  Must handle x = 0 separately

Time: O(100) = O(1)
Space: O(1)

Pattern: "Find nth root" -> Float BS with log power
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 2: Meeting Point Problems}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=lightgray,
    colframe=accentorange,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Meeting Point Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Meeting Place on Line}

\textbf{Complete Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: N people at positions with speeds
  Find point where max arrival time is minimized
  
Key insight: Arrival time function is unimodal
  Can use ternary search OR binary search
  
Binary search on time:
  Check: Can all meet within time T?
  For each person: Reachable range [x-vT, x+vT]
  Check: Ranges intersect?

Time: O(N × 100)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Meeting Point Solutions - NEW PROBLEM]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 13.10: CF 782B - The Meeting Place Cannot Be Changed
// Link: https://codeforces.com/problemset/problem/782/B
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 1900-2000)
//
// Problem Statement:
// There are n friends at points x_1, x_2, ..., x_n on the coordinate line.
// They want to meet at some point. The i-th friend can move with speed v_i.
// What is the minimum time needed for all friends to meet at some point?
//
// Technique: Binary Search on Time (Floating Point)
// ==============================================================

bool canMeetInTime(vector<double>& positions, vector<double>& speeds, 
                   double time) {
    int n = positions.size();
    
    // Calculate reachable range for each person
    double leftmost = -1e18;
    double rightmost = 1e18;
    
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        double left = positions[i] - speeds[i] * time;
        double right = positions[i] + speeds[i] * time;
        
        leftmost = max(leftmost, left);
        rightmost = min(rightmost, right);
    }
    
    // Check if ranges intersect
    return leftmost <= rightmost + 1e-9;  // Small epsilon for precision
}

double findMinimumTime(vector<double>& positions, vector<double>& speeds) {
    double low = 0.0;
    double high = 1e9;  // Maximum possible time
    
    // Fixed iterations for precision
    for (int iter = 0; iter < 100; iter++) {
        double mid = low + (high - low) / 2.0;
        
        if (canMeetInTime(positions, speeds, mid)) {
            high = mid;  // Can meet faster
        } else {
            low = mid;   // Need more time
        }
    }
    
    return high;
}

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

Problem Analysis:
  - N people at different positions
  - Each with different speed
  - Find minimum time for all to meet

Key Insight: Reachable ranges
  Person i at position x_i, speed v_i
  In time T: Can reach [x_i - v_i \times T, x_i + v_i \times T]
  
  All meet: Intersection of all ranges non-empty

Monotonicity:
  If can't meet in time T: Can't meet in < T
  If can meet in time T: Can meet in > T
  
  Pattern: F F F F T T T T
  Find first True (minimum time)

Range intersection:
  For all ranges to intersect:
    max(all left bounds) <= min(all right bounds)
  
  leftmost = max(x_i - v_i \times T)
  rightmost = min(x_i + v_i \times T)
  
  Valid if: leftmost <= rightmost

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N \times 100)
  100 iterations of BS
  Each check: O(N)

Space: O(1)

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Ternary search:
   Also works (function is unimodal)
   Similar complexity
   
   Our solution: Simpler to implement

2. Analytical solution:
   Complex derivative calculation
   Prone to numerical errors
   
   Our solution: Clean and robust

3. Try all points:
   Infinite continuous space
   Not feasible
   
   Our solution: Binary search handles it

Precision handling:
  Use epsilon in comparison: leftmost <= rightmost + 1e-9
  Accounts for floating point errors

Example: positions=[1,4,7], speeds=[1,1,1]
  
  Try T=2:
    Person 0: [1-2, 1+2] = [-1, 3]
    Person 1: [4-2, 4+2] = [2, 6]
    Person 2: [7-2, 7+2] = [5, 9]
    
    leftmost = max(-1, 2, 5) = 5
    rightmost = min(3, 6, 9) = 3
    
    5 > 3: Can't meet \ding{55}
  
  Try T=3:
    Ranges: [-2,4], [1,7], [4,10]
    leftmost = 4, rightmost = 4
    4 <= 4: Can meet \ding{51}
  
  Minimum time = 3.0

Pattern: "Meet at point with speeds" -> BS on time + range intersection
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 3: Equation Solving and Root Finding}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=warnyellow!10,
    colframe=warnyellow,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Equation Solving Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Solve Complex Equations}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Find x where f(x) = 0
  f(x) = p×e^(-x) + q×sin(x) + r×cos(x) + s×tan(x) + t×x² + u
  
Requirements:
  - f(x) continuous
  - f(x) monotonic on interval
  - f(low) and f(high) have opposite signs
  
Binary search on x:
  If f(mid) × f(low) < 0: Root in [low, mid]
  Else: Root in [mid, high]

Time: O(100 × T_eval) where T_eval = function eval time
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Equation Solving Solutions - NEW PROBLEM]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 13.9: UVA 10341 - Solve It
// Link: https://onlinejudge.org/index.php?option=com_onlinejudge&page=show_problem&problem=1282
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1700-1800)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Solve the equation: p \times e^(-x) + q \times sin(x) + r \times cos(x) + s \times tan(x) + t \times $x^2$ + u = 0
// where 0 <= x <= 1.
// Print the value of x with 4 decimal places, or "No solution" if none exists.
//
// Technique: Binary Search on Continuous Function
// ==============================================================

double evaluateFunction(double x, double p, double q, double r, 
                       double s, double t, double u) {
    return p * exp(-x) + q * sin(x) + r * cos(x) + 
           s * tan(x) + t * x * x + u;
}

double solveEquation(double p, double q, double r, double s, double t, double u) {
    double low = 0.0;
    double high = 1.0;
    
    double f_low = evaluateFunction(low, p, q, r, s, t, u);
    double f_high = evaluateFunction(high, p, q, r, s, t, u);
    
    // Check if solution exists
    if (f_low * f_high > 0) {
        return -1.0;  // No solution
    }
    
    // Binary search for root
    for (int iter = 0; iter < 100; iter++) {
        double mid = low + (high - low) / 2.0;
        double f_mid = evaluateFunction(mid, p, q, r, s, t, u);
        
        if (abs(f_mid) < 1e-9) {
            return mid;  // Found exact root
        }
        
        if (f_mid * f_low < 0) {
            // Root in [low, mid]
            high = mid;
            f_high = f_mid;
        } else {
            // Root in [mid, high]
            low = mid;
            f_low = f_mid;
        }
    }
    
    return low;
}

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

Root Finding Method: Bisection
  Classic numerical analysis technique
  
Requirements:
  1. f(x) continuous on [a,b]
  2. f(a) and f(b) have opposite signs
  3. Then: Intermediate Value Theorem guarantees root

Algorithm:
  Check midpoint
  If f(mid) \times f(low) < 0: Root in left half
  Else: Root in right half
  
  Repeat until convergence

Convergence:
  Error halves each iteration
  After 100 iterations: Error < original_range / 2^100
  
  For [0,1]: Error < 10^-30
  Way beyond required 4 decimal places!

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(100) function evaluations
  Each evaluation: O(1) with math library

Space: O(1)

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Newton-Raphson:
   Requires derivative f'(x)
   More complex: f'(x) = -p \times e^(-x) + q \times cos(x) - r \times sin(x) + ...
   
   Our solution: No derivative needed!

2. Secant method:
   Faster convergence
   But less reliable (may diverge)
   
   Our solution: Always converges

3. Fixed-point iteration:
   May not converge
   Requires specific form
   
   Our solution: Works for any continuous function

Edge cases:
  - No solution: f(0) and f(1) same sign
  - Multiple solutions: Returns one in [0,1]
  - Exact solution: Early termination

Example: p=1, q=2, r=3, s=4, t=5, u=-6
  f(0) = 1 + 0 + 3 + 0 + 0 - 6 = -2
  f(1) = 1 \times e^(-1) + 2 \times sin(1) + 3 \times cos(1) + 4 \times tan(1) + 5 - 6
       = 0.368 + 1.683 + 1.621 + 6.244 + 5 - 6 = 8.916
  
  Different signs: Solution exists!
  
  Binary search finds: x = 0.1234 (example)

Pattern: "Find x where f(x)=0" -> Bisection method
*/

// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 13.3: AtCoder ABC 034D - Preparing Cookies  
// Link: https://atcoder.jp/contests/abc034/tasks/abc034_d
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1800)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You have N types of salt water. The i-th water has concentration w_i% and
// volume x_i grams. You can mix any K types. What is the maximum concentration
// you can achieve?
//
// Technique: Binary Search on Concentration (Floating Point)
// ==============================================================

bool canAchieveConcentration(vector<pair<double,double>>& waters, 
                            int k, double targetPercent) {
    int n = waters.size();
    vector<double> values;
    
    // Calculate "value" of each water for target concentration
    // value = x_i \times (w_i - target)
    for (auto [percent, grams] : waters) {
        double value = grams * (percent - targetPercent);
        values.push_back(value);
    }
    
    // Select top K values
    sort(values.rbegin(), values.rend());
    
    double sum = 0.0;
    for (int i = 0; i < k; i++) {
        sum += values[i];
    }
    
    return sum >= -1e-9;  // Can achieve (with small epsilon)
}

double maxConcentration(vector<pair<double,double>>& waters, int k) {
    double low = 0.0;
    double high = 100.0;
    
    for (int iter = 0; iter < 100; iter++) {
        double mid = low + (high - low) / 2.0;
        
        if (canAchieveConcentration(waters, k, mid)) {
            low = mid;  // Can achieve higher
        } else {
            high = mid;
        }
    }
    
    return low;
}

/*
Clever transformation:
  To check if concentration C is achievable:
    Select K waters that maximize:
      sum(x_i \times w_i) / sum(x_i) >= C
  
  Rearrange:
    sum(x_i \times w_i) >= C \times sum(x_i)
    sum(x_i \times (w_i - C)) >= 0
  
  Greedy: Select K with largest x_i \times (w_i - C)

Time: O(N log N \times 100)
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 4: Optimization with Ternary Search}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=dangered!10,
    colframe=dangered,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Ternary Search Algorithms}
]
\textbf{When to Use Ternary Search?}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Ternary Search: Find extremum of unimodal function
  Unimodal: Single peak (max) or valley (min)
  
NOT for monotonic functions!
  Use binary search for those

Algorithm:
  Split range into 3 parts
  mid1 = low + (high - low) / 3
  mid2 = high - (high - low) / 3
  
  For finding minimum:
    If f(mid1) > f(mid2): Min in [mid1, high]
    Else: Min in [low, mid2]

Time: O(100) iterations
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Ternary Search Solutions - NEW PROBLEM]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 13.18: CF 439D - Devu and his Brother
// Link: https://codeforces.com/problemset/problem/439/D
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 1900-2000)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You have two arrays A and B. In one operation, you can increase or decrease
// any element by 1. Find the minimum operations to make all A[i] >= all B[j].
//
// Technique: Ternary Search on Threshold Value
// ==============================================================

long long calculateCost(vector<int>& A, vector<int>& B, double threshold) {
    long long cost = 0;
    
    // Cost to make all A[i] >= threshold
    for (int a : A) {
        if (a < threshold) {
            cost += (long long)ceil(threshold - a);
        }
    }
    
    // Cost to make all B[j] <= threshold
    for (int b : B) {
        if (b > threshold) {
            cost += (long long)(b - floor(threshold));
        }
    }
    
    return cost;
}

long long minimumOperations(vector<int>& A, vector<int>& B) {
    // Find range
    double low = *min_element(A.begin(), A.end());
    double high = *max_element(B.begin(), B.end());
    
    // Ternary search for minimum cost
    for (int iter = 0; iter < 100; iter++) {
        double mid1 = low + (high - low) / 3.0;
        double mid2 = high - (high - low) / 3.0;
        
        long long cost1 = calculateCost(A, B, mid1);
        long long cost2 = calculateCost(A, B, mid2);
        
        if (cost1 > cost2) {
            low = mid1;  // Minimum in [mid1, high]
        } else {
            high = mid2;  // Minimum in [low, mid2]
        }
    }
    
    // Check integer values around the result
    long long minCost = LLONG_MAX;
    for (int delta = -2; delta <= 2; delta++) {
        long long threshold = (long long)round(low) + delta;
        minCost = min(minCost, calculateCost(A, B, threshold));
    }
    
    return minCost;
}

/*
WHY TERNARY SEARCH?

Cost function is unimodal:
  - Too low threshold: High cost for A
  - Too high threshold: High cost for B
  - Optimal threshold: Balances both
  
  Single minimum exists!

Ternary vs Binary:
  Binary: For monotonic functions
  Ternary: For unimodal functions (one extremum)
  
  This problem: Unimodal cost function
  Therefore: Ternary search appropriate

Integer rounding:
  After ternary search: Check nearby integers
  Ensures we don't miss optimal due to rounding

Time: O((N+M) \times 100)
Space: O(1)

Pattern: "Minimize cost with threshold" -> Ternary search if unimodal
*/
\end{lstlisting}

    title=\textbf{Floating Point BS: Complete Mastery}
]
\textbf{All NEW Patterns Covered:}

\textbf{Pattern 1: Root Finding (2 NEW problems)}
\begin{itemize}
\item 13.7 Sqrt(x) Implementation - Basic float BS
\item 13.8 Nth Root - Using log for overflow prevention
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Pattern 2: Meeting Points (1 NEW problem)}
\begin{itemize}
\item 13.10 CF Meeting Place - Range intersection check
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Pattern 3: Equation Solving (2 NEW problems)}
\begin{itemize}

\item 13.3 AtCoder Salt Water - Greedy transformation
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Pattern 4: Ternary Search (1 NEW problem)}
\begin{itemize}
\item 13.18 CF Devu Brother - Unimodal optimization
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Key Recognition:}
\begin{itemize}
\item "Find root / sqrt" → Float BS with 100 iterations
\item "Meet at point with speeds" → BS on time + range check
\item "Solve f(x)=0" → Bisection method
\item "Minimize unimodal function" → Ternary search
\end{itemize}

\textbf{CRITICAL: Fixed Iterations!}
\begin{itemize}
\item Always use 100 iterations (not epsilon)
\item No low <= high condition
\item Update: low = mid or high = mid (not ±1)
\item Return: low or (low+high)/2
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Floating Point Cautions:}
\begin{itemize}
\item Never use == comparison
\item Use abs(diff) < epsilon for checks
\item Watch for overflow in power calculations
\item Use log tricks when needed
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Complexity: O(100) = O(1) for most patterns!}
\end{tcolorbox}

\chapter{Ternary Search}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,
    colback=white,
    colframe=secondaryblue,
    boxrule=1pt,
    arc=2mm,
    left=10pt,
    right=10pt
]
\textbf{\large\textcolor{primaryblue}{Finding Maximum/Minimum of Unimodal Functions}}
\end{tcolorbox}

\vspace{0.5cm}

\begin{center}
\textit{\textcolor{darkgray}{Difficulty: CF 1900-2300 $\bullet$ 25 Problems}}
\end{center}

\section{The Unimodal Property}
Ternary search is applicable to functions that are either:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Strictly Convex (U-shaped):} Decreases to a minimum and then increases.
    \item \textbf{Strictly Concave (Inverted U-shaped):} Increases to a maximum and then decreases.
\end{itemize}

\section{Algorithm Mechanics}
Instead of one $mid$, we calculate two points:
\[ m1 = low + \frac{high - low}{3} \]
\[ m2 = high - \frac{high - low}{3} \]

\subsection{Decision Logic (For Finding Maximum)}
\begin{itemize}
    \item If $f(m1) < f(m2)$, the maximum cannot be in the range $[low, m1]$. We move $low = m1$.
    \item If $f(m1) > f(m2)$, the maximum cannot be in the range $[m2, high]$. We move $high = m2$.
    \item If $f(m1) = f(m2)$, we can technically move both $low = m1$ and $high = m2$.
\end{itemize}


\section{Implementation: Discrete vs Continuous}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Continuous:} Similar to Float Binary Search, use a fixed number of iterations (e.g., 100) to ensure high precision.
    \item \textbf{Discrete:} Be careful with integer division. When $(high - low)$ becomes small (e.g., $< 5$), it is often safer to perform a linear scan to find the exact peak.
\end{itemize}

\section{Ternary Search: Complete Mastery}

\subsection{Complete Theoretical Foundation}

\subsubsection{Why Ternary Search?}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=white,
    colframe=deepblue,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{The Unimodal Property}
]
\textbf{When Binary Search Fails: Finding Extrema}

Binary search: For monotonic functions only
Ternary search: For unimodal functions (single peak/valley)

\textbf{The Core Concept:}
\begin{verbatim}
Monotonic Function (Binary Search):
  Always increasing: $\nearrow\nearrow\nearrow\nearrow\nearrow$
  Or always decreasing: $\searrow\searrow\searrow$
  Can find specific value
  
Unimodal Function (Ternary Search):
  Single maximum: $\nearrow\nearrow\nearrow$ PEAK $\searrow\searrow\searrow$
  Or single minimum: $\searrow\searrow\searrow$ VALLEY $\nearrow\nearrow\nearrow$
  Find the extremum (peak/valley)
  
KEY DIFFERENCE:
  Binary: Find where f(x) = target
  Ternary: Find x where f(x) is maximum/minimum
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Complete Theory:}

1. \textbf{What is Unimodal?}
\begin{verbatim}
Unimodal function: Has single peak OR single valley

Type 1: Strictly Concave (∩ shape)
  Properties:
    - Increases to maximum
    - Then decreases
    - Single global maximum
  
  Example: f(x) = -x² + 4x + 1
    Increases until x=2 (peak)
    Decreases after x=2
  
  Applications:
    - Profit functions
    - Efficiency curves
    - Distance optimization

Type 2: Strictly Convex (∪ shape)
  Properties:
    - Decreases to minimum
    - Then increases
    - Single global minimum
  
  Example: f(x) = x² - 4x + 1
    Decreases until x=2 (valley)
    Increases after x=2
  
  Applications:
    - Cost functions
    - Error minimization
    - Meeting point problems

Mathematical definition:
  Concave: For any x_1 < x_2 < x_3 in increasing part:
           f(x_1) < f(x_2) < f(x_3)
           Then in decreasing part:
           f(x_3) < f(x_2) < f(x_1)
\end{verbatim}

2. \textbf{Why Not Binary Search?}
\begin{verbatim}
Binary search needs: f(mid) compared to target
Unimodal has: No target, looking for extremum

Example: f(x) = -(x-5)² + 10
  Peak at x=5, f(5)=10
  
  At x=3: f(3)=6
  At x=7: f(7)=6
  
  Both equal! Binary search confused
  Which direction has the peak?

Ternary search solution:
  Use TWO midpoints
  Compare f(m1) vs f(m2)
  Eliminates 1/3 of search space
\end{verbatim}

3. \textbf{The Ternary Search Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
CORE IDEA: Use two points to determine which third to eliminate

Algorithm (Finding Maximum):
  while low < high:
    m1 = low + (high - low) / 3
    m2 = high - (high - low) / 3
    
    if f(m1) < f(m2):
      # Maximum cannot be in [low, m1]
      low = m1
    else:
      # Maximum cannot be in [m2, high]
      high = m2
  
  return low (or high, they converge)

Why this works:
  Case 1: f(m1) < f(m2)
    Peak must be to the right of m1
    Eliminate [low, m1]
  
  Case 2: f(m1) > f(m2)
    Peak must be to the left of m2
    Eliminate [m2, high]
  
  Case 3: f(m1) = f(m2)
    Peak between m1 and m2
    Can eliminate both sides

Key insight: Each iteration eliminates 1/3

For Finding Minimum:
  if f(m1) > f(m2):
    low = m1  # Opposite logic!
  else:
    high = m2
\end{verbatim}

4. \textbf{Convergence Analysis:}
\begin{verbatim}
Ternary Search Complexity:

Each iteration: Range reduced by factor of 2/3
After k iterations: Range = Initial × (2/3)^k

For precision ε:
  (high - low) × (2/3)^k < ε
  k > log(ε/(high-low)) / log(2/3)
  \[
  k \approx \log_{1.5}\!\left(\frac{\text{range}}{\varepsilon}\right)
  \]

Comparison with Binary Search:
  Binary: Reduces by 1/2 each iteration
         k = log_2(range/ε)
  
  Ternary: Reduces by 2/3 each iteration
           k = log_{1.5}(range/ε)
  
  Ratio: log_{1.5} / log_2 ≈ 1.71
  
  Ternary needs ~71% more iterations
  BUT: Solves different class of problems!

Typical iterations needed:
  Continuous: 100-200 iterations (safe)
  Discrete: Until high - low ≤ 2, then linear
\end{verbatim}

5. \textbf{Discrete vs Continuous:}
\begin{verbatim}
Continuous Ternary Search:
  Range: Real numbers [a, b]
  Termination: Fixed iterations (100-200)
  
  Code:
    for (int i = 0; i < 200; i++) {
      double m1 = low + (high - low) / 3.0;
      double m2 = high - (high - low) / 3.0;
      if (f(m1) < f(m2)) low = m1;
      else high = m2;
    }
  
  Safe and reliable!

Discrete Ternary Search:
  Range: Integers [a, b]
  Termination: When high - low ≤ 2
  
  Code:
    while (high - low > 2) {
      int m1 = low + (high - low) / 3;
      int m2 = high - (high - low) / 3;
      if (f(m1) < f(m2)) low = m1;
      else high = m2;
    }
    // Check remaining 2-3 values
    int ans = low;
    for (int i = low; i <= high; i++) {
      if (f(i) > f(ans)) ans = i;
    }
  
  Careful with integer division!

Common pitfall: Off-by-one errors
  When m1 == m2 due to rounding
  Always check final 2-3 values manually
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Key Insight:}
Ternary search eliminates 1/3, finds extremum of unimodal functions!
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Pattern 1: Basic Unimodal Optimization}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=successgreen!10,
    colframe=successgreen,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Basic Optimization Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Minimize Cost with Threshold}

\textbf{Complete Framework:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Choose threshold X to minimize total cost
  Cost has two components:
    - Cost increasing with X
    - Cost decreasing with X
  
  Total cost is convex (U-shaped)

Example: Make all A[i] >= threshold, all B[j] <= threshold
  Cost_1 = sum(max(0, threshold - A[i]))
  Cost_2 = sum(max(0, B[j] - threshold))
  Total = Cost_1 + Cost_2 (convex!)

Ternary search finds minimum!

Time: O(N × iterations)
  Usually O(N × 200) = O(200N)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Basic Optimization Solutions - NEW PROBLEMS]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 14.4: SPOJ KOPC12A - K12 - Building Construction
// Link: https://www.spoj.com/problems/KOPC12A/
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1800-1900)
//
// Problem Statement:
// There are N buildings in a row. Building i has height H[i] and construction
// cost C[i] per unit height change. You want to make all buildings the same
// height. For each building, the cost to change from H[i] to target height
// X is C[i] \times |H[i] - X|. Find the minimum total cost.
//
// Technique: Ternary Search on Target Height
// ==============================================================

long long calculateCost(vector<int>& heights, vector<int>& costs, double targetHeight) {
    long long totalCost = 0;
    
    for (int i = 0; i < heights.size(); i++) {
        double diff = abs(heights[i] - targetHeight);
        totalCost += (long long)(costs[i] * diff);
    }
    
    return totalCost;
}

long long minimizeCost(vector<int>& heights, vector<int>& costs) {
    int n = heights.size();
    
    // Search range: [min_height, max_height]
    double low = *min_element(heights.begin(), heights.end());
    double high = *max_element(heights.begin(), heights.end());
    
    // Ternary search on target height
    for (int iter = 0; iter < 200; iter++) {
        double m1 = low + (high - low) / 3.0;
        double m2 = high - (high - low) / 3.0;
        
        long long cost1 = calculateCost(heights, costs, m1);
        long long cost2 = calculateCost(heights, costs, m2);
        
        if (cost1 > cost2) {
            low = m1;  // Minimum in [m1, high]
        } else {
            high = m2;  // Minimum in [low, m2]
        }
    }
    
    // Check integer values near the result
    long long minCost = LLONG_MAX;
    int targetInt = (int)round(low);
    
    for (int delta = -2; delta <= 2; delta++) {
        double target = targetInt + delta;
        if (target >= *min_element(heights.begin(), heights.end()) && 
            target <= *max_element(heights.begin(), heights.end())) {
            minCost = min(minCost, calculateCost(heights, costs, target));
        }
    }
    
    return minCost;
}

/*
WHY TERNARY SEARCH IS CORRECT:

Problem Analysis:
  Cost function: sum of C[i] \times |H[i] - X|
  
  This is CONVEX (U-shaped):
    Too low X: High cost to decrease tall buildings
    Too high X: High cost to increase short buildings
    Optimal X: Balances both costs

Proof of Convexity:
  f(X) = sum(C[i] \times |H[i] - X|)
  
  This is sum of piecewise linear convex functions
  Sum of convex functions is convex!
  
  Therefore: Single global minimum exists \ding{51}

Why Not Derivative?
  f(X) has corners at each H[i]
  Not differentiable everywhere
  Ternary search handles this naturally!

Ternary Search Convergence:
  200 iterations: Precision ~10^-60
  More than enough for any reasonable range

Integer Rounding:
  After ternary search: Result is approximate
  Check nearby integers to find exact optimum
  Essential for discrete problems!

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N \times 200) = O(N)
  200 iterations of ternary search
  Each iteration: O(N) cost calculation

Space: O(1)

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Try all heights:
   Range: [min, max]
   Could be 10^9 different values!
   Time: O(N \times range) - too slow
   
   Our solution: O(N \times 200)

2. Gradient descent:
   Requires tuning step size
   May get stuck at non-smooth points
   
   Our solution: Guaranteed convergence

3. Closed form (weighted median):
   Complex to derive
   Requires sorting and careful implementation
   
   Our solution: Simple and general

Example trace:
  heights = [5, 15, 25], costs = [1, 2, 1]
  
  Cost function:
    X=5:  0 + 2 \times 10 + 20 = 40
    X=10: 5 + 2 \times 5 + 15 = 30
    X=15: 10 + 0 + 10 = 20 <- minimum!
    X=20: 15 + 2 \times 5 + 5 = 30
    X=25: 20 + 2 \times 10 + 0 = 40
  
  Ternary search finds X=15 efficiently!

Pattern: "Minimize cost with threshold" -> Ternary search if convex
*/

// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 14.8: CF 439D - Devu and his Brother  
// Link: https://codeforces.com/problemset/problem/439/D
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 1900-2000)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You have two sequences A and B. In one move, you can increase or decrease
// any element of either sequence by 1. Find the minimum number of moves to
// make min(A) >= max(B).
//
// Technique: Ternary Search on Threshold Value
// ==============================================================

long long calculateMoves(vector<int>& A, vector<int>& B, double threshold) {
    long long moves = 0;
    
    // Cost to make all A[i] >= threshold
    for (int a : A) {
        if (a < threshold) {
            moves += (long long)ceil(threshold - a);
        }
    }
    
    // Cost to make all B[j] <= threshold
    for (int b : B) {
        if (b > threshold) {
            moves += (long long)(b - floor(threshold));
        }
    }
    
    return moves;
}

long long minimumMoves(vector<int>& A, vector<int>& B) {
    // Search range: [min(A), max(B)]
    double low = *min_element(A.begin(), A.end());
    double high = *max_element(B.begin(), B.end());
    
    // If already valid
    if (low >= high) return 0;
    
    // Ternary search on threshold
    for (int iter = 0; iter < 200; iter++) {
        double m1 = low + (high - low) / 3.0;
        double m2 = high - (high - low) / 3.0;
        
        long long cost1 = calculateMoves(A, B, m1);
        long long cost2 = calculateMoves(A, B, m2);
        
        if (cost1 > cost2) {
            low = m1;
        } else {
            high = m2;
        }
    }
    
    // Check integer thresholds near result
    long long minMoves = LLONG_MAX;
    int thresholdInt = (int)round(low);
    
    for (int delta = -5; delta <= 5; delta++) {
        double t = thresholdInt + delta;
        minMoves = min(minMoves, calculateMoves(A, B, t));
    }
    
    return minMoves;
}

/*
WHY TERNARY SEARCH WORKS HERE:

Cost Function Analysis:
  f(X) = cost to make all A[i] >= X + cost to make all B[j] <= X
  
  Component 1: sum(max(0, X - A[i]))
    Increases as X increases
  
  Component 2: sum(max(0, B[j] - X))
    Decreases as X increases
  
  Total: Convex (U-shaped) \ding{51}
    Low X: High B cost
    High X: High A cost
    Optimal X: Balanced

Unimodal Property:
  Single global minimum
  Perfect for ternary search!

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O((N+M) \times 200)
Space: O(1)

Alternative: Binary search?
  Could work with careful analysis
  But ternary search more natural
  
Alternative: Mathematical solution?
  Optimal is between max(B) and min(A)
  But exact value needs checking
  
  Ternary search: Finds it automatically!

Pattern: "Balance two opposing costs" -> Ternary search
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 2: Geometric Optimization}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=lightgray,
    colframe=accentorange,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Geometric Optimization Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Service Centre Placement}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Find point that minimizes sum of distances
  Given N points, find (x,y) minimizing:
    sum(distance from (x,y) to point[i]))
  
  This is geometric median (Weiszfeld problem)

Approaches:
  1. 2D Ternary Search (approximate)
  2. Gradient Descent (Weiszfeld algorithm)
  3. Iterative refinement

2D Ternary Search:
  First: Ternary search on X
  For each X: Ternary search on Y
  
Time: O(N × iterations²) = O(N × 40000)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Geometric Solutions - NEW PROBLEMS]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 14.5: LeetCode 1515 - Best Position for a Service Centre
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/best-position-for-a-service-centre/
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 2000-2100)
//
// Problem Statement:
// A delivery company wants to build a new service centre in a new city. The
// company knows the positions of all the customers in this city on a 2D-Map
// and wants to build the new centre in a position such that the sum of the
// euclidean distances to all customers is minimum.
// Given an array positions where positions[i] = [x_i, y_i] is the position
// of the ith customer on the map, return the minimum sum of distances.
//
// Technique: 2D Ternary Search (or Weiszfeld Algorithm)
// ==============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    double calculateDistance(vector<vector<int>>& positions, double x, double y) {
        double total = 0.0;
        
        for (auto& pos : positions) {
            double dx = pos[0] - x;
            double dy = pos[1] - y;
            total += sqrt(dx * dx + dy * dy);
        }
        
        return total;
    }
    
    double ternarySearchY(vector<vector<int>>& positions, double x) {
        double low = 0.0;
        double high = 100.0;
        
        for (int iter = 0; iter < 100; iter++) {
            double m1 = low + (high - low) / 3.0;
            double m2 = high - (high - low) / 3.0;
            
            double dist1 = calculateDistance(positions, x, m1);
            double dist2 = calculateDistance(positions, x, m2);
            
            if (dist1 > dist2) {
                low = m1;
            } else {
                high = m2;
            }
        }
        
        return low;
    }
    
    double getMinDistSum(vector<vector<int>>& positions) {
        double low = 0.0;
        double high = 100.0;
        double bestX = 50.0;
        double minDist = 1e9;
        
        // Ternary search on X
        for (int iter = 0; iter < 100; iter++) {
            double m1 = low + (high - low) / 3.0;
            double m2 = high - (high - low) / 3.0;
            
            // For each X, find best Y
            double y1 = ternarySearchY(positions, m1);
            double y2 = ternarySearchY(positions, m2);
            
            double dist1 = calculateDistance(positions, m1, y1);
            double dist2 = calculateDistance(positions, m2, y2);
            
            if (dist1 > dist2) {
                low = m1;
                bestX = m2;
                minDist = dist2;
            } else {
                high = m2;
                bestX = m1;
                minDist = dist1;
            }
        }
        
        return minDist;
    }
};

/*
WHY 2D TERNARY SEARCH WORKS:

Problem: Geometric Median
  Find point minimizing sum of Euclidean distances
  
  Function f(x,y) = sum(sqrt($(x-x_i)^2$ + $(y-y_i)^2)$)
  
  This function is:
    - Continuous
    - Convex
    - Has unique global minimum
  
  Perfect for ternary search!

2D Ternary Search Approach:
  1. Outer ternary search: Find optimal X
  2. Inner ternary search: For each X, find optimal Y
  3. Combine to find (X, Y)

Why this works:
  For fixed X: f(x,y) is unimodal in y
  Overall: f(x,y) is unimodal
  
  Nested ternary search finds minimum!

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N \times iterations_x \times iterations_y)
     = O(N \times 100 \times 100) = O(10000N)
  
  For N <= 100: ~1M operations (fast!)

Space: O(1)

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORTH KNOWING:

1. Weiszfeld Algorithm (better!):
   Iterative formula:
     x_new = sum(x_i / d_i) / sum(1 / d_i)
     y_new = sum(y_i / d_i) / sum(1 / d_i)
   
   where d_i = distance from current to point i
   
   Converges faster: O(N \times 100) typically
   
   But: More complex implementation
   Ternary search: Simpler, always works

2. Gradient Descent:
   Calculate gradient of distance function
   Move in direction of steepest descent
   
   Requires careful step size tuning
   Ternary search: No tuning needed!

3. Closed form:
   For special cases (1D, 2 points)
   General case: No closed form
   
   Ternary search: Works for all cases!

Precision note:
  100 iterations: Error < 10^-30
  Way beyond double precision
  Sufficient for any practical problem

Pattern: "Minimize sum of distances" -> 2D ternary search or Weiszfeld
*/

// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 14.15: Minimum Distance Between Two Moving Points
// Link: https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/minimum-distance-two-moving-points/
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1800-1900)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Two points move with constant velocities in 2D space. Point 1 starts at
// (x1,y1) with velocity (vx1,vy1). Point 2 starts at (x2,y2) with velocity
// (vx2,vy2). Find the minimum distance between them over all time t >= 0.
//
// Technique: Ternary Search on Time
// ==============================================================

struct Point {
    double x, y, vx, vy;
    
    double getX(double t) const { return x + vx * t; }
    double getY(double t) const { return y + vy * t; }
};

double distanceAtTime(const Point& p1, const Point& p2, double t) {
    double x1 = p1.getX(t);
    double y1 = p1.getY(t);
    double x2 = p2.getX(t);
    double y2 = p2.getY(t);
    
    double dx = x2 - x1;
    double dy = y2 - y1;
    
    return sqrt(dx * dx + dy * dy);
}

double minimumDistance(const Point& p1, const Point& p2) {
    double low = 0.0;
    double high = 1e9;  // Large enough time
    
    // Ternary search on time
    for (int iter = 0; iter < 200; iter++) {
        double m1 = low + (high - low) / 3.0;
        double m2 = high - (high - low) / 3.0;
        
        double dist1 = distanceAtTime(p1, p2, m1);
        double dist2 = distanceAtTime(p1, p2, m2);
        
        if (dist1 > dist2) {
            low = m1;
        } else {
            high = m2;
        }
    }
    
    return distanceAtTime(p1, p2, low);
}

/*
WHY TERNARY SEARCH IS CORRECT:

Distance Function Analysis:
  d(t) = sqrt((x2(t) - x1(t))^2 + (y2(t) - y1(t))^2)
  
  Where:
    x1(t) = x1 + vx1 \times t
    x2(t) = x2 + vx2 \times t
  
  Expanding:
    dx(t) = (x2-x1) + (vx2-vx1) \times t
    dy(t) = (y2-y1) + (vy2-vy1) \times t
  
    d^2(t) = d$x^2$ + d$y^2$
          = At^2 + Bt + C (quadratic!)
  
  This is convex (parabola opening upward)!

Unimodal Property:
  d^2(t) is quadratic with positive leading coefficient
  Single minimum exists
  Perfect for ternary search \ding{51}

Alternative: Calculus
  $d^2$'(t) = 2A \times t + B = 0
  t_min = -B / (2A)
  
  If t_min < 0: Minimum at t=0
  Else: Minimum at t_min
  
  But ternary search handles all cases automatically!

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(200) = O(1)
Space: O(1)

Pattern: "Minimize over time" -> Check if unimodal, use ternary search
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 3: Discrete Ternary Search}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=warnyellow!10,
    colframe=warnyellow,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Discrete Optimization Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Mountain Array Peak Finding}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Find peak in mountain array
  Mountain: Increases then decreases
  Guaranteed single peak
  
Discrete ternary search:
  while (high - low > 2):
    m1 = low + (high - low) / 3
    m2 = high - (high - low) / 3
    if arr[m1] < arr[m2]: low = m1
    else: high = m2
  
  Check remaining 2-3 values manually

Time: O(log N)
Similar to binary search but for peak finding
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Discrete Solutions - NEW PROBLEM]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 14.12: LeetCode 1095 - Find in Mountain Array
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/find-in-mountain-array/
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 1900)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You may recall that an array arr is a mountain array if and only if:
//   - arr.length >= 3
//   - There exists some i with 0 < i < arr.length - 1 such that:
//     - arr[0] < arr[1] < ... < arr[i - 1] < arr[i]
//     - arr[i] > arr[i + 1] > ... > arr[arr.length - 1]
// Given a mountain array mountainArr, return the minimum index such that
// mountainArr.get(index) == target. If such an index doesn't exist, return -1.
// You can't access the mountain array directly. You may only access the array
// using a MountainArray interface.
//
// Technique: Find Peak with Ternary, then Binary Search Both Sides
// ==============================================================

/**
 * // This is the MountainArray's API interface.
 * class MountainArray {
 *   public:
 *     int get(int index);
 *     int length();
 * };
 */

class Solution {
public:
    int findPeak(MountainArray &mountainArr) {
        int low = 0;
        int high = mountainArr.length() - 1;
        
        while (high - low > 2) {
            int m1 = low + (high - low) / 3;
            int m2 = high - (high - low) / 3;
            
            if (mountainArr.get(m1) < mountainArr.get(m2)) {
                low = m1;
            } else {
                high = m2;
            }
        }
        
        // Check remaining values
        int peak = low;
        for (int i = low + 1; i <= high; i++) {
            if (mountainArr.get(i) > mountainArr.get(peak)) {
                peak = i;
            }
        }
        
        return peak;
    }
    
    int binarySearchAscending(MountainArray &arr, int target, int left, int right) {
        while (left <= right) {
            int mid = left + (right - left) / 2;
            int val = arr.get(mid);
            
            if (val == target) return mid;
            else if (val < target) left = mid + 1;
            else right = mid - 1;
        }
        return -1;
    }
    
    int binarySearchDescending(MountainArray &arr, int target, int left, int right) {
        while (left <= right) {
            int mid = left + (right - left) / 2;
            int val = arr.get(mid);
            
            if (val == target) return mid;
            else if (val > target) left = mid + 1;
            else right = mid - 1;
        }
        return -1;
    }
    
    int findInMountainArray(int target, MountainArray &mountainArr) {
        int n = mountainArr.length();
        
        // Step 1: Find peak using ternary search
        int peak = findPeak(mountainArr);
        
        // Step 2: Binary search in ascending part
        int result = binarySearchAscending(mountainArr, target, 0, peak);
        if (result != -1) return result;
        
        // Step 3: Binary search in descending part
        return binarySearchDescending(mountainArr, target, peak + 1, n - 1);
    }
};

/*
WHY THIS SOLUTION IS CORRECT:

Problem Structure:
  Mountain array: [1,2,3,4,5,3,1]
                         |^
                       peak
  
  Two parts:
    1. Ascending: [1,2,3,4,5]
    2. Descending: [5,3,1]

Step 1: Find Peak (Ternary Search)
  Array is unimodal
  Single maximum (peak) exists
  Ternary search finds it!

Step 2: Search Both Sides (Binary Search)
  Left side: Ascending -> normal binary search
  Right side: Descending -> reversed binary search

Why ternary for peak, binary for search?
  Peak finding: Need maximum of unimodal
  Value finding: Need specific value in monotonic
  
  Different problems -> different techniques!

Discrete Ternary Caution:
  Integer division: m1 and m2 might be equal!
  When high - low <= 2: Check manually
  
  This avoids infinite loops!

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(log N)
  Find peak: O(log N) ternary search
  Search left: O(log N) binary search
  Search right: O(log N) binary search
  Total: O(log N)

Space: O(1)

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Linear search for peak:
   Time: O(N)
   Our solution: O(log N)

2. Binary search for peak:
   Can work! Compare mid with mid+1
   But less intuitive for unimodal
   
   Ternary search: More natural

3. Search without finding peak:
   Must search entire array
   Time: O(log N) but more API calls
   
   Our solution: Minimizes API calls

Pattern: "Find peak in mountain" -> Discrete ternary search
*/

// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 14.9: AtCoder ARC 054B - Moore's Law
// Link: https://atcoder.jp/contests/arc054/tasks/arc054_b
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1900)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You have a task that requires P units of computation. Your computer can
// perform C operations per second initially. However, computer speed improves
// according to Moore's Law: after t seconds, the computer can perform
// C \times 2^(t/1.5) operations per second. You can start the task at any time.
// Find the minimum total time to complete the task.
//
// Technique: Ternary Search on Start Time
// ==============================================================

double totalTime(double startTime, double P, double C) {
    // Computer speed at start: C \times 2^(startTime/1.5)
    double speedAtStart = C * pow(2.0, startTime / 1.5);
    
    // Time to complete: P / speedAtStart
    double computeTime = P / speedAtStart;
    
    // Total time: Wait + Compute
    return startTime + computeTime;
}

double minimumTime(double P, double C) {
    double low = 0.0;
    double high = 100.0;  // Sufficient upper bound
    
    // Ternary search on start time
    for (int iter = 0; iter < 200; iter++) {
        double m1 = low + (high - low) / 3.0;
        double m2 = high - (high - low) / 3.0;
        
        double time1 = totalTime(m1, P, C);
        double time2 = totalTime(m2, P, C);
        
        if (time1 > time2) {
            low = m1;
        } else {
            high = m2;
        }
    }
    
    return totalTime(low, P, C);
}

/*
WHY THIS IS UNIMODAL:

Total Time Function:
  T(t) = t + P / (C \times 2^(t/1.5))
  
  Component 1: t (waiting time)
    Increases linearly
  
  Component 2: P / (C \times 2^(t/1.5))
    Decreases exponentially
  
  Trade-off:
    Start early: Less waiting, slower computer
    Start late: More waiting, faster computer
  
  Optimal: Balance point (unique minimum)

Convexity:
  Take derivative:
    T'(t) = 1 - (P \times \ln 2) / (1.5 \times C \times 2^(t/1.5))
  
  Set T'(t) = 0 for minimum
  Single solution exists
  
  Therefore: Unimodal (convex) \ding{51}

Ternary search finds this minimum!

Time: O(200) = O(1)

Pattern: "Optimize with exponential growth" -> Ternary search
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 4: Advanced Applications}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=dangered!10,
    colframe=dangered,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Advanced Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Subset Optimization}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Find subset with optimal property
  Often involves choosing subset size
  Property function may be unimodal in size
  
Example: Maximum (mean - median)
  For each subset size: Calculate best value
  Subset size vs value: Unimodal!
  
Ternary search on subset size

Time: O(N log N × iterations)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Advanced Solutions - NEW PROBLEM]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 14.25: CF 939E - Maximize!
// Link: https://codeforces.com/problemset/problem/939/E
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 2000-2100)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You are given a sequence of integers a_1, a_2, ..., a_n. You want to find
// a contiguous subsegment (possibly empty) such that:
//   (max_element - mean) is maximized.
// Process queries: add element, then answer the question.
//
// Technique: Ternary Search on Subsegment Size + Greedy Selection
// ==============================================================

double calculateMaxDifference(vector<int>& arr, int subsegSize) {
    int n = arr.size();
    if (subsegSize == 0) return 0.0;
    if (subsegSize > n) return -1e9;
    
    // We want: max_element - mean
    // For fixed size, choose elements greedily
    // To maximize this: Take largest element, then smallest others
    
    vector<int> sorted = arr;
    sort(sorted.begin(), sorted.end());
    
    // Take smallest (size-1) elements + largest element
    double sum = 0.0;
    for (int i = 0; i < subsegSize - 1; i++) {
        sum += sorted[i];
    }
    sum += sorted[n-1];  // Add largest
    
    double mean = sum / subsegSize;
    double maxElem = sorted[n-1];
    
    return maxElem - mean;
}

double maximizeMetric(vector<int>& arr) {
    int n = arr.size();
    
    int low = 1;
    int high = n;
    
    // Discrete ternary search on subset size
    while (high - low > 2) {
        int m1 = low + (high - low) / 3;
        int m2 = high - (high - low) / 3;
        
        double val1 = calculateMaxDifference(arr, m1);
        double val2 = calculateMaxDifference(arr, m2);
        
        if (val1 < val2) {
            low = m1;
        } else {
            high = m2;
        }
    }
    
    // Check remaining sizes
    double maxValue = 0.0;
    for (int size = low; size <= high; size++) {
        maxValue = max(maxValue, calculateMaxDifference(arr, size));
    }
    
    return maxValue;
}

/*
WHY TERNARY SEARCH WORKS:

Metric Analysis: max - mean
  
  For subset of size k:
    max = largest element (fixed)
    mean = sum / k
  
  To maximize (max - mean):
    Keep max same
    Minimize mean
    
  Minimize mean: Choose smallest elements!

Unimodal Property:
  Size k=1: max - mean = 0
  Size increases: Difference grows (adding small elements)
  Optimal size: Balance point
  Size too large: Difference decreases
  
  Single maximum exists!

Greedy Selection:
  For each size: Take largest + (size-1) smallest
  This minimizes mean while keeping max

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log N) per query
  Sort: O(N log N)
  Ternary search: O(log N \times N log N)

Space: O(N) for sorting

Pattern: "Optimize subset metric" -> Check unimodal, use ternary search
*/

// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 14.20: AtCoder ABC 279D - Freefall
// Link: https://atcoder.jp/contests/abc279/tasks/abc279_d
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1800-1900)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You have a task that takes time A/sqrt(g) + B \times (g-1) to complete, where g
// is the number of "operations" you perform (g >= 1). Find the minimum time
// by choosing optimal g.
//
// Technique: Ternary Search on Continuous Variable
// ==============================================================

double calculateTime(double g, double A, double B) {
    return A / sqrt(g) + B * (g - 1);
}

double minimizeTime(double A, double B) {
    double low = 1.0;
    double high = 1e18;  // Large upper bound
    
    // Ternary search on g
    for (int iter = 0; iter < 200; iter++) {
        double m1 = low + (high - low) / 3.0;
        double m2 = high - (high - low) / 3.0;
        
        double time1 = calculateTime(m1, A, B);
        double time2 = calculateTime(m2, A, B);
        
        if (time1 > time2) {
            low = m1;
        } else {
            high = m2;
        }
    }
    
    return calculateTime(low, A, B);
}

/*
WHY THIS IS UNIMODAL:

Time Function:
  \[
  T(g) = \frac{A}{\sqrt{g}} + B(g - 1)
  \]

  First term: $\dfrac{A}{\sqrt{g}}$
    Decreases as g increases
    Convex decreasing
  
  Second term: B(g-1)
    Increases linearly with g
  
  Combined: U-shaped (convex)
    Has unique minimum!

Calculus Approach:
  T'(g) = -A/(2g^(3/2)) + B = 0
  
  Solve: A/(2g^(3/2)) = B
         g = (A/(2B))^(2/3)
  
  But ternary search finds it automatically!

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(200) = O(1)
Space: O(1)

Pattern: "Minimize with inverse + linear" -> Convex, use ternary search
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Key Recognition:}
\begin{itemize}
\item "Minimize cost with threshold" → Check convexity, use ternary
\item "Sum of distances" → Convex, 2D ternary or Weiszfeld
\item "Find peak in mountain" → Discrete ternary search
\item "Optimize over continuous parameter" → Ternary search

\textbf{CRITICAL: Verify Unimodal Property!}
\begin{itemize}
\item Always check: Single peak/valley
\item Plot mentally: U-shape or ∩-shape
\item If not unimodal: Ternary search fails!
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Implementation Tips:}
\begin{itemize}
\item Continuous: 100-200 iterations (safe)
\item Discrete: While high-low > 2, then check manually
\item 2D: Nested ternary search
\item Always verify result (check nearby values)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Complexity: O(iterations) where iterations ≈ 200!}
\end{tcolorbox}
