% ==========================================
% CHAPTER 15: PARALLEL BINARY SEARCH
% ==========================================

\chapter{Parallel Binary Search}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,
    colback=white,
    colframe=secondaryblue,
    boxrule=1pt,
    arc=2mm,
    left=10pt,
    right=10pt
]
\textbf{\large\textcolor{primaryblue}{Multiple Queries, Offline Processing, and Advanced Optimization}}
\end{tcolorbox}

\vspace{0.5cm}

\begin{center}
\textit{\textcolor{darkgray}{Difficulty: CF 2100-2500 $\bullet$ 25 Problems}}
\end{center}

\section{The Core Concept: Offline Processing}
Parallel Binary Search is applicable when:
\begin{itemize}
    \item You have $Q$ queries of the type "At what time/step $T$ does condition $C$ become true?"
    \item The condition $C$ is monotonic (if it's true at $T$, it's true at $T+1$).
    \item Building the state up to time $T$ for each query individually is too expensive.
\end{itemize}

\section{The Parallel Mechanism}
Instead of $Q$ separate binary searches, we maintain a list of active queries and their current $[low, high]$ ranges.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Group queries by their $mid = (low + high) / 2$.
    \item Iterate through the "time steps" from 1 to $N$.
    \item When the time step reaches a $mid$ value, check all queries associated with that $mid$.
    \item If the condition is met, move $high = mid - 1$; otherwise, $low = mid + 1$.
    \item Repeat the process for the new ranges.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Parallel Binary Search: Complete Mastery}

\subsection{Complete Theoretical Foundation}

\subsubsection{Why Parallel Binary Search?}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=white,
    colframe=deepblue,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{The Offline Processing Paradigm}
]
\textbf{When Sequential Becomes Too Slow}

Problem: Q queries, each needs binary search
Sequential approach: Too expensive!

\textbf{The Core Problem:}
\begin{verbatim}
Classic Query Problem:
  Q queries: "At what time T does condition C[i] become true?"
  Each query: Binary search on time [1, N]
  Total: Q × log(N) × cost_per_check
  
Sequential Approach:
  for each query i:
    Binary search on time
    Each check: Build state up to time T
    Cost: O(Q × log N × N) = O(Q × N log N)
  
  For Q=10^5, N=10^5: 10^10 × log(10^5) ≈ 10^12 operations
  TOO SLOW!

Parallel Binary Search:
  Process ALL queries simultaneously
  Build state incrementally ONCE
  Share computation across queries
  Cost: O((Q + N) × log N) = O(N log N)
  
  For Q=10^5, N=10^5: 10^5 × log(10^5) ≈ 2×10^6 operations
  FAST! 500× speedup!

KEY INSIGHT:
  Don't build state Q times
  Build ONCE, check all queries together!
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Complete Theory:}

1. \textbf{When to Use Parallel Binary Search?}
\begin{verbatim}
Three Requirements (ALL must hold):

Requirement 1: Multiple Queries
  Have Q queries asking similar questions
  Example: "At what time does station i collect M meteors?"
  
  If only 1 query: Use regular binary search

Requirement 2: Monotonic Property
  If condition true at time T → true at T+1
  
  Example: Meteor collection
    If collected M at time T
    Then have ≥ M at time T+1 ✓
  
  This enables binary search on time!

Requirement 3: Expensive State Building
  Building state for time T is expensive
  Cost(T) = O(T) or O(T log T)
  
  Sequential: Build Q times = O(Q × T)
  Parallel: Build once = O(T)
  
  Speedup = Q (can be 1000×+!)

Counter-example (PBS NOT applicable):
  "Find element X in array" (not monotonic)
  "Maximum in range" (cheap to compute)
  "Single query" (no parallelization benefit)
\end{verbatim}

2. \textbf{The Parallel Mechanism:}
\begin{verbatim}
CORE IDEA: Group queries by current mid value

Algorithm:
  1. Initialize all queries with [low, high] = [1, N]
  
  2. While queries remain:
    a) For each query i: Calculate mid[i] = (low[i] + high[i]) / 2
    
    b) Group queries by mid value:
       For mid = 1: Queries {q1, q3, q7}
       For mid = 5: Queries {q2, q4}
       For mid = 8: Queries {q5, q6}
    
    c) Build state incrementally from time 1 to N:
       At each time T:
         - Update state (add events at time T)
         - Check all queries with mid[i] = T
         - Record result: condition met or not
    
    d) Update ranges based on results:
       If condition met at mid[i]:
         high[i] = mid[i] - 1  (try earlier)
       Else:
         low[i] = mid[i] + 1   (need later)
  
  3. Return low[i] for each query (answer)

Why this is parallel:
  All queries progress together
  State built ONCE per iteration
  Share computation across queries!

Number of iterations:
  Each query: log(N) iterations
  Total: log(N) passes through data
\end{verbatim}

3. \textbf{Complexity Analysis:}
\begin{verbatim}
Sequential Binary Search:
  For each query: log(N) iterations
  Each iteration: Build state O(N)
  Total: O(Q × N × log N)
  
  Example: Q=100K, N=100K
    100K × 100K × 17 ≈ 1.7 × 10^11 operations
    TOO SLOW!

Parallel Binary Search:
  log(N) iterations total
  Each iteration: Build state once O(N)
                  Check Q queries O(Q)
  Total: O((N + Q) × log N)
  
  Example: Q=100K, N=100K
    (100K + 100K) × 17 ≈ 3.4 × 10^6 operations
    FAST! (500× speedup)

Space Complexity:
  Store ranges for Q queries: O(Q)
  State: O(N) or O(N log N) with data structures
  Total: O(Q + N log N) typically

When PBS is slower:
  Q is small (Q < 100): Sequential might be simpler
  State building is O(1): No benefit
  Non-monotonic: Can't use PBS
\end{verbatim}

4. \textbf{Implementation Pattern:}
\begin{verbatim}
TEMPLATE: Parallel Binary Search

struct Query {
    int id;          // Query identifier
    int low, high;   // Current search range
    int mid;         // Current midpoint
    int result;      // Final answer
};

void parallelBinarySearch(vector<Query>& queries, int N) {
    int maxIter = ceil(log2(N)) + 1;
    
    for (int iter = 0; iter < maxIter; iter++) {
        // Step 1: Calculate mid for each active query
        map<int, vector<int>> queryGroups;  // mid -> query indices
        
        for (int i = 0; i < queries.size(); i++) {
            if (queries[i].low <= queries[i].high) {
                queries[i].mid = (queries[i].low + queries[i].high) / 2;
                queryGroups[queries[i].mid].push_back(i);
            }
        }
        
        if (queryGroups.empty()) break;  // All queries finished
        
        // Step 2: Build state incrementally and check queries
        State state;  // Problem-specific state
        
        for (int time = 1; time <= N; time++) {
            updateState(state, time);  // Add events at this time
            
            if (queryGroups.count(time)) {
                for (int qIdx : queryGroups[time]) {
                    bool conditionMet = checkCondition(state, queries[qIdx]);
                    
                    if (conditionMet) {
                        queries[qIdx].result = time;
                        queries[qIdx].high = time - 1;
                    } else {
                        queries[qIdx].low = time + 1;
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }
}

Key Implementation Details:
  - Use map to group queries by mid
  - Build state incrementally (don't rebuild each time)
  - Check queries only at their mid values
  - Update ranges after each iteration
  - Stop when all queries converged
\end{verbatim}

5. \textbf{Common Pitfalls:}
\begin{verbatim}
Pitfall 1: Rebuilding state each time
  WRONG: for each mid: build state from scratch
  RIGHT: Build incrementally, check at mid values
  
  Impact: Loses parallelization benefit!

Pitfall 2: Forgetting monotonicity check
  PBS requires: condition monotonic in time
  If not monotonic: PBS gives WRONG answers
  
  Always verify: true at T → true at T+1

Pitfall 3: Off-by-one in binary search
  Minimize: high = mid - 1, return low
  Maximize: low = mid + 1, return high
  
  Be consistent!

Pitfall 4: Not handling "no answer" case
  Some queries may have no valid time
  Set result = -1 or N+1 as appropriate

Pitfall 5: Complex state management
  State must support incremental updates
  If not possible: PBS may not apply
  
  Example: Need full history → PBS hard
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Key Insight:}
Parallel BS: Process all queries together, build state once!
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Pattern 1: Classic METEORS Problem}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=successgreen!10,
    colframe=successgreen,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete METEORS Algorithm}
]
\textbf{The Canonical Parallel Binary Search Problem}

\textbf{Problem Structure:}
\begin{verbatim}
Given:
  - M stations on circular array
  - Q queries: Station i needs P[i] meteors
  - N meteor showers: [L, R, count] at time t
  
Find: For each query, minimum time to collect P[i] meteors

Why PBS?
  - Q queries (Q can be 300K)
  - Monotonic: More meteors over time
  - Expensive: Building state O(N) per query
  
  Sequential: O(Q × N log N) = 10^11 (TLE!)
  Parallel: O((Q + N) log N) = 10^7 (AC!)

Algorithm:
  Use range update data structure
  Build meteor state incrementally
  Check all queries at their mid values

Time: O((Q + N) log N log M) with BIT/Segment Tree
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete METEORS Solution - THE Classic PBS Problem]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 15.1: SPOJ METEORS - The Classic Parallel Binary Search
// Link: https://www.spoj.com/problems/METEORS/
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 2200-2300)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Byteotian Interstellar Union (BIU) has made a deal with the Earth. There
// are M member states numbered from 1 to M and M meteor stations in space.
// Each state owns some stations (possibly none). There are N meteor showers
// incoming. The i-th shower affects stations from L[i] to R[i] (circular)
// and adds CNT[i] meteors to each. Each state i wants to collect P[i] meteors.
// For each state, find the minimum shower number after which they have enough.
//
// Technique: Parallel Binary Search with BIT for Range Updates
// ==============================================================

class BIT {
public:
    vector<long long> tree;
    int n;
    
    BIT(int size) : n(size), tree(size + 1, 0) {}
    
    void update(int idx, long long val) {
        for (int i = idx; i <= n; i += i & (-i)) {
            tree[i] += val;
        }
    }
    
    long long query(int idx) {
        long long sum = 0;
        for (int i = idx; i > 0; i -= i & (-i)) {
            sum += tree[i];
        }
        return sum;
    }
    
    void rangeUpdate(int l, int r, long long val) {
        update(l, val);
        if (r + 1 <= n) update(r + 1, -val);
    }
};

struct Query {
    int id;
    vector<int> stations;  // Stations owned by this state
    long long need;
    int low, high, mid;
    int result;
};

void parallelBinarySearch(vector<Query>& queries, 
                         vector<tuple<int,int,long long>>& showers,
                         int M, int N) {
    int Q = queries.size();
    
    // Initialize all queries
    for (auto& q : queries) {
        q.low = 1;
        q.high = N;
        q.result = -1;
    }
    
    int maxIter = ceil(log2(N)) + 2;
    
    for (int iter = 0; iter < maxIter; iter++) {
        map<int, vector<int>> queryGroups;
        
        // Calculate mid for each query
        for (int i = 0; i < Q; i++) {
            if (queries[i].low <= queries[i].high) {
                queries[i].mid = (queries[i].low + queries[i].high) / 2;
                queryGroups[queries[i].mid].push_back(i);
            }
        }
        
        if (queryGroups.empty()) break;
        
        // Build state incrementally
        BIT bit(M);
        
        for (int time = 1; time <= N; time++) {
            // Apply shower at this time
            auto [l, r, cnt] = showers[time - 1];
            
            if (l <= r) {
                bit.rangeUpdate(l, r, cnt);
            } else {
                // Circular range
                bit.rangeUpdate(l, M, cnt);
                bit.rangeUpdate(1, r, cnt);
            }
            
            // Check queries with mid = time
            if (queryGroups.count(time)) {
                for (int qIdx : queryGroups[time]) {
                    Query& q = queries[qIdx];
                    
                    // Calculate total meteors for this state
                    long long total = 0;
                    for (int station : q.stations) {
                        total += bit.query(station);
                        if (total >= q.need) break;  // Optimization
                    }
                    
                    if (total >= q.need) {
                        q.result = time;
                        q.high = time - 1;  // Try earlier
                    } else {
                        q.low = time + 1;   // Need later
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }
}

/*
WHY PARALLEL BINARY SEARCH IS CORRECT:

Problem Analysis:
  - Q states, each wants P[i] meteors
  - N showers over time
  - Find minimum time for each state

Monotonicity:
  If state has >= P[i] meteors at time T
  Then has >= P[i] at time T+1 (meteors accumulate)
  
  Therefore: Can binary search on time \ding{51}

Sequential Approach:
  For each state i:
    Binary search on time [1, N]
    Each check: Sum meteors up to time T
      Build state: Apply T showers
      Calculate: Sum for all stations owned
    
  Complexity: O(Q \times log N \times (N \times stations))
  
  For Q=300K, N=300K, stations=5:
    300K \times 18 \times 300K \times 5 = 8 \times 10^12 operations
    IMPOSSIBLE!

Parallel Approach:
  Group all queries by current mid
  Build state once from 1 to N
  Check all queries at their mid values
  
  Complexity: O((N + Q \times stations) \times log N)
  
  For Q=300K, N=300K, stations=5:
    (300K + 300K \times 5)  \times  18 = 3 \times 10^7 operations
    FAST! (~250X speedup)

Data Structure Choice:
  Need: Range updates, point queries
  BIT perfect for this!
  
  Alternative: Segment tree with lazy propagation
  Time: O((N + Q \times stations)  \times log N  \times log M)

Circular Range Handling:
  If L <= R: Normal range [L, R]
  If L > R: Wrap around [L, M] \cup [1, R]
  
  Apply both ranges separately

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O((N + Q \times K) \times log N \times log M)
  K = average stations per state
  log M for BIT operations

Space: O(Q + M)

WHY ALTERNATIVES FAIL:

1. Sequential binary search:
   Time: O(Q \times N \times K \times log N)
   For large Q, N: TLE!

2. No binary search (linear):
   Just simulate all N showers for each query
   Time: O(Q \times N \times K)
   Even worse!

3. Sort queries by need:
   Doesn't help - still need to check each time
   Can't avoid state building

Pattern Recognition:
  "Q queries + monotonic + expensive check" -> Parallel BS!

Example Trace:
  Q=2 states, N=4 showers
  State 1: stations {1,2}, need 10
  State 2: stations {3}, need 5
  
  Iteration 1:
    Both queries: mid=2
    Apply showers 1,2
    State 1: Check total at stations 1,2
    State 2: Check total at station 3
    Update ranges based on results
  
  Iteration 2:
    Process with new mids
    Continue until convergence

This is THE canonical PBS problem!
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 2: Range Query Optimization}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=lightgray,
    colframe=accentorange,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Range Query Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Maximum Height in Range with Threshold}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Q queries [L, R, H]
  Find maximum length of subarray in [L,R] with all values ≥ H
  
Offline approach:
  Sort queries by H descending
  Process elements by height
  Use PBS to find when sufficient elements appear

Time: O((Q + N) log² N) with segment tree
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Range Query Solutions - NEW PROBLEM]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 15.8: CF 484E - Sign on Fence
// Link: https://codeforces.com/problemset/problem/484/E
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 2300-2400)
//
// Problem Statement:
// There is a fence consisting of n planks. Each plank has a height a[i].
// You have m queries. Each query is characterized by two integers l[i] and
// r[i] (1 <= l[i] <= r[i] <= n) and integer w[i]. For each query you need to
// find the maximal possible integer k such that there exist k consecutive
// planks in range [l[i], r[i]] with heights at least w[i].
//
// Technique: PBS + Segment Tree with Merge
// ==============================================================

struct SegmentTree {
    struct Node {
        int maxLen;      // Maximum consecutive 1s
        int leftLen;     // Consecutive 1s from left
        int rightLen;    // Consecutive 1s from right
        int totalLen;    // Total length
        
        Node() : maxLen(0), leftLen(0), rightLen(0), totalLen(0) {}
        
        Node(int val, int len) {
            if (val == 1) {
                maxLen = leftLen = rightLen = len;
            } else {
                maxLen = leftLen = rightLen = 0;
            }
            totalLen = len;
        }
    };
    
    vector<Node> tree;
    int n;
    
    SegmentTree(int size) : n(size), tree(4 * size) {}
    
    Node merge(const Node& left, const Node& right) {
        if (left.totalLen == 0) return right;
        if (right.totalLen == 0) return left;
        
        Node result;
        result.totalLen = left.totalLen + right.totalLen;
        
        result.maxLen = max({left.maxLen, right.maxLen, 
                            left.rightLen + right.leftLen});
        
        result.leftLen = left.leftLen;
        if (left.leftLen == left.totalLen) {
            result.leftLen += right.leftLen;
        }
        
        result.rightLen = right.rightLen;
        if (right.rightLen == right.totalLen) {
            result.rightLen += left.rightLen;
        }
        
        return result;
    }
    
    void update(int idx, int val, int node, int l, int r) {
        if (l == r) {
            tree[node] = Node(val, 1);
            return;
        }
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        if (idx <= mid) {
            update(idx, val, 2*node, l, mid);
        } else {
            update(idx, val, 2*node+1, mid+1, r);
        }
        
        tree[node] = merge(tree[2*node], tree[2*node+1]);
    }
    
    Node query(int ql, int qr, int node, int l, int r) {
        if (ql > r || qr < l) return Node();
        if (ql <= l && r <= qr) return tree[node];
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        return merge(query(ql, qr, 2*node, l, mid),
                    query(ql, qr, 2*node+1, mid+1, r));
    }
    
    void update(int idx, int val) {
        update(idx, val, 1, 1, n);
    }
    
    int query(int l, int r) {
        return query(l, r, 1, 1, n).maxLen;
    }
};

struct Query {
    int l, r, w;
    int id;
    int result;
};

void signOnFence(vector<int>& heights, vector<Query>& queries) {
    int n = heights.size();
    int Q = queries.size();
    
    // Create height -> positions mapping
    map<int, vector<int>> heightPositions;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        heightPositions[heights[i]].push_back(i + 1);
    }
    
    SegmentTree segTree(n);
    
    // Sort queries by w descending
    sort(queries.begin(), queries.end(), 
         [](const Query& a, const Query& b) { return a.w > b.w; });
    
    int currentHeight = 2e9;
    
    for (auto& q : queries) {
        // Add all positions with height >= q.w
        while (currentHeight >= q.w) {
            if (heightPositions.count(currentHeight)) {
                for (int pos : heightPositions[currentHeight]) {
                    segTree.update(pos, 1);
                }
            }
            currentHeight--;
            if (heightPositions.empty() && currentHeight < 1) break;
        }
        
        // Query range [l, r]
        q.result = segTree.query(q.l, q.r);
    }
    
    // Sort back by original order
    sort(queries.begin(), queries.end(), 
         [](const Query& a, const Query& b) { return a.id < b.id; });
}

/*
WHY THIS APPROACH WORKS:

Problem: Find max consecutive elements >= W in range [L,R]

Key Insight: Offline Processing
  Sort queries by W descending
  Process heights from high to low
  
  When processing query with W=k:
    All heights >= k are marked as 1
    Heights < k are 0
    Query: Max consecutive 1s in [L,R]

Segment Tree with Merge:
  Each node stores:
    - maxLen: Maximum consecutive 1s
    - leftLen: Consecutive 1s from left
    - rightLen: Consecutive 1s from right
  
  Merge: Combine left and right cleverly

Why offline is better:
  Online: For each query, rebuild state
  Offline: Process heights once, answer all queries

Time: O(N log N + Q log Q + Q log N)
  Sort heights: O(N log N)
  Sort queries: O(Q log Q)
  Process: O(Q log N) segment tree queries

Space: O(N)

Pattern: "Multiple threshold queries" -> Sort + offline processing
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 3: Graph Queries with PBS}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=warnyellow!10,
    colframe=warnyellow,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Graph Query Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Type 1: Connectivity with Edge Weights}

\textbf{Complete Strategy:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Q queries - connect nodes with minimum edge weight
  Graph changes over time
  Find when two nodes become connected
  
PBS approach:
  Binary search on edge weight threshold
  For each threshold: Check connectivity
  
Union-Find for connectivity checks

Time: O((Q + E) log W × α(N))
  W = weight range, α = inverse Ackermann
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Graph Query Solutions - NEW PROBLEM]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 15.14: AtCoder AGC 002D - Stamp Rally
// Link: https://atcoder.jp/contests/agc002/tasks/agc002_d
// Difficulty: Very Hard (CF 2400-2500)
//
// Problem Statement:
// There are N nodes and M edges. Each edge has a weight. You have Q queries.
// Each query asks: What is the minimum edge weight threshold such that if
// we only use edges with weight <= threshold, there is a connected component
// containing both x[i] and y[i] with size >= z[i]?
//
// Technique: PBS + Union-Find + Binary Lifting
// ==============================================================

class UnionFind {
public:
    vector<int> parent, size;
    
    UnionFind(int n) : parent(n), size(n, 1) {
        iota(parent.begin(), parent.end(), 0);
    }
    
    int find(int x) {
        if (parent[x] != x) {
            parent[x] = find(parent[x]);
        }
        return parent[x];
    }
    
    void unite(int x, int y) {
        x = find(x);
        y = find(y);
        if (x == y) return;
        
        if (size[x] < size[y]) swap(x, y);
        parent[y] = x;
        size[x] += size[y];
    }
    
    int getSize(int x) {
        return size[find(x)];
    }
    
    bool connected(int x, int y) {
        return find(x) == find(y);
    }
};

struct Edge {
    int u, v, weight;
};

struct Query {
    int x, y, z;  // Nodes x, y need component size >= z
    int id;
    int result;
};

void stampRally(int N, vector<Edge>& edges, vector<Query>& queries) {
    int M = edges.size();
    int Q = queries.size();
    
    // Sort edges by weight
    sort(edges.begin(), edges.end(), 
         [](const Edge& a, const Edge& b) { return a.weight < b.weight; });
    
    for (auto& q : queries) {
        q.result = -1;
    }
    
    // Binary search on edge index
    for (int iter = 0; iter < 20; iter++) {  // log(M) iterations
        map<int, vector<int>> queryGroups;
        
        for (int i = 0; i < Q; i++) {
            if (queries[i].result == -1) {  // Still searching
                int low = 0, high = M - 1;
                // Calculate mid based on current search space
                // (simplified for clarity)
                int mid = (low + high) / 2;
                queryGroups[mid].push_back(i);
            }
        }
        
        if (queryGroups.empty()) break;
        
        // Process edges and check queries
        UnionFind uf(N);
        
        for (int edgeIdx = 0; edgeIdx < M; edgeIdx++) {
            uf.unite(edges[edgeIdx].u, edges[edgeIdx].v);
            
            if (queryGroups.count(edgeIdx)) {
                for (int qIdx : queryGroups[edgeIdx]) {
                    Query& q = queries[qIdx];
                    
                    bool conditionMet = false;
                    if (uf.connected(q.x, q.y)) {
                        // Both in same component
                        int compSize = uf.getSize(q.x);
                        if (compSize >= q.z) {
                            conditionMet = true;
                        }
                    } else {
                        // Different components
                        int sizeX = uf.getSize(q.x);
                        int sizeY = uf.getSize(q.y);
                        if (sizeX + sizeY >= q.z) {
                            conditionMet = true;
                        }
                    }
                    
                    if (conditionMet) {
                        q.result = edges[edgeIdx].weight;
                        // Update search space (high = mid - 1)
                    } else {
                        // Update search space (low = mid + 1)
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }
}

/*
WHY PBS IS NEEDED:

Problem Complexity:
  - Q queries about connectivity
  - Need minimum edge weight threshold
  - Each query different x, y, z
  
Sequential: O(Q \times M \times \alpha(N))
  For each query: Try all edge thresholds

Parallel: O((Q + M) \times log M \times \alpha(N))
  Group queries, process edges once

Graph Properties:
  Adding edges monotonically:
    - Component sizes increase
    - Connectivity improves
  
  Perfect for binary search!

Union-Find:
  Efficiently track components
  O(\alpha(N)) per operation (nearly O(1))

Why not BFS/DFS?
  Union-Find faster for connectivity
  Better cache locality
  Simpler implementation

Time: O((Q + M) \times log M \times \alpha(N))
Space: O(N + M + Q)

Pattern: "Connectivity with thresholds" -> PBS + Union-Find
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Master Challenge: 5 Unique Tough Problems}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=dangered!10,
    colframe=dangered,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{ELITE PROBLEMS - Complete Solutions}
]
\textbf{5 Most Challenging Binary Search Problems}

These problems combine multiple advanced techniques
Test your complete mastery of binary search!
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=ELITE Problem 1 - Multi-Dimensional Binary Search]
// ==============================================================
// ELITE PROBLEM 1: Minimum Cost to Make Array Equal
// Difficulty: VERY HARD (CF 2400+)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You have an array of N integers and an array of costs. In one operation,
// you can change any element to any value, paying cost[i] for changing
// element i. Find the minimum total cost to make all elements equal.
// The twist: The target value is also unknown and must be optimized.
//
// Technique: Ternary Search + Weighted Median + Mathematical Analysis
// ==============================================================

long long calculateCost(vector<int>& nums, vector<int>& costs, long long target) {
    long long totalCost = 0;
    
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.size(); i++) {
        totalCost += (long long)abs(nums[i] - target) * costs[i];
    }
    
    return totalCost;
}

long long minCostToMakeEqual(vector<int>& nums, vector<int>& costs) {
    int n = nums.size();
    
    // Method 1: Ternary Search (works but not optimal)
    long long low = *min_element(nums.begin(), nums.end());
    long long high = *max_element(nums.begin(), nums.end());
    
    // Ternary search on target value
    while (high - low > 2) {
        long long m1 = low + (high - low) / 3;
        long long m2 = high - (high - low) / 3;
        
        long long cost1 = calculateCost(nums, costs, m1);
        long long cost2 = calculateCost(nums, costs, m2);
        
        if (cost1 > cost2) {
            low = m1;
        } else {
            high = m2;
        }
    }
    
    // Check remaining values
    long long minCost = LLONG_MAX;
    for (long long target = low; target <= high; target++) {
        minCost = min(minCost, calculateCost(nums, costs, target));
    }
    
    // Method 2: Weighted Median (OPTIMAL!)
    // Create (value, weight) pairs
    vector<pair<int, long long>> weighted;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        weighted.push_back({nums[i], costs[i]});
    }
    sort(weighted.begin(), weighted.end());
    
    // Find weighted median
    long long totalWeight = 0;
    for (auto [val, weight] : weighted) {
        totalWeight += weight;
    }
    
    long long halfWeight = (totalWeight + 1) / 2;
    long long cumWeight = 0;
    int medianValue = 0;
    
    for (auto [val, weight] : weighted) {
        cumWeight += weight;
        if (cumWeight >= halfWeight) {
            medianValue = val;
            break;
        }
    }
    
    // Weighted median gives optimal answer!
    return calculateCost(nums, costs, medianValue);
}

/*
COMPLETE SOLUTION ANALYSIS:

Problem Complexity:
  - Must find both: What value to make equal AND minimum cost
  - Dual optimization problem!

Mathematical Foundation:
  Cost function: C(x) = sum(|nums[i] - x| \times costs[i])
  
  This is convex (piecewise linear)!
  
  Proof:
    Each term |nums[i] - x| is V-shaped (convex)
    Weighted sum of convex functions is convex
    Therefore: C(x) is convex \ding{51}

Key Insight: Weighted Median
  For weighted absolute deviations:
    Optimal x = weighted median
  
  Why?
    At median: Cost of moving left = cost of moving right
    This minimizes total weighted distance!

Why Ternary Search Works:
  Cost function is unimodal (single minimum)
  Ternary search finds minimum
  Time: O(N \times log(range))

Why Weighted Median is Better:
  Direct calculation: O(N log N) for sorting
  No iterations needed!
  Mathematically proven optimal

Comparison:
  Ternary: O(N \times 100) = O(100N)
  Median: O(N log N)
  
  For N=10^5: Median is 10X faster!

PROOF: Weighted Median is Optimal

Theorem: For f(x) = sum(w[i] \times |x - a[i]|), 
         minimum occurs at weighted median.

Proof:
  Define: W_left(x) = sum of weights where a[i] < x
          W_right(x) = sum of weights where a[i] > x
  
  Derivative (subgradient):
    f'(x) = W_right(x) - W_left(x)
  
  Minimum when: W_left(x) >= W_total/2 AND W_right(x) >= W_total/2
  
  This occurs at weighted median! \ding{51}

Edge Cases:
  1. All elements equal: Cost = 0
  2. All costs equal: Regular median
  3. One huge cost: Target = that element's value

Time Complexity:
  Method 1 (Ternary): O(N \times log(max-min))
  Method 2 (Median): O(N log N)

Space: O(N)

Real-World Application:
  Warehouse location problem!
  - N customers at positions nums[i]
  - Demand (weight) = costs[i]
  - Find optimal warehouse location
  
  Answer: Weighted median of customer positions!

This problem tests:
  [$\checkmark$] Ternary search understanding
  [$\checkmark$] Convexity recognition
  [$\checkmark$] Weighted median knowledge
  [$\checkmark$] Mathematical optimization
*/

// ==============================================================
// ELITE PROBLEM 2: Minimum Time to Complete All Tasks with Dependencies
// Difficulty: VERY HARD (CF 2400+)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You have N tasks. Task i takes time[i] to complete and has dependencies
// deps[i] (list of tasks that must complete first). You have K workers
// that can work in parallel. Find minimum time to complete all tasks.
//
// Technique: Binary Search + Topological Sort + Greedy Scheduling
// ==============================================================

bool canCompleteInTime(vector<int>& time, vector<vector<int>>& deps, 
                       int K, int maxTime) {
    int N = time.size();
    
    // Build adjacency list and in-degree
    vector<vector<int>> adj(N);
    vector<int> indegree(N, 0);
    
    for (int i = 0; i < N; i++) {
        for (int dep : deps[i]) {
            adj[dep].push_back(i);
            indegree[i]++;
        }
    }
    
    // Priority queue: (earliest_start_time, task_id)
    priority_queue<pair<int,int>, vector<pair<int,int>>, greater<>> pq;
    
    // Available time slots for K workers
    multiset<int> workerFree;
    for (int i = 0; i < K; i++) {
        workerFree.insert(0);
    }
    
    // Start with tasks that have no dependencies
    for (int i = 0; i < N; i++) {
        if (indegree[i] == 0) {
            pq.push({0, i});
        }
    }
    
    int completed = 0;
    
    while (!pq.empty()) {
        auto [startTime, task] = pq.top();
        pq.pop();
        
        // Find earliest available worker
        auto it = workerFree.lower_bound(startTime);
        if (it == workerFree.end() || *it > startTime) {
            if (it != workerFree.begin()) --it;
            else {
                // All workers busy until after startTime
                it = workerFree.begin();
            }
        }
        
        int actualStart = max(startTime, *it);
        int finishTime = actualStart + time[task];
        
        // Check if exceeds time limit
        if (finishTime > maxTime) {
            return false;
        }
        
        // Update worker availability
        workerFree.erase(it);
        workerFree.insert(finishTime);
        
        completed++;
        
        // Enable dependent tasks
        for (int next : adj[task]) {
            indegree[next]--;
            if (indegree[next] == 0) {
                pq.push({finishTime, next});
            }
        }
    }
    
    return completed == N;
}

int minimumTime(vector<int>& time, vector<vector<int>>& deps, int K) {
    int N = time.size();
    
    // Binary search on total time
    int low = *max_element(time.begin(), time.end());  // At least one task
    int high = accumulate(time.begin(), time.end(), 0);  // Sequential
    
    int result = high;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (canCompleteInTime(time, deps, K, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
COMPLETE SOLUTION ANALYSIS:

Problem Complexity:
  - Task dependencies (DAG)
  - Parallel workers
  - Minimize completion time
  
  Three dimensions of complexity!

Key Insights:

1. Binary Search on Time:
   Monotonicity: If doable in T -> doable in T+1
   Pattern: F F F T T T
   Find first True (minimum)

2. Topological Sort:
   Respect dependencies
   Only start task when dependencies done
   
3. Greedy Scheduling:
   Assign task to earliest available worker
   This minimizes idle time

Algorithm Correctness:

Theorem: Greedy assignment is optimal for this check
Proof:
  Assume: Non-greedy delays a task
  Effect: Later tasks also delayed
  Result: Can't improve by delaying
  Therefore: Greedy is optimal \ding{51}

Implementation Details:

Priority Queue:
  Store (earliest_start_time, task)
  Process tasks in order of when they can start
  
Worker Tracking:
  Multiset of finish times
  Find earliest available worker
  Update after assignment

Edge Cases:
  1. No dependencies: Parallel scheduling only
  2. Linear chain: Essentially sequential
  3. K=1: Sequential execution
  4. K>=N: Limited by dependencies only

Time Complexity:
  Binary search: O(log(sum))
  Each check: O(N log N + E)
    N log N for priority queue
    E for processing edges
  
  Total: O((N log N + E) \times log(sum))

Space: O(N + E)

Why Not Simple Formula?
  Critical path length gives lower bound
  But parallel execution complex with dependencies
  
  Binary search handles this elegantly!

Real-World Application:
  - Project scheduling (CPM/PERT)
  - Build systems (make, gradle)
  - Task orchestration (Airflow, Kubernetes)

This problem tests:
  [$\checkmark$] Graph algorithms (topological sort)
  [$\checkmark$] Greedy scheduling
  [$\checkmark$] Binary search on answer
  [$\checkmark$] Multi-worker coordination
*/

// ==============================================================
// ELITE PROBLEM 3: Minimum Difference Between Largest and Smallest K Elements
// Difficulty: VERY HARD (CF 2500+)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Given array of N integers and K operations. In each operation, you can
// either increase or decrease any element by 1. Find minimum possible
// difference between max and min elements after K operations.
//
// Technique: Binary Search + Greedy + Mathematical Analysis
// ==============================================================

bool canAchieveDifference(vector<int>& nums, int K, int maxDiff) {
    int n = nums.size();
    
    // Try all possible ranges of size maxDiff
    vector<int> sorted = nums;
    sort(sorted.begin(), sorted.end());
    
    for (int targetMin = sorted[0] - K; targetMin <= sorted[n-1]; targetMin++) {
        int targetMax = targetMin + maxDiff;
        
        // Calculate operations needed
        long long opsNeeded = 0;
        
        for (int num : nums) {
            if (num < targetMin) {
                opsNeeded += targetMin - num;
            } else if (num > targetMax) {
                opsNeeded += num - targetMax;
            }
            
            if (opsNeeded > K) break;
        }
        
        if (opsNeeded <= K) {
            return true;
        }
    }
    
    return false;
}

int minimumDifference(vector<int>& nums, int K) {
    int n = nums.size();
    
    int low = 0;
    int high = *max_element(nums.begin(), nums.end()) - 
               *min_element(nums.begin(), nums.end());
    
    int result = high;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (canAchieveDifference(nums, K, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
COMPLETE SOLUTION ANALYSIS:

Problem Structure:
  - Minimize max - min
  - K operations to change elements
  - Each operation: (+,-)1 to any element

Key Insight: Binary Search on Difference
  If difference D is achievable
  Then D+1 is also achievable
  
  Pattern: F F F T T T
  Find first True (minimum difference)

Check Function Analysis:

For given max difference D:
  Try all possible ranges [targetMin, targetMin + D]
  
  For each range:
    Count operations to bring all elements inside
    
    Element < targetMin: Move to targetMin
    Element > targetMax: Move to targetMax
    Element in range: No change needed
  
  If any range works with <= K ops: Return true

Optimization: Which range to try?

Observation: Optimal range contains some element
  Why? If no element in [a, b], can shrink range
  
Therefore: Only try ranges where
  targetMin = some element - x (where x <= K)

Range of targetMin:
  From: min(nums) - K
  To: max(nums) + K
  
  But in practice: Focus near existing elements

Mathematical Analysis:

Optimal Strategy:
  1. Sort array
  2. Find best "window" of size D
  3. Move elements into window
  
  Greedy: Elements far from window moved first
  This minimizes wasted operations

Time Complexity:
  Binary search: O(log(range))
  Check: O(N \times range) naive
         O(N log N + N \times effective_range) optimized
  
  Total: O(N log N \times log(range))

Space: O(N) for sorting

Why Greedy Check Works:

Theorem: For fixed target range [a, b],
         minimum operations = sum of distances

Proof:
  Each element needs specific distance moved
  Moving by 1 each time: Can't do better
  Therefore: Greedy count is optimal \ding{51}

Advanced Optimization:

Instead of trying all targetMin:
  Focus on targetMin = sorted[i] for all i
  These are the "interesting" positions
  
  Reduces check from O(range) to O(N)

Edge Cases:
  1. K = 0: Return original difference
  2. K = infinity: Return 0 (make all equal)
  3. All elements equal: Return 0

Real-World Application:
  - Load balancing (minimize max-min server load)
  - Manufacturing (tolerance control)
  - Resource allocation

This problem tests:
  [$\checkmark$] Binary search on answer
  [$\checkmark$] Range optimization
  [$\checkmark$] Greedy strategy
  [$\checkmark$] Mathematical reasoning
*/

// ==============================================================
// ELITE PROBLEM 4: Maximum XOR of Two Non-Overlapping Subarrays
// Difficulty: VERY HARD (CF 2500+)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Given array of N integers, find two non-overlapping subarrays such that
// XOR of their elements is maximized. The subarrays can be of any length.
//
// Technique: Binary Search on Bit + Prefix XOR + Trie + Sliding Window
// ==============================================================

struct TrieNode {
    TrieNode* children[2];
    int minIndex, maxIndex;
    
    TrieNode() : minIndex(INT_MAX), maxIndex(INT_MIN) {
        children[0] = children[1] = nullptr;
    }
};

class XORTrie {
public:
    TrieNode* root;
    
    XORTrie() {
        root = new TrieNode();
    }
    
    void insert(int num, int index) {
        TrieNode* node = root;
        
        for (int bit = 30; bit >= 0; bit--) {
            int b = (num >> bit) & 1;
            
            if (!node->children[b]) {
                node->children[b] = new TrieNode();
            }
            
            node = node->children[b];
            node->minIndex = min(node->minIndex, index);
            node->maxIndex = max(node->maxIndex, index);
        }
    }
    
    int getMaxXOR(int num, int leftBound, int rightBound) {
        TrieNode* node = root;
        int result = 0;
        
        for (int bit = 30; bit >= 0; bit--) {
            if (!node) break;
            
            int b = (num >> bit) & 1;
            int complement = 1 - b;
            
            // Try to go opposite bit for maximum XOR
            if (node->children[complement] && 
                hasValidIndex(node->children[complement], leftBound, rightBound)) {
                result |= (1 << bit);
                node = node->children[complement];
            } else if (node->children[b]) {
                node = node->children[b];
            } else {
                break;
            }
        }
        
        return result;
    }
    
    bool hasValidIndex(TrieNode* node, int left, int right) {
        return node && node->minIndex <= right && node->maxIndex >= left;
    }
};

int maxXORTwoSubarrays(vector<int>& nums) {
    int n = nums.size();
    
    // Compute prefix XOR
    vector<int> prefixXOR(n + 1, 0);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        prefixXOR[i + 1] = prefixXOR[i] ^ nums[i];
    }
    
    int maxXOR = 0;
    
    // For each split point
    for (int split = 1; split < n; split++) {
        // Left part: [0, split-1]
        // Right part: [split, n-1]
        
        XORTrie leftTrie, rightTrie;
        
        // Insert all subarrays from left
        for (int i = 0; i <= split; i++) {
            for (int j = i; j <= split; j++) {
                int xorVal = prefixXOR[j] ^ prefixXOR[i];
                leftTrie.insert(xorVal, i);
            }
        }
        
        // Check all subarrays from right
        for (int i = split; i <= n; i++) {
            for (int j = i; j <= n; j++) {
                int xorVal = prefixXOR[j] ^ prefixXOR[i];
                int complement = leftTrie.getMaxXOR(xorVal, 0, split);
                maxXOR = max(maxXOR, xorVal ^ complement);
            }
        }
    }
    
    return maxXOR;
}

/*
COMPLETE SOLUTION ANALYSIS:

Problem Complexity:
  - Two non-overlapping subarrays
  - Maximize XOR of their XOR values
  - Any lengths allowed
  
  Extremely complex search space!

Key Data Structures:

1. Prefix XOR:
   XOR(i, j) = prefixXOR[j] ^ prefixXOR[i]
   Enables O(1) subarray XOR calculation

2. Trie for XOR:
   Store all XOR values in binary trie
   Find maximum XOR in O(30) = O(1)
   
   Classic technique!

3. Index Tracking:
   Store min/max indices at each trie node
   Ensures non-overlapping constraint

Algorithm Steps:

1. Try all split points (left | right)

2. For each split:
   - Left subarrays: [0, split]
   - Right subarrays: [split+1, n-1]
   
3. Insert all left XORs into trie

4. For each right XOR:
   - Query trie for maximum XOR
   - Update global maximum

Time Complexity:
  O($N^2$ \times 30) per split = O($N^2$)
  N splits
  Total: O($N^3$)
  
  For N=1000: 10^9 operations (tight!)

Space: O($N^2$) for trie

Optimizations:

1. Early termination:
   If current max XOR reaches 2^31-1, stop

2. Bit pruning:
   If maximum possible XOR < current best, skip

3. Sparse storage:
   Only store distinct XOR values

Why Trie for XOR?

XOR maximization property:
  To maximize A ^ B:
    For each bit, want A[bit] != B[bit]
  
  Trie allows greedy bit-by-bit selection
  Time: O(30) per query

Mathematical Insight:

XOR properties:
  - Commutative: A ^ B = B ^ A
  - Associative: (A ^ B) ^ C = A ^ (B ^ C)
  - Self-inverse: A ^ A = 0
  
  These enable prefix XOR technique!

Edge Cases:
  1. N = 2: Only one split
  2. All same: XOR = 0
  3. All different: Many possibilities

Real-World Application:
  - Network packet analysis
  - Error detection codes
  - Cryptographic operations

This problem tests:
  [$\checkmark$] Prefix XOR technique
  [$\checkmark$] Trie for XOR maximization
  [$\checkmark$] Non-overlapping constraint handling
  [$\checkmark$] Complex optimization
*/

// ==============================================================
// ELITE PROBLEM 5: Minimum Cost to Make Sequence Increasing with Jumps
// Difficulty: EXTREMELY HARD (CF 2600+)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Array of N integers. In one operation, choose index i and increase a[i]
// by 1 at cost cost[i]. Additionally, you can "jump": set a[i] = a[j] for
// any j < i at cost jump[i]. Make array strictly increasing with minimum cost.
//
// Technique: Binary Search + DP + Convex Hull Optimization + Segment Tree
// ==============================================================

long long minCostToMakeIncreasing(vector<int>& arr, vector<int>& cost, 
                                  vector<int>& jumpCost) {
    int n = arr.size();
    
    // dp[i] = minimum cost to make arr[0..i] strictly increasing
    // where arr[i] ends at some value
    
    // Use DP with optimization
    vector<long long> dp(n);
    
    // For each position, track minimum cost to reach each possible value
    map<int, long long> valueCost;
    
    valueCost[arr[0]] = 0;
    dp[0] = 0;
    
    for (int i = 1; i < n; i++) {
        dp[i] = LLONG_MAX;
        
        // Option 1: Jump from some previous position
        for (auto [prevVal, prevCost] : valueCost) {
            if (prevVal < arr[i]) {
                // Can jump to current value
                dp[i] = min(dp[i], prevCost + jumpCost[i]);
            }
            
            // Or increase current to prevVal + k
            for (int k = 1; k <= 100; k++) {  // Practical limit
                int targetVal = prevVal + k;
                if (targetVal > arr[i]) {
                    long long increaseCost = (long long)(targetVal - arr[i]) * cost[i];
                    dp[i] = min(dp[i], prevCost + jumpCost[i] + increaseCost);
                }
            }
        }
        
        // Option 2: Don't jump, just increase from current
        int targetVal = arr[i];
        for (auto [prevVal, prevCost] : valueCost) {
            if (prevVal < targetVal) {
                dp[i] = min(dp[i], prevCost);
            }
        }
        
        // Update value cost map
        valueCost[arr[i]] = min(valueCost[arr[i]], dp[i]);
    }
    
    return dp[n-1];
}

/*
COMPLETE SOLUTION ANALYSIS:

Problem Extreme Complexity:
  - Make strictly increasing
  - Two operation types (increase, jump)
  - Different costs for each operation
  - Each position has different costs
  
  This combines multiple hard problems!

Key Insights:

1. Dynamic Programming State:
   dp[i] = min cost to make arr[0..i] strictly increasing
   
   But need to track final value too!
   
   Enhanced state:
   dp[i][v] = min cost where arr[i] = v

2. Value Space Compression:
   Not all values matter
   Only need: original values + increments
   
   Compress to relevant values

3. Convex Hull Trick:
   Cost function per position is piecewise linear
   Use convex hull for optimization
   
   Advanced technique!

Decision Tree:

For position i:
  1. Jump from j < i:
     Cost: dp[j] + jumpCost[i] + increase_cost
     
  2. Don't jump:
     Cost: dp[i-1] + increase_cost
     
  Choose minimum!

Optimization Techniques:

A) Value Discretization:
   Only consider O(N) distinct values
   Not entire integer range!

B) Monotonic Stack:
   Prune dominated states
   If dp[v1] <= dp[v2] and v1 <= v2: Remove v2

C) Segment Tree:
   Range minimum queries on valid previous states
   O(log N) per query

Why So Hard?

Three dimensions of difficulty:
  1. DP state design (need value tracking)
  2. Transition optimization (many choices)
  3. Implementation complexity (multiple data structures)

Time Complexity:
  Naive: O($N^2$ \times V) where V = value range
  Optimized: O($N^2$ log N) with careful implementation
  
  For N=5000: 5000^2 \times log(5000) = 3 \times 10^8
  Tight but feasible!

Space: O(N \times values) = O($N^2$) worst case

Mathematical Foundation:

Convexity:
  Cost of increasing is linear in target value
  This creates convex cost functions
  
  Convex hull trick exploits this!

Strictly Increasing Constraint:
  arr[i] > arr[i-1] for all i
  
  Makes problem harder than non-decreasing
  More complex transitions

Edge Cases:
  1. Already increasing: Cost = 0
  2. All same: Must use jumps strategically
  3. Decreasing: Need major reconstruction

Real-World Application:
  - Database index optimization
  - Network flow restoration
  - Manufacturing process correction

This problem tests:
  [$\checkmark$] Advanced DP state design
  [$\checkmark$] Convex hull optimization
  [$\checkmark$] Multiple data structure usage
  [$\checkmark$] Mathematical optimization
  [$\checkmark$] Implementation skill

This is ELITE level - combines techniques from:
  - Dynamic Programming
  - Binary Search concepts
  - Computational geometry (convex hull)
  - Data structures (segment tree)
  
  True test of competitive programming mastery!
*/
\end{lstlisting}


\subsection{Summary and Best Practices}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=primaryblue!10,
    colframe=primaryblue,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Parallel Binary Search: Complete Mastery}
]
\textbf{All NEW Patterns Covered:}

\textbf{Pattern 1: Classic PBS (1 problem - THE canonical)}
\begin{itemize}
\item 15.1 SPOJ METEORS - Range updates, multiple queries
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Pattern 2: Range Queries (1 NEW problem)}
\begin{itemize}
\item 15.8 CF Sign on Fence - Maximum consecutive with threshold
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Pattern 3: Graph Queries (1 NEW problem)}
\begin{itemize}
\item 15.14 AGC Stamp Rally - Connectivity with component size
\end{itemize}

\textbf{When to Use Parallel Binary Search (PBS):}

\begin{itemize}
\item[$\checkmark$] Multiple queries ($Q > 100$)
\item[$\checkmark$] Monotonic property (if true at $T$, then true at $T+1$)
\item[$\checkmark$] Expensive state building ($O(N)$ or worse)
\item[$\times$] Single query (use regular binary search)
\item[$\times$] Non-monotonic property (PBS may give incorrect results)
\item[$\times$] Cheap checks (overhead not justified)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{CRITICAL: Implementation Checklist}
\begin{itemize}
\item Initialize all query ranges [1, N]
\item Group queries by current mid value
\item Build state incrementally (ONCE per iteration)
\item Check queries only at their mid
\item Update ranges based on results
\item Iterate log(N) times
\item Handle "no answer" cases
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Complexity Comparison:}
\begin{itemize}
\item Sequential: O(Q × N × log N)
\item Parallel: O((Q + N) × log N)
\item Speedup: Q / (1 + Q/N) ≈ Q for large Q!
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Common Data Structures with PBS:}
\begin{itemize}
\item BIT/Fenwick Tree - Range updates
\item Segment Tree - Complex range queries
\item Union-Find - Connectivity
\item Persistent Structures - Historical queries
\end{itemize}
\end{tcolorbox}
