% ==========================================
% CHAPTER 16: BINARY SEARCH WITH DP
% ==========================================

\chapter{Binary Search with DP}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,
    colback=white,
    colframe=secondaryblue,
    boxrule=1pt,
    arc=2mm,
    left=10pt,
    right=10pt
]
\textbf{\large\textcolor{primaryblue}{Combining Binary Search and Dynamic Programming}}
\end{tcolorbox}

\vspace{0.5cm}

\begin{center}
\textit{\textcolor{darkgray}{Difficulty: CF 2000-2400 $\bullet$ 25 Problems}}
\end{center}

\section{Optimizing Transitions}

The most common use of Binary Search in DP is to speed up the search for the "best" previous state. 

\subsection{The LIS Pattern}
In the Longest Increasing Subsequence problem, a naive DP takes $O(N^2)$. By maintaining a sorted list of the smallest possible tail for all increasing subsequences of various lengths, we can use \texttt{lower\_bound} to update the DP state in $O(\log N)$.

\subsection{BS on Answer + DP Check}
Often, we binary search on a value $X$ and use DP as the \texttt{check(X)} function. For example, "Is it possible to achieve a total profit of $P$ such that the maximum weight of any item is $\le X$?" The DP determines feasibility for a fixed $X$.

\section{Binary Search with Dynamic Programming: Complete Mastery}

\subsection{Complete Theoretical Foundation}

\subsubsection{Why Combine Binary Search and DP?}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=white,
    colframe=deepblue,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{The BS+DP Synergy}
]
\textbf{Two Powerful Techniques United}

DP: Optimal substructure + overlapping subproblems
Binary Search: Efficient searching in monotonic space

\textbf{Three Main Patterns:}
\begin{verbatim}
Pattern 1: BS to Optimize DP Transitions
  Problem: DP transition requires finding best previous state
  Naive: O(N) scan for each state
  With BS: O(log N) search
  
  Example: Longest Increasing Subsequence
    Naive DP: O(N²)
    With BS: O(N log N)
    Speedup: N / log N (up to 1000× for N=10^6!)

Pattern 2: BS on Answer, DP for Checking
  Problem: Find optimal value satisfying constraint
  Approach: Binary search on answer
  Check: Use DP to verify feasibility
  
  Example: "Minimum K to achieve goal"
    BS on K
    DP checks if K is sufficient

Pattern 3: DP State Space Reduction
  Problem: DP state space too large
  Approach: BS to find relevant states
  
  Example: Convex Hull Trick
    Find optimal line from set
    BS on sorted slopes

KEY INSIGHT:
  BS reduces search from O(N) to O(log N)
  Applied to DP: Massive speedup possible!
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Complete Theory:}

1. \textbf{Pattern 1: LIS with Binary Search}
\begin{verbatim}
Classic Problem: Longest Increasing Subsequence

Naive DP:
  dp[i] = max(dp[j] + 1) for all j < i where arr[j] < arr[i]
  Time: O(N²)
  For N=10^6: 10^12 operations (TLE!)

Optimized with BS:
  Maintain array: tails[len] = smallest tail for LIS of length len
  
  For each element:
    Find position with binary search: O(log N)
    Update tails array: O(1)
  
  Time: O(N log N)
  For N=10^6: 2×10^7 operations (AC!)

Why BS works:
  tails array is SORTED by construction!
  Finding position: Binary search applicable
  
Proof of correctness:
  Greedy choice: Always use smallest possible tail
  This maximizes future extension possibilities
  Therefore: Optimal ✓
\end{verbatim}

2. \textbf{Pattern 2: Job Scheduling}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Schedule non-overlapping jobs, maximize profit

DP State: dp[i] = max profit ending at or before job i

Transition: For job i with end time e[i]
  Find last job j where e[j] <= start[i]
  dp[i] = max(dp[i-1], profit[i] + dp[j])

Without BS:
  Scan all previous jobs: O(N)
  Total: O(N²)

With BS:
  Sort jobs by end time
  Binary search for last compatible job: O(log N)
  Total: O(N log N)

When to use:
  Jobs sorted by some dimension
  Need to find "last valid" or "first valid"
  
  Perfect for binary search!
\end{verbatim}

3. \textbf{Pattern 3: Convex Hull Trick}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: DP with linear transition
  dp[i] = min(dp[j] + a[j] × b[i] + c[i])

Observation: Transitions form lines!
  Line j: y = a[j] × x + (dp[j] + c[i])
  Query: x = b[i]

Naive: Check all lines O(N)
Optimized: Maintain convex hull of lines
  
Binary Search Application:
  Hull is convex → slopes sorted
  Find best line: Binary search on slope
  Time: O(log N) per query

Total: O(N log N) instead of O(N²)

Advanced technique used in:
  - Optimization problems
  - Competitive programming
  - Online judges (USACO, CF)
\end{verbatim}

4. \textbf{When Each Pattern Applies:}
\begin{verbatim}
Use LIS Pattern when:
  ✓ Finding longest/shortest sequence
  ✓ Maintaining sorted auxiliary structure
  ✓ Elements have ordering property
  
  Examples: LIS, Russian Dolls, Sequence decomposition

Use Job Scheduling Pattern when:
  ✓ Events/intervals with times
  ✓ Non-overlapping constraint
  ✓ Need previous compatible state
  
  Examples: Job scheduling, projects, events

Use Convex Hull Pattern when:
  ✓ DP transition is linear equation
  ✓ Coefficients monotonic
  ✓ Query points ordered
  
  Examples: Transport, allocation, optimization

Wrong to use BS+DP when:
  ✗ No monotonicity in search space
  ✗ DP transitions not reducible
  ✗ Overhead exceeds benefit (small N)
\end{verbatim}

5. \textbf{Complexity Analysis:}
\begin{verbatim}
Naive DP:
  States: N
  Transitions per state: N
  Total: O(N²)

With Binary Search:
  States: N
  Transitions per state: O(log N)
  Total: O(N log N)

Speedup Factor:
  Ratio = N / log N
  
  N = 1,000: ~100× faster
  N = 10,000: ~750× faster
  N = 100,000: ~6,000× faster
  N = 1,000,000: ~50,000× faster!

When BS overhead too much:
  N < 100: Naive might be simpler
  Simple transition: Overhead not worth it
  
For competitive programming:
  N > 10^5: Almost always need BS+DP
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Key Insight:}
BS+DP combines optimization power with search efficiency!
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Pattern 1: Longest Increasing Subsequence (LIS)}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=successgreen!10,
    colframe=successgreen,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete LIS Algorithms}
]
\textbf{The Canonical BS+DP Problem}

\textbf{Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Maintain: tails[len] = smallest ending value for LIS of length len

For each element x:
  pos = binary_search(tails, x)
  tails[pos] = x
  if pos == len: len++

Why this works:
  - tails is always sorted
  - Binary search finds correct position
  - Greedy: Always use smallest tail
  - Optimal: Maximizes future options

Time: O(N log N)
Space: O(N)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete LIS Solutions - NEW PROBLEMS]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 16.1: LeetCode 300 - Longest Increasing Subsequence
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/longest-increasing-subsequence/
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1600)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Given an integer array nums, return the length of the longest strictly
// increasing subsequence.
//
// Technique: Binary Search + DP (O(N log N) solution)
// ==============================================================

int lengthOfLIS(vector<int>& nums) {
    vector<int> tails;  // tails[i] = smallest tail for LIS of length i+1
    
    for (int num : nums) {
        // Binary search for position
        auto it = lower_bound(tails.begin(), tails.end(), num);
        
        if (it == tails.end()) {
            // Extends longest subsequence
            tails.push_back(num);
        } else {
            // Replaces existing tail
            *it = num;
        }
    }
    
    return tails.size();
}

/*
COMPLETE SOLUTION ANALYSIS:

Problem: Find longest strictly increasing subsequence

Naive DP:
  dp[i] = 1 + max(dp[j]) for all j < i where nums[j] < nums[i]
  Time: O($N^2$)
  
  For each position: Scan all previous
  N=10^5: 10^10 operations (TLE!)

Optimized with Binary Search:
  Key insight: Maintain smallest possible tail for each length
  
  Why "smallest tail"?
    Smaller tail -> easier to extend
    Greedy choice -> optimal!

Data Structure: tails array
  tails[len] = smallest ending value for LIS of length len
  
  Property: tails is ALWAYS sorted!
  
  Proof:
    Assume: tails[i] > tails[i+1]
    Then: Subsequence of length i+1 with smaller tail
    Can: Replace tail of length i with smaller value
    Contradiction! Therefore: tails[i] < tails[i+1] \ding{51}

Algorithm:
  For each element x:
    1. Binary search: Find smallest tails[i] >= x
    2. Update: tails[i] = x
    3. If beyond end: Append (found longer LIS!)

Binary Search Details:
  lower_bound finds first element >= x
  Time: O(log N)
  
  If found: Replace (better tail for same length)
  If not found: Extend (longer LIS possible!)

WHY THIS IS CORRECT:

Correctness Proof:
  Invariant: tails[i] = smallest tail for LIS length i+1
  
  Base: Initially empty, invariant holds
  
  Induction: After processing x
    Case 1: x larger than all tails
      Extends longest LIS
      New tail for length+1: x (smallest by construction)
    
    Case 2: x replaces tails[i]
      Found better tail for length i+1
      Maintains "smallest tail" property
    
  Therefore: Algorithm maintains invariant \ding{51}

Greedy Optimality:
  Always using smallest possible tail
  Maximizes chances of extension
  
  Proof by exchange:
    Suppose: Use larger tail t' instead of t
    Result: Harder to extend (need x > t' instead of x > t)
    Therefore: No benefit to using larger tail
    Greedy is optimal \ding{51}

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log N)
  N elements
  Each: O(log N) binary search
  Total: N \times log N

Space: O(N) for tails array

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Naive DP: O($N^2$)
   Our solution: 100X faster for N=10^5

2. Segment tree DP: O(N log(max_val))
   More complex, similar complexity
   Our solution: Simpler

3. Patience sorting: O(N log N)
   Equivalent algorithm, different view
   Our solution: Same complexity

Example Trace:
  nums = [10, 9, 2, 5, 3, 7, 101, 18]
  
  Process 10: tails = [10]
  Process 9:  tails = [9]    (replace)
  Process 2:  tails = [2]    (replace)
  Process 5:  tails = [2, 5] (extend)
  Process 3:  tails = [2, 3] (replace)
  Process 7:  tails = [2, 3, 7] (extend)
  Process 101: tails = [2, 3, 7, 101] (extend)
  Process 18: tails = [2, 3, 7, 18] (replace)
  
  LIS length = 4
  
  One LIS: [2, 3, 7, 18]
  Another: [2, 3, 7, 101]

Pattern: "Longest increasing/decreasing sequence" -> LIS with BS
*/

// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 16.2: LeetCode 354 - Russian Doll Envelopes
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/russian-doll-envelopes/
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 1900-2000)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You are given a 2D array of integers envelopes where envelopes[i] = [w_i, h_i]
// represents the width and height of an envelope. One envelope can fit into
// another if both width and height are strictly greater. Return the maximum
// number of envelopes you can Russian doll (put one inside another).
//
// Technique: 2D LIS - Sort + 1D LIS
// ==============================================================

int maxEnvelopes(vector<vector<int>>& envelopes) {
    int n = envelopes.size();
    
    // Sort by width ascending, height descending
    sort(envelopes.begin(), envelopes.end(), [](auto& a, auto& b) {
        return a[0] < b[0] || (a[0] == b[0] && a[1] > b[1]);
    });
    
    // Find LIS on heights
    vector<int> tails;
    
    for (auto& env : envelopes) {
        int h = env[1];
        auto it = lower_bound(tails.begin(), tails.end(), h);
        
        if (it == tails.end()) {
            tails.push_back(h);
        } else {
            *it = h;
        }
    }
    
    return tails.size();
}

/*
WHY THIS WORKS:

2D LIS Problem:
  Can't directly apply 1D LIS
  Need to consider both dimensions

Key Trick: Clever Sorting
  Sort by width ascending
  For same width: Sort height DESCENDING
  
  Why descending for height?
    Prevents selecting multiple envelopes with same width!
    
    Example: [(3,4), (3,5), (3,6)]
    If sorted ascending: Might select multiple (wrong!)
    Sorted descending: Can only select one (correct!)

After Sorting:
  Widths are non-decreasing
  For same width: Heights decreasing
  
  Find LIS on heights: This is the answer!

Correctness:
  If heights are increasing:
    - Either widths different (valid nesting)
    - Or widths same but heights decreasing (impossible to select both)
  
  Therefore: LIS on heights = max envelopes \ding{51}

Time: O(N log N)
  Sort: O(N log N)
  LIS: O(N log N)

Space: O(N)

Pattern: "2D ordering with nesting" -> Sort one dimension + LIS on other
*/

// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 16.6: AtCoder ABC 134E - Sequence Decomposing
// Link: https://atcoder.jp/contests/abc134/tasks/abc134_e
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1800)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Given a sequence of N integers, find the minimum number of strictly
// increasing subsequences needed to partition the entire sequence.
//
// Technique: Dilworth's Theorem + LIS (Inverse Problem)
// ==============================================================

int minSubsequences(vector<int>& seq) {
    // Minimum partitions = Length of longest non-increasing subsequence
    // Equivalently: Max number of chains = min antichain size
    
    // We maintain: For each chain, store its current maximum
    multiset<int> chains;
    
    for (int num : seq) {
        // Find chain where we can add this number
        // Need: chain max < num (strictly increasing)
        auto it = chains.lower_bound(num);
        
        if (it != chains.begin()) {
            // Found a chain we can extend
            --it;
            chains.erase(it);
        }
        // If no chain found, create new chain
        
        chains.insert(num);
    }
    
    return chains.size();
}

/*
MATHEMATICAL FOUNDATION:

Dilworth's Theorem:
  In any partially ordered set:
    min(chain cover) = max(antichain size)
  
  Chain: Increasing subsequence
  Antichain: Elements not comparable (non-increasing)
  
  Minimum partitions = Length of longest non-increasing subseq

Algorithm (Greedy):
  Maintain chains (increasing subsequences)
  For each element:
    - Try to extend existing chain
    - Choose chain with largest element < current
    - If no such chain: Create new chain

Why Greedy Works:
  Always extending smallest valid chain
  Minimizes number of chains needed
  
  Proof: Any other strategy uses >= chains

Binary Search Optimization:
  Chains stored in multiset (sorted)
  Finding valid chain: O(log N)
  Total: O(N log N)

Time: O(N log N)
Space: O(N)

Pattern: "Minimum partitions into increasing" -> Dilworth + greedy + BS
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 2: Job Scheduling with Binary Search}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=lightgray,
    colframe=accentorange,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Job Scheduling Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Non-Overlapping Intervals + DP}

\textbf{Framework:}
\begin{verbatim}
Sort jobs by end time
dp[i] = max profit using jobs 0..i

For each job i:
  Find last job j where end[j] <= start[i]  # Binary search!
  dp[i] = max(dp[i-1], profit[i] + dp[j])

Time: O(N log N)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Job Scheduling Solutions - NEW PROBLEMS]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 16.10: CSES 1140 - Projects
// Link: https://cses.fi/problemset/task/1140
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1800-1900)
//
// Problem Statement:
// There are n projects. For each project, you know start day, end day, and
// reward. You can only work on one project at a time. Find the maximum total
// reward you can achieve.
//
// Technique: DP + Binary Search (Job Scheduling)
// ==============================================================

struct Project {
    int start, end;
    long long reward;
};

long long maxReward(vector<Project>& projects) {
    int n = projects.size();
    
    // Sort by end time
    sort(projects.begin(), projects.end(), 
         [](const Project& a, const Project& b) {
             return a.end < b.end;
         });
    
    // dp[i] = max reward ending at or before project i
    vector<long long> dp(n);
    dp[0] = projects[0].reward;
    
    for (int i = 1; i < n; i++) {
        // Option 1: Skip this project
        dp[i] = dp[i-1];
        
        // Option 2: Take this project
        // Find last compatible project using binary search
        int left = 0, right = i - 1;
        int lastCompatible = -1;
        
        while (left <= right) {
            int mid = left + (right - left) / 2;
            
            if (projects[mid].end < projects[i].start) {
                lastCompatible = mid;
                left = mid + 1;
            } else {
                right = mid - 1;
            }
        }
        
        long long withCurrent = projects[i].reward;
        if (lastCompatible != -1) {
            withCurrent += dp[lastCompatible];
        }
        
        dp[i] = max(dp[i], withCurrent);
    }
    
    return dp[n-1];
}

/*
COMPLETE ANALYSIS:

Problem: Schedule non-overlapping projects, maximize reward

DP State Design:
  dp[i] = maximum reward using projects 0..i (sorted by end time)
  
  Not necessarily using project i
  Just "ending at or before i"

Transition:
  Two choices:
    1. Skip project i: dp[i] = dp[i-1]
    2. Take project i: dp[i] = reward[i] + dp[last_compatible]
  
  Take maximum of both

Finding Last Compatible:
  Need: end[j] < start[i]
  Array sorted by end time
  
  Binary search for largest j satisfying constraint!

Binary Search Implementation:
  Pattern: Find last element satisfying condition
  Update: lastCompatible = mid when found
          left = mid + 1 (search for larger)

WHY THIS IS CORRECT:

Optimal Substructure:
  Optimal solution for projects 0..i either:
    - Doesn't use i: Optimal for 0..i-1
    - Uses i: Optimal for compatible + reward[i]
  
  DP captures both cases \ding{51}

Greedy + DP:
  Sorting by end time: Greedy choice
  DP: Considers all possibilities
  
  Combined: Optimal!

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log N)
  Sort: O(N log N)
  DP: N states \times O(log N) per state
  Total: O(N log N)

Space: O(N) for DP array

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Naive DP without BS: O($N^2$)
   Scan all previous: O(N) per state
   Our solution: 100X faster

2. Greedy only: Incorrect!
   Example: projects with varying rewards
   Need DP to consider all options

3. Brute force (try all combinations): O(2^N)
   Exponential!
   Our solution: Polynomial

Example Trace:
  projects = [(1,4,5), (3,5,6), (6,7,2), (5,8,11)]
  Sort by end: [(1,4,5), (3,5,6), (6,7,2), (5,8,11)]
  
  dp[0] = 5 (take project 0)
  dp[1] = max(5, 6) = 6 (take project 1)
  dp[2] = max(6, 5+2) = 7 (take both 0 and 2)
  dp[3] = max(7, 5+11) = 16 (take 0 and 3)
  
  Maximum reward = 16

Pattern: "Non-overlapping intervals + optimization" -> Sort + DP + BS
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 3: Convex Hull Trick and CHT}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=warnyellow!10,
    colframe=warnyellow,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Convex Hull Trick Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Advanced DP Optimization}

\textbf{When to Use CHT:}
\begin{verbatim}
DP Transition: dp[i] = min(dp[j] + a[j] × b[i] + c[i])

This is: y = mx + c form (linear equations!)

Naive: Check all j → O(N)
CHT: Maintain convex hull of lines → O(log N)

Total: O(N²) → O(N log N)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Convex Hull Trick Solutions - NEW PROBLEMS]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 16.23: CF 311B - Cats Transport
// Link: https://codeforces.com/problemset/problem/311/B
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 2100-2200)
//
// Problem Statement:
// There are N hills and M cats. Hills are at distances on a line. You have
// P people starting at hill 1. Each person moves at speed 1. Cat i appears
// at hill h[i] at time t[i]. If no person arrives by time t[i], the cat
// gets sad (cost = wait time). Assign cats to people to minimize total sadness.
//
// Technique: DP + Convex Hull Trick
// ==============================================================

struct Line {
    long long m, c;  // y = mx + c
    
    long long eval(long long x) {
        return m * x + c;
    }
    
    // Intersection with another line (x-coordinate)
    double intersectX(const Line& other) {
        return (double)(other.c - c) / (m - other.m);
    }
};

class ConvexHullTrick {
private:
    deque<Line> hull;
    
public:
    void addLine(long long m, long long c) {
        Line newLine = {m, c};
        
        // Remove lines that become irrelevant
        while (hull.size() >= 2) {
            int n = hull.size();
            Line& last = hull[n-1];
            Line& secondLast = hull[n-2];
            
            // Check if last line is now useless
            if (secondLast.intersectX(newLine) <= secondLast.intersectX(last)) {
                hull.pop_back();
            } else {
                break;
            }
        }
        
        hull.push_back(newLine);
    }
    
    long long query(long long x) {
        // Binary search for best line
        int left = 0, right = hull.size() - 1;
        
        while (right - left > 2) {
            int mid1 = left + (right - left) / 3;
            int mid2 = right - (right - left) / 3;
            
            if (hull[mid1].eval(x) > hull[mid2].eval(x)) {
                left = mid1;
            } else {
                right = mid2;
            }
        }
        
        long long minVal = LLONG_MAX;
        for (int i = left; i <= right; i++) {
            minVal = min(minVal, hull[i].eval(x));
        }
        
        return minVal;
    }
};

long long catsTransport(int N, int M, int P, 
                        vector<long long>& dist,
                        vector<int>& hill,
                        vector<long long>& time) {
    // Precompute prefix distances
    vector<long long> prefixDist(N + 1, 0);
    for (int i = 1; i <= N; i++) {
        prefixDist[i] = prefixDist[i-1] + dist[i-1];
    }
    
    // Transform: effective time = time - distance from start
    vector<long long> effectiveTime(M);
    for (int i = 0; i < M; i++) {
        effectiveTime[i] = time[i] - prefixDist[hill[i]];
    }
    
    // Sort cats by effective time
    sort(effectiveTime.begin(), effectiveTime.end());
    
    // Prefix sum for quick calculation
    vector<long long> prefixSum(M + 1, 0);
    for (int i = 0; i < M; i++) {
        prefixSum[i+1] = prefixSum[i] + effectiveTime[i];
    }
    
    // DP: dp[i][j] = min cost using i people for first j cats
    vector<vector<long long>> dp(P + 1, vector<long long>(M + 1, LLONG_MAX));
    dp[0][0] = 0;
    
    for (int p = 1; p <= P; p++) {
        ConvexHullTrick cht;
        
        for (int j = 0; j < M; j++) {
            if (dp[p-1][j] != LLONG_MAX) {
                // Add line: y = -effectiveTime[j] * x + (dp[p-1][j] + j*effectiveTime[j] - prefixSum[j])
                long long m = -effectiveTime[j];
                long long c = dp[p-1][j] + (long long)j * effectiveTime[j] - prefixSum[j];
                cht.addLine(m, c);
            }
        }
        
        for (int j = 1; j <= M; j++) {
            // Query: x = j, get minimum
            long long cost = cht.query(j) + prefixSum[j];
            dp[p][j] = min(dp[p][j], cost);
        }
    }
    
    return dp[P][M];
}

/*
COMPLETE CHT ANALYSIS:

Problem Complexity:
  - P people, M cats
  - Assign cats to people
  - Minimize total waiting time

DP Formulation:
  dp[p][j] = minimum cost using p people for first j cats
  
  Transition:
    dp[p][j] = min(dp[p-1][k] + cost(k+1, j)) for all k < j
    
    Where cost(k+1, j) = sum of wait times for cats k+1..j

Naive Transition: O(M)
  For each state: Check all previous splits
  Total: O(P \times $M^2$) = too slow for P=100, M=100K

Convex Hull Trick:
  Observation: Transition is linear!
    
  Rewrite cost function:
    cost = sum((j - i) \times time[i]) for i in range
        = j \times sum(time[i]) - sum(i \times time[i])
  
  This has form: y = mx + c
  
  Lines: One per previous state
  Query: Find minimum at point x=j

CHT maintains convex hull:
  Only keep "useful" lines
  Binary search for best line
  
  Time per query: O(log P)
  Total: O(P \times M \times log P)

Why CHT Works:

Mathematical Proof:
  Lines with slopes m_1 < m_2 < m_3
  
  If line 2 is never optimal:
    It's "covered" by lines 1 and 3
    Can remove from hull
  
  Keep only hull: Minimum set of potentially optimal lines

Intersection Property:
  Lines in hull intersect at specific x-values
  Between intersections: One line optimal
  
  Binary search finds which segment x falls in!

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(P \times M \times \log P)
  P iterations
  M states per iteration
  log P for CHT query

Space: O(P \times M) for DP table

Compare with Naive: O(P \times $M^2$)
  For M=100K: 100X speedup!

Implementation Details:

1. Line representation:
   y = mx + c
   Need: slope m and intercept c

2. Hull maintenance:
   Add lines in order (usually by slope)
   Remove useless lines

3. Query optimization:
   Binary/ternary search on hull
   Find line with minimum value at x

Edge Cases:
  - No cats: dp[p][0] = 0
  - No people left: Impossible
  - All cats at same time: Easy assignment

Real-World Application:
  - Task scheduling
  - Resource allocation
  - Transportation optimization

This tests:
  [$\checkmark$] DP transformation
  [$\checkmark$] CHT implementation
  [$\checkmark$] Geometric reasoning
  [$\checkmark$] Optimization techniques
*/

// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 16.14: LeetCode 1751 - Maximum Number of Events That Can Be Attended II
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/maximum-number-of-events-that-can-be-attended-ii/
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 2000-2100)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You are given an array of events where events[i] = [startDay_i, endDay_i, value_i].
// You can attend at most k events. You cannot attend two events where one starts
// when the other ends (must have gap). Return maximum sum of values you can get.
//
// Technique: DP + Binary Search (Multi-dimensional)
// ==============================================================

int maxValue(vector<vector<int>>& events, int k) {
    int n = events.size();
    
    // Sort by end time
    sort(events.begin(), events.end(), [](auto& a, auto& b) {
        return a[1] < b[1];
    });
    
    // dp[i][j] = max value attending j events from first i events
    vector<vector<int>> dp(n + 1, vector<int>(k + 1, 0));
    
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        int start = events[i-1][0];
        int end = events[i-1][1];
        int value = events[i-1][2];
        
        // Find last event that doesn't overlap
        int left = 0, right = i - 2;
        int lastCompatible = -1;
        
        while (left <= right) {
            int mid = left + (right - left) / 2;
            
            if (events[mid][1] < start) {
                lastCompatible = mid;
                left = mid + 1;
            } else {
                right = mid - 1;
            }
        }
        
        // Update DP
        for (int j = 0; j <= k; j++) {
            // Option 1: Don't take this event
            dp[i][j] = dp[i-1][j];
            
            // Option 2: Take this event (if we have capacity)
            if (j > 0) {
                int prevValue = (lastCompatible >= 0) ? dp[lastCompatible + 1][j-1] : 0;
                dp[i][j] = max(dp[i][j], prevValue + value);
            }
        }
    }
    
    return dp[n][k];
}

/*
MULTI-DIMENSIONAL DP:

State Design:
  dp[i][j] = max value from first i events, attending exactly j
  
  Two dimensions: position and count

Transition:
  For event i:
    1. Skip: dp[i][j] = dp[i-1][j]
    2. Take: dp[i][j] = value[i] + dp[last_compatible][j-1]

Binary Search Role:
  Find last compatible event
  Events sorted by end time
  
  Search for: largest index where end < start[i]

Why Two Dimensions:
  Must track: How many events attended
  Constraint: At most k events
  
  Need both position and count!

Complexity:
  Time: O($N^2$ log N) naive
        O(NK log N) with optimization
  
  Space: O(NK)

Optimization:
  Can reduce space to O(K) with rolling array
  
Pattern: "Limited selections + non-overlapping" -> Multi-dim DP + BS
*/

// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 16.3: CF 10D - LCIS (Longest Common Increasing Subsequence)
// Link: https://codeforces.com/problemset/problem/10/D
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 2000)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Given two sequences A and B, find the longest common increasing subsequence.
// A common increasing subsequence must appear in both arrays in increasing order.
//
// Technique: 2D DP + Binary Search Optimization
// ==============================================================

vector<int> LCIS(vector<int>& A, vector<int>& B) {
    int n = A.size();
    int m = B.size();
    
    // dp[j] = length of LCIS ending with B[j]
    vector<int> dp(m, 0);
    vector<int> parent(m, -1);
    
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        int curLength = 0;
        int curParent = -1;
        
        for (int j = 0; j < m; j++) {
            if (A[i] == B[j] && dp[j] < curLength + 1) {
                dp[j] = curLength + 1;
                parent[j] = curParent;
            }
            
            if (B[j] < A[i] && dp[j] > curLength) {
                curLength = dp[j];
                curParent = j;
            }
        }
    }
    
    // Find maximum length
    int maxLen = 0;
    int lastIdx = -1;
    for (int j = 0; j < m; j++) {
        if (dp[j] > maxLen) {
            maxLen = dp[j];
            lastIdx = j;
        }
    }
    
    // Reconstruct sequence
    vector<int> result;
    int idx = lastIdx;
    while (idx != -1) {
        result.push_back(B[idx]);
        idx = parent[idx];
    }
    
    reverse(result.begin(), result.end());
    return result;
}

/*
LCIS Complexity:

Problem: Combine LCS (common) + LIS (increasing)

DP State:
  dp[i][j] = LCIS length ending at A[i] and B[j]
  
  Naive: O($N^2$M) transition
  Optimized: O(NM) with clever bookkeeping

Key Insight:
  For fixed A[i], scan B[j]
  Track best previous state
  Update when A[i] == B[j]

Time: O(NM)
  Better than naive O($N^2$M)
  
Space: O(M) with optimization

Pattern: "Common + increasing" -> LCIS algorithm
*/
\end{lstlisting}

// ==============================================================
// ELITE DP+BS 1: Meet-in-the-Middle Knapsack with BS
// Difficulty: VERY HARD (CF 2300+)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Standard 0-1 knapsack but N up to 40 (too large for normal DP).
// Use meet-in-the-middle: split into two halves, generate all subsets,
// then use binary search to combine optimally.
//
// Technique: Meet-in-Middle + Binary Search + DP
// ==============================================================

int knapsackMeetMiddle(vector<int>& weights, vector<int>& values, int capacity) {
    int n = weights.size();
    int mid = n / 2;
    
    // Generate all subsets for first half
    map<int, int> firstHalf;  // weight -> max value
    
    for (int mask = 0; mask < (1 << mid); mask++) {
        int w = 0, v = 0;
        
        for (int i = 0; i < mid; i++) {
            if (mask & (1 << i)) {
                w += weights[i];
                v += values[i];
            }
        }
        
        if (w <= capacity) {
            firstHalf[w] = max(firstHalf[w], v);
        }
    }
    
    // Sort first half by weight, keep max value prefix
    vector<pair<int,int>> sorted1;
    int maxVal = 0;
    for (auto [w, v] : firstHalf) {
        maxVal = max(maxVal, v);
        sorted1.push_back({w, maxVal});
    }
    
    // Generate subsets for second half and combine
    int maxTotal = 0;
    
    for (int mask = 0; mask < (1 << (n - mid)); mask++) {
        int w = 0, v = 0;
        
        for (int i = mid; i < n; i++) {
            if (mask & (1 << (i - mid))) {
                w += weights[i];
                v += values[i];
            }
        }
        
        if (w <= capacity) {
            // Binary search for best compatible weight from first half
            int remaining = capacity - w;
            
            auto it = upper_bound(sorted1.begin(), sorted1.end(),
                                 make_pair(remaining, INT_MAX));
            
            if (it != sorted1.begin()) {
                --it;
                maxTotal = max(maxTotal, v + it->second);
            }
        }
    }
    
    return maxTotal;
}

/*
MEET-IN-THE-MIDDLE ANALYSIS:

Why Needed:
  Normal DP: O(N \times capacity)
  For N=40, capacity=10^6: Too slow
  
  Meet-in-middle: O(2^(N/2) \times log(2^(N/2)))
  For N=40: 2^20 \times 20 = 2 \times 10^7 (feasible!)

Algorithm:
  1. Split items into two halves
  2. Generate all 2^(N/2) subsets for each half
  3. For each subset in half 2:
     Binary search for best compatible subset in half 1

Binary Search Role:
  First half sorted by weight
  For each second-half subset:
    Find largest first-half weight that fits
    This gives best possible combination

Optimization:
  Prefix maximum: Keep best value for each weight prefix
  Avoids checking worse options

Time: O(2^(N/2) \times N/2)
  Generate: O(2^(N/2) \times N/2)
  Sort: O(2^(N/2) \times \log(2^(N/2)))
  Combine: O(2^(N/2) \times \log(2^(N/2)))

Space: O(2^(N/2))

Pattern: "Exponential but N<=40" -> Meet-in-middle + BS
*/

// ==============================================================
// ELITE DP+BS 2: Minimum Jumps with Range Query Optimization
// Difficulty: VERY HARD (CF 2400+)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Array of N integers. From position i, you can jump to any j where
// i < j <= i + arr[i]. Find minimum jumps to reach end. But with twist:
// After each jump, all values in jumped range increase by 1.
//
// Technique: DP + Segment Tree + Binary Search
// ==============================================================

class SegmentTree {
private:
    vector<int> tree, lazy;
    int n;
    
    void push(int node, int l, int r) {
        if (lazy[node] != 0) {
            tree[node] += lazy[node];
            if (l != r) {
                lazy[2*node] += lazy[node];
                lazy[2*node+1] += lazy[node];
            }
            lazy[node] = 0;
        }
    }
    
    void update(int node, int l, int r, int ql, int qr, int val) {
        push(node, l, r);
        if (ql > r || qr < l) return;
        
        if (ql <= l && r <= qr) {
            lazy[node] += val;
            push(node, l, r);
            return;
        }
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        update(2*node, l, mid, ql, qr, val);
        update(2*node+1, mid+1, r, ql, qr, val);
        
        push(2*node, l, mid);
        push(2*node+1, mid+1, r);
        tree[node] = max(tree[2*node], tree[2*node+1]);
    }
    
    int query(int node, int l, int r, int ql, int qr) {
        push(node, l, r);
        if (ql > r || qr < l) return 0;
        if (ql <= l && r <= qr) return tree[node];
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        return max(query(2*node, l, mid, ql, qr),
                  query(2*node+1, mid+1, r, ql, qr));
    }
    
public:
    SegmentTree(int size) : n(size) {
        tree.resize(4 * n, 0);
        lazy.resize(4 * n, 0);
    }
    
    void rangeUpdate(int l, int r, int val) {
        update(1, 0, n-1, l, r, val);
    }
    
    int rangeMax(int l, int r) {
        return query(1, 0, n-1, l, r);
    }
};

int minJumpsWithUpdate(vector<int>& arr) {
    int n = arr.size();
    vector<int> dp(n, INT_MAX);
    dp[0] = 0;
    
    SegmentTree seg(n);
    
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        if (dp[i] == INT_MAX) continue;
        
        int maxJump = min(n - 1, i + arr[i]);
        
        // Update DP for reachable positions
        for (int j = i + 1; j <= maxJump; j++) {
            dp[j] = min(dp[j], dp[i] + 1);
        }
        
        // Update values in range (dynamic values!)
        seg.rangeUpdate(i + 1, maxJump, 1);
        
        // Binary search for furthest reachable with updated values
        int left = i + 1, right = n - 1;
        int furthest = i;
        
        while (left <= right) {
            int mid = left + (right - left) / 2;
            int maxInRange = seg.rangeMax(i + 1, mid);
            
            if (i + arr[i] + maxInRange >= mid) {
                furthest = mid;
                left = mid + 1;
            } else {
                right = mid - 1;
            }
        }
    }
    
    return dp[n-1];
}

/*
DYNAMIC DP WITH SEGMENT TREE:

Complexity:
  Values change during computation!
  Cannot precompute reachability
  
  Need: Dynamic range queries and updates

Segment Tree:
  Range updates: Lazy propagation
  Range queries: Maximum in range
  Both: O(log N)

Binary Search:
  Find furthest reachable position
  With updated values
  
  Check: Can reach position mid?
  Monotonic: If can reach mid, can reach mid-1

Combined:
  DP: O($N^2$) naive
  With Seg Tree + BS: O(N $(log (N))^2$)

Why This Hard:
  Three techniques combined:
    1. DP for optimal path
    2. Segment tree for range operations
    3. Binary search for reachability

Time: O(N $(log (N))^2$)
Space: O(N)

Pattern: "Dynamic values + range queries" -> Seg tree + BS + DP
*/

// ==============================================================
// ELITE DP+BS 3: Partition into K Subsets with Balanced Sums
// Difficulty: EXTREMELY HARD (CF 2500+)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Array of N integers. Partition into exactly K non-empty contiguous subarrays
// such that difference between max and min subarray sums is minimized.
//
// Technique: Binary Search on Answer + DP + Optimization
// ==============================================================

bool canPartition(vector<long long>& prefixSum, int n, int k, long long maxDiff) {
    // dp[i][j] = can partition first i elements into j subarrays
    //            with max sum - min sum <= maxDiff
    
    vector<vector<bool>> dp(n + 1, vector<bool>(k + 1, false));
    dp[0][0] = true;
    
    long long globalMin = LLONG_MAX;
    long long globalMax = LLONG_MIN;
    
    for (int parts = 1; parts <= k; parts++) {
        vector<pair<long long, long long>> validRanges;  // [minSum, maxSum] for each split
        
        for (int i = parts; i <= n; i++) {
            if (!dp[i-1][parts-1]) continue;
            
            // Try all possible last subarray
            for (int j = i; j <= n; j++) {
                long long subarraySum = prefixSum[j] - prefixSum[i-1];
                
                // Check if this sum fits within maxDiff of existing range
                bool fits = true;
                for (auto [minS, maxS] : validRanges) {
                    if (subarraySum < minS - maxDiff || subarraySum > maxS + maxDiff) {
                        fits = false;
                        break;
                    }
                }
                
                if (fits || validRanges.empty()) {
                    dp[j][parts] = true;
                    
                    globalMin = min(globalMin, subarraySum);
                    globalMax = max(globalMax, subarraySum);
                    
                    validRanges.push_back({globalMin, globalMax});
                }
            }
        }
    }
    
    return dp[n][k] && (globalMax - globalMin <= maxDiff);
}

long long minMaxDiff(vector<int>& arr, int k) {
    int n = arr.size();
    
    vector<long long> prefixSum(n + 1, 0);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        prefixSum[i+1] = prefixSum[i] + arr[i];
    }
    
    // Binary search on answer
    long long low = 0;
    long long high = prefixSum[n];
    long long result = high;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        long long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (canPartition(prefixSum, n, k, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
ULTIMATE COMPLEXITY:

Three Levels:
  1. Binary search on difference
  2. DP for partition feasibility
  3. Optimization for valid ranges

Why Extremely Hard:
  - Must partition into EXACTLY k parts
  - Minimize difference (not sum or max)
  - Contiguous subarrays
  - All three constraints together!

Algorithm Layers:
  Outer: BS on max difference
  Middle: DP for partition
  Inner: Check if partition valid

Time: O($N^2$ K \times log(sum))
  BS: O(log(sum))
  DP: O($N^2$ K) per check
  
  For N=100, K=10: Tight but feasible

Space: O(NK)

Optimization Techniques:
  1. Prefix sums for fast range sum
  2. Pruning invalid partitions early
  3. Range validation with bounds

Real-World:
  - Load balancing servers
  - Parallel task distribution
  - Resource allocation

Tests:
  [$\checkmark$] BS on answer
  [$\checkmark$] Complex DP state
  [$\checkmark$] Multiple constraints
  [$\checkmark$] Optimization skills
  [$\checkmark$] Implementation mastery
*/
\end{lstlisting}

% CHAPTER 17: BINARY SEARCH ON GRAPHS
% ==========================================

\chapter{Binary Search on Graphs}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,
    colback=white,
    colframe=secondaryblue,
    boxrule=1pt,
    arc=2mm,
    left=10pt,
    right=10pt
]
\textbf{\large\textcolor{primaryblue}{Shortest Path Variants, Network Flow, and Graph Optimization}}
\end{tcolorbox}

\vspace{0.5cm}

\begin{center}
\textit{\textcolor{darkgray}{Difficulty: CF 2000-2400 $\bullet$ 20 Problems}}
\end{center}

\section{The Bottleneck Concept}

In graph optimization, we often seek to minimize the "bottleneck" (the maximum weight edge in a path). 

\subsection{Reachability as a Decision Problem}
To find the minimum bottleneck weight $W$:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Guess a weight $X$ using Binary Search.
    \item Build a temporary graph containing only edges with $weight \leq X$.
    \item Check if a path exists from source $S$ to sink $T$ using BFS or DFS.
    \item If reachable, $X$ is a candidate answer; try a smaller value.
\end{enumerate}



\subsection{BS with Dijkstra and Flow}
For more complex constraints, the `check(X)` function can be:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Dijkstra:} If edges have costs and we have a total budget, check if $dist(S, T) \leq \text{Budget}$ while ignoring edges $> X$.
    \item \textbf{Max Flow:} Check if the max flow $\geq \text{Target}$ using only edges with capacity or weight constraints defined by $X$.
\end{itemize}

\section{Binary Search on Graphs: Complete Mastery}

\subsection{Complete Theoretical Foundation}

\subsubsection{Why Binary Search on Graphs?}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=white,
    colframe=deepblue,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{The Bottleneck and Reachability Paradigm}
]
\textbf{Graph Optimization Meets Binary Search}

Graphs: Complex structures with paths and connectivity
Binary Search: Efficient optimization technique

\textbf{The Core Patterns:}
\begin{verbatim}
Pattern 1: Minimize Maximum (Bottleneck)
  Problem: Find path minimizing largest edge weight
  Example: Minimum effort path, swim in water
  
  Naive: Try all paths O(exponential)
  BS: Search on bottleneck weight O(log W × path_check)

Pattern 2: Maximize Minimum  
  Problem: Find path maximizing smallest edge weight
  Example: Max bandwidth path
  
  Same BS approach, opposite direction

Pattern 3: Binary Search + Graph Algorithms
  Check function uses:
    - BFS/DFS for reachability
    - Dijkstra for shortest path
    - Max Flow for capacity
    - MST for connectivity

KEY INSIGHT:
  Graph property is monotonic in edge weights
  This enables binary search on weight threshold!
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Complete Theory:}

1. \textbf{The Bottleneck Shortest Path Problem}
\begin{verbatim}
Definition: Path where maximum edge weight is minimized

Example: Grid navigation
  Grid: [[1,2,2],[3,8,2],[5,3,5]]
  Path: Top-left to bottom-right
  Goal: Minimize maximum difference between adjacent cells

Traditional Dijkstra:
  Tracks sum of edge weights
  Finds minimum total cost
  
Bottleneck Path:
  Tracks maximum edge along path
  Finds minimum of these maximums
  Different problem!

Binary Search Approach:
  For weight threshold W:
    Can we reach destination using only edges ≤ W?
  
  If yes: Try smaller W (minimize)
  If no: Need larger W
  
  Find minimum W where path exists!

Monotonicity:
  If reachable with W → reachable with W+1
  Pattern: F F F T T T
  Find first True (minimum bottleneck)
\end{verbatim}

2. \textbf{Reachability as Decision Problem}
\begin{verbatim}
Transform: Optimization → Decision

Optimization: "What is minimum bottleneck?"
Decision: "Can we do it with bottleneck ≤ W?"

Algorithm:
  bool canReach(threshold W):
    Build graph with only edges where weight ≤ W
    Run BFS/DFS from source to target
    Return: target reachable?
  
  int minimizeBottleneck():
    Binary search on W
    Use canReach(W) as check function
    Return minimum W where canReach(W) = true

Why This Works:
  Monotonicity: More edges → more connectivity
  If unreachable with W: Also unreachable with W-1
  If reachable with W: Also reachable with W+1
  
  Perfect for binary search!

Time Complexity:
  Binary search: O(log W) iterations
  Each check: O(V + E) for BFS/DFS
  Total: O((V + E) × log W)
  
  Much better than trying all paths!
\end{verbatim}

3. \textbf{Common Check Functions}
\begin{verbatim}
Check 1: Simple Reachability (BFS/DFS)
  Question: Can reach target with edges ≤ W?
  Algorithm: BFS ignoring edges > W
  Time: O(V + E)
  
  Use when: Simple path existence

Check 2: Shortest Path (Dijkstra)
  Question: Can reach with distance ≤ D using edges ≤ W?
  Algorithm: Dijkstra on filtered graph
  Time: O((V + E) log V)
  
  Use when: Distance constraint exists

Check 3: Max Flow (Flow Algorithm)
  Question: Can achieve flow ≥ F using edges ≤ W?
  Algorithm: Max flow on filtered graph
  Time: O(V²E) or better with advanced algorithms
  
  Use when: Capacity/flow constraints

Check 4: Connected Components (DSU)
  Question: Are nodes in same component with edges ≤ W?
  Algorithm: Union-Find on filtered edges
  Time: O(E × α(V))
  
  Use when: Connectivity queries

Choosing Right Check:
  Problem constraint → Appropriate graph algorithm
  Binary search provides the wrapper!
\end{verbatim}

4. \textbf{When NOT to Use BS on Graphs}
\begin{verbatim}
Don't use when:
  ✗ No monotonicity in edge weights
  ✗ Path property not weight-dependent
  ✗ Small graph (direct algorithm faster)
  ✗ Check function too expensive

Example - NO monotonicity:
  "Find path with exactly K edges"
  Adding edge doesn't monotonically help
  
Example - Not weight-dependent:
  "Count cycles in graph"
  Doesn't depend on edge weights
  
Use direct algorithms instead!

When BS is overkill:
  Small W range: Direct iteration faster
  Simple property: Specialized algorithm exists
  
  Always analyze: Is BS really beneficial?
\end{verbatim}

5. \textbf{Complexity Analysis}
\begin{verbatim}
General Formula:
  Time = O(log(W_max - W_min) × T_check)
  
  Where:
    W_max - W_min = search space size
    T_check = time for check function

Common Cases:

Case 1: BFS/DFS Check
  Time: O((V + E) × log W)
  Example: Reachability queries
  
Case 2: Dijkstra Check  
  Time: O((V + E) log V × log W)
  Example: Distance-constrained paths

Case 3: Max Flow Check
  Time: O(V²E × log W) or better
  Example: Flow optimization

Case 4: DSU Check
  Time: O(E α(V) × log W)
  Example: Connectivity

Space:
  Usually O(V + E) for graph storage
  Plus algorithm-specific space

Optimization:
  Cache repeated computations
  Use efficient data structures
  Prune search space when possible
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Key Insight:}
BS on graphs transforms hard optimization into feasibility checking!
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Pattern 1: Minimize Maximum Edge (Bottleneck)}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=successgreen!10,
    colframe=successgreen,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Bottleneck Path Algorithms}
]
\textbf{The Classic Graph BS Problem}

\textbf{Framework}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Path from S to T minimizing max edge weight

Algorithm:
  1. Binary search on edge weight threshold
  2. Check: Can reach T using only edges ≤ threshold?
  3. Use BFS/DFS for reachability
  4. Find minimum threshold where reachable

Time: O((V + E) × log(max_weight))
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Complete Bottleneck Path Solutions - NEW PROBLEMS]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 17.1: LeetCode 1631 - Path With Minimum Effort
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/path-with-minimum-effort/
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1800-1900)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You are a hiker preparing for an upcoming hike. You are given heights, a
// 2D array where heights[row][col] represents the height of cell (row, col).
// You start at (0,0) and want to reach (rows-1, cols-1). A route's effort
// is the maximum absolute difference in heights between two consecutive cells.
// Return the minimum effort required to travel from top-left to bottom-right.
//
// Technique: Binary Search on Effort + BFS for Reachability
// ==============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    bool canReach(vector<vector<int>>& heights, int maxEffort) {
        int rows = heights.size();
        int cols = heights[0].size();
        
        vector<vector<bool>> visited(rows, vector<bool>(cols, false));
        queue<pair<int,int>> q;
        
        q.push({0, 0});
        visited[0][0] = true;
        
        int dx[] = {-1, 1, 0, 0};
        int dy[] = {0, 0, -1, 1};
        
        while (!q.empty()) {
            auto [x, y] = q.front();
            q.pop();
            
            if (x == rows - 1 && y == cols - 1) {
                return true;
            }
            
            for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) {
                int nx = x + dx[i];
                int ny = y + dy[i];
                
                if (nx >= 0 && nx < rows && ny >= 0 && ny < cols && !visited[nx][ny]) {
                    int effort = abs(heights[nx][ny] - heights[x][y]);
                    
                    if (effort <= maxEffort) {
                        visited[nx][ny] = true;
                        q.push({nx, ny});
                    }
                }
            }
        }
        
        return false;
    }
    
    int minimumEffortPath(vector<vector<int>>& heights) {
        int rows = heights.size();
        int cols = heights[0].size();
        
        // Find range for binary search
        int low = 0;
        int high = 0;
        
        for (auto& row : heights) {
            for (int h : row) {
                high = max(high, h);
            }
        }
        
        int result = high;
        
        while (low <= high) {
            int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
            
            if (canReach(heights, mid)) {
                result = mid;
                high = mid - 1;
            } else {
                low = mid + 1;
            }
        }
        
        return result;
    }
};

/*
COMPLETE SOLUTION ANALYSIS:

Problem Type: Bottleneck Shortest Path
  Not minimize sum (traditional shortest path)
  Minimize maximum (bottleneck)

Key Observation:
  If reachable with effort E -> reachable with E+1
  Monotonic property enables binary search!

Binary Search:
  Search space: [0, max_height_diff]
  Question: "Can reach with max effort <= mid?"
  
  Pattern: F F F T T T
  Find first True (minimum effort)

Check Function (BFS):
  Build graph with only edges <= threshold
  BFS from source to target
  
  Why BFS? O(V + E) reachability check
  Why not Dijkstra? Don't need distances, just reachability

WHY THIS IS CORRECT:

Monotonicity Proof:
  Assume: Reachable with effort E
  Claim: Reachable with effort E+1
  
  Proof: Same path works (all edges <= E <= E+1)
  Therefore: Monotonic \ding{51}

Binary Search Correctness:
  Searches for minimum E where reachable
  Monotonicity guarantees this is optimal
  
  Why? If smaller E doesn't work, it's not feasible
       If larger E works, we want smaller
  
  BS finds exact boundary!

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(RC \times log(max_height))
  Binary search: O(log(max_height))
  Each BFS: O(RC) for grid with R rows, C cols
  Total: O(RC \times log(max_height))

Space: O(RC) for visited array

WHY ALTERNATIVES ARE WORSE:

1. Dijkstra with modified distance:
   Track maximum edge on path
   Time: O(RC \times log(RC))
   
   Comparison: Similar complexity
   Our BS: Simpler, more intuitive

2. Try all possible efforts:
   For effort = 0 to max: Check reachability
   Time: O(RC \times max_height)
   
   For max_height = 10^6: Too slow!
   Our solution: O(RC \times log(max_height)) = 60X faster

3. DFS with memoization:
   Memo[x][y][max_effort] = reachable?
   Space: O(RC \times max_height) - too much!
   
   Our solution: O(RC) space

Example Trace:
  heights = [[1,2,2],[3,8,2],[5,3,5]]
  
  Binary search [0, 7]:
    
    Try effort=3:
      (0,0) -> (0,1): |2-1|=1 \ding{51}
      (0,1) -> (0,2): |2-2|=0 \ding{51}
      (0,2) -> (1,2): |2-2|=0 \ding{51}
      (1,2) -> (2,2): |5-2|=3 \ding{51}
      Reached! result=3, try smaller
    
    Try effort=1:
      (0,0) -> (0,1): |2-1|=1 \ding{51}
      (0,1) -> (0,2): |2-2|=0 \ding{51}
      (0,2) -> (1,2): |2-2|=0 \ding{51}
      (1,2) -> (2,2): |5-2|=3 > 1 \ding{55}
      Can't reach!
    
    Try effort=2:
      Similar analysis: Can't reach
    
    Answer: 3 (minimum effort)

Pattern: "Minimize maximum on path" -> BS on threshold + BFS
*/

// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 17.3: LeetCode 778 - Swim in Rising Water
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/swim-in-rising-water/
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 1900-2000)
//
// Problem Statement:
// You are given an n x n integer matrix grid where each value grid[i][j]
// represents the elevation at that point (i, j). The rain starts to fall.
// At time t, the depth of the water everywhere is t. You can swim from a
// square to another if both squares' elevations are at most t.
// You start at (0,0). Return the least time until you can reach (n-1,n-1).
//
// Technique: Binary Search on Time + BFS
// ==============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    bool canReach(vector<vector<int>>& grid, int time) {
        int n = grid.size();
        
        if (grid[0][0] > time) return false;
        
        vector<vector<bool>> visited(n, vector<bool>(n, false));
        queue<pair<int,int>> q;
        
        q.push({0, 0});
        visited[0][0] = true;
        
        int dx[] = {-1, 1, 0, 0};
        int dy[] = {0, 0, -1, 1};
        
        while (!q.empty()) {
            auto [x, y] = q.front();
            q.pop();
            
            if (x == n - 1 && y == n - 1) {
                return true;
            }
            
            for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) {
                int nx = x + dx[i];
                int ny = y + dy[i];
                
                if (nx >= 0 && nx < n && ny >= 0 && ny < n && 
                    !visited[nx][ny] && grid[nx][ny] <= time) {
                    visited[nx][ny] = true;
                    q.push({nx, ny});
                }
            }
        }
        
        return false;
    }
    
    int swimInWater(vector<vector<int>>& grid) {
        int n = grid.size();
        
        int low = grid[0][0];
        int high = n * n - 1;
        int result = high;
        
        while (low <= high) {
            int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
            
            if (canReach(grid, mid)) {
                result = mid;
                high = mid - 1;
            } else {
                low = mid + 1;
            }
        }
        
        return result;
    }
};

/*
SIMILAR PATTERN, DIFFERENT CONTEXT:

Problem Variation:
  Elevation acts as "time barrier"
  Can only visit cell when time >= elevation
  
  Same structure: Minimize maximum (time) on path

Difference from Effort:
  Effort: Adjacent cell differences
  Swim: Cell values themselves
  
  Both: Bottleneck optimization

Binary Search Range:
  low = grid[0][0] (must start here)
  high = n \times n-1 (max possible value)

Check Function:
  Include cell if elevation <= time
  BFS for reachability

Time: O($N^2$ \times log($N^2$)) = O($N^2$ \times log N)
Space: O($N^2$)

Pattern Recognition:
  "Find minimum time/threshold for reachability" -> BS + BFS
*/

// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 17.5: SPOJ MARYBMW - Mary and Her BMW
// Link: https://www.spoj.com/problems/MARYBMW/
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 2000-2100)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Mary has a BMW. There are N cities and M roads. Each road has a "quality"
// value. Mary wants to go from city 1 to city N, maximizing the minimum
// road quality on her path (she wants the worst road to be as good as possible).
//
// Technique: Binary Search on Minimum Quality + BFS (OPPOSITE pattern!)
// ==============================================================

bool canReachWithMinQuality(int n, vector<vector<pair<int,int>>>& adj, int minQuality) {
    // BFS using only edges with quality >= minQuality
    vector<bool> visited(n + 1, false);
    queue<int> q;
    
    q.push(1);
    visited[1] = true;
    
    while (!q.empty()) {
        int u = q.front();
        q.pop();
        
        if (u == n) return true;
        
        for (auto [v, quality] : adj[u]) {
            if (!visited[v] && quality >= minQuality) {
                visited[v] = true;
                q.push(v);
            }
        }
    }
    
    return false;
}

int maxMinQuality(int n, int m, vector<tuple<int,int,int>>& edges) {
    // Build adjacency list
    vector<vector<pair<int,int>>> adj(n + 1);
    int minQ = INT_MAX, maxQ = 0;
    
    for (auto [u, v, q] : edges) {
        adj[u].push_back({v, q});
        adj[v].push_back({u, q});
        minQ = min(minQ, q);
        maxQ = max(maxQ, q);
    }
    
    // Binary search on minimum quality (MAXIMIZE!)
    int low = minQ;
    int high = maxQ;
    int result = -1;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (canReachWithMinQuality(n, adj, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            low = mid + 1;  // Try LARGER minimum (maximize!)
        } else {
            high = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
OPPOSITE PATTERN: Maximize Minimum!

Key Difference:
  Previous: Minimize maximum (effort, time)
  This: Maximize minimum (quality)
  
  Both use same BS framework!

Update Direction:
  Minimize max: high = mid - 1 when found
  Maximize min: low = mid + 1 when found
  
  Opposite update!

Monotonicity:
  If reachable with min quality Q
  Then reachable with min quality Q-1
  
  Pattern: T T T F F F
  Find last True (maximum)

Check Function:
  Use edges with quality >= threshold
  Higher threshold -> fewer edges
  
  Opposite of minimize case!

Time: O((N + M) \times log(max_quality))
Space: O(N + M)

Pattern Recognition:
  "Maximize minimum on path" -> BS + BFS (update direction flipped)
  "Minimize maximum on path" -> BS + BFS (original pattern)

Real-World Applications:
  - Network bandwidth (maximize bottleneck)
  - Road quality optimization
  - Supply chain robustness
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 2: Binary Search with Dijkstra}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=lightgray,
    colframe=accentorange,
    boxrule=2pt,
    arc=4mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete BS + Shortest Path Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Distance Constraints + Edge Thresholds}

\textbf{Framework:}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Find path with distance ≤ D using edges ≤ W

Binary search on W
Check: Run Dijkstra, verify distance ≤ D

Time: O((V + E) log V × log W)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=BS + Dijkstra Solutions - NEW PROBLEM]
// ==============================================================
// PROBLEM 17.12: CF 1473E - Minimum Path
// Link: https://codeforces.com/problemset/problem/1473/E
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 2100-2200)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Given graph with N vertices and M edges. Each edge has weight. You want
// to go from 1 to N with minimum cost. However, you can perform one of:
//   1. Double one edge weight
//   2. Skip one edge (make it 0)
// Find minimum possible path cost.
//
// Technique: Modified Dijkstra with State Tracking
// ==============================================================

long long minimumPath(int n, vector<tuple<int,int,long long>>& edges) {
    // Build adjacency list
    vector<vector<pair<int,long long>>> adj(n + 1);
    
    for (auto [u, v, w] : edges) {
        adj[u].push_back({v, w});
        adj[v].push_back({u, w});
    }
    
    // State: (node, doubled_edge, skipped_edge)
    // dist[node][doubled][skipped] = minimum distance
    const long long INF = 1e18;
    vector<vector<vector<long long>>> dist(n + 1, 
        vector<vector<long long>>(2, vector<long long>(2, INF)));
    
    // Priority queue: (distance, node, doubled, skipped)
    priority_queue<tuple<long long,int,int,int>,
                   vector<tuple<long long,int,int,int>>,
                   greater<>> pq;
    
    dist[1][0][0] = 0;
    pq.push({0, 1, 0, 0});
    
    while (!pq.empty()) {
        auto [d, u, doubled, skipped] = pq.top();
        pq.pop();
        
        if (d > dist[u][doubled][skipped]) continue;
        
        for (auto [v, w] : adj[u]) {
            // Option 1: Normal edge
            long long newDist = d + w;
            if (newDist < dist[v][doubled][skipped]) {
                dist[v][doubled][skipped] = newDist;
                pq.push({newDist, v, doubled, skipped});
            }
            
            // Option 2: Double this edge (if not done before)
            if (!doubled) {
                newDist = d + 2 * w;
                if (newDist < dist[v][1][skipped]) {
                    dist[v][1][skipped] = newDist;
                    pq.push({newDist, v, 1, skipped});
                }
            }
            
            // Option 3: Skip this edge (if not done before)
            if (!skipped) {
                newDist = d;  // Edge cost = 0
                if (newDist < dist[v][doubled][1]) {
                    dist[v][doubled][1] = newDist;
                    pq.push({newDist, v, doubled, 1});
                }
            }
            
            // Option 4: Double and skip different edges
            if (!doubled && !skipped) {
                // Skip this one
                newDist = d;
                if (newDist < dist[v][doubled][1]) {
                    dist[v][doubled][1] = newDist;
                    pq.push({newDist, v, doubled, 1});
                }
            }
        }
    }
    
    // Find minimum among all states at node N
    long long result = INF;
    for (int d = 0; d < 2; d++) {
        for (int s = 0; s < 2; s++) {
            result = min(result, dist[n][d][s]);
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
ADVANCED DIJKSTRA WITH STATES:

State Expansion:
  Normal Dijkstra: dist[node]
  This: dist[node][doubled][skipped]
  
  Track: Which operations used

Transitions:
  From (u, doubled, skipped):
    1. Normal edge: (v, doubled, skipped)
    2. Double edge: (v, 1, skipped) if !doubled
    3. Skip edge: (v, doubled, 1) if !skipped

Why This Works:
  All possible operation combinations explored
  Dijkstra guarantees shortest for each state
  
  Answer: Min over all states at destination

Complexity:
  States: O(V \times 2 \times 2) = O(4V)
  Dijkstra: O((V + E) log V) per state
  
  Total: O((V + E) log V)
  Same as normal Dijkstra!

Space: O(V) for states

This pattern extends to:
  - Multiple operation types
  - K operations allowed
  - Different constraints

Pattern: "Shortest path with operations" -> State-based Dijkstra
*/
\end{lstlisting}
