\end{center}

\section{Descending the Tree}

\subsection{Segment Tree Walking}
Instead of calling a query function inside a binary search (which is $O(\log^2 N)$), we can "walk" down the segment tree. At each node, we decide whether to go left or right based on the values stored in the children (e.g., if the sum of the left child is $\geq K$, we go left; otherwise, we subtract the left sum from $K$ and go right). This is $O(\log N)$.



\subsection{Persistent Segment Trees}
Persistent Segment Trees allow us to access the state of an array after $i$ updates. To find the $k$-th smallest number in a range $[L, R]$, we use the root of version $R$ and version $L-1$, and binary search on the difference between their node counts as we descend.

\section{Binary Search with Data Structures: Complete Mastery}

\subsection{Complete Theoretical Foundation}

\subsubsection{Why Combine BS with Data Structures?}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=white,
    colframe=deepblue,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{The Ultimate Optimization Paradigm}
]
\textbf{Data Structures + Binary Search = Ultimate Power}

Problem: Efficient queries on complex data
Solution: Combine structural power with search efficiency

\textbf{Three Revolutionary Patterns:}
\begin{verbatim}
Pattern 1: Tree Walking (Segment Trees)
  Problem: BS inside segment tree query
  Naive: O(log² N) - BS on each node
  Walking: O(log N) - Decide left/right while descending
  
  Example: Find first element ≥ K
    Instead of: query(left) + query(right) repeatedly
    Do: Walk down tree, checking sums at each node

Pattern 2: Persistent Structures
  Problem: Query on historical versions
  Naive: Store all versions - O(N × versions) space
  Persistent: Share unchanged parts - O(N log N) space
  
  Example: K-th smallest in range [L,R]
    Compare version[R] with version[L-1]
    Binary search on difference!

Pattern 3: Merge Sort Trees + BS
  Problem: Range queries with ordering
  Naive: Sort each query range - O(N log N) per query
  MST: Preprocess - O(N log N), query - O(log² N)
  
  With FC: Can reduce to O(log N) per query!

KEY BREAKTHROUGH:
  DS provides structure
  BS provides search efficiency
  Together: Solve previously impossible problems!
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Complete Theory - Segment Tree Walking:}

1. \textbf{Classic vs Walking Approach}
\begin{verbatim}
Problem: Find first index where prefix_sum ≥ K

Classic Segment Tree Query:
  binarySearch(1, N):
    mid = (low + high) / 2
    if query(1, mid) >= K: high = mid
    else: low = mid + 1
  
  Time: O(log N) BS iterations × O(log N) per query
       = O(log² N)

Tree Walking:
  node = root
  remaining = K
  
  while not leaf:
    if left_child_sum >= remaining:
      node = left_child
    else:
      remaining -= left_child_sum
      node = right_child
  
  return node.index
  
  Time: O(log N) - single descent!

Speedup: log N factor (20X for N=10^6)
\end{verbatim}

2. \textbf{Persistent Segment Trees - Complete Theory}
\begin{verbatim}
Core Idea: Version Control for Trees

Construction:
  Version 0: Empty tree
  Version i: After i-th update
  
  Update creates NEW nodes on path from root to leaf
  Other nodes SHARED with previous version
  
  Space per version: O(log N) new nodes
  Total space for V versions: O(V log N)

Path Copying:
  To update position p in version i:
    1. Create new root
    2. Copy nodes on path to p
    3. Share all other nodes from version i
  
  Result: Version i+1 with updated value

K-th Smallest in Range [L,R]:
  Use version[R] and version[L-1]
  
  At each node:
    diff = count[R][node] - count[L-1][node]
    if diff in left child >= K:
      Go left
    else:
      K -= diff in left child
      Go right
  
  Time: O(log(max_value))
  Space: O(N log(max_value))

Applications:
  - Range K-th queries
  - Historical data access
  - Functional data structures
  - Parallel algorithms
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Key Insight:}
BS + DS = Solve problems impossible with either alone!
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Pattern 1: Segment Tree Walking}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=10+ COMPLETE DETAILED SOLUTIONS]
// ===============================================================
// DETAILED SOLUTION 1: CF 438D - The Child and Sequence
// Link: https://codeforces.com/problemset/problem/438/D
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 2200-2300)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Array of N integers. Three operations:
//   1. Sum of range [L,R]
//   2. Modulo: arr[i] %= x for all i in [L,R]
//   3. Update: arr[i] = x
//
// Key insight: After mod operation, value can only decrease
// Maximum decreases by at least half each time
//
// Technique: Segment Tree with Lazy Propagation + Early Termination
// ===============================================================

struct SegmentTreeModulo {
    struct Node {
        long long sum;
        int maxVal;
    };
    
    vector<Node> tree;
    int n;
    
    SegmentTreeModulo(vector<int>& arr) : n(arr.size()), tree(4 * n) {
        build(arr, 1, 0, n-1);
    }
    
    void build(vector<int>& arr, int node, int l, int r) {
        if (l == r) {
            tree[node].sum = arr[l];
            tree[node].maxVal = arr[l];
            return;
        }
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        build(arr, 2*node, l, mid);
        build(arr, 2*node+1, mid+1, r);
        
        tree[node].sum = tree[2*node].sum + tree[2*node+1].sum;
        tree[node].maxVal = max(tree[2*node].maxVal, tree[2*node+1].maxVal);
    }
    
    void rangeModulo(int node, int l, int r, int qL, int qR, int mod) {
        if (r < qL || l > qR) return;
        
        // Key optimization: If max < mod, no change needed!
        if (tree[node].maxVal < mod) return;
        
        if (l == r) {
            tree[node].sum %= mod;
            tree[node].maxVal %= mod;
            return;
        }
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        rangeModulo(2*node, l, mid, qL, qR, mod);
        rangeModulo(2*node+1, mid+1, r, qL, qR, mod);
        
        tree[node].sum = tree[2*node].sum + tree[2*node+1].sum;
        tree[node].maxVal = max(tree[2*node].maxVal, tree[2*node+1].maxVal);
    }
    
    long long rangeSum(int node, int l, int r, int qL, int qR) {
        if (r < qL || l > qR) return 0;
        if (qL <= l && r <= qR) return tree[node].sum;
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        return rangeSum(2*node, l, mid, qL, qR) + 
               rangeSum(2*node+1, mid+1, r, qL, qR);
    }
    
    void update(int node, int l, int r, int pos, int val) {
        if (l == r) {
            tree[node].sum = val;
            tree[node].maxVal = val;
            return;
        }
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        if (pos <= mid) update(2*node, l, mid, pos, val);
        else update(2*node+1, mid+1, r, pos, val);
        
        tree[node].sum = tree[2*node].sum + tree[2*node+1].sum;
        tree[node].maxVal = max(tree[2*node].maxVal, tree[2*node+1].maxVal);
    }
};

/*
COMPLETE ANALYSIS:

Problem Difficulty:
  Range modulo seems expensive: O(N) per operation
  Naive: O(QN) total - TLE for Q,N=10^5

Key Insight: Value Halving
  After x %= m:
    If x >= m: x becomes x%m < m (at least halved)
    If x < m: x unchanged
  
  Each element can be halved at most log(max_val) times
  After log(max_val) mods: All values small
  
  Further mods: Many early terminations!

Optimization: Track Maximum
  Store max value in each segment
  If max < mod: Entire segment unchanged!
  
  This enables massive pruning!

Complexity Analysis:
  Each element modified at most log($10^9$) = 30 times
  Each mod: O(log N) to find elements
  Total mods: O(N \times 30 \times log N) = O(N log N log(max))
  
  Amortized per operation: O(log N log(max))

Why This Works:
  Values decrease exponentially with mods
  Max tracking allows skipping unchanged segments
  
  Amortized complexity much better than worst case!

Implementation Details:
  1. Store both sum and max in each node
  2. Check max before recursing
  3. Early return if max < mod
  4. Update both sum and max on changes

Time: O(Q log N log(max_val))
Space: O(N)

Real-World Applications:
  - Rate limiting (mod for overflow)
  - Cryptographic operations
  - Hash table resizing

This tests:
  [$\checkmark$] Segment tree mastery
  [$\checkmark$] Amortized analysis
  [$\checkmark$] Optimization techniques
  [$\checkmark$] Early termination logic
*/

// ===============================================================
// DETAILED SOLUTION 2: CSES 1143 - Hotel Queries
// Link: https://cses.fi/problemset/task/1143
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1900-2000)
//
// Problem Statement:
// N hotels with capacity h[i]. M groups arrive with size r[j].
// For each group, assign to first hotel with enough capacity.
// Update hotel capacity after assignment. Output hotel number or 0 if none.
//
// Technique: Segment Tree Walking
// ===============================================================

struct HotelSegmentTree {
    vector<int> tree;  // tree[node] = max capacity in range
    int n;
    
    HotelSegmentTree(vector<int>& hotels) : n(hotels.size()), tree(4 * n) {
        build(hotels, 1, 0, n-1);
    }
    
    void build(vector<int>& arr, int node, int l, int r) {
        if (l == r) {
            tree[node] = arr[l];
            return;
        }
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        build(arr, 2*node, l, mid);
        build(arr, 2*node+1, mid+1, r);
        
        tree[node] = max(tree[2*node], tree[2*node+1]);
    }
    
    int findAndUpdate(int node, int l, int r, int required) {
        // Tree walking: Find first hotel with capacity >= required
        
        if (tree[node] < required) return -1;  // No hotel in this range
        
        if (l == r) {
            // Found the hotel!
            tree[node] -= required;  // Update capacity
            return l;
        }
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        
        // Try left child first
        if (tree[2*node] >= required) {
            int result = findAndUpdate(2*node, l, mid, required);
            tree[node] = max(tree[2*node], tree[2*node+1]);
            return result;
        }
        
        // Left doesn't have capacity, try right
        int result = findAndUpdate(2*node+1, mid+1, r, required);
        tree[node] = max(tree[2*node], tree[2*node+1]);
        return result;
    }
    
    int assignGroup(int groupSize) {
        int hotel = findAndUpdate(1, 0, n-1, groupSize);
        return hotel == -1 ? 0 : hotel + 1;  // 1-indexed
    }
};

/*
TREE WALKING MASTERY:

Classic Approach (Binary Search + Query):
  for each group:
    Binary search on hotel index:
      Check if any hotel in [1, mid] has capacity
      This requires segment tree query: O(log N)
    Total: O(log N) BS \times O(log N) query = O($(log N)^2$)

Tree Walking:
  Walk down tree, deciding left/right based on max capacity
  Update while backtracking
  Time: O(log N) per group!

Algorithm:
  1. Check if current node has capacity
  2. If leaf: Assign and update
  3. If internal: Try left first, then right
  4. Update max while backtracking

Why Walking is Better:
  Decision at each level: O(1)
  Total levels: O(log N)
  No redundant queries!

Correctness:
  Always try left first: Guarantees first hotel
  Max tracking: Ensures capacity check
  Update on backtrack: Maintains invariant

Time: O(M log N) for M groups
Space: O(N)

Comparison:
  Binary Search: O(M $(log N)^2$)
  Tree Walking: O(M log N)
  Speedup: log N factor!

Real-World:
  - Resource allocation
  - Memory management
  - Load balancing

This tests:
  [$\checkmark$] Tree walking technique
  [$\checkmark$] Online algorithms
  [$\checkmark$] Greedy with DS
  [$\checkmark$] Implementation precision
*/

// ===============================================================
// DETAILED SOLUTION 3: SPOJ MKTHNUM - K-th Number (Persistent Segment Tree)
// Link: https://www.spoj.com/problems/MKTHNUM/
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 2300-2400)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Array of N integers. Q queries: Find K-th smallest in range [L,R].
// N, Q up to 10^5. Values up to 10^9.
//
// Technique: Persistent Segment Tree + Coordinate Compression
// ===============================================================

struct PersistentSegmentTree {
    struct Node {
        int count;
        Node *left, *right;
        
        Node() : count(0), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
        Node(int c, Node* l, Node* r) : count(c), left(l), right(r) {}
    };
    
    vector<Node*> roots;
    vector<int> sorted;  // Sorted unique values
    
    Node* build(int l, int r) {
        Node* node = new Node();
        if (l == r) return node;
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        node->left = build(l, mid);
        node->right = build(mid+1, r);
        return node;
    }
    
    Node* update(Node* prev, int l, int r, int pos) {
        if (l == r) {
            return new Node(prev->count + 1, nullptr, nullptr);
        }
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        Node* node = new Node();
        
        if (pos <= mid) {
            node->left = update(prev->left, l, mid, pos);
            node->right = prev->right;  // Share right subtree
        } else {
            node->left = prev->left;  // Share left subtree
            node->right = update(prev->right, mid+1, r, pos);
        }
        
        node->count = node->left->count + node->right->count;
        return node;
    }
    
    int kthSmallest(Node* vL, Node* vR, int l, int r, int k) {
        if (l == r) return l;
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        // Count in left child = difference between versions
        int leftCount = vR->left->count - vL->left->count;
        
        if (k <= leftCount) {
            return kthSmallest(vL->left, vR->left, l, mid, k);
        } else {
            return kthSmallest(vL->right, vR->right, mid+1, r, k - leftCount);
        }
    }
    
    void buildPersistent(vector<int>& arr) {
        int n = arr.size();
        
        // Coordinate compression
        sorted = arr;
        sort(sorted.begin(), sorted.end());
        sorted.erase(unique(sorted.begin(), sorted.end()), sorted.end());
        
        int m = sorted.size();
        roots.resize(n + 1);
        roots[0] = build(0, m - 1);
        
        // Build versions
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            int compressed = lower_bound(sorted.begin(), sorted.end(), arr[i]) 
                           - sorted.begin();
            roots[i + 1] = update(roots[i], 0, m - 1, compressed);
        }
    }
    
    int queryKth(int L, int R, int K) {
        int m = sorted.size();
        int compressed = kthSmallest(roots[L-1], roots[R], 0, m - 1, K);
        return sorted[compressed];
    }
};

/*
PERSISTENT SEGMENT TREE MASTERY:

Why Persistent?
  Need to query historical states (prefix [1,L-1] and [1,R])
  Building new tree per query: O(QN log N) - TLE!
  
  Persistent: Share unchanged parts - O(N log M) total space

Version Construction:
  Version 0: Empty (all counts = 0)
  Version i: After adding arr[i]
  
  Adding element: Update O(log M) nodes on path
  Other nodes: Shared with previous version!

Query Algorithm:
  For range [L,R], use version[R] and version[L-1]
  
  At each level:
    diff_left = count[R].left - count[L-1].left
    
    if K <= diff_left:
      Go left (K-th is in left subtree)
    else:
      Go right (K-th is (K - diff_left)-th in right)
  
  Continue until leaf!

Coordinate Compression:
  Values up to 10^9: Can't use as indices
  Compress to [0, unique_count)
  
  Map back to original values when returning

Space Optimization:
  Each version: O(log M) new nodes
  N versions: O(N log M) space
  
  Much better than O(NM) for full trees!

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N log M + Q log M)
  Build: O(N log M)
  Each query: O(log M)
  
  M = number of unique values <= N

Space: O(N log M)

Alternative Approaches:
  1. Sort each query range: O(Q \times N log N) - TLE!
  2. Merge sort tree: O(N log N + Q $(log N)^2$) - Slower
  3. Fractional cascading: O(N log N + Q log N) - Complex!
  
  Persistent ST: Simpler and fast enough!

Real-World Applications:
  - Version control systems
  - Database snapshots
  - Time-travel debugging
  - Functional programming

This tests:
  [$\checkmark$] Persistent data structures
  [$\checkmark$] Path copying
  [$\checkmark$] Coordinate compression
  [$\checkmark$] Binary search on tree
*/

// ===============================================================
// DETAILED SOLUTION 4: CF 484E - Sign on Fence (Persistent ST)
// Link: https://codeforces.com/problemset/problem/484/E
// Difficulty: Very Hard (CF 2400-2500)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Fence with N planks of heights h[i]. M queries: Find maximum length of
// consecutive planks in range [L,R] with all heights >= W.
//
// Technique: Persistent Segment Tree + Merge Info
// ===============================================================

struct FencePersistentST {
    struct Node {
        int maxLen;      // Maximum consecutive length
        int leftLen;     // Length from left boundary
        int rightLen;    // Length from right boundary
        int totalLen;    // Total length of segment
        Node *left, *right;
        
        Node() : maxLen(0), leftLen(0), rightLen(0), totalLen(0),
                left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
    };
    
    vector<Node*> roots;
    
    Node* merge(Node* l, Node* r) {
        if (!l || !r) return l ? l : r;
        
        Node* node = new Node();
        node->totalLen = l->totalLen + r->totalLen;
        
        node->maxLen = max({l->maxLen, r->maxLen, 
                           l->rightLen + r->leftLen});
        
        node->leftLen = l->leftLen;
        if (l->leftLen == l->totalLen) {
            node->leftLen += r->leftLen;
        }
        
        node->rightLen = r->rightLen;
        if (r->rightLen == r->totalLen) {
            node->rightLen += l->rightLen;
        }
        
        return node;
    }
    
    Node* build(int l, int r) {
        Node* node = new Node();
        if (l == r) {
            node->totalLen = 1;
            return node;
        }
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        node->left = build(l, mid);
        node->right = build(mid+1, r);
        return merge(node->left, node->right);
    }
    
    Node* update(Node* prev, int l, int r, int pos) {
        if (l == r) {
            Node* node = new Node();
            node->maxLen = node->leftLen = node->rightLen = node->totalLen = 1;
            return node;
        }
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        Node* node = new Node();
        
        if (pos <= mid) {
            node->left = update(prev->left, l, mid, pos);
            node->right = prev->right;
        } else {
            node->left = prev->left;
            node->right = update(prev->right, mid+1, r, pos);
        }
        
        return merge(node->left, node->right);
    }
    
    Node* query(Node* node, int l, int r, int qL, int qR) {
        if (r < qL || l > qR) return nullptr;
        if (qL <= l && r <= qR) return node;
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        Node* leftResult = query(node->left, l, mid, qL, qR);
        Node* rightResult = query(node->right, mid+1, r, qL, qR);
        
        return merge(leftResult, rightResult);
    }
    
    void buildByHeight(vector<pair<int,int>>& heightPos, int n) {
        // Sort by height descending
        sort(heightPos.begin(), heightPos.end(), greater<>());
        
        roots.resize(heightPos.size() + 1);
        roots[0] = build(0, n - 1);
        
        for (int i = 0; i < heightPos.size(); i++) {
            int pos = heightPos[i].second;
            roots[i + 1] = update(roots[i], 0, n - 1, pos);
        }
    }
    
    int queryFence(int n, int L, int R, int minHeight, 
                   vector<pair<int,int>>& heightPos) {
        // Binary search on versions (heights)
        int left = 0, right = heightPos.size() - 1;
        int bestVersion = -1;
        
        while (left <= right) {
            int mid = (left + right) / 2;
            if (heightPos[mid].first >= minHeight) {
                bestVersion = mid;
                left = mid + 1;
            } else {
                right = mid - 1;
            }
        }
        
        if (bestVersion == -1) return 0;
        
        Node* result = query(roots[bestVersion + 1], 0, n - 1, L, R);
        return result ? result->maxLen : 0;
    }
};

/*
ADVANCED PERSISTENT ST:

Problem Complexity:
  Range query + Height threshold + Consecutive length
  Three constraints simultaneously!

Offline Processing:
  Sort queries by height descending
  Build versions: Each adds one plank
  
  Version i: All planks with height >= i-th height

Node Information:
  maxLen: Maximum consecutive in segment
  leftLen: Consecutive from left edge
  rightLen: Consecutive from right edge
  
  These allow merging segments correctly!

Merge Logic:
  When merging [L] and [R]:
    maxLen = max(L.max, R.max, L.right + R.left)
    leftLen = L.left (or L.total + R.left if L full)
    rightLen = R.right (or R.total + L.right if R full)

Query Process:
  1. Find version where all planks >= minHeight
  2. Query range [L,R] in that version
  3. Return maxLen

Why Persistent Needed:
  Must query different height thresholds
  Each threshold = different tree state
  
  Persistent allows accessing all states!

Time: O((N+Q) log N)
  Build: O(N log N)
  Each query: O(log N) for version + O(log N) for range

Space: O(N log N)

This tests:
  [$\checkmark$] Complex node information
  [$\checkmark$] Merge operations
  [$\checkmark$] Offline query processing
  [$\checkmark$] Persistent tree mastery
*/

// ===============================================================
// DETAILED SOLUTION 5: Wavelet Tree - Range K-th Queries
// Difficulty: Very Hard (CF 2500+)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Array of N integers. Q queries: Find K-th smallest in range [L,R].
// Space-efficient solution using Wavelet Tree.
//
// Technique: Wavelet Tree (Recursive Bitmask Partitioning)
// ===============================================================

struct WaveletTree {
    int low, high;
    WaveletTree *left, *right;
    vector<int> counts;  // Prefix counts of 0s
    
    WaveletTree(vector<int>& arr, int l, int r, int _low, int _high) 
        : low(_low), high(_high), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {
        
        if (low == high || l > r) return;
        
        int mid = (low + high) / 2;
        counts.resize(r - l + 2);
        counts[0] = 0;
        
        // Count elements going to left child
        for (int i = l; i <= r; i++) {
            counts[i - l + 1] = counts[i - l] + (arr[i] <= mid);
        }
        
        // Partition array
        vector<int> leftArr, rightArr;
        for (int i = l; i <= r; i++) {
            if (arr[i] <= mid) leftArr.push_back(arr[i]);
            else rightArr.push_back(arr[i]);
        }
        
        // Build children
        if (!leftArr.empty()) {
            left = new WaveletTree(leftArr, 0, leftArr.size()-1, low, mid);
        }
        if (!rightArr.empty()) {
            right = new WaveletTree(rightArr, 0, rightArr.size()-1, mid+1, high);
        }
    }
    
    int kthSmallest(int l, int r, int k) {
        if (low == high) return low;
        
        int leftCount = counts[r+1] - counts[l];
        int mid = (low + high) / 2;
        
        if (k <= leftCount) {
            // K-th is in left subtree
            int newL = counts[l];
            int newR = counts[r+1] - 1;
            return left->kthSmallest(newL, newR, k);
        } else {
            // K-th is in right subtree
            int newL = l - counts[l];
            int newR = r - counts[r+1];
            return right->kthSmallest(newL, newR, k - leftCount);
        }
    }
    
    int countLess(int l, int r, int k) {
        if (low == high) return 0;
        if (k <= low) return 0;
        if (k > high) return r - l + 1;
        
        int mid = (low + high) / 2;
        int leftCount = counts[r+1] - counts[l];
        
        if (k <= mid) {
            int newL = counts[l];
            int newR = counts[r+1] - 1;
            return left->countLess(newL, newR, k);
        } else {
            int newL = l - counts[l];
            int newR = r - counts[r+1];
            return leftCount + right->countLess(newL, newR, k);
        }
    }
};

/*
WAVELET TREE THEORY:

Core Idea:
  Recursively partition array by value
  At each level: Split by median
  
  Similar to merge sort tree but space-efficient!

Structure:
  Each node represents value range [low, high]
  Stores: Which elements go left (<= mid) vs right (> mid)
  
  Encoding: Bitmap + prefix counts

Query Mechanics:
  To find K-th smallest in [L,R]:
    Count elements in left child: C
    If K <= C: Recurse left
    Else: Recurse right with K-C

Mapping Indices:
  From parent range [L,R] to child:
    Left child: [counts[L], counts[R+1]-1]
    Right child: [L-counts[L], R-counts[R+1]]
  
  Prefix counts enable O(1) mapping!

Space: O(N log(max_val))
  Each element appears once per level
  Height = log(max_val)

Time: O(log(max_val)) per query
  One level per bit of value range

Comparison with Persistent ST:
  Wavelet: O(N log V) space, O(log V) query
  Persistent: O(N log N) space, O(log N) query
  
  For V << N: Wavelet better space
  For N << V: Persistent better query

Applications:
  - Range quantile queries
  - Compression algorithms
  - Succinct data structures

This tests:
  [$\checkmark$] Wavelet tree implementation
  [$\checkmark$] Bitmap manipulation
  [$\checkmark$] Index mapping
  [$\checkmark$] Space optimization
*/

// ===============================================================
// DETAILED SOLUTION 6: CF 1216F - Wi-Fi
// Link: https://codeforces.com/problemset/problem/1216/F
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 2300)
//
// Problem Statement:
// N positions. Position i can have router (cost i) with range i.
// Each position must be covered by some router.
// Find minimum cost to cover all positions.
//
// Technique: DP + Segment Tree for Range Minimum Query
// ===============================================================

long long minimumCostWiFi(int n, vector<int>& range) {
    // dp[i] = minimum cost to cover [1, i]
    vector<long long> dp(n + 1, LLONG_MAX);
    dp[0] = 0;
    
    // Segment tree for range minimum
    vector<long long> tree(4 * n, LLONG_MAX);
    
    function<void(int, int, int, int, long long)> update = 
        [&](int node, int l, int r, int pos, long long val) {
        if (l == r) {
            tree[node] = min(tree[node], val);
            return;
        }
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        if (pos <= mid) update(2*node, l, mid, pos, val);
        else update(2*node+1, mid+1, r, pos, val);
        
        tree[node] = min(tree[2*node], tree[2*node+1]);
    };
    
    function<long long(int, int, int, int, int)> query = 
        [&](int node, int l, int r, int qL, int qR) -> long long {
        if (r < qL || l > qR) return LLONG_MAX;
        if (qL <= l && r <= qR) return tree[node];
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        return min(query(2*node, l, mid, qL, qR),
                  query(2*node+1, mid+1, r, qL, qR));
    };
    
    update(1, 0, n, 0, 0);
    
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        // Router at position i covers [max(1, i-range[i]), i+range[i]]
        int left = max(0, i - range[i-1] - 1);
        int right = i - 1;
        
        // Find minimum dp[j] for j in [left, right]
        long long minPrev = query(1, 0, n, left, right);
        
        if (minPrev != LLONG_MAX) {
            dp[i] = minPrev + i;
            update(1, 0, n, i, dp[i]);
        }
    }
    
    return dp[n];
}

/*
DP WITH SEGMENT TREE OPTIMIZATION:

DP Formulation:
  dp[i] = min cost to cover positions [1,i]
  
  Transition:
    Place router at position i:
      Covers [i-range[i], i+range[i]]
      Need to cover [1, i-range[i]-1] first
      Cost = dp[j] + i where j \in [i-range[i]-1, i-1]
  
  dp[i] = min(dp[j] + i) for valid j

Without Segment Tree:
  For each i: Scan all valid j
  Time: O($N^2$) - TLE for N=10^6

With Segment Tree:
  Maintain min dp[j] in segment tree
  Range minimum query: O(log N)
  Total: O(N log N)

Why Segment Tree:
  Need range minimum efficiently
  Updates: Add new dp[i]
  Queries: Find min in range
  
  Perfect for segment tree!

Time: O(N log N)
Space: O(N)

This tests:
  [$\checkmark$] DP optimization with DS
  [$\checkmark$] Segment tree RMQ
  [$\checkmark$] Transition design
*/

// ===============================================================
// DETAILED SOLUTION 7: CF 914D - Bash and a Tough Puzzle
// Link: https://codeforces.com/problemset/problem/914/D
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 2300-2400)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Array of N integers. Two types of queries:
//   1. Check if range [L,R] can have GCD=x by changing at most 1 element
//   2. Update: arr[pos] = x
//
// Technique: Segment Tree with GCD + Counting
// ===============================================================

struct GCDSegmentTree {
    vector<int> tree;
    vector<int> arr;
    int n;
    
    int gcd(int a, int b) {
        return b == 0 ? a : gcd(b, a % b);
    }
    
    void build(int node, int l, int r) {
        if (l == r) {
            tree[node] = arr[l];
            return;
        }
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        build(2*node, l, mid);
        build(2*node+1, mid+1, r);
        
        tree[node] = gcd(tree[2*node], tree[2*node+1]);
    }
    
    int rangeGCD(int node, int l, int r, int qL, int qR) {
        if (r < qL || l > qR) return 0;
        if (qL <= l && r <= qR) return tree[node];
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        return gcd(rangeGCD(2*node, l, mid, qL, qR),
                  rangeGCD(2*node+1, mid+1, r, qL, qR));
    }
    
    int countBad(int node, int l, int r, int qL, int qR, int target) {
        if (r < qL || l > qR) return 0;
        
        // If segment GCD divisible by target, all elements OK
        if (tree[node] % target == 0) return 0;
        
        // If leaf and not divisible, this element is bad
        if (l == r) return 1;
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        return countBad(2*node, l, mid, qL, qR, target) +
               countBad(2*node+1, mid+1, r, qL, qR, target);
    }
    
    bool canMakeGCD(int L, int R, int target) {
        // Count elements not divisible by target
        int bad = countBad(1, 0, n-1, L, R, target);
        return bad <= 1;  // Can change at most 1
    }
    
    void update(int node, int l, int r, int pos, int val) {
        if (l == r) {
            arr[l] = val;
            tree[node] = val;
            return;
        }
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        if (pos <= mid) update(2*node, l, mid, pos, val);
        else update(2*node+1, mid+1, r, pos, val);
        
        tree[node] = gcd(tree[2*node], tree[2*node+1]);
    }
};

/*
GCD SEGMENT TREE OPTIMIZATION:

Key Insight:
  If GCD of range = x: All elements divisible by x
  If not: Count non-divisible elements
  
  If count <= 1: Can achieve by changing that one!

Optimization: Early Termination
  If segment GCD divisible by target:
    All elements in segment are divisible
    No need to recurse further!
  
  This gives massive pruning!

Counting Algorithm:
  countBad(L, R, x):
    If gcd(L, R) % x == 0: return 0
    If leaf: return 1
    Else: return countBad(left) + countBad(right)

Amortized Analysis:
  Each element checked at most once per query
  Early termination skips entire subtrees
  
  Average: Much faster than O(N)

Time per query: O(log N) average, O(N) worst
Update: O(log N)

This tests:
  [$\checkmark$] GCD properties
  [$\checkmark$] Segment tree optimization
  [$\checkmark$] Early termination
  [$\checkmark$] Amortized analysis
*/

// ===============================================================
// DETAILED SOLUTION 8: Merge Sort Tree with Binary Search
// Link: SPOJ KQUERY
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 2100-2200)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Array of N integers. Q queries: Count elements > K in range [L,R].
//
// Technique: Merge Sort Tree + Binary Search per Node
// ===============================================================

struct MergeSortTree {
    vector<vector<int>> tree;
    int n;
    
    void build(vector<int>& arr, int node, int l, int r) {
        if (l == r) {
            tree[node] = {arr[l]};
            return;
        }
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        build(arr, 2*node, l, mid);
        build(arr, 2*node+1, mid+1, r);
        
        // Merge sorted lists
        tree[node].resize(tree[2*node].size() + tree[2*node+1].size());
        merge(tree[2*node].begin(), tree[2*node].end(),
              tree[2*node+1].begin(), tree[2*node+1].end(),
              tree[node].begin());
    }
    
    int queryCount(int node, int l, int r, int qL, int qR, int K) {
        if (r < qL || l > qR) return 0;
        
        if (qL <= l && r <= qR) {
            // Binary search in this node's sorted list
            int pos = upper_bound(tree[node].begin(), tree[node].end(), K)
                     - tree[node].begin();
            return tree[node].size() - pos;
        }
        
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        return queryCount(2*node, l, mid, qL, qR, K) +
               queryCount(2*node+1, mid+1, r, qL, qR, K);
    }
};

/*
MERGE SORT TREE:

Structure:
  Each node stores sorted list of elements in range
  Size: O(N log N) total
  
  Build: Merge children's sorted lists

Query:
  Decompose range into O(log N) nodes
  Each node: Binary search for > K
  Total: O($(log N)^2$)

With Fractional Cascading:
  Can reduce to O(log N) per query!
  
  This chapter: Classic O($(log N)^2$) version
  Chapter 18: FC-optimized O(log N) version

Space: O(N log N)
Build: O(N $(log N)^2$)
Query: O($(log N)^2$)

Applications:
  - Range counting queries
  - Order statistics
  - Preprocessing for multiple queries

This tests:
  [$\checkmark$] Merge sort tree structure
  [$\checkmark$] Binary search in nodes
  [$\checkmark$] Query decomposition
*/

// ===============================================================
// DETAILED SOLUTION 9: 2D Range Counting with Fenwick Tree
// Link: CF 1093E - Intersection of Permutations
// Difficulty: Very Hard (CF 2500-2600)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Two permutations A and B of [1,N]. Q queries:
//   1. \[\text{Count common elements: } \left| A[l_1 \dots r_1] \cap B[l_2 \dots r_2] \right|\]
//   2. Swap B[i] and B[i+1]
//
// Technique: Coordinate Transform + 2D Fenwick Tree
// ===============================================================

struct Fenwick2D {
    vector<vector<int>> tree;
    int n;
    
    Fenwick2D(int size) : n(size), tree(size + 1, vector<int>(size + 1, 0)) {}
    
    void update(int x, int y, int delta) {
        for (int i = x; i <= n; i += i & (-i)) {
            for (int j = y; j <= n; j += j & (-j)) {
                tree[i][j] += delta;
            }
        }
    }
    
    int query(int x, int y) {
        int sum = 0;
        for (int i = x; i > 0; i -= i & (-i)) {
            for (int j = y; j > 0; j -= j & (-j)) {
                sum += tree[i][j];
            }
        }
        return sum;
    }
    
    int rangeQuery(int x1, int y1, int x2, int y2) {
        return query(x2, y2) - query(x1-1, y2) 
             - query(x2, y1-1) + query(x1-1, y1-1);
    }
};

struct PermutationIntersection {
    Fenwick2D fenwick;
    vector<int> posA, posB;  // posA[val] = position in A
    int n;
    
    PermutationIntersection(vector<int>& A, vector<int>& B) 
        : n(A.size()), fenwick(n), posA(n+1), posB(n+1) {
        
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            posA[A[i]] = i + 1;
            posB[B[i]] = i + 1;
        }
        
        // Add all elements as points
        for (int val = 1; val <= n; val++) {
            fenwick.update(posA[val], posB[val], 1);
        }
    }
    
    int countIntersection(int l1, int r1, int l2, int r2) {
        return fenwick.rangeQuery(l1, l2, r1, r2);
    }
    
    void swapInB(int i) {
        int val1 = /* element at B[i] */;
        int val2 = /* element at B[i+1] */;
        
        // Remove old positions
        fenwick.update(posA[val1], posB[val1], -1);
        fenwick.update(posA[val2], posB[val2], -1);
        
        // Update positions
        swap(posB[val1], posB[val2]);
        
        // Add new positions
        fenwick.update(posA[val1], posB[val1], 1);
        fenwick.update(posA[val2], posB[val2], 1);
    }
};

/*
2D COORDINATE TRANSFORMATION:

Key Insight:
  Transform to 2D point problem!
  
  Each value v: Point at (posA[v], posB[v])
  
  Intersection: Count points in rectangle
    [l1, r1] \times [l2, r2]

Fenwick Tree 2D:
  Update: Add/remove point at (x, y)
  Query: Count points in rectangle
  
  Time: O($(log N)^2$) per operation

Why This Works:
  Value in both ranges <==> Point in rectangle
  
  Geometric interpretation enables 2D DS!

Swap Handling:
  Swap B[i], B[i+1]:
    Remove old points
    Update coordinates
    Add new points
  
  3 operations: O($(log N)^2$)

Time: O(Q $(log N)^2$)
Space: O($N^2$) for 2D Fenwick

This tests:
  [$\checkmark$] Coordinate transformation
  [$\checkmark$] 2D data structures
  [$\checkmark$] Geometric thinking
  [$\checkmark$] Dynamic updates
*/

// ===============================================================
// DETAILED SOLUTION 10: CF 786C - Till I Collapse
// Link: https://codeforces.com/problemset/problem/786/C
// Difficulty: Very Hard (CF 2600+)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Array of N integers. For K = 1 to N: Find minimum number of segments
// such that each segment has at most K distinct elements.
//
// Technique: Binary Search + Segment Tree + Two Pointers
// ===============================================================

int minSegments(vector<int>& arr, int K) {
    int n = arr.size();
    map<int, int> freq;
    int distinct = 0;
    int segments = 0;
    int left = 0;
    
    for (int right = 0; right < n; right++) {
        if (freq[arr[right]]++ == 0) distinct++;
        
        while (distinct > K) {
            if (--freq[arr[left]] == 0) distinct--;
            left++;
        }
        
        // Can extend current segment to right
    }
    
    // For K distinct: Use greedy approach
    distinct = 0;
    freq.clear();
    segments = 1;
    
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        if (freq[arr[i]]++ == 0) distinct++;
        
        if (distinct > K) {
            segments++;
            freq.clear();
            freq[arr[i]] = 1;
            distinct = 1;
        }
    }
    
    return segments;
}

vector<int> allKValues(vector<int>& arr) {
    int n = arr.size();
    vector<int> result(n + 1);
    
    for (int K = 1; K <= n; K++) {
        result[K] = minSegments(arr, K);
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
GREEDY WITH BINARY SEARCH:

For Fixed K:
  Greedy: Extend segment as far as possible
  When > K distinct: Start new segment
  
  Time: O(N) with two pointers

For All K:
  Naive: Compute each K separately: O($N^2$)
  
  Optimization: Use monotonicity
    As K increases, segments decrease
    Binary search + clever reuse
  
  Time: O(N log N \times log N) with optimizations

Key Insight:
  Segments form optimal substructure
  Can use DP with segment tree optimization
  
  This is VERY HARD problem!

This tests:
  [$\checkmark$] Multiple techniques combined
  [$\checkmark$] Greedy algorithms
  [$\checkmark$] Two pointers
  [$\checkmark$] Optimization thinking
*/

\end{lstlisting}

\chapter{Geometric Binary Search}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,
    colback=white,
    colframe=secondaryblue,
    boxrule=1pt,
    arc=2mm,
    left=10pt,
    right=10pt
]
\textbf{\large\textcolor{primaryblue}{Computational Geometry, Circle Packing, and Spatial Optimization}}

\vspace{0.5cm}

\begin{center}
\textit{\textcolor{darkgray}{Difficulty: CF 2400-2800 $\bullet$ 20 Problems}}
\end{center}

\section{Spatial Monotonicity}

In geometry, Binary Search is typically applied to a "threshold" parameter.

\subsection{The Inclusion Logic}
If a circle of radius $R$ can cover a set of points, any circle with radius $R' > R$ can also cover them. This allows us to search for the **Minimum Enclosing Circle** radius by checking if there exists a point $P$ such that all given points are within distance $R$ of $P$.



\subsection{2D and 3D Search}
For problems involving coordinates, we often use nested binary searches or combine Binary Search with a sweep-line algorithm. For instance, in "Maximum Square in a Polygon," we binary search the side length $S$, and for a fixed $S$, the problem becomes checking the intersection of shifted polygonal boundaries.

\section{Geometric Binary Search: Complete Mastery}

\subsection{Complete Theoretical Foundation}

\subsubsection{Why Geometric Binary Search?}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=white,
    colframe=deepblue,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{Spatial Monotonicity and Continuous Optimization}
]
\textbf{When Geometry Meets Binary Search}

Problem: Optimize geometric parameters
Solution: Binary search on continuous/discrete spatial values

\textbf{The Geometric Paradigm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Classic Geometry: Find exact solutions analytically
Computational Geometry: Approximate with binary search

Three Core Patterns:

Pattern 1: Threshold on Radius/Distance
  Problem: Find minimum radius to cover points
  Property: Larger radius → more coverage (monotonic)
  
  Example: Minimum enclosing circle
    If radius R works → radius R+ε works
    Binary search for minimum R!

Pattern 2: Coordinate Optimization
  Problem: Find optimal point (x, y)
  Approach: Nested BS or ternary search
  
  Example: Meeting point minimizing sum of distances
    Ternary search on x, then y
    Or: Gradient descent with BS

Pattern 3: Area/Volume Optimization
  Problem: Maximize/minimize geometric measure
  Property: Measure monotonic in parameter
  
  Example: Pie cutting - maximize minimum slice
    Binary search on slice area
    Check if can divide into equal pieces

KEY INSIGHT:
  Geometric problems often have hidden monotonicity!
  Transform to threshold problem → Binary search
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Complete Theory:}

1. \textbf{Continuous vs Discrete Binary Search}
\begin{verbatim}
Continuous Domain (Real numbers):
  \[
  \text{Search space: } [a, b] \subset \mathbb{R}
  \]
  Termination: Fixed iterations (100-200)
  
  Algorithm:
    for i in 1..iterations:
      mid = (low + high) / 2.0
      if check(mid): high = mid
      else: low = mid
  
  Precision: After N iterations, error ≤ (b-a) / 2^N
  
  For N=100: Precision ≈ 10^-30 (beyond double!)

Discrete Domain (Integers):
  \[
  \text{Search space: } [a, b] \subset \mathbb{Z}
  \]
  Termination: while low < high
  
  Algorithm:
    while low < high:
      mid = (low + high) / 2
      if check(mid): high = mid
      else: low = mid + 1
  
  Result: Exact integer answer

Floating Point Cautions:
  Never use ==, <=, >=
  Use: abs(a - b) < epsilon
  
  Epsilon: 1e-9 typically safe
  For geometry: 1e-6 often sufficient

Conversion: Continuous → Discrete
  Multiply by 10^6, round to integer
  Do discrete BS
  Divide by 10^6 for answer
  
  Avoids floating point errors!
\end{verbatim}

2. \textbf{Geometric Monotonicity Properties}
\begin{verbatim}
Property 1: Radius Monotonicity
  If circle of radius R covers points
  Then circle of radius R' > R also covers
  
  Therefore: Binary search for minimum R

Property 2: Distance Monotonicity
  If meeting time T allows convergence
  Then time T' > T also allows
  
  Therefore: Binary search for minimum T

Property 3: Area Monotonicity
  If can divide into pieces of area A
  Then can divide into pieces of area A' < A
  
  Therefore: Binary search for maximum A

Counter-example (NOT monotonic):
  "Find exact point where three circles intersect"
  Multiple solutions possible
  No monotonicity!
  
  Cannot use binary search directly

Identifying Monotonicity:
  Ask: "If X works, does X+ε work?"
  If yes: Monotonic → BS applicable
  If no: Non-monotonic → Need other approach
\end{verbatim}

3. \textbf{2D and 3D Binary Search}
\begin{verbatim}
2D Binary Search: Nested Search

Approach 1: Ternary-Ternary
  Ternary search on X
  For each X: Ternary search on Y
  
  Time: O(iterations_x × iterations_y)
       = O(100 × 100) = O(10^4)
  
  Use when: Function unimodal in both dimensions

Approach 2: Binary-Ternary
  Binary search on one dimension
  Ternary on other
  
  Use when: One dimension has special structure

Approach 3: Gradient Descent + BS
  Use gradient descent for coarse search
  Binary search for refinement
  
  Hybrid: Fast and accurate

3D Search:
  Extend to three dimensions
  Triple nesting: O(100³) = O(10^6)
  
  Still feasible for most problems!

Optimization: Early Termination
  If improvement < epsilon: Stop
  Saves iterations in practice
\end{verbatim}

4. \textbf{Check Function Design}
\begin{verbatim}
For Geometric BS: Check function is critical!

Example: Minimum Enclosing Circle

Naive Check (radius R):
  Try all possible centers (infinite!)
  Time: Impossible
  
Optimized Check:
  Observation: Optimal center has special property
  
  For 2 points: Center on line between them
  For 3+ points: Can use iterative refinement
  
  Or: Use Welzl's algorithm O(N)
  
  Time: O(N) per check
  Total: O(N × log(precision))

Example: Meeting Point

Check (time T):
  For each person: Can reach circle of radius v*T
  Check: Do all circles intersect?
  
  Algorithm:
    Find intersection of all reachable regions
    If non-empty: Yes
    Else: No
  
  Time: O(N) per check

Key Principle:
  Check should be O(N) or O(N log N)
  Not O(N²) or worse!
  
  Total: O(N log precision) or O(N log N log precision)
\end{verbatim}

5. \textbf{Common Pitfalls and Solutions}
\begin{verbatim}
Pitfall 1: Floating Point Comparison
  WRONG: if (a == b)
  RIGHT: if (abs(a - b) < 1e-9)

Pitfall 2: Infinite Loop in Continuous BS
  WRONG: while (low <= high)
  RIGHT: for (int i = 0; i < 100; i++)

Pitfall 3: Wrong Update Direction
  Minimizing: high = mid when condition met
  Maximizing: low = mid when condition met
  
  Mix these up: Wrong answer!

Pitfall 4: Precision Loss
  Problem: Computing with very small/large numbers
  Solution: Normalize coordinates first

Pitfall 5: Edge Cases
  - All points at same location
  - Collinear points
  - Degenerate polygons
  
  Always test edge cases!

Best Practices:
  ✓ Use fixed iterations for continuous
  ✓ Normalize coordinates
  ✓ Check edge cases
  ✓ Verify monotonicity
  ✓ Test precision requirements
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Key Insight:}
Geometry + BS = Solve impossible optimization problems!
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Pattern 1: Coordinate Binary Search}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,breakable,
    colback=successgreen!10,
    colframe=successgreen,
    boxrule=1.5pt,
    arc=3mm,
    title=\textbf{Complete Coordinate Optimization Algorithms}
]
\textbf{Finding Optimal Meeting Points and Positions}

\textbf{Problem Type: Find point minimizing some metric}

\textbf{Algorithm:}
\begin{verbatim}
Ternary search on X coordinate
For each X: Ternary search on Y
Return best (X, Y)

Time: O(iterations² × check_cost)
\end{verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=COMPLETE Coordinate BS Solutions - DETAILED]
// ===============================================================
// DETAILED SOLUTION 1: CF 782B - The Meeting Place
// Link: https://codeforces.com/problemset/problem/782/B
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 2200-2300)
//
// Problem Statement:
// N people at positions x[i] with maximum speeds v[i] on a line.
// Find minimum time T such that all people can meet at some point.
//
// Technique: Binary Search on Time + Intersection of Intervals
// ===============================================================

bool canMeetInTime(vector<double>& positions, vector<double>& speeds, double time) {
    // Each person can reach interval [x[i] - v[i]*T, x[i] + v[i]*T]
    // Check if intersection of all intervals is non-empty
    
    double leftMost = -1e18;
    double rightMost = 1e18;
    
    for (int i = 0; i < positions.size(); i++) {
        double reachLeft = positions[i] - speeds[i] * time;
        double reachRight = positions[i] + speeds[i] * time;
        
        leftMost = max(leftMost, reachLeft);
        rightMost = min(rightMost, reachRight);
    }
    
    return leftMost <= rightMost + 1e-9;  // With epsilon for floating point
}

double minimumMeetingTime(vector<double>& positions, vector<double>& speeds) {
    double low = 0.0;
    double high = 1e9;  // Large enough upper bound
    
    // Binary search on time
    for (int iter = 0; iter < 100; iter++) {
        double mid = (low + high) / 2.0;
        
        if (canMeetInTime(positions, speeds, mid)) {
            high = mid;
        } else {
            low = mid;
        }
    }
    
    return high;
}

/*
COMPLETE GEOMETRIC ANALYSIS:

Problem Interpretation:
  Each person creates a "reachable interval"
  Person at x with speed v can reach [x-vT, x+vT] in time T
  
  Meeting possible <==> Intervals intersect

Intersection Check:
  Intersection = [max(left bounds), min(right bounds)]
  Non-empty <==> max(left) <= min(right)

Monotonicity:
  If can meet in time T -> can meet in time T+\varepsilon 
  Intervals only expand with more time
  
  Pattern: F F F T T T
  Find first True (minimum time)

Binary Search:
  Continuous domain: Time \in [0, \infty)
  Fixed iterations: 100 sufficient
  
  Precision: 2^-100 = 10^-30

Edge Cases:
  1. All at same position: T = 0
  2. Two people, opposite directions: May be impossible
  3. One person: T = 0

WHY THIS IS CORRECT:

Interval Intersection:
  n intervals: [l1,r1], [l2,r2], ..., [ln,rn]
  Intersection = [max(li), min(ri)]
  
  Empty if max(li) > min(ri)

Time: O(100 \times N) = O(N)
  100 iterations
  Each: O(N) interval check

Space: O(1)

Real-World Applications:
  - Delivery route optimization
  - Rendezvous planning
  - Network synchronization

This tests:
  [$\checkmark$] Continuous binary search
  [$\checkmark$] Interval intersection
  [$\checkmark$] Floating point handling
  [$\checkmark$] Geometric reasoning
*/

// ===============================================================
// DETAILED SOLUTION 2: LeetCode 1515 - Best Position for Service Centre
// Link: https://leetcode.com/problems/best-position-for-a-service-centre/
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 2100-2200)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Given N customers at positions (xi, yi). Find point (x, y) minimizing
// sum of Euclidean distances to all customers.
//
// Technique: 2D Ternary Search (Weiszfeld Algorithm Alternative)
// ===============================================================

class Solution {
public:
    double calculateDistance(vector<vector<int>>& positions, double x, double y) {
        double total = 0.0;
        for (auto& pos : positions) {
            double dx = pos[0] - x;
            double dy = pos[1] - y;
            total += sqrt(dx * dx + dy * dy);
        }
        return total;
    }
    
    double ternarySearchY(vector<vector<int>>& positions, double x) {
        double low = 0.0;
        double high = 100.0;
        
        for (int iter = 0; iter < 100; iter++) {
            double m1 = low + (high - low) / 3.0;
            double m2 = high - (high - low) / 3.0;
            
            double dist1 = calculateDistance(positions, x, m1);
            double dist2 = calculateDistance(positions, x, m2);
            
            if (dist1 > dist2) {
                low = m1;
            } else {
                high = m2;
            }
        }
        
        return low;
    }
    
    double getMinDistSum(vector<vector<int>>& positions) {
        double low = 0.0;
        double high = 100.0;
        double bestDist = 1e9;
        
        // Ternary search on X
        for (int iter = 0; iter < 100; iter++) {
            double m1 = low + (high - low) / 3.0;
            double m2 = high - (high - low) / 3.0;
            
            double y1 = ternarySearchY(positions, m1);
            double y2 = ternarySearchY(positions, m2);
            
            double dist1 = calculateDistance(positions, m1, y1);
            double dist2 = calculateDistance(positions, m2, y2);
            
            bestDist = min(bestDist, min(dist1, dist2));
            
            if (dist1 > dist2) {
                low = m1;
            } else {
                high = m2;
            }
        }
        
        return bestDist;
    }
};

/*
2D TERNARY SEARCH MASTERY:

Problem: Geometric Median
  Find point minimizing sum of Euclidean distances
  
  Function: f(x,y) = sum sqrt((x-xi)^2 + (y-yi)^2)
  
  Properties:
    - Continuous
    - Convex
    - Has unique global minimum

Unimodal Property:
  For fixed x: f(x,y) is unimodal in y
  For fixed y: f(x,y) is unimodal in x
  
  Perfect for 2D ternary search!

Algorithm:
  Outer ternary: Search optimal x
  Inner ternary: For each x, search optimal y
  
  Nested structure finds global minimum

Convergence:
  100 iterations per dimension
  Total: 100^2 = 10,000 evaluations
  
  Precision: (100/3)^100 = 10^-48

Alternative: Weiszfeld Algorithm
  Iterative formula:
    x_new = sum(xi / di) / sum(1 / di)
    y_new = sum(yi / di) / sum(1 / di)
  
  where di = distance to point i
  
  Converges faster but more complex!

WHY THIS IS OPTIMAL:

Time: O(N \times iterations^2)
     = O(N \times 10,000) = O($10^4$N)
  
  For N=100: 10^6 operations (fast!)

Space: O(1)

Comparison:
  Gradient descent: Requires tuning
  Ternary search: No tuning needed!
  
  Ternary: Simple and reliable

Real-World:
  - Warehouse location
  - Server placement
  - Emergency response center

This tests:
  [$\checkmark$] 2D ternary search
  [$\checkmark$] Geometric median
  [$\checkmark$] Nested optimization
  [$\checkmark$] Convexity recognition
*/

// ===============================================================
// DETAILED SOLUTION 3: UVA 10341 - Solve It
// Link: https://onlinejudge.org/index.php?...&problem=1282
// Difficulty: Medium-Hard (CF 1900-2000)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Find x \in [0,1] such that:
// p*e^(-x) + q*sin(x) + r*cos(x) + s*tan(x) + t*$x^2$ + u = 0
//
// Technique: Bisection Method (Binary Search for Root Finding)
// ===============================================================

double evaluateFunction(double x, double p, double q, double r, 
                       double s, double t, double u) {
    return p * exp(-x) + q * sin(x) + r * cos(x) 
         + s * tan(x) + t * x * x + u;
}

double solveEquation(double p, double q, double r, 
                    double s, double t, double u) {
    double low = 0.0;
    double high = 1.0;
    
    double fLow = evaluateFunction(low, p, q, r, s, t, u);
    double fHigh = evaluateFunction(high, p, q, r, s, t, u);
    
    // Check if root exists (opposite signs required)
    if (fLow * fHigh > 0) {
        return -1.0;  // No root in [0,1]
    }
    
    // Bisection method
    for (int iter = 0; iter < 100; iter++) {
        double mid = (low + high) / 2.0;
        double fMid = evaluateFunction(mid, p, q, r, s, t, u);
        
        if (abs(fMid) < 1e-9) {
            return mid;  // Found root
        }
        
        if (fLow * fMid < 0) {
            high = mid;
            fHigh = fMid;
        } else {
            low = mid;
            fLow = fMid;
        }
    }
    
    return (low + high) / 2.0;
}

/*
BISECTION METHOD - ROOT FINDING:

Mathematical Foundation:
  Intermediate Value Theorem:
    If f continuous on [a,b]
    And f(a) \times f(b) < 0
    Then \exists  c \in (a,b) where f(c) = 0

Algorithm:
  Start with [a, b] where f(a) \times f(b) < 0
  Repeatedly:
    c = (a + b) / 2
    If f(c) = 0: Found root!
    If f(a) \times f(c) < 0: Root in [a,c]
    Else: Root in [c,b]

Convergence:
  After n iterations: Error <= (b-a) / 2^n
  
  For [0,1] and 100 iterations:
    Error <= 1 / 2^100 = 10^-30

Function Properties:
  Combination of:
    - Exponential: e^(-x)
    - Trigonometric: sin, cos, tan
    - Polynomial: x^2
  
  Complex but continuous!

Why Bisection Works:
  Only requires: Continuity + opposite signs
  No derivatives needed
  Always converges (if root exists)

Time: O(iterations) = O(100)
Space: O(1)

Alternative: Newton-Raphson
  Requires derivative
  Faster convergence
  But may not converge!
  
  Bisection: Guaranteed but slower

Applications:
  - Solving transcendental equations
  - Game AI (decision thresholds)
  - Physics simulations

This tests:
  [$\checkmark$] Bisection method
  [$\checkmark$] Root finding
  [$\checkmark$] Continuous functions
  [$\checkmark$] Floating point precision
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Circle and Area Solutions - DETAILED]
// ===============================================================
// DETAILED SOLUTION 4: SPOJ PIE - Pie Distribution
// Link: https://www.spoj.com/problems/PIE/
// Difficulty: Medium (CF 1700-1800)
//
// Problem Statement:
// N circular pies with radii r[i]. Divide into F+1 pieces of equal area.
// Find maximum possible area of each piece.
//
// Technique: Binary Search on Area
// ===============================================================

bool canDivide(vector<double>& radii, int pieces, double targetArea) {
    int totalPieces = 0;
    
    for (double r : radii) {
        double pieArea = M_PI * r * r;
        totalPieces += (int)(pieArea / targetArea);
        
        if (totalPieces >= pieces) return true;
    }
    
    return totalPieces >= pieces;
}

double maximumPieceArea(vector<double>& radii, int people) {
    int pieces = people + 1;  // F people + 1 (you)
    
    double low = 0.0;
    double high = 0.0;
    
    // Find maximum possible area
    for (double r : radii) {
        high += M_PI * r * r;
    }
    
    // Binary search on piece area
    for (int iter = 0; iter < 100; iter++) {
        double mid = (low + high) / 2.0;
        
        if (canDivide(radii, pieces, mid)) {
            low = mid;  // Can achieve this, try larger
        } else {
            high = mid;
        }
    }
    
    return low;
}

/*
PIE CUTTING OPTIMIZATION:

Problem Analysis:
  Have N pies with areas A[i]
  Want F+1 equal pieces
  Maximize area per piece

Key Insight:
  For target area X:
    From pie i: Get floor(A[i] / X) pieces
    Total pieces: sum(floor(A[i] / X))
  
  Larger X -> Fewer pieces (monotonic!)

Monotonicity:
  If area X achievable -> area X- \varepsilon achievable
  If area X not achievable -> area X+\varepsilon not achievable
  
  Pattern: T T T F F F
  Find last True (maximum)

Binary Search Direction:
  Maximizing area -> low = mid when achievable

Floating Point:
  Area involves \phi
  Use double precision
  Fixed iterations for stability

Check Function:
  For each pie: Count pieces of size X
  Sum all pieces
  Compare with required (F+1)
  
  Time: O(N) per check

Total Time: O(N \times 100) = O(N)
Space: O(1)

Edge Cases:
  - Total area < required pieces: Impossible
  - One large pie: Simple division
  - Many small pies: Need aggregation

Real-World:
  - Resource allocation
  - Fair division problems
  - Cake cutting (literally!)

This tests:
  [$\checkmark$] Binary search on continuous
  [$\checkmark$] Maximize vs minimize direction
  [$\checkmark$] Floating point precision
  [$\checkmark$] Geometric area calculation
*/

// ===============================================================
// DETAILED SOLUTION 5: AtCoder ABC 151F - Enclosing Circle
// Link: https://atcoder.jp/contests/abc151/tasks/abc151_f
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 2200-2300)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Given N points in 2D plane. Find minimum radius of circle that
// encloses all points.
//
// Technique: Binary Search on Radius + Circle Center Check
// ===============================================================

struct Point {
    double x, y;
    
    double distTo(const Point& other) const {
        double dx = x - other.x;
        double dy = y - other.y;
        return sqrt(dx * dx + dy * dy);
    }
};

bool canEncloseWithRadius(vector<Point>& points, double radius) {
    int n = points.size();
    
    if (n == 1) return true;
    
    // Try all pairs as potential diameter
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        for (int j = i + 1; j < n; j++) {
            double dist = points[i].distTo(points[j]);
            
            if (dist > 2 * radius + 1e-9) continue;
            
            // Find center of circle passing through i and j
            double midX = (points[i].x + points[j].x) / 2.0;
            double midY = (points[i].y + points[j].y) / 2.0;
            
            double halfDist = dist / 2.0;
            if (halfDist > radius + 1e-9) continue;
            
            double offset = sqrt(radius * radius - halfDist * halfDist);
            
            // Direction perpendicular to i-j
            double dx = points[j].x - points[i].x;
            double dy = points[j].y - points[i].y;
            double perpX = -dy / dist;
            double perpY = dx / dist;
            
            // Try both possible centers
            for (int sign = -1; sign <= 1; sign += 2) {
                Point center;
                center.x = midX + sign * offset * perpX;
                center.y = midY + sign * offset * perpY;
                
                // Check if all points within radius
                bool allInside = true;
                for (const Point& p : points) {
                    if (p.distTo(center) > radius + 1e-9) {
                        allInside = false;
                        break;
                    }
                }
                
                if (allInside) return true;
            }
        }
    }
    
    // Also check if all points fit in circle centered at any point
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        bool allInside = true;
        for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) {
            if (points[i].distTo(points[j]) > radius + 1e-9) {
                allInside = false;
                break;
            }
        }
        if (allInside) return true;
    }
    
    return false;
}

double minimumEnclosingRadius(vector<Point>& points) {
    double low = 0.0;
    double high = 2000.0;  // Coordinate range
    
    // Binary search on radius
    for (int iter = 0; iter < 100; iter++) {
        double mid = (low + high) / 2.0;
        
        if (canEncloseWithRadius(points, mid)) {
            high = mid;
        } else {
            low = mid;
        }
    }
    
    return high;
}

/*
MINIMUM ENCLOSING CIRCLE:

Mathematical Properties:
  For N points, minimum enclosing circle has:
    - 2 points on diameter, OR
    - 3+ points on circumference
  
  This limits center candidates!

Algorithm Strategy:
  Binary search on radius R
  Check: \exists  center where all points within R?

Check Function:
  Try all O($N^2$) pairs as potential diameter
  For each pair:
    - Find two possible centers
    - Check if all points within R
  
  If any works: Return true

Geometric Construction:
  Given two points A, B and radius R:
    Center on perpendicular bisector
    Distance from midpoint: sqrt($R^2$ - (|AB|/2)^2)
  
  Two possible centers (both sides)

Complexity:
  Check: O($N^3$) - Try O($N^2$) pairs, check O(N) points each
  Binary search: O(100) iterations
  Total: O(100 \times $N^3$) = O($N^3$)
  
  For N=50: ~12.5M operations (acceptable)

Optimization: Welzl's Algorithm
  Randomized O(N) algorithm
  More complex to implement
  
  Our approach: Simpler, sufficient for N<=50

Precision:
  100 iterations: Error < 10^-28
  More than enough!

Real-World:
  - Coverage optimization
  - Facility location
  - Surveillance planning

This tests:
  [$\checkmark$] Computational geometry
  [$\checkmark$] Circle equations
  [$\checkmark$] Geometric construction
  [$\checkmark$] Complex check function
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Pattern 3: Distance Optimization}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++, caption=Distance Optimization - DETAILED SOLUTIONS]
// ===============================================================
// DETAILED SOLUTION 6: USACO Gold - Angry Cows
// Link: http://www.usaco.org/index.php?page=viewproblem2&cpid=597
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 2100-2200)
//
// Problem Statement:
// N haybales at positions x[i] on a line. Start explosion at one position
// with radius R. Any haybale within R explodes with radius R, creating
// chain reaction. Find minimum R to explode all haybales.
//
// Technique: Binary Search on Radius + Greedy Simulation
// ===============================================================

bool canExplodeAll(vector<int>& positions, double radius, int K) {
    int n = positions.size();
    vector<bool> exploded(n, false);
    
    // Try starting from each position
    for (int start = 0; start < n; start++) {
        fill(exploded.begin(), exploded.end(), false);
        
        // Simulate explosion
        queue<int> q;
        q.push(start);
        exploded[start] = true;
        
        while (!q.empty()) {
            int pos = q.front();
            q.pop();
            
            // Explode neighbors within radius
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
                if (!exploded[i] && 
                    abs(positions[i] - positions[pos]) <= radius + 1e-9) {
                    exploded[i] = true;
                    q.push(i);
                }
            }
        }
        
        // Check if all exploded
        bool allExploded = true;
        for (bool ex : exploded) {
            if (!ex) {
                allExploded = false;
                break;
            }
        }
        
        if (allExploded) return true;
    }
    
    return false;
}

double minimumExplosionRadius(vector<int>& positions, int K) {
    sort(positions.begin(), positions.end());
    
    double low = 0.0;
    double high = positions.back() - positions.front();
    
    // Binary search on radius
    for (int iter = 0; iter < 100; iter++) {
        double mid = (low + high) / 2.0;
        
        if (canExplodeAll(positions, mid, K)) {
            high = mid;
        } else {
            low = mid;
        }
    }
    
    return high;
}

/*
CHAIN REACTION OPTIMIZATION:

Problem Mechanics:
  Explosion propagates through space
  Each haybale explodes neighbors within R
  Chain reaction continues
  
  Find minimum R for full coverage

Simulation:
  BFS from starting position
  Mark exploded haybales
  Add neighbors to queue
  
  Check if all haybales exploded

Optimization:
  Try each position as start
  Some positions better than others
  
  For sorted array: Middle positions often best

Monotonicity:
  Larger radius -> More reach -> More likely full coverage
  
  Pattern: F F F T T T
  Find first True

Time: O(100 \times N \times $N^2$) = O($N^3$)
  100 iterations
  N starting positions
  $N^2$ BFS per start
  
  For N=100: Tight but feasible

Optimization Possible:
  Greedy: Only need 2 starting positions
    Leftmost and rightmost reach analysis
  
  Can reduce to O(100 \times $N^2$)

Real-World:
  - Forest fire simulation
  - Virus spread modeling
  - Network failure cascades

This tests:
  [$\checkmark$] Chain reaction simulation
  [$\checkmark$] BFS for propagation
  [$\checkmark$] Binary search on physical parameter
  [$\checkmark$] Multiple starting points
*/

// ===============================================================
// DETAILED SOLUTION 7: CF 1117C - Magic Ship
// Link: https://codeforces.com/problemset/problem/1117/C
// Difficulty: Hard (CF 2100-2200)
//
// Problem Statement:
// Ship at (x1,y1) wants to reach (x2,y2). Each day: move in one of
// 4 directions, then wind displaces by periodic pattern. Find minimum days.
//
// Technique: Binary Search on Days + Periodic Movement Simulation
// ===============================================================

struct Point {
    long long x, y;
    
    Point operator+(const Point& other) const {
        return {x + other.x, y + other.y};
    }
    
    Point operator*(long long k) const {
        return {x * k, y * k};
    }
    
    long long manhattanDist(const Point& other) const {
        return abs(x - other.x) + abs(y - other.y);
    }
};

bool canReach(Point start, Point target, vector<Point>& windDeltas, 
              Point periodSum, long long days) {
    // Wind displacement after 'days' days
    long long fullPeriods = days / windDeltas.size();
    long long remaining = days % windDeltas.size();
    
    Point windTotal = periodSum * fullPeriods;
    
    for (int i = 0; i < remaining; i++) {
        windTotal = windTotal + windDeltas[i];
    }
    
    // After days with wind, position is: start + wind
    // Can move up to 'days' Manhattan distance
    Point afterWind = start + windTotal;
    
    return afterWind.manhattanDist(target) <= days;
}

long long minimumDays(Point start, Point target, string wind) {
    int n = wind.size();
    vector<Point> windDeltas(n);
    Point periodSum = {0, 0};
    
    // Parse wind pattern
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        if (wind[i] == 'U') windDeltas[i] = {0, 1};
        else if (wind[i] == 'D') windDeltas[i] = {0, -1};
        else if (wind[i] == 'L') windDeltas[i] = {-1, 0};
        else windDeltas[i] = {1, 0};
        
        periodSum = periodSum + windDeltas[i];
    }
    
    // Binary search on number of days
    long long low = 0;
    long long high = 1e15;  // Large enough
    long long result = -1;
    
    while (low <= high) {
        long long mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
        
        if (canReach(start, target, windDeltas, periodSum, mid)) {
            result = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    
    return result;
}

/*
PERIODIC MOVEMENT ANALYSIS:

Problem Complexity:
  - Ship can move (controllable)
  - Wind displaces (periodic, uncontrollable)
  - Find minimum time to reach target

Key Insight: Periodic Pattern
  Wind repeats every N days
  After K full periods: Displacement = K \times period_sum
  Plus: Remaining days' displacement
  
  Efficient calculation!

Reachability:
  After D days with wind at position P:
    Can reach any point within Manhattan distance D
    
  Check: dist(P, target) <= D?

Binary Search:
  On number of days
  Pattern: F F F T T T
  Find first True

Handling Large Numbers:
  Days up to 10^15
  Need long long
  Period optimization crucial

Time: O(log(max_days) \times N)
  log(10^15) = 50 iterations
  Each check: O(N) for remaining days
  Total: O(50N)

Space: O(N)

Edge Cases:
  - Already at target: 0 days
  - Wind helps: Fewer days needed
  - Wind opposes: More days needed
  - Impossible: Return -1

Real-World:
  - Navigation planning
  - Ocean current routing
  - Wind-powered travel

This tests:
  [$\checkmark$] Periodic pattern handling
  [$\checkmark$] Large number binary search
  [$\checkmark$] Manhattan distance
  [$\checkmark$] Movement simulation
*/
\end{lstlisting}

\chapter{Alien's Trick and Lagrange Multipliers}

\begin{tcolorbox}[
    enhanced,
    colback=white,
    colframe=secondaryblue,
    boxrule=1pt,
    arc=2mm,
    left=10pt,
    right=10pt
]
\textbf{\large\textcolor{primaryblue}{WQS Binary Search, Penalty Method, and Convex Optimization}}
